# Field notes — English

522 species. Each note gives the bird's jizz, plumage, bare parts, what to use in the field, and the species it can be confused with.

Compiled from the identification sections of the English, German and Swedish Wikipedia (CC BY-SA 4.0), with body length, wingspan and mass cross-checked between those editions; see `scripts/field_id.json` for the sources behind each entry.

## African Blue Tit
*Cyanistes teneriffae* · `afbtit2`

- Jizz — 11–12 cm, about 10 g. Small, compact, sharp-billed tit; slightly smaller than Eurasian Blue Tit.
- Plumage — Forehead and supercilium white to mid-nape, framing a deep glossy blue crown blackening on the neck; blackish lores and eye-stripe joined across the blue-grey nape, with black chin and collar enclosing white cheeks; back, rump and coverts blue-grey; underparts deep yellow with a black belly stripe. Sexes alike. Juvenile: supercilium, nape and ear-coverts yellow, upperparts greenish-blue, belly stripe usually absent.
- Bare parts — Bill small and pointed, slate-grey to black with paler cutting edges; iris dark brown to black; legs dark grey.
- In the field — Song a variable repetition of one or two notes; some calls recall Great Tit, others Crested Tit.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blue Tit: Back yellowish-green, not plain grey-blue; crown paler blue and less sharply demarcated; wing lacks the ultramarine tone and shows a white bar, which Canary Islands birds lack; 10.5–12 cm.
  - Coal Tit: Head glossy blue-black with a large white nape spot and two white wingbars; underparts whitish to rufous-buff, never yellow with a black stripe; 10–11.5 cm.
  - Crested Tit: Sources give only range and mass, 10.8–11 g; go on this bird's white-framed blue crown, grey-blue back and yellow black-striped underparts.
  - Great Tit: Much larger at 12.5–15 cm, 14–22 g; crown and bib bluish-black, cheeks white, back green, breast lemon-yellow with a broad black mid-line to the vent.

## African Chaffinch
*Fringilla spodiogenys* · `afrcha1`

- Jizz — 13.8–18.5 cm, 21–24 g. Medium-sized to large finch.
- Plumage — Male: head blue-grey with a small black patch on the forehead; mantle moss-green; wings black with two bold white stripes and white-edged tertials and secondaries; tail blue-grey, white-sided; throat, breast and belly pale pink, whitening on lower belly and undertail. Winter male duller, head grey-brown, mantle brownish-green. Female and juvenile: greyish brown, wings striped dark brown and white, underparts paler.
- Bare parts — Bill silvery blue-grey with a black tip; legs dark pinkish-brown, brown in female and juvenile.
- In the field — Shares northwest Africa in winter with Eurasian chaffinch, which occurs there as a non-breeding visitor.
- Similar species —
  - Common Chaffinch: Cheeks, throat and breast dull rust-red and the mantle brown over an olive-green rump, not moss-green; African is pink below with a plain grey face; 14–16 cm.
  - Brambling: White rump, orange breast against a white belly and orange scapulars; breeding male black-headed — never a moss-green mantle with pink underparts.
  - White-winged Crossbill: Crossed bill and forked tail; male raspberry-red, female greenish-yellow. Both show two white wing-bars but never a blue-grey head over pink underparts.
  - Crimson-winged Finch: Sources give little: a pale, thickset finch with a heavy, dull yellowish bill against the African chaffinch's slim silvery blue-grey one; 13–18 cm.

## African Sacred Ibis
*Threskiornis aethiopicus* · `sacibi2`

- Jizz — 65–75 cm; wingspan 112–124 cm, 1350–1500 g. Large white ibis with a bald black head and neck and a thick, strongly decurved bill.
- Plumage — Body white; blue-black ornamental plumes over the square tail and the closed wing. Wing tips black, forming a broad black rear border in flight. Bare red skin runs along the leading underwing and on the breast sides, deepest when breeding. Juvenile: dirty white, some feathering on the neck, greenish-brown scapulars, more black in the primary coverts.
- Bare parts — Head, neck, bill and legs black, the decurved bill greyer; iris brown with a dark red orbital ring.
- In the field — Males slightly larger, otherwise sexes alike and no seasonal change. Usually silent, occasionally a puppy-like yelping.
- Similar species —
  - Glossy Ibis: Dark throughout — reddish-brown body, iridescent green-to-purple wings — not white; smaller, 48–66 cm, and the head is feathered, not bald and black.
  - Eurasian Spoonbill: Also white, but with a flat, spoon-tipped black bill with a yellow tip, a feathered white head with a nape crest, and no black wing border.

## Alexandrine Parakeet
*Psittacula eupatria* · `alepar2`

- Jizz — 50–62 cm, 200–300 g. One of the largest parakeets; narrow wings and a very long, narrow, tapering tail, 28–35 cm of it; bill massive.
- Plumage — Largely green; light blue-grey sheen on cheeks and nape; abdomen yellow-green; red shoulder patch, on a perched bird a red-brown block on the inner forewing. Uppertail green shading to blue, tipped yellow; undertail yellow. Male: black stripe across the lower cheek and a pink nape band. Female: both lacking. Juvenile: as female but shorter-tailed.
- Bare parts — Bill massive, red with yellow tips.
- In the field — Asian species; two feral populations in Germany from escaped cage birds, established since 2012.
- Similar species —
  - Rose-ringed Parakeet: Smaller, 37–43 cm against 50–62 cm; bill far slighter and never that massive red wedge; no red shoulder patch. Male shows a pink and black neck ring.

## Alpine Accentor
*Prunella collaris* · `alpacc1`

- Jizz — 15–17.5 cm, 25–35 g. Large, robust accentor, sparrow-sized and bigger than dunnock; bill fine and pointed.
- Plumage — Head and breast grey; throat finely vermiculated black and white, showing frontally as a narrow white bar; flanks and underparts streaked red-brown; upperparts streaked black, grey and brown. Wing-coverts black, edged in fresh autumn plumage by two white bars; tail tip whitish. Sexes alike. Juvenile: head browner, throat plain grey, underparts spotted brownish.
- Bare parts — Bill sharp and black with a yellow-orange base.
- In the field — Song fast and varied; also a strong lark-like call and a thrush-like one.
- Similar species —
  - Dunnock: Smaller, 13–14.5 cm, and drabber: brown below without red-brown streaking, head dull grey, throat plain; flicks its wings frequently.
  - Radde's Accentor: Smaller, 13–14 cm, with dark cheeks and crown and a broad dirty-white supercilium; underparts warm beige, orange-toned on the breast, not streaked red-brown.
  - House Sparrow: Stout conical seed-eater's bill against the accentor's fine pointed one; compact, big rounded head, short tail; 14–18 cm, 24–39.5 g.

## Alpine Swift
*Tachymarptis melba* · `alpswi1`

- Jizz — 20–23 cm, wingspan 51–58 cm, 76–125 g. By far the largest western Palearctic swift; streamlined body, long sickle-shaped pointed wings, broad shallow-forked tail; can suggest a falcon or a large swallow.
- Plumage — Upperparts olive-brown, wing-tips looking blacker. Underside diagnostic: white throat and whole fore-belly cut by a sharply defined dark breast-band, the oval white belly patch ringed by olive-brown flanks and undertail-coverts. Sexes alike; throat patch often hard to see. Races vary from pale grey-brown to blackish.
- Bare parts — Bill black; iris brown-black; feet black in adults, flesh-coloured in juveniles, claws black.
- In the field — Glides and hawks insects on the wing. Breeds in mountains from southern Europe to the Himalaya; strongly migratory, wintering in southern Africa and wandering widely.
- Similar species —
  - Barn Swallow: Steel-blue above with rufous forehead and throat and deeply forked tail streamers; far smaller, 17–19 cm, wingspan 32–34.5 cm against 51–58 cm.
  - Common Swift: Entirely blackish-brown but for a small pale chin patch — no white belly oval or breast-band; much smaller, 16–17 cm, wingspan 38–40 cm.
  - Pallid Swift: Dark with a large white throat patch only; no white belly oval and no dark breast-band; smaller, 16–17 cm, browner, scaly-looking on the belly.

## American Barn Owl
*Tyto alba* · `brnowl`

- Jizz — 33–39 cm, wingspan 80–95 cm. Medium-sized, pale, slim owl; long wings, long legs, short squarish tail; heart-shaped facial disc.
- Plumage — Facial disc very pale — white to pale grey or faintly rufous — with black eyes, like a flat mask with oversized oblique eye-slits. Head and upperparts pale brown to grey, often golden-brown finely speckled grey. Underparts vary from pure white to pale brown; subspecies differ markedly, guttata darker-backed with a yellow-orange breast.
- Bare parts — Bill pale yellow; iris dark brown to black; claws horn-coloured; toes nearly bare, dark grey-brown.
- In the field — Flight undulating on long wings with the feathered legs dangling, looking long-necked and short-tailed; hovers before dropping on prey. Tail shape separates it from typical owls in the air.
- Similar species —
  - Tawny Owl: Robust and darker, 37–46 cm; large round head with a plain facial disc and dark brown eyes; rufous or grey-brown above, whitish below streaked brown.

## Arctic Loon
*Gavia arctica* · `arcloo`

- Jizz — 58–75 cm; 1.3–3.4 kg. One of the smaller Gavia: slim, long-bodied, dagger-billed; slightly larger than a Mallard but far more elongated. Sexes alike.
- Plumage — Breeding: grey crown and hindneck; black, purple-glossed throat and foreneck; white necklace of short lines on lower throat, long white stripes down neck-sides onto chest; blackish above with bold white squares on mantle and scapulars, white spots on lesser and median coverts; flanks and underparts white. Non-breeding: upperparts plain blackish-grey, chin to foreneck sharply white. Juvenile: browner.
- Bare parts — Bill black to blackish-grey, pale grey in non-breeding; legs black outside, grey inside; webs grey or flesh; iris deep brown-red.
- In the field — Swims low with the dagger bill held horizontal; head passes into the neck with almost no step.
- Similar species —
  - Red-throated Loon: Smallest, lightest loon, 53–69 cm against 58–75 cm; sources give nothing beyond size, so fall back on Arctic's white mantle squares and glossy black throat.
  - Common Loon: Larger and heavier, 66–91 cm and 2.2–7.6 kg against 58–75 cm and 1.3–3.4 kg; the sources give nothing else to separate them.
  - Yellow-billed Loon: Largest loon, 76–97 cm and 4–6.4 kg; breeding adult likewise black and white with purple gloss, so separated here on size.

## Arctic Tern
*Sterna paradisaea* · `arcter`

- Jizz — Wingspan 66–85 cm, length 33–39 cm across the sources, adult tail 7–11.5 cm of it; 86–127 g. Slim, long-winged, long-tailed; tail forked at least half its length and projecting past the closed wingtips; legs very short.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: crown and nape solid black, cheeks and upper throat white; upperparts and upperwing even pale grey, underparts grey-toned, primaries translucent from below with a narrow black line along the outer trailing edge; tail white. Winter: forehead and crown white. Juvenile: no grey belly, cap not reaching the forehead, back brownish and streaked, dark carpal bar, short streamers.
- Bare parts — Bill uniform dark blood-red without a black tip when breeding, black in winter; legs and webs short, red, grey-black in winter.
- In the field — Flight light, buoyant and springy; the wings look set forward of the bird's mid-point. Call nasal and rasping, kirrarr; alarm call at intruders in the colony and an advertising call are the commonest.
- Similar species —
  - Common Tern: Bill red with a black tip, cap running well down the nape, legs longer, tail no longer than the folded wingtips, wings set mid-body.
  - Armenian Gull: A large gull (52–62 cm, span 120–145 cm, 600–960 g), grey-backed with a heavy black-banded bill and dark eyes; nothing tern-like in shape.
  - Audouin's Gull: Sources give nothing of its plumage or size: separated here only as a large Mediterranean gull against a 33–39 cm black-capped, deep-forked tern.
  - Black-headed Gull: A gull: 34–39 cm, span 100–110 cm, chocolate hood in summer, white leading edge to the outer wing, black primary tips; no tail streamers.

## Arctic Warbler
*Phylloscopus borealis* · `arcwar1`

- Jizz — 11.5–13 cm, 7.5–15 g (average about 10 g); one of the larger leaf warblers, about Wood Warbler size and bigger than Willow Warbler. Body strong but head small; bill heavy and dagger-like.
- Plumage — Upperparts greyish-green to olive-brown, underparts off-white with some small streaks on the breast; long, clear supercilium and dark eye-stripe; a single narrow white wingbar.
- Bare parts — Bill heavy, with a dark tip to the lower mandible.
- In the field — Song a fast, low-pitched whirring trill. Call quite unlike other Phylloscopus — a sharp dzri recalling a Dipper. Breeds in birch or mixed birch forest near water; insectivorous, nest on the ground in a low shrub.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Smaller, 11–12.5 cm, with plain wings and no wingbar; pale pinkish-yellow legs, longer paler bill; song a simple repetitive descending whistle.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Smaller and dumpier, 10–12 cm; no wingbar, only a short whitish supercilium, short primary projection; dark legs, fine dark bill; call a monosyllabic hweet.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Much smaller, 9.5–11 cm; double yellowish-white wingbars, yellow-margined tertials and a long yellow supercilium; almost constantly in motion.
  - Greenish Warbler: Very close, also one wingbar, but slightly smaller with a thinner bill lacking the dark tip to the lower mandible; song a high jerky trill.

## Armenian Gull
*Larus armenicus* · `armgul1`

- Jizz — 52–62 cm, wingspan 120–145 cm, 600–960 g. Smallest of the herring gull complex; bill short and blunt, 41–57 mm; wingtip compact, the black often looking triangular.
- Plumage — Adult: head, breast and underparts white in summer; in winter head finely streaked brown, more heavily on neck and breast sides. Upperparts medium grey; extensive black wingtip with only small white mirrors; broad white trailing edge to the inner wing. First-winter: mainly brown with whitish rump, pale inner primaries and a narrow, sharply defined black tail band.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow, short, black band before the pale tip, red gonys spot; iris usually dark, orbital ring red; legs yellow, greener in winter.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Larger, 55–67 cm, paler grey above, pale eye in yellow skin, pink legs and plain yellow bill with a red spot – never a black band before the tip.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Much larger, 64–79 cm, wingspan 144–166 cm, and blackish above rather than medium grey.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Bigger, 51–64 cm, wingspan 124–150 cm, with a distinctly darker back and no black band before the bill tip.
  - Iceland Gull: Little else in the sources: separated here on size and build, slender and light with a thin bill, 50–64 cm and wingspan 115–150 cm against 52–62 cm.

## Atlantic Puffin
*Fratercula arctica* · `atlpuf`

- Jizz — 28–34 cm, wingspan 47–63 cm; stands about 20 cm on land. Sturdy and thick-necked with short wings and tail; huge bill, broad and triangular in profile but very narrow from above. Male slightly larger.
- Plumage — Forehead, crown, nape, back, wings and tail glossy black, a broad black collar round neck and throat, underparts white, a diffuse dark grey zone along the flanks. Large lozenge-shaped very pale grey face patch tapering to a point at the nape, with a crease from the eye to its rear tip. Winter face dark grey.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey, yellow and red with a round yellow gape rosette; iris brown to very dark blue, red orbital ring; legs and feet bright orange.
- In the field — Legs set well back, giving an upright stance ashore. Call a deep grunting arr-uh, heard at the colony. Winter bill duller and smaller; juvenile's bill much narrower and ungrooved.
- Similar species —
  - Black Guillemot: Summer bird all black but for a large white upperwing patch, with coral-red legs and a thin pointed black bill; winter head and underparts white, upperparts barred.
  - Razorbill: Larger, 37–43 cm, 505–890 g; bill black, deep and laterally compressed with a blunt tip, vertical grooves and a white line, plus a white line from eye to bill.

## Atlas Flycatcher
*Ficedula speculigera* · `atlfly1`

- Jizz — Black-and-white Ficedula flycatcher; no measurements given in the sources.
- Plumage — Male: coal-black above with large white patches on both forehead and wing, and an all-black tail. Female: tail blacker above than in its close relatives, rump pale grey.
- In the field — Call a viit and the song like a pied flycatcher's, but slower, deeper and with wider intervals. Breeds only in north-west Africa.
- Similar species —
  - European Pied Flycatcher: Less black above with a small forehead patch and white tail sides, where Atlas is coal-black with an all-black tail.
  - Collared Flycatcher: Look for the male's white neck collar, which Atlas lacks; the Atlas female's tail is blacker above.
  - Red-breasted Flycatcher: Brown above, white below with a grey head and orange throat, white eye-ring and white bases to the outer tail feathers; smaller at 11–12 cm.
  - Semicollared Flycatcher: Intermediate between the collared and pied patterns, showing a part collar; Atlas is coal-black with an all-black tail and big white forehead.

## Audouin's Gull
*Ichthyaetus audouinii* · `audgul1`

- Jizz — 48–52 cm. Medium-sized, elegant gull; lighter-built than a Herring Gull despite similar size, more the habitus of a large Common Gull; bill short and stubby.
- Plumage — Head, neck, underparts and tail white; back and upperwing pale grey; primaries black with small white tips forming a string of pearls, not large mirrors; a weak white trailing edge to the secondaries. Winter: head-sides and nape freckled brown. Four years to adult plumage.
- Bare parts — Bill coral- to dark red with a black ring near the yellow or pale tip; legs grey-green to dark olive; iris dark.
- In the field — Strictly coastal and pelagic; a fish specialist that rarely scavenges. Feeds at night well out to sea, and patrols slowly close in along beaches, at times dangling the legs to increase drag.
- Similar species —
  - Mediterranean Gull: White primaries with no black tips; black hood with white eye crescents in summer, a dusky bandit mask in winter; bill sharp-tipped and parallel-sided.
  - Pallas's Gull: Far bigger, 55–72 cm on a 140–162 cm span; summer adults wear a black hood, unique at that size.
  - European Herring Gull: Bigger and heavier, 55–67 cm; bill yellow with a red spot, pale eye in yellow skin, legs pink, and big white mirrors in the black wingtip.
  - Common Gull: Smaller, 40–46 cm; bill greenish-yellow and unmarked in summer, legs yellow, and large white mirrors in the black wingtip.

## Baillon's Crake
*Zapornia pusilla* · `baicra1`

- Jizz — 15–18 cm. Europe's smallest rail; short straight bill, long toes, distinctly short wings and a short tail.
- Plumage — Crown, nape and hindneck cinnamon with diffuse black streaks; supercilium, face and foreneck light blue-grey, some birds cinnamon on ear-coverts and lores; upperparts warm ochre-brown streaked black and spotted white on mantle and coverts; belly white, flanks, thighs and undertail-coverts sharply barred black and white. Female duller brown above. Immature: duller, underparts extensively barred; chicks black.
- Bare parts — Bill green to yellow-green with a dark ridge and tip, never red at the base; iris bright red; legs and feet olive-green to yellowish olive.
- In the field — Male's call likened to a drake Garganey's and to an edible frog.
- Similar species —
  - Little Crake: Bill yellow with a red base, which Baillon's never shows; flanks plain, lacking the bold black-and-white barring and the white spotting above; slightly larger, 17–19 cm, longer-winged.
  - Western Swamphen: Chicken-sized with huge feet, bright purple plumage and a red bill and frontal shield – unmistakable beside a 15–18 cm crake.
  - Spotted Crake: Larger, 19–22.5 cm; brown above, blue-grey breast, dark barring and white spots on the flanks; short straight bill yellow with a red base.

## Balearic Shearwater
*Puffinus mauretanicus* · `balshe1`

- Jizz — 30–40 cm, wingspan 76–93 cm. Medium shearwater, only a little smaller than a black-headed gull; pot-bellied look in flight; short tail overtopped by the projecting toes.
- Plumage — Upperparts greyish-brown, underparts dirty white with a grey-brown wash thickening from the lower belly to the undertail-coverts; flanks never pure white, the border with the upperparts diffuse; centre of underwing diffusely ash-grey to off-white with a brown bar in the axillaries, the underwing looking dark. Juvenile as adult, told June–September by fresh upperpart feathering while adults moult.
- In the field — Typical shearing flight: canting side to side on stiff wings with few beats, wingtips almost touching the water, body like a flying cross. Does not follow boats. Silent at sea; loud cackling at colonies at night.
- Similar species —
  - Manx Shearwater: Cleanly black above and white below, the contrast sharp; Balearic is brown above with dirty, diffusely bordered underparts. Sizes overlap, 30–38 cm against 30–40 cm.
  - Barolo Shearwater: Pale face with a contrasting dark eye, a silvery panel in the upperwing, shorter more rounded wings and blue feet.
  - Yelkouan Shearwater: About 15% smaller and more contrasted; flanks clean white with a sharp border and a contrastingly white underwing centre. Some birds not safely separable in the field.
  - Bulwer's Petrel: Smaller-bodied, 25–29 cm, but very long-winged; all-brown with a long pointed tail; buoyant twisting flight picking food from the surface.

## Band-rumped Storm-Petrel
*Hydrobates castro* · `barpet`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm, 29–56 g; wingspan 43–46 cm. Long-winged storm-petrel; tail only weakly forked, the fork hard to see at sea.
- Plumage — Very dark throughout — sooty black above, sootier brown below — but for a square white rump patch, broader than long, and a few pale feathers on flanks and undertail-coverts; upperwing-coverts slightly paler, the bar diffuse and not sharply marked.
- In the field — Flight straighter and faster than similar species, wingbeats weaker; shearwater-like, gliding on stiff wings with the wing less angled at the carpal. Voice only at the colony: a cooing note and a squeaky one, like a finger rubbed on glass.
- Similar species —
  - European Storm-Petrel: Smaller, 14–18 cm, span 37–41 cm; square tail, broad white band along the underwing, white rump running onto the sides of the tail base.
  - Leach's Storm-Petrel: Tail more deeply forked, white rump V-shaped or triangular and often split by a dark line; upperwing bar sharper; flight tern-like.

## Bank Swallow
*Riparia riparia* · `banswa`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm, wingspan 26.5–29 cm, 11–16 g. Smallest European swallow; small, only shallowly forked tail, bill short, broad and flat, toes and tarsi almost bare.
- Plumage — Dull grey-brown to earth-brown above, wings and tail blackish-brown; white below with a broad, clear brown band across the breast; throat white. Juvenile: rufous tips to the coverts and pale margins above, chin grey-buff instead of white.
- Bare parts — Bill black and flat; legs and toes brown.
- In the field — Flight quick and jerky, less purposeful than other swallows, unsteady and fluttering; very sociable, breeding in colonies; call a rasping tschrrip given constantly on the wing, alarm a short brrit.
- Similar species —
  - Western House-Martin: Steel-blue above with a white rump and wholly white underparts and underwings, no breast band; white downy feathering on the legs and pink toes.
  - Pale Crag-Martin: Light brown above, paler on the lower back, pale grey throat and upper breast, dirty white below with no breast band; square tail with small white feather spots.
  - Eurasian Crag-Martin: Larger, 13–15 cm with a 32–34.5 cm span; ash-brown above, paler below with no breast band, blackish underwing and undertail-coverts, square white-spotted tail.
  - European Red-rumped Swallow: Blue-glossed black above with a pale orange-red rump and an orangey-red nape collar, buffy face and throat, no breast band; deeply forked, black-ended tail.

## Bar-headed Goose
*Anser indicus* · `bahgoo`

- Jizz — 70–78 cm, 1.87–3.2 kg; wingspan 140–165 cm, wing 40–50 cm. Mid-sized goose, about the size of a Greylag; overall impression very pale. Female usually slightly smaller.
- Plumage — White head crossed by two black-brown bars, the first arching from eye to eye over the hindcrown, the second shorter and lower on the nape; hindneck black with two white lines down the sides; throat dark grey; body pale silver-grey, belly white, primaries black; tail grey with a white tip, rump and undertail white. Juvenile: lacks the black head bars.
- Bare parts — Bill light to orange-yellow; feet orange; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Call in flight a typical goose honking. At distance easily passed over among other geese.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Head and neck rich dark brown with no black bars; bill short, pink with black base and tip, feet pink; body mid-grey-brown rather than pale silver-grey.
  - Graylag Goose: Bulkier and grey-brown, thick-necked with a large head; bill orange or pink, legs pink; head plain, without the black bars.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Head plain brown, unbarred; bill black with an orange band across the middle, legs orange; upperwing-coverts dark brown with narrow white fringes.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Greyish brown with a white facial blaze, not black head bars; breast blotched and barred black; bill pinkish, legs bright orange.

## Bar-tailed Godwit
*Limosa lapponica* · `batgod`

- Jizz — 33–41 cm, wingspan 70–80 cm; males 190–400 g, females 260–630 g. Relatively short-legged godwit; bill long, tapering and slightly upturned, 7–11 cm.
- Plumage — Breeding male: neck, breast and belly unbroken brick-red, dark brown above; female duller, chestnut to cinnamon below. Non-breeding: plain grey-brown with darker feather centres giving a striped look, whitish beneath, head, neck and breast grey-streaked. Juvenile as non-breeding but buffier, flanks and breast streaked. Tail barred; no white wing-bar.
- Bare parts — Legs blue-grey to dark grey; bill bi-coloured, pink at the base, black towards the tip.
- In the field — Probes sand and mud at low tide for worms and crabs; can double its weight in the winter quarters. Calls markedly nasal — display kuvä-kuvä-kuvä, contact a shrill kuvi-kuvi.
- Similar species —
  - Black-tailed Godwit: Longer-legged, longer-billed, tail black rather than barred, and bold black-and-white wings with a white rump in flight; winter breast and upperparts uniform brown-grey, not streaked.
  - Eurasian Woodcock: 33–38 cm, span 55–65 cm; cryptic reddish-brown, head barred not striped, bill straight, wings rounded, eyes huge and high-set — a woodland bird, not a mudflat wader.
  - Eurasian Whimbrel: 37–47 cm, span 75–90 cm; greyish brown with white back and rump, and a long bill curved down with a kink — never upturned. Call a rippling whistle.
  - Spotted Redshank: Smaller at 29–33 cm, span 61–67 cm; red legs and red bill, black in breeding plumage and very pale in winter, with a white oval on the back in flight.

## Barbary Partridge
*Alectoris barbara* · `barpar2`

- Jizz — 32–36 cm. Rotund, squat partridge; wings short and rounded.
- Plumage — Back grey-brown, breast grey, belly rust-buff; flanks white with rufous streaks. Throat and side of head pale grey, no white chin patch; instead a broad chestnut collar spotted white, a band down the centre of the crown and a streak back from the cheek. Supercilium pale blue-grey; a long pink stripe runs vertically behind the eye. Outer tail feathers rufous.
- Bare parts — Legs red.
- In the field — Ground-dweller, rarely flying up; wary, running from danger and flying only a short distance on rounded wings. Flight low, fast beats and stiff glides. Song a noisy tre-tre-tre-tre-tre-cheeche-tre-tre-tre.
- Similar species —
  - Red-legged Partridge: Cream throat ringed by black streaking, and a narrow off-white supercilium; this bird has a pale grey throat, a chestnut white-spotted collar and a blue-grey supercilium.
  - Chukar: White face with a sharply defined black gorget, not a grey face and chestnut white-spotted collar; back light brown rather than grey-brown.
  - Black Grouse: Much bigger, 45–60 cm; male glossy black with white wingbar, white undertail-coverts and red eye-wattles, hen drab and cryptic. Neither shows a chestnut collar.
  - Black Francolin: Male black-bodied with a white cheek patch and white-spotted flanks; female brown with a chestnut hindneck and black throat. Neither has grey breast and rufous-streaked white flanks.

## Barn Swallow
*Hirundo rustica* · `barswa`

- Jizz — 17–19 cm including 2–7 cm of elongated outer tail feathers, wingspan 32–34.5 cm, 16–22 g. Very slender and long-winged; tail long and deeply forked.
- Plumage — Upperparts glossy steel blue-black; forehead, chin and throat chestnut-red, cut off from the off-white underparts by a broad dark blue breast band; wing and tail feathers black, a line of white spots across the outer tail. Female: streamers shorter, blue duller, underparts paler, less rufous on throat and forehead. Juvenile: browner, face pale rufous, underparts whiter, no streamers.
- In the field — Flight fast and tearing, 4–10 beats per second — slower-beating than House Martin; hunts in lower air, mostly seven to eight metres up. Calls witt or witt-witt, loud splee-plink when excited; alarm siflitt at cats, flitt-flitt at raptors.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Crag-Martin: Ash-brown above, paler below, broader in body, wing and tail; tail short and square with white spots near the tips; no red face, blue breast band or streamers.
  - Western House-Martin: Smaller (13 cm, span 26–29 cm), steel-blue above with a bold white rump, wholly white underparts and white-feathered legs; short-forked tail, no streamers.
  - European Red-rumped Swallow: Buffier below with a pale orange-red rump, buff face and throat and an orange-red nape collar; no breast band, undertail black, no white tail spots.

## Barnacle Goose
*Branta leucopsis* · `bargoo`

- Jizz — 55–70 cm, wingspan 120–145 cm. Medium-sized, compact goose; short round-looking neck, rounded head, small stubby bill.
- Plumage — White face and forehead, so a black line runs from bill-base to eye; crown, nape, neck and breast black against a white belly. Upperparts silver-grey barred black and white; back dark grey, rump black, undertail white; tail white with a broad black terminal band; adults show pale grey vertical flank bars. Juvenile: browner, flank bars ill-defined.
- Bare parts — Bill small, stubby, black; legs black to dark grey. Sexes alike.
- In the field — In flight a V-shaped white rump patch and silver-grey underwing linings.
- Similar species —
  - Canada Goose: Canada goose is much bigger, 75–110 cm, brownish-bodied, bigger-billed, and white on the head only as a chinstrap — no white forehead.
  - Brant: Brant is uniformly dark grey-brown, flanks and belly no paler than the back, head and neck black with only a small white neck patch; no white face.
  - Red-breasted Goose: Red-breasted goose shows a red breast, dark at distance, and is smaller at 53–56 cm against 55–70 cm.
  - Cackling Goose: Cackling goose is brown-bodied with a white chinstrap only; barnacle is grey and white with white face and forehead.

## Barolo Shearwater
*Puffinus baroli* · `litshe1`

- Jizz — 25–30 cm, wingspan 56–63 cm. Small black-and-white seabird; wings short, rounded and broad.
- Plumage — Dark above, pale below; face pale with a darkly contrasting eye — the white cheek reaching up over the eye; silvery panel in the upperwing, and at close range clearly white covert tips above.
- Bare parts — Feet blue.
- In the field — Holds course less steadily than Manx Shearwater, flapping quicker and more often; carries the head high and sometimes raises it in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Manx Shearwater: Larger, 30–38 cm, with longer and narrower wings and a dark cheek; lacks the silvery upperwing panel and white covert tips, and flies more steadily.
  - Balearic Shearwater: Much larger, 30–40 cm, wingspan 76–93 cm; dark brown above and dirty white below, without the pale face, silvery upperwing panel or blue feet.
  - Yelkouan Shearwater: Larger, 30–39 cm, wingspan 70–90 cm; very dark brown above with paler underside, and none of the pale face or silvery upperwing panel.
  - Atlantic Puffin: An auk, not a shearwater: thick-set, short-winged, 28–34 cm, with a stout bill, glossy black cap and back, black collar and large pale grey face patches.

## Barred Warbler
*Curruca nisoria* · `barwar1`

- Jizz — 15.5–17 cm, 22–36 g; wingspan 26–29 cm. Largest Curruca: big, heavily built, bill fairly pointed, tail shaped like a Sparrowhawk's.
- Plumage — Male: dark grey above with white tips to wing-coverts and tail feathers, whitish below heavily barred dark grey. Female: paler, grey-brown above, only lightly barred. Juvenile: buffy grey-brown above, pale buff-grey below, barred only on lower belly and rump, pale fringes to the wing-coverts; little else distinctive.
- Bare parts — Iris bright yellow in adult male, brown-yellow in female, dark in immature; bill blackish with a paler base; legs stout, grey-brown.
- In the field — Song a chatter of many clear Blackbird-like notes, hard and rasping, whistles woven in; alarm a loud sparrow-like rattle trailing off, trrrr-t-t-t. Male song-flights from mid-height trees, clapping the wings like a Woodpigeon.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above with chestnut fringes to the secondaries, buff and unbarred below; male's head grey with a clean white throat; no yellow iris.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Much lighter, 12–16 g against 22–36 g; grey back, plain whitish underparts with no barring, dark bandit mask through the eye, white throat.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male black-headed with white throat and red eyes; female brown above with a grey head and buff underparts. Neither is barred below.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male grey above with brick-red underparts and a white malar stripe; female brown above, pink-flushed below. No barring, no yellow iris.

## Bearded Reedling
*Panurus biarmicus* · `bearee1`

- Jizz — 14.5–17 cm. Small, long-tailed, tit-like bird of large reedbeds.
- Plumage — Mostly orange-brown, cinnamon on back and flanks, whitish on throat and breast; head and nape ash-grey; tertials black edged rusty, tail rusty with white tips to the outer feathers. Male: black moustaches of elongated cheek feathers, black undertail-coverts. Female: paler, head browner, no moustaches, undertail-coverts pale buff. Juvenile: buffier, rectangular black back patch, much black in the tail.
- Bare parts — Bill bright orange-yellow, duller in the adult female.
- In the field — Confined to large reedbeds; flight undulating. Calls rapidly repeated ping and tjing, sometimes followed by a drawn-out tjirr; song subdued.

## Bearded Vulture
*Gypaetus barbatus* · `lammer1`

- Jizz — 94–125 cm, wingspan 230–283 cm, 4.5–7.8 kg. Huge but slender and small-headed on a thick neck; wings long, narrow and sharply pointed; tail long and wedge-shaped, longer than the wing is wide — a giant falcon in silhouette.
- Plumage — Adult: slate to grey-black above with pale shafts; head, neck and underparts white to sandy-beige or rusty red, got by dust-bathing; creamy forehead cut by a black band across lores and eyes, running into black chin bristles — the beard. Underwing grey-brown, coverts very dark. Juvenile: grey-black with dark head, blunter wingtip, shorter tail; adult after five to seven years.
- Bare parts — Bill strong and pale; iris yellow, ringed by a red sclera that brightens as the bird grows excited.
- In the field — Glides on stiff wings held flat, sometimes slightly bowed, working the least updraught along cliffs and summits. Head not bald. Gait waddling, feet large and powerful.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Griffon: Broad-winged and short-tailed, with a white head and white neck ruff, yellow bill, and buff body and coverts against dark flight feathers; 93–122 cm.
  - Golden Eagle: Broader-winged and much shorter-tailed, 66–102 cm with a 180–234 cm span; no black beard, no wedge tail and no sharply pointed wingtips.
  - Black-winged Kite: Grey and white with black shoulder patches, wingtips and eye-stripe; tail short and square, not long and wedge-shaped; perches on roadside wires, jerking its tail.
  - Black Kite: Brown with a paler head, streaked below, and a forked tail; far smaller, wingspan 130–155 cm against 230–283 cm.

## Black Francolin
*Francolinus francolinus* · `blkfra`

- Jizz — 33–36 cm, 450–500 g. Gamebird slightly larger than Gray Partridge and looking larger still from its upright carriage; tail rounded; male spurred.
- Plumage — Male: black head and breast divided by a chestnut collar; large white cheek patch; flanks and breast sides spotted white, the marks U-shaped lower down; back and wings scalloped golden-brown with tawny-buff subterminal bands; tail black, barred white or grey. Female: yellow-brown, heavily vermiculated, chestnut hindneck, black throat, no cheek patch, outer tail feathers black; wings brown in both sexes.
- Bare parts — Legs reddish-brown to red, the male's with a backward-pointing spur; iris brown.
- In the field — Flushed birds show cinnamon-barred flight feathers. Male calls at dawn from an earth mound, rock or low branch: a loud ringing klik cheek-cheek-cheerakik in seven rhythmic syllables, the voice cracked.
- Similar species —
  - Chukar: Light brown back, grey breast, buff belly and a white face sharply ringed by a black gorget; rufous-streaked flanks, red legs, coral-red bill; 32–35 cm.
  - Rock Ptarmigan: White in winter, grey and brown in summer, wings always white; black eye-line and outer tail feathers; high barren ground; 34–36 cm, wingspan 54–60 cm.
  - Hazel Grouse: Finely patterned grey above with brown wings and chestnut-flecked white underparts; short erectile crest, white-bordered black throat on the male, black-tipped grey tail; 35–39 cm.
  - Gray Partridge: Brown-backed with grey flanks and chest and a chestnut horseshoe on the white belly; no white cheek or chestnut collar; smaller, 29–31 cm, wingspan 45–48 cm.

## Black Grouse
*Lyrurus tetrix* · `blagro1`

- Jizz — 45–60 cm; cock 55–60 cm and 820–1750 g, hen about 45 cm and 750–1120 g. Cock's outer tail feathers long and curled outwards, lyre-shaped; dense plumage covers nostrils and feet.
- Plumage — Cock: blue-black, darkest on neck and breast, head, neck and rear back glossed steel-blue shot with violet; white wingbars and wing patch, undertail-coverts white, tail white beneath; bare intense red crescent over each eye, swelling in the breeding season. Hen: cryptic brown, darker above, whiter on the belly, tail relatively short. Juvenile: barred black and rusty-buff above.
- Bare parts — Iris brown; bill short and stout, horn-black; feet brownish.
- In the field — Both sexes show white wingbars in flight. Display call loud, bubbling and somewhat dove-like.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Ptarmigan: Willow ptarmigan is smaller, 35–44 cm and 430–810 g, with white wings and underparts and a black tail; no lyre-shaped tail and no white wingbars.
  - Barbary Partridge: Barbary partridge is a small rotund bird, grey-brown above, grey-breasted, with light grey face, broad reddish-brown gorget, rufous-streaked white flanks and red legs.
  - Red-legged Partridge: Red-legged partridge is sandy-brown with a black-streaked gorget, bold rufous and black flank bars, cream throat, pink legs and red bill and eye-ring.
  - Western Capercaillie: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, cock capercaillie 74–85 cm and about 4.1 kg against black grouse cock 55–60 cm and 820–1750 g.

## Black Guillemot
*Cepphus grylle* · `blkgui`

- Jizz — 30–38 cm, wingspan 49–58 cm, 300–460 g. Clearly smaller than Razorbill and the murres, larger than Puffin; pear-shaped body, small head, pointed bill, rear-heavy profile.
- Plumage — Summer: entirely brown-black, green-glossed when fresh, with a big oval white patch on the upperwing formed by the outer greater and median coverts; underwing-coverts and axillaries largely white. Winter: head and underparts white, upperparts barred grey and white, rump white, wing pattern retained. Sexes alike. Juvenile darker and browner; immatures' wing patch mottled grey-brown, visible far off.
- Bare parts — Bill black; mouth lining bright coral-red and prominent in display; legs and feet coral-red in summer, pale red in winter.
- In the field — Flies low over the water on fast whirring beats, looking rear-heavy. Breeding call a high whistle; displaying males give fine piping sipp-sipp-sipp, sawing sipp-sy-sipp-sy and drawn-out siiiiiyy.
- Similar species —
  - Atlantic Puffin: Smaller, 28–34 cm; huge deep triangular red, yellow and blue-grey bill, large pale grey face patch, black collar and white underparts; orange legs, upright ashore.
  - Razorbill: Larger, 37–43 cm, 505–890 g and thick-set; white belly against black head, neck and back, deep blunt laterally compressed grooved bill, white line from eye to bill.
  - Common Murre: Larger, 38–46 cm; black head, back and wings, white underparts, thin dark pointed bill, small rounded black tail; no white wing patch, winter face white with a dark spur.
  - Thick-billed Murre: Larger still, 40–48 cm, 700–1481 g; black head, neck, back and wings, white underparts, long pointed bill, small rounded black tail; no white upperwing patch.

## Black Kite
*Milvus migrans* · `blakit1`

- Jizz — Wingspan 130–155 cm, about 735 g; sources give 48–58 cm long, roughly buzzard-sized. Wings long and narrow, sharply angled at the carpal, six spread primary fingers; tail shallowly forked, triangular when fanned.
- Plumage — Uniformly dark brown and weakly contrasted. Adult head, throat and nape pale grey, dark-streaked; darker patch behind the eye; breast and belly paler, often rufous-brown, dark feather shafts giving a streaked look; outer flight feathers black; pale coverts band contrasts with near-black remiges. Juvenile: paler and more mottled with whitish-yellow feather fringes, belly streaked, dark mask through the eye.
- Bare parts — Cere and gape yellow, upper mandible black, lower yellowish; legs yellow, claws black; iris brown, turning yellow only in old birds.
- In the field — Voice a shrill whistle followed by a rapid whinnying; territorial call as Red Kite's but coarser and more drawn out.
- Similar species —
  - Red Kite: Larger, 60–70 cm, rufous body, deeply forked twisting tail and white primary patches, soaring on a dihedral; Black Kite darker, less contrasted, tail barely forked.
  - Black-winged Kite: Grey and white with black shoulder patches, wing-tips and eye-stripe; short square tail, not forked; long wings project beyond the tail when perched.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, wingspan 130–155 cm against 142–175 cm and 1.4–2.5 kg.
  - Booted Eagle: Smaller eagle, wingspan 110–132 cm; pale morph light grey with darker head and flight feathers, dark morph mid-brown with dark grey remiges.

## Black Redstart
*Phoenicurus ochruros* · `blared1`

- Jizz — 13–14.5 cm, 14–20 g (mean 16.2 g); wingspan c. 26 cm. Slim chat, slighter than House Sparrow; wings relatively long; bill fairly long and broad at the base, ringed with long bristles; legs notably long; stance upright.
- Plumage — Male slate-grey above, forehead black, sometimes with a white forehead spot; lores, cheeks and chin to belly black; whitish panel on the secondaries (western birds); belly and undertail blackish-grey, or orange-red in eastern birds. Rump and tail rusty-orange in all plumages, central pair dark brown. Female grey to grey-brown. First-summer male duller, lacking the wing panel.
- Bare parts — Bill brown-black; legs black and slender.
- In the field — Constant bobbing and tail-shivering; perches upright. Song a short pressed strophe, 3–4 rising notes then a brief trill, often followed by a scraping rattle like a stick stirred in gravel; among the first birds to sing at dawn.
- Similar species —
  - Common Redstart: Male Common has orange-chestnut flanks and underparts, not black; female browner, less grey. At any age check axillaries and underwing-coverts: grey here, buff to orange-red in Common.
  - Moussier's Redstart: Smallest redstart, only 12 cm; male black-headed with a broad white stripe over the eye and down the neck-side, and a white wing patch on black upperparts.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: A black-and-white flycatcher with large white patches on the wing and forehead; the sources describe no rusty rump or tail on it.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Whitish crown, buff mantle, black mask from bill to ear-coverts, and white back, uppertail-coverts and tail — no orange in the tail. 13.5–15.5 cm.

## Black Stork
*Ciconia nigra* · `blasto1`

- Jizz — 90–105 cm, around 3 kg, standing as tall as 102 cm. Large dark stork, slightly smaller than White Stork but alike in shape and movement; long red legs, long neck, long straight pointed red bill.
- Plumage — Black with a purple-green metallic sheen over head, neck, breast, back and upperwing; lower breast, belly, axillaries and undertail-coverts white, showing as a white triangle in the armpit. Breast feathers long and shaggy, forming a courtship ruff. In strong sun the upperwing can look pale. Juvenile: browner, duller, green-black without sheen, pale tips to scapulars and coverts.
- Bare parts — Adult: bill and legs bright red when breeding, brownish to dull dark red otherwise; bare eye-skin red; iris brown. Juvenile: legs and bill grey-green.
- In the field — Walks slowly and steadily; flies with neck outstretched and legs projecting well beyond the tail. Rarely bill-clatters; rasping filing series at the nest and a buzzard-like mewing.
- Similar species —
  - White Stork: Mainly white with black flight feathers and wing coverts, not black with white only on lower breast, belly and axillaries; slightly larger, 95–115 cm, and clatters its bill.

## Black Swan
*Cygnus atratus* · `blkswa`

- Jizz — 3.7–9 kg; sources give 110–142 cm long with a 1.6–2 m wingspan. Huge swan, slightly smaller than Mute Swan; neck relatively the longest of any swan, over half the total length in flight.
- Plumage — Black-feathered throughout with white flight feathers, hidden at rest and flashing only on open wings or in flight; feathers along the wing edges curled. Male slightly larger, its bill longer and straighter. Cygnet: greyish-brown with pale-edged feathers, plain dark silver-grey in down.
- Bare parts — Bill bright red with a pale bar and white tip; legs and feet greyish-black; iris orange to pale brown.
- In the field — Swims with neck arched or erect, often raising the wings in aggressive display. Flies in line or V, necks undulating, wings whistling; voice a musical far-carrying bugle with softer crooning notes and whistles.
- Similar species —
  - Whooper Swan: White throughout and much heavier, 7–14 kg against 3.7–9 kg; Black Swan is black-feathered with a red white-tipped bill.
  - Mute Swan: White plumage, dark grey legs and a knobbed bill; larger at 140–160 cm. Black Swan is black with a bright red bill.
  - Tundra Swan: Entirely white with black feet and a mostly black bill, 115–150 cm; Black Swan is black-feathered with a red bill and white flight feathers.
  - Bar-headed Goose: A pale grey goose with a black-barred white head, far smaller at 71–76 cm and 1.87–3.2 kg.

## Black Tern
*Chlidonias niger* · `blkter`

- Jizz — 22–26 cm, wingspan 56–62 cm, about 62 g. Small slim marsh tern, clearly smaller than Common Tern; wings short and broad, tail short, square and little forked; bill short, slender, nearly head-length.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: head, neck, breast and belly deep black, back and upperwing even dark grey with a white forewing, underwing pale grey, vent and undertail-coverts white, tail brownish-grey. Non-breeding and juvenile: black cap running down the nape and behind the eye, rest of head, breast and belly white, dark patch on the breast-side at the wing base, upperparts pale grey.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs dark, red-black; in winter and juvenile plumage orange-red.
- In the field — Flight light and erratic, throwing from side to side rather than holding the straighter course of Sterna terns; dips to the surface for insects instead of hovering and plunge-diving. Call a high 'kik'.
- Similar species —
  - White-winged Tern: Wings mainly white with white rump and near-white tail, and no dark patch on the breast-side; bill smaller and stubbier, 22–25 mm.
  - Whiskered Tern: Larger with a stronger bill and more positive flight; summer adult has white cheeks and red bill and legs, the black reduced to a C-shaped cap and ear-coverts.
  - Little Tern: White forehead in breeding plumage, yellow black-tipped bill and yellow legs; wingspan only 41–55 cm against 56–62 cm.
  - Little Gull: A gull: black hood, pale grey back and upperwings with white primaries against very dark grey underwings, bright red legs and dark red bill; 24–28 cm.

## Black Wheatear
*Oenanthe leucura* · `blawhe1`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm. Large, heavy wheatear, clearly bigger than Northern Wheatear.
- Plumage — Male: all black, glossy and intense after the moult, duller when breeding; rump, undertail-coverts and tail white, the tail with a bold black inverted T; flight feathers pale-fringed. Female: as male but duller, brownish-black — usually only told in direct comparison.
- In the field — Loud thrush-like song.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: Much smaller, 14–16.5 cm; summer male grey above with buff throat and black mask, female pale brown — never all black, though the white tail carries the same black T.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Forehead, crown and mantle white or near-white with a buff tinge, underparts white, black mask from bill to ear-coverts; body never black.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: White forehead and crown, buff mantle, blacker wings, white underparts, black face mask; smaller, 13.5–15.5 cm; body never black.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Pale sandy-isabelline throughout, creamy supercilium, pale brown ear-coverts; tail brownish-black with a large white base; smaller, 16.5 cm; nothing black on it.

## Black Woodpecker
*Dryocopus martius* · `blawoo1`

- Jizz — 40–57 cm, 64–84 cm span, 250–400 g. Crow-sized and the largest woodpecker in range; near Rook-sized but slimmer and much longer-tailed, neck long, bill long (5–6.7 cm) and broad-based.
- Plumage — Entirely black; upperparts darker and glossier, sometimes shimmering bluish, underparts duller and a shade paler, tinged dark grey or blackish-brown; primaries often paler and browner; worn birds matt charcoal. Male: whole crown red. Female: red confined to the hindcrown. Juvenile: less glossy, crown duller red, throat paler grey.
- Bare parts — Bill grey-white to bone-white with a distinct dark grey tip; iris pale cream, looking white at distance; toes pale grey, claws darker.
- In the field — The long, unmarked, two-parted tail props the perched bird. Drumming deep and rolling, up to 17 strikes a second, carrying 4 km; male's 3–4 seconds, female's far shorter. Calls whistling kree-kree or klyi-klyi, flight kryck-kryck, a drawn-out kly-ääää after landing.
- Similar species —
  - Iberian Green Woodpecker: Medium-sized and green, not black; little black on the head and a dusky rather than black ring round each eye. Voice sibilant, hiss-like.

## Black-bellied Plover
*Pluvialis squatarola* · `bkbplo`

- Jizz — 25–30 cm, 170–320 g. Large, thickset plover with a heavy head and long legs; larger, stockier and longer-legged than the other plovers, and with a heavier bill, 24–34 mm.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: face, throat, breast and flanks black; mantle boldly spangled black and white, running up nape and crown; white supercilium joining on the forehead and running behind the ear-coverts into white patches at the breast sides. Female: black duller, greyer, flecked white. Winter and juvenile: plain grey above, breast grey-speckled, belly white; juvenile back feathers blacker with creamy edges.
- Bare parts — Bill short, heavy, black to grey-black in all plumages; legs grey to grey-black; eyes dark.
- In the field — In flight, black axillaries and inner flanks — separates it at once from the other Pluvialis; broad white wingbar, white rump. Moves sluggishly; autumn birds singly or in small flocks on outer coastal bars, rarely inland. Call a melancholy pieyt.
- Similar species —
  - European Golden-Plover: Thickset with wings barely longer than the tail; distinctive white S-shaped band from forehead to flanks; lacks the black axillaries shown in flight.
  - Northern Lapwing: Crested, with rounded wings; black and white with a green-tinged back, the male's black crown, throat and breast set against an otherwise white face.
  - Eurasian Dotterel: Smaller and more compact, striking whitish supercilium in all plumages, plain wings in flight; summer adults chestnut-breasted with a white breast line; legs yellow.
  - Kentish Plover: Tiny by comparison, around 40 g against 170–320 g; breeding male has a black head bar, black ear-coverts, rufous nape and two incomplete breast-bands.

## Black-bellied Sandgrouse
*Pterocles orientalis* · `blbsan1`

- Jizz — 30–39 cm, 300–615 g, wingspan 60–65 cm. Likely the largest sandgrouse; small pigeon-like head and neck on a stocky compact body; long pointed wings; pigeon-shaped but partridge-patterned.
- Plumage — Male: head, neck and breast grey; throat chestnut with a black spot; upperparts golden-brown with darker markings, belly black; black band across the buff breast and a thin black border round the lower breast. Female: browner and more finely marked above, head and neck more strongly streaked; underparts and breast band as male.
- In the field — Fast direct flight; white underwings and black belly make it easy to name in the air. Flocks fly to watering holes at dawn. Call a soft rolling chowrrr rrrr-rrrr, or a trilling tjürr-re-ka.
- Similar species —
  - Pin-tailed Sandgrouse: Underparts and feathered legs dull white, not black; underwing-coverts yellowish-green; cheeks and neck yellow, cryptically barred olive-green above; sizes overlap, 31–39 cm.

## Black-bellied Storm-Petrel
*Fregetta tropica* · `bbspet1`

- Jizz — About 20 cm, 43–63 g; medium-sized storm-petrel, compactly built, wing 15.4–18.3 cm. Legs long — feet project well beyond the tail in flight. Female slightly larger, longer-winged and longer-tailed.
- Plumage — Head, neck, upperparts, throat, upper breast and tail black-brown; white rump band; belly white with a broad black stripe down its centre linking the dark head to the black undertail-coverts — extent of white varies individually, and the stripe may be broken or absent. Flanks white; underwing white with dark leading edge and dark flight feathers. Juvenile as adult.
- Bare parts — Bill, legs and feet black.
- In the field — Flight erratic and zigzagging; frequently touches the water surface with the feet. Mostly silent at sea; on the ground at colonies gives a drawn-out shrill whistle.
- Similar species —
  - White-faced Storm-Petrel: 19–21 cm, span 41–44 cm; pale brown-grey back, rump and wings with black flight feathers, white below, white face with a black phalarope-like mask; no belly stripe.
  - Wilson's Storm-Petrel: 16–19 cm; dark brown but for white rump and flanks, belly dark and unstriped; diffuse pale band along the upperwing coverts; feet project only just beyond the square tail.

## Black-crowned Night Heron
*Nycticorax nycticorax* · `bcnher`

- Jizz — 58–65 cm, wingspan 90–100 cm, 727–1014 g. Stocky heron with short thick bill, short neck and short legs; wings broad and rounded; resting posture hunched, neck extended when hunting.
- Plumage — Adult: crown, nape-crest, back and scapulars black; forehead and face white; wings and rest of body ash-grey; one to eight long slender white nape plumes, raised in greeting and courtship. Male slightly larger, with a longer crest in the breeding season. Immature: dull grey-brown head, wings and back with numerous pale teardrop spots, underparts paler and brown-streaked.
- Bare parts — Iris red, yellow to amber in young birds; bill black; legs short and pale yellow-green, turning bright salmon-pink with blue lores briefly in courtship.
- In the field — In flight compact and short-tailed, heavy at the rear with the bill angled slightly down, recalling a bittern; can look all grey. Voice a hoarse raven-like qua, quak or quark; frog-like ko ark.
- Similar species —
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Mainly white with yellow bill and greyish-yellow legs, orange-buff plumes on crown, back and breast when breeding; smaller, 46–56 cm, wingspan 88–96 cm.
  - Gray Heron: Much larger, 84–102 cm, wingspan 155–195 cm; ashy-grey above, white head and neck with a broad black supercilium ending in a dangling crest, neck-front black-streaked.
  - Eurasian Bittern: Buffy-brown throughout, heavily streaked and barred, crown and nape black; thickset and bigger, 69–81 cm, wingspan 100–130 cm.
  - Great Egret: All white and far bigger, 80–104 cm, wingspan 131–170 cm, standing to 1 m; yellow bill with black legs and feet.

## Black-headed Bunting
*Emberiza melanocephala* · `blhbun1`

- Jizz — 15–16.5 cm. Long-tailed bunting, about House Sparrow size and larger than Reed Bunting.
- Plumage — Breeding male: hood black; upperparts and rump chestnut, unstreaked; underparts bright unstreaked yellow with a yellow breast band; no white in the tail. Autumn male after partial moult: head brown, like the female. Female: head grey-brown, upperparts duller olive-brown, underparts paler but unstreaked. Juvenile: as female, vent yellow; first-year male grey-crowned with chestnut and grey patches on the back.
- Bare parts — Bill conical, pale grey.
- In the field — Call a soft zrit; song a jerky zrit zrit zrit srut srut srut.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Bulky and streaked, 16–19 cm; grey-brown above, whitish below with breast streaking forming a gorget; no yellow or chestnut; dark eye, yellowish mandibles.
  - Reed Bunting: Smaller, 13.5–15.5 cm; male black-headed but with a white neck collar, white underparts and heavily barred brown back, not yellow and chestnut.
  - Yellowhammer: Head bright yellow, not black; back heavily streaked, rump rufous and outer tail feathers white — Black-headed shows no white in the tail.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 13–15 cm against 15–16.5 cm.

## Black-headed Gull
*Chroicocephalus ridibundus* · `bkhgul`

- Jizz — 34–39 cm, wingspan 86–99 cm, 166–400 g. Small gull, the smallest breeding regularly in central Europe; males average heavier than females.
- Plumage — Summer adult: chocolate-brown hood, black at distance, narrow white eye-crescents, open in front; white neck, underparts and tail, pale grey mantle and coverts, black primary tips. Winter: hood gone, dark smudge above and spot behind the eye. Juvenile: mottled brown with ochre in the grey mantle, black tail band, white rump; first-winter grey-backed, keeping carpal bar and tail band.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs red in summer, iris dark brown; winter bill paler red, tip blackish; juvenile legs yellow-orange, bill yellow with a black tip.
- In the field — In flight the white leading edge of the outer wing makes a broad dark-edged white wedge, usable at long range; inner primaries dark below.
- Similar species —
  - Slender-billed Gull: No dark hood in summer; head, bill and neck long and tapering, bill and legs dark red, iris yellow, breast faintly pink. Slightly larger, 37–40 cm.
  - Common Tern: A tern: black cap not a hood, deeply forked tail, narrow pointed bill red with a black tip, orange-red legs, grey wedge on the outer wing. 31–37 cm.
  - Black-legged Kittiwake: White head year-round, grey wings tipped solid black with no white leading edge, black legs, yellow bill. Larger, 37–42 cm.
  - Sandwich Tern: A tern: very pale in flight, thin sharp black bill with a yellow tip, short black legs; forehead extensively white outside the breeding season. 36–43 cm.

## Black-legged Kittiwake
*Rissa tridactyla* · `bklkit`

- Jizz — 37–42 cm, wingspan 91–105 cm, 305–525 g. Only a little larger than a Black-headed Gull; strikingly short-legged; tail barely forked or square-cut; hind toe reduced to a stub, hence three toes.
- Plumage — Adult white on head, body, underwing and tail; back and upperwing grey in two tones with the outer three primaries solid black and without white tips, so the wing looks three-toned at distance. Winter: dark grey smudge behind the eye and a grey hind-neck collar. Juvenile and first-winter show a dark ear-crescent.
- Bare parts — Bill plain greenish-yellow, dusky-olive in winter, blackish-grey in juveniles; legs black, occasionally pinkish-grey to reddish; gape and orbital ring bright red; iris dark.
- In the field — Flight stiff and springy, recalling a tern more than other gulls. Only two age classes. Pairs greet by gaping to flash the red mouth.
- Similar species —
  - Sabine's Gull: Smaller, 27–33 cm against 37–42 cm; striking wing pattern of grey coverts, four black outer primaries and white inner primaries; slightly forked tail; dark hood when breeding.
  - Little Gull: Much smaller, 24–28 cm; white primaries above contrasting with a very dark grey underwing, black hood or cap and red legs — never the solid black wingtips.
  - Sandwich Tern: A tern, not a gull: black bill with a yellow tip, black legs, very pale upperwing without black tips, black or white-flecked forehead. Similar length, 36–43 cm.
  - Slender-billed Gull: Long tapering head, neck and dark red bill; dark red legs and yellow iris, not the black legs, greenish-yellow bill and dark eye. Similar size, 37–40 cm.

## Black-tailed Godwit
*Limosa limosa* · `bktgod`

- Jizz — 37–42 cm, 63–82 cm span, 160–500 g. Large, elegant wader; very long straight bill (7.5–12 cm), long neck and long dark legs; stands tall.
- Plumage — Breeding male: head, neck and breast orange to deep rust, belly white barred black to a variable extent, crown streaked; female duller. Winter: plain brown-grey breast and upperparts, mantle and wings pale grey. Juvenile: pale orange wash on neck and breast. White tail base with a broad black terminal band all year.
- Bare parts — Bill orange-pink at the base with a dark tip in summer, pink with a black tip in winter; legs dark grey, brown or black.
- In the field — In flight bold black-and-white wings with a white wing-bar and white rump. Commonest call a strident weeka weeka weeka.
- Similar species —
  - Bar-tailed Godwit: Shorter-legged with a slightly upturned bill and a streaked back; no wing-bar, and barred rather than black-banded tail. Stands lower; 37–41 cm.
  - Broad-billed Sandpiper: Tiny beside it, 16–18 cm; short bill with a kinked-down tip, pale crown stripe and a split supercilium.
  - Common Greenshank: Smaller and grey-brown with long greenish legs and a grey-based bill looking upturned; shows a white wedge up the back, not a wing-bar. Call teu-teu-teu.
  - Common Redshank: Red legs and a black-tipped red bill; marbled brown, showing white up the back as well as on the wings. 24–30 cm.

## Black-winged Kite
*Elanus caeruleus* · `bkskit1`

- Jizz — 31–36 cm, wingspan 80–90 cm, 197–343 g; about Hobby size. Long falcon-like wings project beyond the tail at rest; wings fairly pointed and broad, head large, tail short and square, never forked.
- Plumage — Predominantly white to pale grey with black shoulder patches, black wingtips and a black eye-stripe; black on the underside of the primaries and on part of the upperwing-coverts; plumage velvety; sexes alike. Juvenile: crown browner, breast rust-coloured.
- Bare parts — Eyes bright red, large and forward-facing under a shading bony shelf; legs orange-yellow.
- In the field — Flapping flight broken by short glides; soars with wings held in a V; hovers into the wind with slower beats than Kestrel, sinking stepwise between hovers. Hunts much from high perches, often roadside wires, jerking the tail.
- Similar species —
  - Black Kite: Far bigger, wingspan 130–155 cm, and dark brown throughout with paler head and neck and a forked tail; no white body or black shoulder patch.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: A medium-sized eagle, 55–74 cm, wingspan 142–175 cm, 1.4–2.5 kg, female larger than male. Little else in the sources: separated here on size.
  - Booted Eagle: Much larger, 40–51 cm, wingspan 110–132 cm; pale morph light grey with darker head and flight feathers, dark morph mid-brown; no black shoulder patch.
  - Cinereous Vulture: Vast beside it, 98–120 cm long, wingspan 2.5–3.1 m, 6.3–14 kg — the largest accipitrid. Separated on size alone.

## Black-winged Pratincole
*Glareola nordmanni* · `blwpra1`

- Jizz — 24–28 cm, wingspan 60–68 cm, 84–105 g. Swallow-like in shape and flight, with long pointed wings, a forked tail, short legs and a short bill; on the ground plover-like, primaries well past the tail-tip.
- Plumage — Dark olive-brown above, head and back brown, flight feathers black; chin and throat cream, ringed by a black band against the grey-brown breast; belly white, rump white; underwing-coverts wholly black. Juvenile: throat band absent, mantle feathers with large cream tips.
- Bare parts — Bill short and black with a red base; no eye-ring.
- In the field — Feeds on the wing. The diagnostic black underwing and absence of a white trailing edge are seldom seen well in the field.
- Similar species —
  - Collared Pratincole: Chestnut underwing, which often looks black, plus a white trailing edge to the wing and an eye-ring; longer-tailed and shorter-legged.

## Black-winged Stilt
*Himantopus himantopus* · `bkwsti`

- Jizz — 33–36 cm including a needle-fine bill of 6–6.5 cm, wingspan 67–83 cm, 150–210 g. Slender pied wader on 14–17 cm legs that trail far past the tail; wings pointed.
- Plumage — Black above, white below and on head, neck and tail; head and neck variably black: usually a grey or black spot behind the eye, sometimes black hindcrown and hindneck. Male: back and scapulars green-glossed. Female: back browner, contrasting with the black remiges. Juvenile: grey rather than black, wings sandy with pale fringes showing as a white line in flight.
- Bare parts — Legs pink to rose-red, flesh-coloured in juveniles; bill black, straight and needle-fine.
- In the field — Noisy while breeding, silent the rest of the year. Commonest call a shrill, squeaky, rapid kjyck kjyck kjyck, brighter and harder than avocet's; alarm an impure krit krit.
- Similar species —
  - Pied Avocet: Larger, 42–46 cm, wingspan 67–77 cm; black bill slender and upturned, not straight; black cap running to the back of the neck; legs bluish, not pink.

## Blue Rock-Thrush
*Monticola solitarius* · `burthr`

- Jizz — 21–23 cm. Starling-sized thrush, a little smaller than a Starling, with a long, slim bill.
- Plumage — Male: entirely blue-grey, blackish-blue, apart from darker wings; unmistakable in summer. Female and immature much plainer: dark brown above, paler brown and scaly below, grey-black with pale spots. Both sexes lack reddish outer tail feathers. Male of philippensis rufous-chestnut from mid-breast to undertail.
- In the field — Shy, its manner recalling wheatears and thrushes. Male's song clear and melodious, louder than Rufous-tailed Rock-Thrush's; short and long songs of melodious whistles, trills and burry notes.
- Similar species —
  - Rufous-tailed Rock-Thrush: Male blue-grey only on the head, with orange underparts and white back; both sexes show reddish outer tail feathers, which this bird never has. 17–20 cm.
  - Black Wheatear: All black, female dark brown, with a white rump and largely white tail carrying a black inverted T; smaller, 16–18 cm.
  - Thrush Nightingale: Dark olive-brown above, never blue, with dark rufous-brown tail, brownish-black lores and ear-coverts and a mottled breast; smaller, 16–18 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Pale sandy above with white rump and a broad white tail base, creamy supercilium, sexes alike; much smaller, 16.5 cm.

## Blue-cheeked Bee-eater
*Merops persicus* · `bcbeat1`

- Jizz — 28–32 cm including a 4–8 cm tail-streamer. Slender bee-eater; central tail feathers drawn out to fine points about a third of the tail's length, longer and narrower than European Bee-eater's.
- Plumage — Predominantly green; crown, upperparts and wings dark, iridescent olive-green. Broad black eye-stripe from the bill base almost to the nape, white supercilium, cheeks and ear-coverts pale sky-blue, pale yellow at the bill base and on the chin, throat rust-red. Undertail-coverts pale green, tail grey below. Sexes alike, the female's streamers shorter.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs dark; iris red-brown.
- In the field — Hunts from an open perch, often telephone wires, in semi-desert with scattered acacias; takes more dragonflies than anything else, plus bees, wasps and hornets. Underwing warm red-brown in flight. Call flatter and less fluty than European Bee-eater's.
- Similar species —
  - European Bee-eater: Upperparts brown and yellow, not green; tail-streamers shorter and broader, and length shorter at 25–29 cm. Call fluty rather than flat.

## Bluethroat
*Luscinia svecica* · `blueth`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm, 12–25 g; about robin-sized, a shade smaller than nightingale. Slim and long-legged; male wing about 78 mm, tail 54 mm, female 74 and 52 mm.
- Plumage — Best mark in all plumages: two-toned outer tail — basal half rust-red, terminal half black-brown; plus a clear whitish supercilium. Male breeding: chin, throat and upper breast silky bright blue with a rust-red centre (svecica), white (cyanecula) or plain (magna). Upperparts dark grey-brown. Female: cream throat with a blackish crescent, little or no blue. Juvenile freckled dark brown, then female-like.
- Bare parts — Bill thin, dark horn-brown to blackish, bright lemon-yellow inside; legs dark horn-brown, tarsus reddish translucent; iris blackish.
- In the field — Mostly on the ground, hopping with wings slightly drooped, pausing to jerk the tail and flash the rust-red tail sides. Male's song strong, varied and mimicking, at times recalling nightingale.
- Similar species —
  - Common Nightingale: Larger at 15–16.5 cm; plain brown above with a wholly reddish tail and buff to white underparts — no blue throat and no black-and-rust tail pattern. Sexes alike.
  - Thrush Nightingale: About 16 cm; deep dark brown above with olive tinge and dark rufous-brown tail; lores and ear-coverts brownish-black, throat pale buff, breast mottled with brown central bands.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: A black-and-white flycatcher with large white wing patches and a white forehead — nothing resembling a blue throat or two-toned tail base.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: 13.5–15.5 cm; white or near-white crown, buff mantle, black wings, black mask from bill to ear-coverts, and back, upper tail-coverts and most of the tail white.

## Blyth's Reed Warbler
*Acrocephalus dumetorum* · `blrwar1`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, 8–16 g. Slim, with relatively short rounded wings and a short primary projection; bill rather long and thick; forehead flat.
- Plumage — Plain olive- to greyish-brown above, lacking the warmer tones of its relatives, the short rounded wings less olive; underparts plain off-white, flanks more olive-grey than buff. Short whitish supercilium from the bill-base to just behind the eye, with a dusky stripe through the eye; tertials uniform, without contrast. Sexes alike; juvenile yellower below.
- Bare parts — Bill greyish brown, lower mandible pale pinkish-brown at the base with a diffuse dark tip; legs pale pinkish-brown.
- In the field — Best told by song: slow and repeating, each phrase eight to ten times, with masterly mimicry alternating with scales and whistles; often at night. Calls a smacking zäck and a rolling zrrrrt.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Warbler: Song separates them: marsh mimics far faster, Blyth's repeats phrases slowly. Marsh also avoids pure reed stands. Sexes alike in both; hybrids recorded.
  - Sedge Warbler: 11.5–13 cm; streaked brown back and wings, black-streaked crown, prominent whitish supercilium and warm unstreaked rump — Blyth's is plain and only faintly supercilied.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Warmer brown, back faintly olive-grey with rust-brown rump; supercilium shorter and duller; longer primary projection. Its song a slow chattering jit-jit-jit with whistles and mimicry.
  - Great Reed Warbler: Thrush-sized at 16–21 cm, 22–38 g, with a much stronger supercilium and a very loud, far-carrying song.

## Bohemian Waxwing
*Bombycilla garrulus* · `bohwax`

- Jizz — 18–23 cm, 50–60 g, wingspan 32–35.5 cm. Starling-sized but looks bulkier: compact body, thick neck, large head, long pointed backswept crest; wings long and pointed, tail short; bill short, broad at the base.
- Plumage — Mainly rust-grey-brown; black mask through eye to nape, finely white-edged in adults; white streak behind bill, white curve below eye; black throat; lower belly chestnut; rump grey; tail black-banded with bright yellow tip; primaries black, yellow-edged with white 'fishhooks'; secondaries tipped with red sealing-wax droplets. Male: yellow tail-tip 6–11 mm, throat sharply defined, 6–8 waxy tips. Juvenile: throat whitish, grey-streaked.
- Bare parts — Iris dark brown; bill mainly black; legs dark grey or black.
- In the field — Crest unmistakable. Sociable, usually in flocks; long wings and short tail recall starling, flight equally fast and direct. Clambers easily through bushes and trees, only shuffles on the ground. Call a thin trilled sirrr, like jingling keys or silver bells.

## Bonelli's Eagle
*Aquila fasciata* · `boneag2`

- Jizz — 55–74 cm, wingspan 142–175 cm, 1.4–2.5 kg. Medium-sized, powerfully built eagle, clearly larger and heavier than a buzzard; broad wings with moderately fingered primaries and an S-curved trailing edge; tail long, square-ended. Female averages 10% larger.
- Plumage — Adult: upperparts and crown uniform blackish-brown, with a whitish patch in the middle of the back. Underbody and lesser underwing-coverts white with variable black streaking, contrasting sharply with the uniformly black median and greater underwing-coverts. Tail grey above, white below, finely barred with a broad dark subterminal band. Juvenile: underparts and underwing-coverts yellow-brown, tail closely barred, no black terminal band.
- In the field — Legs relatively long and feathered to the toes, the toes and claws very large and strong. Rather silent, but barking and shrill calls are heard at the nest.
- Similar species —
  - Golden Eagle: No white back patch and no white black-streaked underbody set against black underwing-coverts; much bigger, 66–102 cm, wingspan 1.8–2.34 m, up to 7 kg.
  - Spanish Eagle: Blackish-brown from throat to belly, pale beige nape and a broad white shoulder band, not white-bodied below; larger, 72–85 cm and wingspan 177–220 cm.
  - Imperial Eagle: Candidate text gives only size: 68–90 cm and wingspan 176–220 cm against 55–74 cm and 142–175 cm; otherwise separate on the white underbody and back patch.
  - Steppe Eagle: Bulky and mainly dark brown with a very thick neck, small head, long gape-line and rounded tail; no white underbody or back patch; often stands about on the ground.

## Booted Eagle
*Hieraaetus pennatus* · `booeag1`

- Jizz — 40–51 cm, 110–134 cm span; male 510–770 g, female 840–1,025 g. Small eagle, buzzard-sized but slimmer-winged; tail long and square-ended, about the wing's breadth; hand full, six fingered primaries.
- Plumage — Two morphs. Pale: underbody and underwing-coverts white against black flight feathers, three inner primaries paler and barred, fine brown streaks on the crop. Dark: uniformly dark brown, inner primaries still paler. Both: mottled upperparts, broad pale beige-brown band diagonally across the median coverts, pale nape and uppertail-coverts, white 'landing lights' on the forward scapulars, white at the bill base.
- Bare parts — Bill dark with a yellow cere; legs yellow; iris golden-yellow in adults, brown in juveniles.
- In the field — Fast, straight course with long glides. White patch at the inner leading edge of the wing, visible head-on, is the key mark. Usually in pairs or alone; vocal when breeding, a shrill kli-kli-kli.
- Similar species —
  - Pallid Harrier: A harrier: long wings held in a shallow V in low flight; male whitish grey with narrow black tips, female a ringtail with white uppertail-coverts.
  - Montagu's Harrier: Slighter, 39–50 cm and 96–116 cm span; male pale grey with black bands along the secondaries and rusty flank streaks, female a white-rumped ringtail.
  - Hen Harrier: Male grey above, white below with a grey breast, white rump and black wingtips; female a brown ringtail. Longer-winged and longer-tailed, 41–52 cm.
  - Common Buzzard: Same length, 40–58 cm, but round-headed, fairly short-tailed and broader-winged; lacks the white scapular flashes and the paler inner primaries.

## Booted Warbler
*Iduna caligata* · `boowar1`

- Jizz — 11–12.5 cm; smallest Hippolais, only slightly larger than Common Chiffchaff and about Willow Warbler size. Short-winged, rather pot-bellied, with short primary projection, short square-tipped tail and a short bill.
- Plumage — Plain greyish-brown above, beige-brown when fresh, whitish below with rust-buff flanks; short pale supercilium and a clearer dark eye-stripe, sometimes a hint of a dark lateral crown-stripe; narrow white edge to the outer tail feather, hard to see. Juvenile olive-brown with an ochre cast, yellowish-brown on head and underparts.
- Bare parts — Bill short, dark above with a distinct dark tip and pale brown-pink base; legs brownish-pink, toes darker grey.
- In the field — Restless and hard to see, keeping to bushes or the ground; flicks tail and wings but never dips the tail. Song a frantic 2–6 second phrase building in speed and volume; call a smacking tjreck, alarm a dry zerrr.
- Similar species —
  - Eastern Olivaceous Warbler: Larger, 11.5–14 cm, plainer pale brown with a strong pointed bill and grey legs, and dips its tail downward regularly; booted only flicks tail and wings.
  - Western Olivaceous Warbler: Larger again, 13–14.5 cm, wingspan 18–21 cm; browner-tinged pale brown above with a notably larger, strong pointed bill and grey legs.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Nearly the same size, 10–12 cm, but dull green-brown above and yellowish on the flanks, with fine dark bill and dark legs; booted's bill is stouter and pale-based, legs pink-brown.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Longer, 12–14 cm, with a long thick bill and short rounded wings; whitish supercilium runs only from bill to eye; plain olive-brown, without rust-buff flanks.

## Boreal Owl
*Aegolius funereus* · `borowl`

- Jizz — 21–26 cm, wingspan 54–62 cm. Small owl with a large head and long wings; no ear-tufts; short legs feathered white to the claws. Female heavier, 132–215 g against the male's 93–139 g.
- Plumage — Brown above with conspicuous pearly white spots on back, wings and scapulars; underparts whitish-grey, streaked rust-brown. Crown olive-brown with small white droplets; dull white facial disc framed dark, white eyebrows giving a startled expression. Flight feathers dark olive-brown with white spots, tail with three rows that stay separate when folded. Juvenile: dark brown, face patches, eyebrows and moustache strikingly white.
