- Bare parts — Bill light yellow; iris bright yellow.
- In the field — Flight essentially noiseless and straight. Male's territorial song a very characteristic series of three to ten strong po-po-po notes, repeated, carrying a kilometre; almost only after dark, late February to May.
- Similar species —
  - Little Owl: Flat-topped head and frowning face rather than a dark-framed white disc and startled look; greyish-brown, spotted, streaked and barred white, without the pearly spotting; long legs, unfeathered.
  - Eurasian Scops-Owl: Smaller, 19–21 cm; perches upright showing small ear-tufts, grey-brown with a pale shoulder line and no white pearl spots; call a deep whistle.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the candidate text: separated here on size, wingspan 54–62 cm against 131–188 cm.
  - Snowy Owl: Mostly pure white, usually appearing to lack ear-tufts, small head with a shallow facial disc; far bigger, wingspan 116–166 cm against 54–62 cm.

## Brambling
*Fringilla montifringilla* · `brambl`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, 17–30 g; wingspan 25–26 cm. Sparrow-sized finch, in size and shape like a Chaffinch; bill short and conical.
- Plumage — White rump in every plumage, and an orange breast contrasting with a white belly. Male in summer: glossy blue-black head, nape and mantle; throat, breast and lesser coverts unspotted rusty-orange; flanks dark-spotted. Female duller — grey-brown head with two faint dark crown-stripes, warm brown mantle, orange-buff breast. Winter male brown-grey-headed; wear reveals the breeding dress gradually.
- Bare parts — Bill straw-yellow with a black tip in winter in both sexes, black in the summer male.
- In the field — Song monotonous and dreary, a drawn-out buzzing note likened to a small circular saw in the distance; flight call loud and characteristically nasal, croaking. On passage big flocks roll across stubble and fields.
- Similar species —
  - Common Chaffinch: Grey-green rump, uniform pink or buff underparts, grey-brown scapulars, plain flanks and white outer tail feathers; bill dull pinkish, not yellow.
  - African Chaffinch: Male blue-grey-headed with a moss-green mantle, two bold white wing-stripes and a white-sided blue-grey tail; underparts pale pinkish, never orange breast on white belly.
  - European Greenfinch: Mainly green with yellow in wing and tail, bill thick and conical; no white rump and no orange breast. Female and young duller, brown-toned above.
  - Crimson-winged Finch: Pale, thickset finch with a heavy dull-yellowish bill; lacks the black head, orange breast and white rump.

## Brant
*Branta bernicla* · `brant`

- Jizz — 55–66 cm. Small, slim goose with a round head, short stubby bill, short narrow wings and the shortest tail of any goose.
- Plumage — Head, neck and primaries black, a small white crescent each side of the neck; body dark grey-brown, flanks barred black and white; white rump cut by a black wedge from the back; undertail white, tail black-sided. Pale-bellied hrota: belly pale grey against the black breast. Dark-bellied bernicla: belly as dark as the back.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black.
- In the field — Up-ends to feed while swimming, when the dark wedge on the white undertail shows. Juveniles cannot safely be assigned to taxon in the field.
- Similar species —
  - Canada Goose: Much larger, 75–110 cm, and marked with a broad white chinstrap on the black head, not a small white neck crescent.
  - Barnacle Goose: White face and white belly, back silver-grey barred black and white; broader wings, 120–145 cm span. Brant is smaller, slimmer, longer-necked, dark-bodied.
  - Red-breasted Goose: Red breast, unmistakable close to, but at range the red looks dark and it hides easily in Brant flocks; slightly smaller, 53–56 cm.
  - Cackling Goose: White chinstrap on the black head rather than a small neck crescent; heavier, 1,400–2,380 g at 60–65 cm.

## Broad-billed Sandpiper
*Calidris falcinellus* · `brbsan`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm, wingspan 36–40 cm, 30–45 g. Small wader, slightly smaller than Dunlin, larger than Little Stint; legs short and set well back; bill medium-long (about 3 cm), straight, soft, with a sharply kinked-down tip.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: upperparts dark, boldly patterned with narrow pale fringes forming a double V down the mantle; pale crown-stripe and a distinctive split supercilium forking before the eye; white below, breast with blackish arrowhead marks reaching the flanks. Winter: much paler grey above, white below, head pattern kept. Juvenile: paler and cleaner; white flanks and belly diagnostic.
- Bare parts — Bill dark brown.
- In the field — In flight looks very dark above with only a faint pale wingbar; tail patterned as Dunlin but sides paler. Contact call a dry whistling dree-it and a clicking dik dik; display a buzzing, mostly at night over the breeding ground.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Much bigger, 23–26 cm, small-headed and short-necked with a straight bill no longer than the head; winter birds uniform pale grey; no split supercilium.
  - Dunlin: Larger, 16–21 cm, longer-legged; bill evenly decurved, not kinked at the tip; breeding adult shows a black belly patch; no split supercilium.
  - Ruff: Pot-bellied and small-headed on long pale legs (pink to orange in adults); deeper, slower wingbeats and white ovals at the tail sides; male 29–32 cm.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Larger, 19–22 cm, dark above with a purplish gloss and grey-smeared breast; short yellow legs and a yellow-based bill.

## Brown-necked Raven
*Corvus ruficollis* · `brnrav1`

- Jizz — 48–56 cm, wingspan to 120 cm. Large raven, bigger than a carrion crow but smaller than a common raven; bill slender for a raven, wings more pointed, hand narrow, tail short and wedge-shaped; throat feathering shaggy.
- Plumage — Head and throat distinctly brownish-black, giving the name; rest of the plumage black glossed purple, blue or purplish-blue, with a bronze-brown tone on the nape that shows only at close range. Feathers fade fast, so birds can look plainly brown by the time they moult.
- Bare parts — Bill, legs and feet black; toes often yellow, especially in the Middle East.
- In the field — In flight holds the bill characteristically angled downwards; pale legs often contrast when seen from below. Calls far higher than a common raven's — a crow-like kri-äh or a strained grreu grreu grreu.
- Similar species —
  - Carrion Crow: Smaller, 44–52 cm, all black with a green or purple sheen; no brown head or nape, no wedge-shaped tail, bill blunter.
  - Hooded Crow: Body ash-grey with dark shafts, only head, throat, wings, tail and thighs black; flight slow, heavy and straight.
  - Rook: Bare whitish skin before the eye and round the bill base; smaller, 44–46 cm, glossed blue-purple, head and neck feathers dense and silky.
  - Common Raven: Bigger, 54–71 cm, with a heavier bill, longer tail and broader hand; wholly black, without the brown head and nape; calls much deeper.

## Budgerigar
*Melopsittacus undulatus* · `budger`

- Jizz — 18 cm, wingspan 30 cm, 30–40 g. Tiny slim parrot; tail graduated, the central feathers very long.
- Plumage — Body light green; head and upper back finely barred black on yellow, broadening into crossbars on the coverts; forehead, eye-region, throat and fore-cheek yellow; iridescent blue-violet cheek patches and a row of black round throat spots. Tail cobalt to greenish-blue, outer feathers with central yellow flashes; flight feathers greenish-black, coverts black fringed yellow. Juvenile: duller, barring reaching forehead and cere.
- Bare parts — Bill olive-grey; legs and feet blue-grey; cere blue in males, brown, beige, white or pale blue in females.
- In the field — Wild and feral birds are wild-type green; blue, grey, yellow and white are captive-bred. Wild Australian birds are noticeably smaller than captive ones.
- Similar species —
  - Rose-ringed Parakeet: Far larger, 37–43 cm against 18 cm, much of it tail; male shows a pink and black neck ring, and there is no black barring or yellow face.
  - Alexandrine Parakeet: Far larger, 50–62 cm, with a massive red bill and a red shoulder patch; green without black barring or a yellow face.

## Bulwer's Petrel
*Bulweria bulwerii* · `bulpet`

- Jizz — 25–29 cm, 78–130 g. Small slender petrel; very long narrow wings; long tapering tail, usually held closed and looking narrow, wedge-shaped on the rare occasions it is fanned.
- Plumage — Very dark sooty-brown throughout, looking almost wholly dark; paler greater coverts form a pale diagonal band along each upperwing; underwing uniformly dark.
- Bare parts — Bill black; feet flesh-coloured.
- In the field — Buoyant twisting flight, picking planktonic food from the sea surface.
- Similar species —
  - Manx Shearwater: Manx shearwater is bigger, 30–38 cm with a 75–89 cm span, black above and white below — a flying cross flashing black to white as it shears on stiff wings.
  - Yelkouan Shearwater: Yelkouan shearwater, 30–39 cm, is dark above with paler underside flashing white as it shears; Bulwer's stays uniformly dark but for the pale upperwing band.
  - Balearic Shearwater: Balearic shearwater is larger, 30–40 cm, dark brown above and dirty white below, shearing from side to side on stiff wings.
  - Barolo Shearwater: Barolo shearwater has a pale face with a contrastingly dark eye, a silvery upperwing panel, shorter rounder wings and blue feet.

## Cackling Goose
*Branta hutchinsii* · `cacgoo1`

- Jizz — 60–65 cm, wingspan 108–111 cm, 1400–2380 g. Small Branta goose, shorter-necked and shorter-billed than Canada Goose; minima the smallest race at about 60 cm, wingspan 110 cm and 1600 g.
- Plumage — Black head and neck with a white chinstrap; body brownish, most races darker than Canada Goose. hutchinsii palest-breasted, though greyer than the pale-breasted Canada races, with a square head and steeply sloping forehead; minima rounder-headed and smallest; leucopareia with a white neck-ring between black neck and dark brown breast. Female virtually identical, slightly lighter.
- In the field — Voice higher and more yelping than Canada Goose: gek-gek, jilk-jilk a-lick or aik; the female's differs from the male's. Breeds far north with a short season, often returning to a site that succeeded the year before.
- Similar species —
  - Canada Goose: Larger, 75–110 cm, longer-necked and longer-billed, body plumage browner and mostly paler; only the smallest Canada races overlap, and those are genuinely hard to call.
  - Barnacle Goose: White face and black breast, body silver-grey and white rather than brown, more white on the head; V-shaped white rump patch in flight; 55–70 cm, wingspan 120–145 cm.
  - Brant: No chinstrap – only a small white patch each side of the black neck; body uniformly dark grey-brown, undertail pure white, tail very short; 55–66 cm.
  - Red-breasted Goose: Brightly marked red breast and face, unmistakable close to, though the red looks dark at range; smaller, 53–56 cm, 1000–1620 g.

## Calandra Lark
*Melanocorypha calandra* · `callar1`

- Jizz — 17.5–20 cm, wingspan about 40 cm. Large, robust lark with a big thick bill, broad wings and a short, barely notched tail.
- Plumage — Streaked greyish brown to pale sandy above, white below, with a large black patch on each side of the breast and neck; pale supercilium; throat and breast creamy, the breast dark-streaked; tail feathers brown-black with white outer edges.
- Bare parts — Bill large and thick, brown above, yellowish below; feet brownish-pink; iris brown.
- In the field — In flight short broad wings, dark below with a broad white trailing edge, and a short white-edged tail. Song given in flight, like a slower Skylark with many call-like notes and mimicry.
- Similar species —
  - Horned Lark: Striking black and yellow face pattern and black horns on the summer male; tail mostly black against a paler body; no black breast-side patches.
  - Crested Lark: Smaller, 17–18 cm, bulkier about the head, with a conspicuous crest at all times and light brown outer tail feathers; no black neck patches.
  - Dupont's Lark: Slim, long-necked and long-legged with a fine, slightly curved bill and a thin pale crown stripe; breast dark-streaked but without black patches; song thin, ventriloquial.
  - Eurasian Skylark: Short blunt crest that can be raised and lowered; white edges to tail and rear wing but no dark underwing or black breast-side patches; song faster in tempo.

## Canada Goose
*Branta canadensis* · `cangoo`

- Jizz — 75–110 cm; males 3.5–6.5 kg, females 3–5.5 kg, maxima race to 7.5 kg. On average the largest true goose, and the largest and longest-necked goose breeding in Europe.
- Plumage — Head and neck black with a broad white chinstrap running from the throat up behind the eye; black neck cut sharply against a pale grey breast; upperparts grey-brown to red-brown by race; flanks and belly beige-brown, clearly barred; vent and uppertail-coverts white, tail black-grey. Sexes alike. Odd birds show dark cheeks, white foreheads or white necks.
- Bare parts — Bill and feet black to grey-black; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Nothing on flight, gait, habitat or voice in the sources.
- Similar species —
  - Barnacle Goose: White face against black head, neck and upper breast, belly white, back silver-grey barred black and white; much smaller (55–70 cm, 1.2–2.2 kg), bill small and stubby.
  - Brant: Small (55–66 cm) and uniformly dark grey-brown, with only a small white neck patch instead of a chinstrap; undertail pure white, tail the shortest of any goose.
  - Red-breasted Goose: Far smaller (53–56 cm, 1.05–1.62 kg) and boldly marked with red on the breast, though the red can look dark at long range.
  - Cackling Goose: Same black head and white chinstrap; most races much smaller, with shorter neck, smaller bill and a square head with steep forehead; hutchinsii pale-breasted but greyer.

## Carrion Crow
*Corvus corone* · `y00743`

- Jizz — 44–52 cm, wingspan 84–104 cm, 400–600 g. Compact, thickset crow; tall slightly curved heavy bill, close-lying thigh feathers, bulky body plumage; wings fairly long and moderately fingered, tail broad and slightly rounded; wingtips just past the tail at rest.
- Plumage — Black with a green or purple sheen, much greener than a Rook's gloss. Two morphs: wholly black birds in western Europe and eastern Asia, black-and-grey birds between — black head, chin, upper breast, flight feathers, some coverts and tail, ash-grey elsewhere; intermediates occur. Juvenile browner and duller.
- Bare parts — Bill, legs and feet black; iris dark brown. Juvenile iris blue to greyish, gape red at first.
- In the field — Waddling gait; flies powerfully and slowly, usually fairly straight. One moult a year, in autumn. Odd birds carry scattered white feathers.
- Similar species —
  - Hooded Crow: Ash-grey body with black head, throat, wings and tail; separates from wholly black western Carrion Crows, but grey-and-black Carrion Crows occur between the ranges.
  - Rook: Bare whitish skin before the eye and around the bill base; gloss blue to bluish-purple, not green; head looks more domed and the bill longer.
  - Common Raven: Much bigger, 54–71 cm against 44–52 cm, with a wingspan of 116–153 cm and a large heavy bill.
  - Brown-necked Raven: Larger, 52–56 cm; head and throat distinctly brownish-black, the rest glossed purple or blue; bill less deep than a raven's, wings more pointed.

## Caspian Gull
*Larus cachinnans* · `casgul2`

- Jizz — 56–68 cm, wingspan 137–155 cm, 680–1590 g. Slender large gull; small head, long sloping forehead, angular rear crown, long slim bill with a weak gonys angle; long wings, neck and legs.
- Plumage — Adult: head, neck, underparts, rump and tail pure white; upperparts pale grey without a bluish tone. Broad white trailing edge running into white primary tips; outermost primary with a large white tip and white tongue up the inner web; black crescent-shaped and covering six primaries. First-winter: pale head, streaked hindneck, greyish back, two pale lines across the coverts.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow to greenish-yellow, gonys spot orange or red; eye brown to yellow-brown, often looking dark; orbital ring orange or red; legs pink to yellowish.
- In the field — Stands upright with the wings held rather low, chest pushed out or neck stretched. Breeds and moults earlier than northern gulls, so a bird out of step in moult is a pointer; adult plumage after the fourth winter.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Stouter-billed and bigger-headed, mantle a shade paler with a bluish cast; eye pale in a yellow orbital ring, legs pink; black in a triangle over five primaries only.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Sources give only size: much larger, 61–79 cm and 0.75–2.3 kg against 56–68 cm and 0.68–1.59 kg; no plumage characters offered.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Little in the sources beyond size, and it overlaps: 51–64 cm and 452–1100 g against 56–68 cm and 680–1590 g; Caspian is built much like it.
  - Iceland Gull: Sources give only structure and size: 50–64 cm, 480–1100 g, slender, light and thin-billed, usually smaller than a Herring Gull; no plumage characters.

## Caspian Tern
*Hydroprogne caspia* · `caster1`

- Jizz — 48–60 cm, 530–782 g. The world's largest tern; coarse, gull-like build with a heavy head and long black tarsi; bill long and thick; tail short and only slightly forked.
- Plumage — White head with a black cap, white neck, belly and tail; upperwing and back pale grey; underwing pale with dark primaries, so the wingtips look black from below and can show a dark triangular wedge from above. Winter: cap retained, but the forehead streaked white. Juvenile: crown, wings, back and tail mottled grey-brown.
- Bare parts — Bill thick, coral to orange-red with a black tip or broad dark subterminal band, duller orange and shorter in juveniles; legs black to dark brown.
- In the field — Flies with slower wingbeats than other terns. Voice a few harsh, heron-like croaks; a drawn-out ra-ra-ra-ratschau as adults and young meet, given with a bow on the ground or in the air.
- Similar species —
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: A gull: no black cap and no thick red bill; the candidate text gives only size, 51–64 cm and 452–1,100 g against 48–60 cm and 530–782 g.
  - Iceland Gull: Gull build, relatively slender, light and thin-billed; no black cap or red bill. Candidate text gives only structure and size, 50–66 cm against 48–60 cm.
  - Yellow-legged Gull: Grey back and yellow legs, no black cap and no heavy red bill; larger, 52–68 cm and 550–1,600 g against 48–60 cm and 530–782 g.
  - European Herring Gull: Light grey back and upperwings, white head, black wingtips with white mirrors, yellow bill with a red spot, pink legs; no black cap.

## Cetti's Warbler
*Cettia cetti* · `cetwar1`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm, 12–18 g. Compact warbler: rounded head, short neck, short strongly rounded wings, pointed bill, and a tail longer and broader than most warblers'. Ten tail feathers, not twelve.
- Plumage — Upperparts rich chestnut to dark reddish-brown; throat and breast pale grey-brown, dirty grey-white below with brownish tones on flanks and belly. Narrow, washed-out pale grey stripe arching over the eye. Sexes alike, but males 26–32% heavier with wings 11–13% longer. Juvenile: as adult, plumage fresh, two dark spots on the tongue.
- Bare parts — Iris black and conspicuous.
- In the field — Announces itself with loud song in short bursts of unique structure; the female does not sing and is very hard to see. Rendered plitt, plitt-plytt … titjutt-titjutt-titjutt tjytt, tjutt!

## Chilean Flamingo
*Phoenicopterus chilensis* · `chifla1`

- Jizz — 100–140 cm, wingspan 120–145 cm, 2.5–3.5 kg. Very large flamingo; neck long, held in an S at rest and straight in flight; the legs alone 40–50 cm. Female smaller.
- Plumage — Pink throughout, the depth of colour depending on diet; chicks grey or peach, gaining pink as they age.
- Bare parts — More than half the bill black, the base much paler; legs grey to beige-grey with strikingly red intertarsal joints and feet, the diagnostic mark.
- In the field — Call a goose-like trumpeting; neck stretched straight in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Flamingo: Larger at 110–150 cm and paler pinkish-white; legs entirely pink and the bill pink with only a small black tip, not black over half its length.

## Chiloe Wigeon
*Mareca sibilatrix* · `chiwig1`

- Jizz — 43–56 cm, 800–939 g, wingspan 75–86 cm. Compact, heavy-bodied wigeon — heavier than both Eurasian and American Wigeon, and with less difference between the sexes.
- Plumage — Male: forehead and face below the eye white, rest of the head black glossed green or purple, white ear-patch; neck and hindcrown black; breast barred black and white; back grey-black with sharply contrasting white feather tips, uppertail-coverts white; belly white; flanks and undertail-coverts chestnut. Female: duller, flanks paler brown, head without gloss. Juvenile: rust on the flanks reduced or absent.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey with a black tip; legs and feet grey; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Very vocal; the male's two-syllable whistle carries even at night. Sexes hard to tell on plumage alone — males only somewhat larger, heavier and more strongly glossed on the cap.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Drake has a chestnut head with creamy crown, grey flanks and back, pink breast, black stern and a brilliant white upper-wing patch; lighter, 500–1073 g, 42–52 cm.
  - Gadwall: Drake patterned grey with black stern and a brilliant white speculum; female mallard-like with white belly and orange-edged bill; longer, 47–58 cm; no white face.
  - Red-crested Pochard: Diving duck with rounded orange head, red bill, black breast, white flanks and brown back; female pale brown with whitish face; 45–55 cm, up to 1500 g.
  - Wood Duck: Crested perching duck, 47–54 cm; drake multicoloured with red eyes and a white neck flare; female with white eye-ring and whitish throat; blue-green white-edged speculum.

## Chukar
*Alectoris chukar* · `chukar`

- Jizz — 32–36 cm. Rotund, thickset partridge; tail of 14 feathers, third primary longest. Female slightly smaller and without the spur.
- Plumage — Back light brown, breast grey, belly buff, flanks streaked rufous; face white with a sharply defined black gorget, throat patch cream to yellowish; black band round the eyes enclosing a red-brown ear patch, broad diffuse grey-white supercilium; crown wine-red, ash-grey at sides and hindneck, forehead black; rump and lower back ash-grey; outer tail feathers rufous. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill coral red; eye cere coral red; legs coral red.
- In the field — Sociable but wary, running rather than flying when disturbed. Flight low, with fast beats and stiff glides. Song a noisy chuck-chuck-chukar-chukar.
- Similar species —
  - Red-legged Partridge: Black collar breaks into dark streaking down onto the breast instead of a clean gorget; cream throat, pink legs, red bill and eye-ring; sandy-brown above.
  - Barbary Partridge: Gorget broad and reddish-brown, not black; face light grey; back grey-brown; song a noisy tre-tre-tre-cheeche-tre.
  - Black Francolin: Male black with a white cheek patch, chestnut collar and white flank spots, tail black barred white; female brown with a chestnut hindneck; 33–36 cm, 450–500 g.
  - Rock Ptarmigan: White wings and underparts, all white in winter but for black tail and eye-line; higher, more barren ground; 31–36 cm, wingspan 54–60 cm.

## Cinereous Vulture
*Aegypius monachus* · `cinvul1`

- Jizz — 98–120 cm, wingspan 2.5–3.1 m, 6.3–14 kg. Largest accipitrid and one of the heaviest flying birds; broad wings, often slightly wedge-shaped tail, short bare neck.
- Plumage — Uniformly dark brown, looking black at any distance; short bare bluish-pink neck ringed by a dark feather ruff. Female averages slightly heavier and larger than male.
- Bare parts — Bill tip black; iris brown; legs grey-blue to whitish-yellow, pink in juveniles, whose bill may also be pink.
- In the field — Nothing on flight action, gait, habitat or voice in the sources; identification here rests on sheer bulk, the broad wings and the bare, ruff-ringed neck.
- Similar species —
  - Black-winged Kite: Small, grey or white raptor with black shoulder patches, wingtips and eye stripe; long falcon-like wings overshoot the short square tail at rest; jerks its tail when perched.
  - Black Kite: Far smaller, wingspan 130–155 cm, about 735 g; brown with paler head and neck, dark patch behind the eye, dark-shafted streaked underparts, tail slightly forked.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: A medium-sized eagle, 55–74 cm, wingspan 142–175 cm, 1.4–2.5 kg — a fraction of this bulk. The sources give no plumage marks for it.
  - Booted Eagle: Small eagle, 40–51 cm, wingspan 110–132 cm, 510–1,025 g; pale morph light grey with darker head and flight feathers, dark morph mid-brown.

## Cirl Bunting
*Emberiza cirlus* · `cirbun1`

- Jizz — 15–16.5 cm, about 25 g — house-sparrow size, like a small yellowhammer; thick seed-eater's bill; tail dark with white outer feathers.
- Plumage — Male: black crown, eyestripe and throat on a bright yellow head; greenish breast band across yellow underparts; flanks chestnut; back chestnut and heavily streaked; rump olive. Non-breeding male duller, brown feathers veiling the face pattern. Female: like female yellowhammer but rump streaked grey-brown to olive, shoulders chestnut, finely streaked above and below. Juvenile: paler, more yellow-brown.
- In the field — Song a monotonous rattling trill, like an Arctic warbler or the terminal rattle of a lesser whitethroat.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Larger and bulky, 16–19 cm, lark-like; sexes alike, streaked grey-brown above, whitish below with a streaked gorget, conspicuously dark eye, yellowish mandibles, plain brown tail.
  - Reed Bunting: 13.5–15.5 cm; male's black head and throat come with a white neck collar and white underparts, no yellow anywhere; female's head streaked brown, underparts barred.
  - Yellowhammer: Rump rufous, not olive; male's yellow head lacks the black crown, eyestripe and throat and there is no greenish breast band; 16–16.5 cm, wingspan 23–29.5 cm.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 13–15 cm against 15–16.5 cm.

## Citril Finch
*Carduelis citrinella* · `citfin1`

- Jizz — 12–12.5 cm, 12.5–15 g. Small finch with a short, sharply pointed conical bill; relatively long tail, giving a slimmer profile than Eurasian Siskin.
- Plumage — Male: face, underparts and rump yellow-green to lemon; crown, nape and neck sides ash-grey; back olive-grey to olive-green with black streaks; two yellow-green wing bars on a dark wing; tail dark. Female: duller and greyer, grey on the breast as well; young females duller below. Juvenile: brown, with no yellow or green at all.
- In the field — Song a silvery twittering, short and paused, recalling European Goldfinch with buzzing redpoll-like notes; call a single teh, repeated in flight, or a tee-ee.
- Similar species —
  - European Goldfinch: Red face on a black and white head, warm brown upperparts, white underparts; broad yellow wing flash, ivory pointed bill, forked tail; wingspan 21–25 cm.
  - Corsican Finch: Back brown and dark-streaked, not olive; face and underparts yellow with less green; uppertail-coverts greyish; call weaker and tremulous, song longer and wren-like.
  - European Serin: Heavily streaked above and below, yellow breast against a white belly and a yellow rump; shorter-tailed, 11–12 cm; song a buzzing trill.
  - Eurasian Siskin: Male black-capped with a black bib, a bright yellow wing stripe and yellow tail sides; streaked below; shorter-tailed, 11–12.5 cm, wingspan 20–23 cm.

## Citrine Wagtail
*Motacilla citreola* · `citwag`

- Jizz — 15.5–17 cm; tail 6.5–8.0 cm, wing 80–90 mm. Slender wagtail with the long, constantly wagging tail of the genus; slightly larger and longer-tailed than Western Yellow Wagtail.
- Plumage — Breeding male: head bright lemon-yellow, yellow continuing over the underparts and whitening to white undertail-coverts; black band about 1 cm wide across the nape onto the upper breast-sides; mantle mid-grey, rump dark grey; white on the remiges; two outer tail-feather pairs mostly white. Winter: yellow diluted white, head brownish with yellowish supercilium. Female: washed-out winter male.
- Bare parts — Bill slender, blackish, sometimes paler grey to flesh at the base below; feet and legs blackish; iris dark brown, grey-brown in younger birds.
- In the field — Tail wagged constantly, as in all wagtails.
- Similar species —
  - White Wagtail: Larger, 16.5–19 cm; nominate pale grey above, no yellow anywhere. Little else in the sources: otherwise separated on size.
  - Western Yellow Wagtail: Olive above rather than grey and without the black nape band; shortest-tailed European wagtail, 15–16 cm, against Citrine's slightly larger, longer-tailed build.
  - Gray Wagtail: Yellow confined to the vent, contrasting with whitish underparts; narrow white supercilium and broken eye-ring; breeding male black-throated with white moustachial stripes.
  - Rock Pipit: A pipit: smoky-olive above, buff below and heavily streaked, dark bill, legs and iris, pale eye-ring; no yellow and no long wagging tail.

## Coal Tit
*Periparus ater* · `coatit2`

- Jizz — 10–11.5 cm, 8–10 g. Tiny, short-tailed tit, the smallest in Sweden.
- Plumage — Head, throat and neck glossy blue-black, with a large brilliant white nape spot drawn out as a stripe; face sides off-white, tinged grey or yellow by race; upperparts bluish- to olive-grey; two white wingbars from white-tipped coverts; underparts whitish, buff to rufous on the flanks. Juvenile: duller, head not glossy, nape and cheeks tinged yellow.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs lead-coloured; irides dark brown.
- In the field — Flocks keep contact with incessant short dee or see-see calls while feeding; song a strident if-he, if-he, if-he, like a great tit's but much faster and higher.
- Similar species —
  - Great Tit: Much larger, 12.5–14 cm and 14–22 g; lemon-yellow below with a broad black stripe from bib to vent, green back, white cheeks, one white wingbar.
  - Crested Tit: Nothing in the sources separates them on plumage: only weight, 10.8–11 g against 8–10 g, and its coniferous forest and Scottish pinewood range.
  - Eurasian Blue Tit: Blue crown, dark blue line through the eye ringing the white cheeks, blue wings and tail, yellowish-green back, sulphur-yellow below with a dark belly line.
  - Willow Tit: Larger, 11.5 cm; dull black cap running down onto the mantle, no white nape spot, white face sides, grey-brown back, buff underparts.

## Cockatiel
*Nymphicus hollandicus* · `cockat`

- Jizz — 30–33 cm, 73–102 g. Smallest of the cockatoos, which otherwise run 30–60 cm; long tail makes up roughly half the total length; prominent mobile crest.
- Plumage — Normal grey: mainly grey with prominent white flashes along the outer edge of each wing; round orange ear patch in both sexes. Male: face yellow or white, cheek patch vivid, tail underside plain. Female: face grey, cheek patch muted, tail underside barred yellow and black, plumage paler. Juvenile like the female. Many bred colour varieties differ widely.
- In the field — Crest reads the bird's mood: vertical when startled or excited, gently oblique at rest, flat when angry or defensive, flat but flared behind when displaying, half-raised with a curled tip when tired.

## Collared Flycatcher
*Ficedula albicollis* · `colfly1`

- Jizz — 12–13.5 cm, 12–18 g. Small, compactly built flycatcher; head relatively large, bill short and thin, wings broad and pointed, tail long, thin and fanned.
- Plumage — Male: black above, white below, with a broad white collar, large white forehead patch, large white wing patch and pale rump; tail black, a minority with white sides. Female: grey-brown above, no collar, forehead patch small or absent, pale eye-ring, wing patch smaller. Autumn males, juveniles brown-grey and female-like.
- Bare parts — Bill black, broad-based but pointed; legs slate-grey.
- In the field — Deciduous woodland, parks and gardens; takes insects in flight, hunts caterpillars in oak foliage, takes berries. Call a thin indrawn whistle iihp; song pressed whistles with big jumps in pitch.
- Similar species —
  - European Pied Flycatcher: No white collar and only a small forehead patch; tail sides white; bill tilts slightly up, not down. Non-breeders barely separable, and the two hybridise.
  - Red-breasted Flycatcher: Smaller, 11–12 cm; brown above with a grey head and orange throat, bold white eyering, black tail with white bases to the outer feathers, pale-based bill.
  - Semicollared Flycatcher: Same 12–13.5 cm and intermediate between this bird and Pied — the collar incomplete; hybridises here, so clinal individuals occur.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: Large white wing and forehead patches as here; the sources give nothing further to separate them in the field, and no measurements.

## Collared Pratincole
*Glareola pratincola* · `colpra`

- Jizz — 24–28 cm, wingspan 60–70 cm, 60–95 g; about Grey Plover size but shorter-legged and longer-winged. Short legs, long pointed wings, long forked tail projecting past the closed wing-tips at rest, short bill for feeding on the wing.
- Plumage — Dull mid-brown above and on head, chin and fore-breast; upper wing-coverts brown against darker flight feathers; white trailing edge to the wing; belly, rump and uppertail-coverts white, tail black; underwing chestnut but often looking wholly dark. White streak under the large black eye. Summer: cream-yellow throat ringed black. Winter: ring lost. Juvenile scaly, greyer, with a dark breast-band.
- Bare parts — Bill short and dark, red at the base in summer adults; eye large and black.
- In the field — Feeds in the air, flight swallow-like. On the ground runs fast, plover-fashion, with sudden movements of head and neck and a conspicuous bobbing of the rear body.
- Similar species —
  - Black-winged Pratincole: Underwing black and no white trailing edge — diagnostic but often unseeable in the field; marginally larger on the same 24–28 cm, shorter-tailed and longer-legged.

## Common Bulbul
*Pycnonotus barbatus* · `combul2`

- Jizz — 18–20 cm. Thrush-like bulbul with a long tail; bill short and fine, the upper mandible slightly decurved.
- Plumage — Head dark brown to blackish, contrasting; upperparts brown, wings and tail grey to dark grey; underparts dull grey-brown, paler whitish on belly and vent. A small crest is sometimes raised. Sexes alike, male averaging larger. Some races show yellow undertail-coverts.
- Bare parts — Bill, legs and feet black; iris dark brown, with an indistinct dark orbital ring.
- In the field — Call a loud doctor-quick doctor-quick be-quick be-quick.
- Similar species —
  - White-spectacled Bulbul: Larger, 20–25 cm against 18–20, and shows obvious pale eye-rings; the dark orbital ring here is barely visible in the field.

## Common Buzzard
*Buteo buteo* · `combuz1`

- Jizz — 40–58 cm, wingspan 109–140 cm. Compact medium raptor; round head, slender bill, broad wings reaching about the tail tip when perched, fairly short rounded tail, short bare tarsi.
- Plumage — Highly variable, from almost white to almost black-brown; most European birds dark brown above and on head and mantle. Tail grey-brown to rust with eight to twelve narrow dark bars, a pale tip and a broad dark subterminal band. Breast-patch usually dark-streaked; underwing usually paler than the body; primary tips always dark. Juvenile breast band streaked, adult barred.
- Bare parts — Iris dark brown to almost yellow.
- In the field — Soars with the wings held in a shallow V. Vocal for a raptor: commonest call a falling mewing, mostly given in flight; young beg similarly but more vibrant. Extremely pale birds are white-headed with white tail base and underwings.
- Similar species —
  - Rough-legged Hawk: Larger and longer-winged, 46–68 cm, span 120–153 cm; upper tail usually pale and dark carpal patches obvious.
  - Long-legged Buzzard: Clearly larger, 50–66 cm, and longer-winged; large bill on a small head, long tail and long legs; more contrast between dark belly and pale breast; tail more translucent.
  - Booted Eagle: Similar span, 110–132 cm, but only 40 cm long; pale morph light grey with darker head and flight feathers, dark morph mid-brown with dark grey flight feathers.
  - Hen Harrier: Long-winged and long-tailed, 41–52 cm, and much lighter at 290–400 g in the male; male grey above, white below with a grey upper breast and white rump.

## Common Chaffinch
*Fringilla coelebs* · `comcha`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, wingspan 24.5–29 cm, 18–29 g. House sparrow-sized but slimmer and longer-tailed; bill short, thick and conical.
- Plumage — Male: black forehead, blue-grey crown, nape and upper mantle, browner-grey in winter; lower mantle and scapulars a brown saddle; rump light olive-green; face, throat and breast dull rust-red fading to creamy-pink on the belly. Female: olive-grey above, paler below. Juvenile like female. Both sexes: white shoulder patch, white wingbar and white wedges in the outer tail feathers.
- Bare parts — Iris dark brown; legs grey-brown. Male's bill bluish-grey with a small black tip in spring, pale grey in winter; female's horn-brown year-round.
- In the field — Walks on the ground with rhythmic head-nodding; flight undulating, in powerful bows. Fairly tame. Song loud and penetrating from March, ending in a flourish 'tsitsitsi-tsjatsja-soritiu-tsip'; call a loud 'fink, fink', flight note a muffled 'jupp'.
- Similar species —
  - Brambling: White rump against the chaffinch's grey-green, orange breast contrasting with a white belly, orange scapulars; breeding male black-headed. Same size and shape.
  - African Chaffinch: Male has a moss-green mantle, not reddish-brown, and lacks the reddish cheeks; two bold white wing stripes, white edges to the tertials, blue-grey tail with white sides.
  - European Greenfinch: Mainly green with yellow in wings and tail; thick conical bill; females and young duller, brown-toned on the back. Song trilling and wheezing.
  - Crimson-winged Finch: Pale, thickset finch with a heavy dull yellowish bill; the sources give nothing else, and lengths overlap, 13–18 cm against 14–16 cm.

## Common Chiffchaff
*Phylloscopus collybita* · `comchi1`

- Jizz — 10–12 cm, 6–10 g. Small, dumpy, compact leaf warbler; rather large-headed, short-winged, with a short primary projection and a fine dark bill. Sexes alike in size and plumage.
- Plumage — Brown-washed dull green above, rump often brighter green; off-white below, yellowish on the flanks, throat and breast variably yellow and buff, flanks washed beige-brown in autumn. Short yellowish-white supercilium, clear before the eye and vague behind; dark eyestripe splits the pale eyering; lower face and ear-coverts dark. Worn birds duller and browner.
- Bare parts — Bill fine, dark horn with an inconspicuous pale orange base and sides; legs dark brown to grey-black, rarely paler brown.
- In the field — Song a repetitive cheerful chiff-chaff, one of the first signs of spring; call a hweet, less disyllabic than Willow Warbler's hooeet.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Paler pinkish-yellow legs against dark brown to blackish, longer paler bill, longer primary projection, more elegant shape; song a descending whistle, call disyllabic hoo-eet.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Smaller, 9.5–11 cm, 4–9 g; two bold yellowish-white wingbars, yellow-margined tertials and a long yellow supercilium, sometimes a pale crown stripe.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above with a single pale wingbar and a thin bill; no wingbar is described for chiffchaff. Song a high jerky trill.
  - Western Bonelli's Warbler: Plain grey-green back with green-toned rump and wings, whitish underparts, small pointed bill, brown legs; call hu-it against chiffchaff's hweet.

## Common Crane
*Grus grus* · `comcra`

- Jizz — 100–130 cm, 3–6.1 kg. Tall, long-legged and long-necked; slender wedge-shaped bill over ten centimetres; elongated inner secondaries droop over the short true tail as a bushy plume.
- Plumage — Slate-grey overall, darkest on back and rump, palest on breast and wings. Forehead and lores blackish, crown a bare red patch, cheeks and neck black, a white streak from behind the eye to the upper back. Primaries, secondary tips, alula and tail-tip black. Juvenile: yellowish-brown feather tips, plain sandy head, feathered crown, no plume.
- Bare parts — Iris either red or yellow, the two types unrelated to sex or age; very dark in juveniles.
- In the field — Migrates in flocks in V-formation; loud nasal rasping trumpet. In the breeding season stains back and scapulars brown with iron-rich mud.

## Common Cuckoo
*Cuculus canorus* · `comcuc`

- Jizz — 32–34 cm, about 130 g; wingspan 55–60 cm, tail 13–15 cm. Slim and long-tailed with pointed wings and short legs; recalls a sparrowhawk in flight, but wing-tips more pointed.
- Plumage — Male slate-grey above; chin, throat and upper breast pale grey, cut off sharply from white underparts broadly barred grey-brown; tail dark slate-grey, white-tipped. Female grey morph: as male but breast washed pinkish-buff and finely barred. Rufous morph, mostly females: upperparts reddish-brown with black bars narrower than the rufous ones. Juvenile: variable, chestnut-brown and heavily barred or plain grey, feathers whitish-edged.
- Bare parts — Iris, orbital ring, bill-base and feet yellow. Outer toe reversible, as in all cuckoos.
- In the field — Perches openly on posts in the breeding season, wings drooped below the body, stepped tail raised and often fanned. Wingbeats regular. Call a two-syllable go-gå, stress on the first note; the territorial version accelerates.
- Similar species —
  - Great Spotted Cuckoo: Larger, 35–39 cm, and looks bigger still on broad wings and a long narrow tail; grey cap, yellowish face and upper breast, white below; magpie-like flight; call cher-cher-kri-kri.

## Common Eider
*Somateria mollissima* · `comeid`

- Jizz — 50–71 cm, wingspan 95–105 cm, 0.81–3.04 kg. Bulky, heavily built sea duck; short thick neck, big head, long wedge-shaped bill running straight into the forehead.
- Plumage — Male: white back and breast, breast faintly pink-flushed; black crown, belly, flanks, rump-centre, tail and tail-coverts; pale moss-green nape, feathers slightly lengthened into a small crest. Female: brown, told from all ducks but other eiders by size and head shape. Subspecies differ slightly in drake bill colour, from yellow-green to bright orange-yellow.
- Bare parts — Drake's bill yellow-green in breeding plumage, otherwise blue-grey to green-grey; bill sides feathered to the nostrils, leaving two bare wedges towards the eye.
- In the field — Clumsy ashore; swims and dives well even in a heavy sea. Bulky shape, thick short neck and head profile identify it in flight. Drake's display an almost human ah-ooo, duck hoarse quacks; often approachable.
- Similar species —
  - King Eider: Slightly smaller with a shorter bill; male has a large yellow bill knob, pale bluish-grey head and nape and pale green cheek; female redder, chevroned.
  - Red-breasted Merganser: Slim sawbill with spiky crest and long thin red serrated bill; male has a green-glossed dark head, white collar and rusty breast.
  - Common Merganser: Slimmer, with a long thin red bill and a smooth rounded crest; male white-bodied with black green-glossed head; female red-brown head, white chin.
  - Velvet Scoter: Black or dull brown, never black-and-white; large white wing patch, small white mark behind the eye, and a partly orange bill.

## Common Firecrest
*Regulus ignicapilla* · `firecr1`

- Jizz — 9 cm, wingspan 13–16 cm, 4–7 g. Tiny, plump kinglet with a minute black pointed bill; among the smallest birds in Europe.
- Plumage — Bright olive-green above with a bronze patch on each shoulder; whitish below washed brownish-grey on breast and flanks; two white wingbars. Head boldly marked: black eye stripe, long white supercilium, black-edged crown stripe mainly orange in the male, bright yellow in the female; neck sides vivid yellow-green. Juvenile: greyer and duller, no crown colour, head markings weaker.
- Bare parts — Bill tiny, pointed and black; legs brownish-black.
- In the field — Hops with the body held horizontal; flight weak and whirring with occasional quick evasive turns. Call a thin level sisisisi, song a slightly rising series of those notes ending in a short trill.
- Similar species —
  - Goldcrest: Plain face — no white supercilium and no black eye stripe — with conspicuous black irises; duller green above and no yellow-green neck sides; 8.5–9.5 cm.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Slightly larger, 9.5–11 cm; long yellow supercilium rather than white, no orange or yellow crown stripe, yellowish-white double wingbars.
  - Pallas's Leaf Warbler: Crown stripe pale lemon and supercilium pale yellow, not bright white; lemon-yellow rump, obvious in flight; 9–10 cm, large-headed and short-tailed.

## Common Goldeneye
*Bucephala clangula* · `comgol`

- Jizz — 40–50 cm, wingspan 62–83 cm, 500–1,300 g. Compact diving duck; large, slightly peaked head like a rounded knob on a short neck; small triangular bill.
- Plumage — Male: green-glossed blackish head with a large round white patch between bill and eye, black above, white neck and belly. Female: brown head, grey body, white belly, variable white neck-ring. Eclipse male female-like but head darker brown, often keeping breeding pattern on the wing. Juvenile: as female, no collar, browner, white wing panel smaller.
- Bare parts — Iris golden-yellow; female's pearl-white, juvenile's pale grey. Female's grey bill has a yellow and black tip; legs orange-yellow to reddish.
- In the field — Both sexes show a white panel on the inner wing, the female's crossed by dark bars. Wings whistle loudly in flight, hyhyhyhy, carrying far, strongest in the male.
- Similar species —
  - Surf Scoter: Male velvety black with white forehead and nape patches and a swollen orange-looking bill; female uniform brown with indistinct pale cheek patches.
  - Common Pochard: Sloping forehead and long bill; male chestnut-headed with black breast and finely vermiculated grey body; bill dark grey with a broad blue-grey band.
  - Greater Scaup: Rounded head, light blue bill with small black nail; male dark-headed with a light back and bright white flanks, and no white face patch.
  - Ring-necked Duck: Smaller, 39–46 cm; angular glossy black head, black back, white breast and two white rings round the grey bill.

## Common Grasshopper Warbler
*Locustella naevia* · `cogwar1`

- Jizz — 12.5–13.5 cm, 11.5–20 g. Small, slender, skulking warbler with a wedge-shaped tail and slender legs.
- Plumage — Pale olive-brown above, each feather with a dark central streak; cheeks greyish; only a diffuse hint of a supercilium, faint dark streak behind the eye; underparts cream to yellow-white with a few dark spots and streaks on breast and flanks; undertail-coverts long and streaked; rump faintly spotted, concolorous with the back; tail reddish-brown, faintly barred.
- Bare parts — Upper mandible dark brown, lower yellowish-brown; legs pale yellowish to reddish-brown; iris brown.
- In the field — Skulking; creeps on the ground and avoids flight, heard far more than seen. Song a high reeling mechanical sirrrr, unbroken for seconds to minutes, ventriloquial, carrying 500 m; mostly at dusk and by night. Call sharp psvitt.
- Similar species —
  - River Warbler: Back unstreaked grey-brown, not dark-streaked; undertail dark with contrasting white feather tips; breast streaked, underparts whitish grey. Larger.
  - Savi's Warbler: Uniform dark reddish-brown above, unstreaked, underparts sandy brown; around 14 cm. Song lower-pitched and shorter, preceded by low ticks.
  - Lanceolated Warbler: Little in the sources beyond size, 12.5–13.5 cm against 11.5–12.5 cm; its name points to spear-shaped breast streaks.

## Common Greenshank
*Tringa nebularia* · `comgre`

- Jizz — 30–34 cm, wingspan 55–62 cm, up to 280 g. Slender, long-necked, long-winged and notably long-legged — larger and longer-legged than Common Redshank; bill long and stout, slightly upturned. Sexes alike.
- Plumage — Pale in all plumages: grey above with black spotting, white below, breast finely vermiculated black on white; brown-toned when breeding, grey-brown in winter. In flight a long, broad white wedge runs up the back onto the tail, sharply against grey-black upperwings; no white in the wing.
- Bare parts — Legs long, grey-green to yellow-green with age and season; bill long, stout, grey at the base.
- In the field — Shy; swims and dives. Flight fast and powerful, at times a restless zigzag. Call a loud three-note fluty whistle tju-tju-tju; song in high display flight a grinding klu-vy klu-vy. Takes invertebrates, small fish and amphibians.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Sandpiper: Smaller and more delicate, bill shorter and finer, back brown, legs yellowish; rump-patch small and weakly contrasting rather than a long white wedge.
  - Common Redshank: Smaller, 24–30 cm, and shorter-legged; legs red and bill red with a black tip; shows white on the wings as well as up the back.
  - Green Sandpiper: Plumper and smaller, 21–24 cm, dark greenish-brown above with a white-spotted back; wings dark above and below against a brilliant white rump; legs and short bill dark green.
  - Spotted Redshank: Black in breeding plumage, very pale in winter; legs and bill red; a white oval on the back, not a long wedge; call a creaking teu-it.

## Common Gull
*Larus canus* · `mewgul`

- Jizz — 40–46 cm, wingspan 100–130 cm, 394–586 g. A daintily built version of the large gulls: rounded head, fine bill, narrow wings; sitting birds show a bulging breast and slim rear.
- Plumage — Adult bluish-grey above, white head, neck, underparts, rump and tail, broad white trailing edge; wingtip black over the outer six primaries with large white mirrors on p9–p10 and white tips. Winter head grey-streaked. Juvenile scaly grey-brown above with a broad grey-brown breast band and broad dark tail band; first-winter has a grey saddle.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs yellow, varying orange- to greenish-yellow, duller in winter when a blackish band crosses the bill tip; iris brown, red orbital ring.
- In the field — Flight leisurely with powerful beats, wings slightly angled when gliding, springier and faster than Herring Gull's. Call a high-pitched laughing cry. Adult from the third year.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Much bigger, 55–67 cm on 125–155 cm wings; heavier bill with a red gonys spot, pale eye ringed by bare yellow skin and pink legs, not yellow.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Far larger — the world's largest gull, 61–79 cm, 144–166 cm span, 750–2300 g against 40–46 cm; the sources describe no plumage for it, so separate on bulk.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 49–64 cm and 118–158 cm span, 450–1300 g, against 40–46 cm and 100–130 cm.
  - Iceland Gull: Larger, 50–66 cm; very pale in every plumage with no black in wing or tail, and wing-tips projecting well past the tail at rest.

## Common Hoopoe
*Upupa epops* · `hoopoe`

- Jizz — 25–32 cm, 46–89 g; wingspan 44–48 cm. About thrush-sized but looks far bigger; broad, rounded, deeply fingered wings; long thin tapering bill 4–5 cm; erectile crest 5–6 cm.
- Plumage — Body beige-orange to buff. Wings boldly barred black and white with distinct yellow tones; tail black with a broad white band across the last third and white at the base. Crest tipped black and white, usually laid back like a nape-tuft, raised straight up when excited. Female slightly smaller and a shade duller.
- Bare parts — Bill black with a fawn base.
- In the field — Unmistakable. Undulating butterfly-like flight, broad wings half-closing after each beat, long pulls alternating with short flutters, so the flight looks unsteady. Constant head-nodding when feeding or excited; probes soil with the bill. Call a trisyllabic oop-oop-oop.

## Common Kingfisher
*Alcedo atthis* · `comkin1`

- Jizz — Wingspan 24–26 cm, 34–46 g; length given as 16–19.5 cm. Dumpy, short-tailed, short-necked, large-headed; bill straight, dagger-like, about 4 cm; feet small; wings short, rounded and broad.
- Plumage — Male ispida: crown, nape, wing-coverts and tail green-blue with azure barring on the head feathers; back and rump brilliant pale azure to cobalt; rufous patch by the bill base and rufous ear-coverts; blue-green malar stripe; sharp white neck blaze and white throat; underparts warm rufous-chestnut. Juvenile: duller, greener above, paler below.
- Bare parts — Male's bill all black; female's lower mandible orange-red at the base. Legs and feet bright red; juvenile bill black, feet grey.
- In the field — Sits upright and very still watching for fish, diving vertically; sometimes hovers. Flight fast, direct and low over water on whirring wings, showing an electric-blue flash down the back. Only small blue kingfisher in Europe.
- Similar species —
  - Pied Kingfisher: Black and white, not blue: white supercilium, black mask, black breast bands, black-barred upperparts, neat crest; larger at about 25 cm.
  - White-throated Kingfisher: Much larger, 27–28 cm; white throat and breast against a chestnut head and belly, bright red bill and legs, big white wing patches in flight.

## Common Loon
*Gavia immer* · `comloo`

- Jizz — 66–91 cm, wingspan 118–148 cm, 2.2–7.6 kg; averages 81 cm and 136 cm. Second largest diver; thick-necked, forehead steep, crown flat, bill large and straight. Males average larger; feet project 13 cm in flight.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: head and neck glossy black, ending abruptly against the white breast; black neck-ring and white necklace of parallel streaks; mantle chequered with large white squares, rear back and flanks finely white-spotted; underparts white. Non-breeding: dark grey-brown above, throat and foreneck white, blackish half-collar, white round the eye. Full breeding dress only from the fourth calendar year.
- Bare parts — Bill large and straight, dark grey to black, sometimes pale-tipped; greyish-white with dark culmen in winter; legs and feet grey; iris red.
- In the field — Unmistakable in breeding plumage. Swims with the head held so that the heavy bill stays roughly horizontal; underwing white with a grey-black trailing edge.
- Similar species —
  - Arctic Loon: Smaller, 58–73 cm; breeding bird grey-headed with black throat and foreneck patch and long parallel white lines down throat-sides and chest.
  - Red-throated Loon: Smallest diver, 53–69 cm, wingspan 91–120 cm; slighter, long and streamlined, with a sharply pointed bill.
  - Yellow-billed Loon: Larger, 76–97 cm, wingspan 135–160 cm, with a longer bill; separated on profile — this bird has the steeper forehead, flat crown and horizontally held bill.
  - Brant: A small goose, not a diver: black head and neck with a small white neck-patch, dark grey-brown body, short stubby bill, very short black tail, white undertail.

## Common Merganser
*Mergus merganser* · `commer`

- Jizz — 58–72 cm, wingspan 78–94 cm, 0.9–2.1 kg. Large, long-bodied sawbill; long neck, often drawn in when swimming; long narrow round red bill hooked at the tip.
- Plumage — Male: black green-glossed head and back, crest smooth and rounded, body white with a salmon-pink tinge, grey rump and tail, wing white on the inner half, black on the outer. Female: red-brown head with white chin, cut sharply off the pale neck, ash-grey above, white below. Juvenile: as female, plus a short black-edged white stripe from eye to bill.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs red to brownish-red, brightest on adult males, dullest on juveniles.
- In the field — In flight recalls a diver or grebe rather than a duck: neck outstretched, flight path straight, wingbeats shallow and fast. Display calls quiet — male uig-a and a bell-like rüh-roh, female aik-aik.
- Similar species —
  - Red-breasted Merganser: Smaller, 51–64 cm, slimmer, bill thinner; ragged spiky crest, male with rusty breast and dark neck, female's brown head fading into the breast.
  - Common Eider: Bulky, big-headed, wedge-shaped bill; male black-bellied with white back and green nape; female brown, no crest and no thin red bill.
  - King Eider: Bulky sea duck with red knobbed bill, black body, white breast and pale bluish-grey head; female warm brown with blackish chevrons.
  - Velvet Scoter: All glossy black, female dull brown; thick neck, long broad bill, pointed tail; white wing patch and small white mark behind the eye.

## Common Murre
*Uria aalge* · `commur`

- Jizz — 38–46 cm, wingspan 61–73 cm, about 1 kg; the feet project some 4 cm beyond the tail. Mallard-sized, but with a long thin pointed dark bill and a small rounded black tail.
- Plumage — Breeding adult black-brown on head, back, tail and upperwing, white below and on the underwing-coverts; nominate birds black, albionis dark brown, most obviously in southern Britain. Winter: face and chin white with a dark spur running back from the eye, flanks often dark-streaked. 'Bridled' birds show a white eye-ring and a white line back across the cheek.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs dark grey; the grey-black feet stand out against the body in flight.
- In the field — Swims with alternating paddle strokes and is correspondingly slow. Very vocal at colonies — a repeated nasal 'wha wha wha' running into a lowing note; silent in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Thick-billed Murre: Larger on average, 40–48 cm against 38–46 cm, and heavier-billed; the sources give no plumage mark — both are black above and white below with a long pointed bill.
  - Razorbill: Darker-headed, with a deep, blunt, laterally compressed black bill grooved near the tip and a thin white line from the eye to the bill-tip.
  - Black Guillemot: Smaller, 30–38 cm against 38–46 cm; summer birds all black but for a large white upperwing patch, with coral-red legs and mouth.

## Common Myna
*Acridotheres tristis* · `commyn`

- Jizz — 23–25 cm, about 106 g. Fairly large, heavy-billed starling; wings short and rounded, tail square-tipped.
- Plumage — Body chestnut-brown; head and breast black and slightly glossy, forming a hood; large white patches on both surfaces of the wing contrasting with black primaries; undertail-coverts white to pale grey; tail brown shading to black with a white tip. Sexes alike. Juvenile: duller and darker, the black parts without gloss.
- Bare parts — Bill and stout legs bright yellow; bare yellow patch of skin behind the eye.
- In the field — Usually in pairs; white wing patches flash in flight. Song repeated harsh, bubbling phrases, piu-piu-piu che-che-che; alarm a rasping traaah; also croaks, squawks, clicks and whistles, with head-bobbing and fluffed feathers.
- Similar species —
  - Spotless Starling: Smaller, 21–23 cm, and wholly oily black, spotless in summer, with shaggy throat feathers and bright pink legs; no brown body or white wing patches.
  - European Starling: Smaller, 19–23 cm; iridescent black spangled with white; no brown body, no white wing patches and no bare yellow patch behind the eye.
  - Rosy Starling: Adult pink-bodied with glossy black head, wings and tail and a wispy crest; legs and bill pale orange, not bright yellow.

## Common Nightingale
*Luscinia megarhynchos* · `comnig1`

- Jizz — 15–17 cm, 18–27 g. Small, plain, house-sparrow-sized chat; eye comparatively large.
- Plumage — Warm light brown above, tail and rump distinctly rufous; buff to dirty white below, breast lightly mottled brown. Sexes alike. Eastern and Caucasian races paler above with a stronger face pattern, including a pale supercilium.
- Bare parts — Bill pink and yellow; eye black, ringed white.
- In the field — Male's song famous, melodic, with a typical crescendo of whistled notes; females do not sing.
- Similar species —
  - Bluethroat: Male shows a blue throat pattern, obscured by pale feather tips after the summer moult; female a blackish crescent and little blue on a cream throat and breast.
  - Thrush Nightingale: Darker, dark brown with an olive tinge above and not at all rufous; breast mottled with brown central bands, throat sides spotted; tail dark rufous-brown.
  - White-throated Robin: Breeding male lead-grey above with a black face, white throat and supercilium, orange underparts and a black tail; female plain grey with a black tail.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Pale sandy isabelline throughout, white rump and upper tail-coverts, creamy supercilium, and a large white base to the brownish-black tail.

## Common Pochard
*Aythya ferina* · `compoc`

- Jizz — 42–49 cm, wingspan 67–82 cm; winter weight 467–1,240 g. Plump, stocky diving duck; short tail, sloping forehead, fairly long bill, strong legs set far back; swims low in the water. Males average larger.
- Plumage — Male: head and neck chestnut, breast and stern black, body pale grey finely vermiculated, back pale blue-grey; eclipse as breeding but the black washed out to grey. Female: head grey-brown all year with pale patches at bill-base and behind the eye, darker below the eye; breast dark grey to light brown; body pale grey but looking dirty; stern dark grey.
- Bare parts — Male's bill dark with a broad blue-grey band, iris orange-red; female's band smaller or absent, iris brown, pale eye-ring; juvenile iris yellow-olive; legs grey throughout.
- In the field — Rather silent; drake whistles softly in display — a whining note cut off by a sharp ricochet-like snap; female growls krrr when flushed. In flight the diffuse grey primaries show dark contrasting tips, speculum ash-grey.
- Similar species —
  - Tufted Duck: Drake black with white flanks, blue-grey bill, golden eye and a drooping crest; female brown with paler flanks, sometimes a little white at the bill-base.
  - Greater Scaup: Drake's head dark with green to purple sheen, not chestnut; belly and flanks bright white; bill light blue with a small black nail; eyes yellow.
  - Ferruginous Duck: Male dark chestnut on head, breast and flanks with pure white undertail-coverts and a white belly and underwing patch in flight, yellow eye; female duller, dark-eyed.
  - Ring-necked Duck: Head angular and glossy black, back black, breast white; two white rings round the grey bill, eyes yellow; smaller, 39–46 cm, wingspan 62–63 cm.

## Common Quail
*Coturnix coturnix* · `comqua1`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm, wingspan 32–35 cm, 70–140 g. Tiny, compact, pear-shaped gamebird with long wings, unlike the typically short-winged gamebirds. Female generally slightly heavier.
- Plumage — Male: grey-brown, densely marked rufous and black; white shaft streaks form three pale lines along the crown centre and sides; yellowish supercilium; head sides dark-streaked; a rufous band from the gape frames the whitish throat, a brown stripe down the throat centre; chin white; upper breast cream, finely streaked, belly whitish-grey. Female: head pattern weaker, breast and foreneck plain whitish-grey.
- Bare parts — Bill small and curved in both sexes.
- In the field — Skulks in low vegetation and slips away on foot rather than flushing; flies faster and more skilfully than a partridge. Usually detected by the male's loud whistled three-syllable wet-my-lips, repeated three to eight times, chiefly at dusk.
- Similar species —
  - Barbary Partridge: Far larger, rotund partridge: grey-brown back, grey breast, buff belly, light grey face with broad reddish-brown gorget, rufous-streaked white flanks, red legs.
  - Red-legged Partridge: Much larger; bold black-streaked gorget, rufous and black flank bars, cream throat, red bill and eye-ring, pink legs, blue-grey fore crown.
  - Western Capercaillie: Enormous by comparison — cocks 74–85 cm and about 4 kg against the quail's 16–18 cm and 70–140 g; hen much smaller than cock.

## Common Raven
*Corvus corax* · `comrav`

- Jizz — 54–67 cm, 690–2,250 g; wingspan 115–130 cm. Much the largest European corvid, bigger than a buzzard; massive slightly decurved bill (culmen 5.7–8.5 cm), thick neck, big head, long strongly graduated wedge tail, wings narrow in the hand.
- Plumage — Adult uniformly black, glossed metallic green or blue-violet according to the light; throat feathers elongated and lanceolate, standing out clearly when calling; bases of the neck feathers pale brownish-grey. Juvenile: gloss almost absent, brown-black above and brown below, throat feathers not elongated, tail less wedge-shaped.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black, feet stout and strong; iris dark brown, blue-grey in the juvenile, gape pinkish at first.
- In the field — In flight told by the wedge tail, long narrow-handed wings, strong neck and big head and bill. Call a deep, rasping, hollow korrp repeated three or four times, diagnostic even to a beginner; also a high knocking krack-krack-krack.
- Similar species —
  - Carrion Crow: Smaller bill, smooth throat, square tail; and far smaller overall, 44–52 cm and 400–600 g against 54–67 cm and up to 2,250 g.
  - Hooded Crow: Ash-grey body with dark shaft-streaks, contrasting with black head, throat, wings and tail; flight slow, heavy and straight; 48–54 cm, 400–600 g.
  - Rook: Adult shows a bare whitish patch in front of the eye and around the bill-base; gloss bluer, head and neck feathers silky. Much smaller, 44–46 cm, 280–340 g.
  - Brown-necked Raven: Smaller at 52–56 cm, bill less large and deep, wings a little more pointed; head and throat distinctly brownish-black against a purple- or blue-glossed black body.

## Common Redshank
*Tringa totanus* · `comred1`

- Jizz — 24–30 cm; wingspan 47–53 cm, about 130 g. Slender wader, bill and legs both fairly long; slightly smaller than greenshank. Female averages larger, longer-winged.
- Plumage — Breeding: marbled brown, mottled black and grey above, variably brown-and-white below; head finely streaked, short cream supercilium, white eyering. Winter: plainer grey-brown above, white below, breast washed grey and finely streaked. Juvenile: warmer brown above with cream feather fringes, breast buff-brown, underparts dark-streaked.
- Bare parts — Bill orange-red at base, black-tipped; legs dark red when breeding, orange-red in winter; juvenile legs orange-yellow, bill mostly grey.
- In the field — In flight, a clear white wedge up the back and broad white trailing edges to black-primaried wings. A noisy bird: flight call a two-note tju-hu, alarm a hammering kly-kly-kly.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Sandpiper: Longer-legged and more delicate, with shorter finer bill, brown back and yellowish legs; rump-patch small and weakly contrasting, never a long white wedge up the back.
  - Common Greenshank: Larger; long greenish legs and a long grey-based bill that looks upturned. It too shows a white wedge on the back — go on legs, bill and the rapid fluty teu-teu-teu.
  - Green Sandpiper: 21–24 cm and plumper; dark greenish-brown above, greyish breast, wings dark above and below with a brilliant white rump; legs and short bill dark green.
  - Spotted Redshank: 29–33 cm, with longer bill and legs; sooty black when breeding, very pale in winter; a white oval on the back, not a wedge; call a creaking teu-it.

## Common Redstart
*Phoenicurus phoenicurus* · `comred2`

- Jizz — 13–14.5 cm, 11–23 g. Carriage and actions of a robin but slimmer and lighter; slender, upright stance; bill sharp and fine.
- Plumage — Male summer: slate-grey crown, nape and back; white forehead and supercilium; face and throat black; breast and flanks orange-chestnut fading whitish on the belly; rump and tail bright orange-red, central feathers dark brown. Autumn male washed out under pale feather fringes. Female: grey-brown above, buff-white to light orange below, throat usually whitish; tail as male.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black.
- In the field — Sits upright and quivers the rust-red tail constantly; chat-like actions. Song short wistful flute notes then trills, with mimicry; call a soft rising huit often ending tick-tick-tick.
- Similar species —
  - Black Redstart: Same size; male dark grey to black above and on the breast, with no white forehead; belly blackish-grey or orange-red. Female grey to grey-brown, not buff below.
  - Moussier's Redstart: Smallest redstart at 12 cm, 14–15 g; male black-headed with a broad white stripe over the eye and down the neck side, black upperparts with a white wing patch.
  - Cyprus Wheatear: A wheatear, 14–15 cm, and sexes alike — so no grey-backed, white-fronted, orange-breasted male. Sources otherwise separate it from pied wheatear on biometrics and song.
  - Spotted Flycatcher: 13–15 cm, 14–20 g; dull grey-brown above, off-white below, crown, throat and breast brown-streaked, and no red in the tail. Juvenile ochre-spotted above.

## Common Reed Warbler
*Acrocephalus scirpaceus* · `eurwar1`

- Jizz — 12.5–14 cm, wingspan 17–21 cm, 10–15 g. Forehead flattened; bill long, narrow, strong and pointed; unstreaked and featureless in build.
- Plumage — Brown above, the back often with a faint olive-grey tone, rump warmer rust-brown; underparts buff to yellowish white, throat whitish. Unstreaked throughout. Sexes identical; young birds richer buff below.
- Bare parts — Legs grey-brown.
- In the field — Favours stands of pure reed. Song a slow chattering jit-jit-jit with acrocephaline whistles and mimicry added, heard mostly at dusk and dawn; short calls tscharr and tschirrak.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Warbler: Barely separable by sight — go on song: no other member of the genus mimics other birds to any extent. It also avoids the pure reed stands reed warbler favours.
  - Sedge Warbler: 11.5–13 cm; back and wings streaked brown, crown streaked black, prominent whitish supercilium, and a warm unstreaked rump contrasting with duller wings.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Plainer olive-brown and lacking warmer tones; short whitish supercilium ending just behind the eye with a dusky eye-stripe; wings short and rounded; bill long and thick.
  - Great Reed Warbler: Thrush-sized at 16–21 cm, 22–38 g — like a giant reed warbler but with a much stronger supercilium, and a very loud, far-carrying song.

## Common Ringed Plover
*Charadrius hiaticula* · `corplo`

- Jizz — 17–20 cm, wingspan 35–55 cm, 40–80 g. Small, compact, thickset plover; short bill, short legs, rounded head.
- Plumage — Grey-brown back and wings; white belly and white breast cut by one broad black neckband; brown cap, white forehead, black mask through the eye; white wingbar. Female: head markings and breast band often carry much brown. Juvenile: duller, breast band often incomplete and grey-brown.
- Bare parts — Bill short, dark at the tip, orange to yellow at the base; legs orange to yellowish; iris black.
- In the field — White wingbar shows plainly in flight. Only the outer two toes slightly webbed. Contact and alarm call a two-syllable tu-ipp.
- Similar species —
  - Little Ringed Plover: Smaller, 15–18 cm; clear yellow eye-ring, flesh-coloured legs, all-dark bill, and no white wingbar in flight.
  - Eurasian Dotterel: Striking whitish supercilium in every plumage and plain wings in flight; summer birds chestnut-breasted with a black belly; bill all black, legs yellow.
  - Kentish Plover: Black bill and dark legs; breast band broken into two dark side patches, never a complete collar; male shows rufous nape and crown.
  - Sociable Lapwing: Medium-sized lapwing: black crown and eyestripe bordered white above and below, longish black legs, white tail with a black terminal band.

## Common Rosefinch
*Carpodacus erythrinus* · `comros`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, 20–25 g. Small, stocky finch with a short, stout, rounded conical bill and a beady dark eye.
- Plumage — Male: head, upper breast and rump carmine-red with a broad dark lore; belly and undertail-coverts white to pale grey; back, wings and tail dark brown with two narrow, indistinct wing-bars. Female and males up to the third calendar year: dull olive-brown and streaked, buff below, wing-bars rusty-buff — inseparable from each other. Juvenile: more intensely olive-brown, bars clearer.
- Bare parts — Bill stout and dark; eyes dark.
- In the field — Sings from a raised perch — a memorable oriole-like fluting, vy vídjio, falling at the end and nearly as loud as a wolf-whistle; females sing too. Call a soft vy, wüh or djü, recalling Greenfinch.
- Similar species —
  - Desert Finch: Stout black bill, black-and-white flight and tail feathers and a rosy-pink slash on each wing instead of a red head, breast and rump. Same length, 13–15 cm.
  - Twite: No red on head or breast; brown streaked black above, buff-white streaked below, male with a pink rump; bill yellow in winter, grey in summer. 13–13.5 cm.
  - Brambling: White rump, orange breast against a white belly and orange scapulars; breeding male black-headed. Larger, 14–16 cm.
  - Common Chaffinch: Male has a blue-grey crown and black forehead, dull rust-red face and breast, a white wing panel and white tail wedges; larger, about 14.5 cm.

## Common Sandpiper
*Actitis hypoleucos* · `comsan`

- Jizz — 18–22 cm, 32–41 cm span, 40–80 g. Small, short-legged wader, smaller than Green Sandpiper; tail projects well beyond the folded wingtips.
- Plumage — Upperparts greyish olive-brown, in breeding plumage finely dark-marked and irregularly barred, mantle bronze-glossed; breast-sides brown-grey spotted and cross-barred, with a white wedge pushing up between them and the wing; underparts white. Winter plainer and duller with more barring on the wings. Juvenile more heavily barred above with buff feather edges. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill medium-long, dark brown with a pale orange-brown base; legs short, pale yellow; eye black.
- In the field — Flies fast, level and low over water on rapid short beats with the wings bowed down, showing a white wing-bar and white-barred tail sides. Bobs the rear body constantly on the ground; swims and dives. Call shrill, falling hi-diii-dii-dii.
- Similar species —
  - Curlew Sandpiper: Black bill, dark grey to black feet, a large white rump patch and a white stripe across the wing; no white breast-side wedge. 18–23 cm.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Dark above with a purplish gloss, grey-smeared breast and black rump; short yellow legs and a dark bill with a yellow base. 20–22 cm.
  - Sanderling: Winter bird almost white apart from a dark shoulder patch, face and throat brick-red in summer; lacks a hind toe. 18–20 cm.
  - Jack Snipe: Mottled brown snipe with a dark eye-stripe, two pale lateral crown-stripes and no central one, yellow back stripes in flight; bobs as if on springs.

## Common Scoter
*Melanitta nigra* · `blksco1`

- Jizz — 44–54 cm. Medium-sized, compact and bulky sea duck with a large bill; no cross-checked measurements in the pack, length from the Swedish text.
- Plumage — Male all black, with a yellow patch in the middle of the bill and a small knob at the bill base. Female brown-grey with pale cheeks and a blackish-brown crown. No white anywhere on the drake and none in the wing; the female's pale areas are more extensive than in other scoters.
- Bare parts — Legs blackish-brown to blackish-grey in both sexes; male's bill shows yellow around the nostrils.
- In the field — Resting flocks pack tighter and hold more birds than Velvet Scoter's. Dives with a little jump, wings folded. Male whistles a soft pjy about once a second, also from night migrants in spring.
- Similar species —
  - Velvet Scoter: Larger, 51–56 cm, and shows a white wing patch plus a small white mark behind the eye; dives without a jump, wings already open.
  - Surf Scoter: Male has white patches on forehead and nape and a swollen bill patterned white, red and yellow; female shows indistinct pale cheek patches.
  - Common Eider: Much bulkier, 50–71 cm, with a wedge-shaped bill; male black and white with a green nape, never all black.
  - Common Goldeneye: Male has a round white patch between bill and eye and white neck and belly; female grey-bodied with a brown head and yellow eye.

## Common Shelduck
*Tadorna tadorna* · `comshe`

- Jizz — 55–65 cm, wingspan 100–120 cm. Shaped like a small short-necked goose: long neck, long legs, long pointed wings, heavy body, large clearly upturned bill.
- Plumage — Boldly pied: dark green head and neck, white body with a broad chestnut breast-band, black belly stripe, black scapular bands and black primaries. Wing-coverts white, secondaries black with strong green gloss in flight, outer tertial orange-brown, inner tertials white. Female slightly smaller, usually with white flecks on the green face. Juvenile greyish above, mostly white below, adult wing pattern already.
- Bare parts — Bill reddish-pink, bright red on the breeding male and then with a prominent knob at the base; feet pink.
- In the field — Noisy; call a loud honk. Male gives swishing whistles and a repeated two-syllable pju-pu in courtship, female a nasal whinnying.
- Similar species —
  - Ruddy Shelduck: Larger, 58–70 cm, and orange-brown all over with a paler head; male has a narrow black collar; bill black and legs dark grey.
  - Brant: Small dark goose, 55–66 cm, short stubby bill; head and neck black with a small white neck patch, undertail pure white, tail very short.
  - Lesser White-fronted Goose: Goose, 53–66 cm, with a conspicuous white face blaze, broad black belly bars, bright orange legs and an obvious yellow eye-ring.
  - Ross's Goose: White goose with a rounded head on a short neck, short triangular bill with warty bluish base, and deep red legs.

## Common Snipe
*Gallinago gallinago* · `comsni`

- Jizz — 25–27 cm; thrush-sized, squat body on short legs, very long straight dark bill 5.5–7 cm, about twice the head length; wings pointed, tail projecting well past the wingtips at rest.
- Plumage — Body mottled brown with straw-yellow stripes above, pale below, belly extensively white. Head striped: dark crown split by a buff central stripe, dark eye-stripe with pale stripes above and below, dark lores broadening towards the bill-base. Neck and breast streaked yellowish-brown, coarsening to V-shaped zigzags on breast sides and flanks. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Legs short, stout, greenish-grey to yellowish-green; bill reddish-brown at base, dark brown at tip; iris brown.
- In the field — Crouching stance when standing. Flushed birds show a broad white trailing edge to the secondaries.
- Similar species —
  - Great Snipe: Great snipe is bulkier, 26–30 cm, barred below, with a shorter 6–7 cm bill, broad wings with a pale wingbar and conspicuous white tail sides; flight recalls woodcock.
  - Common Redshank: Redshank is plain marbled brown with red legs and a black-tipped red bill, showing white up the back and in the wing; bill straight but far shorter.
  - Red Knot: Red knot is uniform pale grey in winter and cinnamon-faced when breeding, with a short tapering bill no longer than the head; 23–26 cm, no head stripes.
  - Lesser Yellowlegs: Lesser yellowlegs has bright yellow legs and a slim straight bill about head length, mottled grey-brown above with a streaked breast; no bold head stripes.

## Common Swift
*Apus apus* · `comswi`

- Jizz — 16–17 cm, about 40 g. Swallow-shaped but larger than the European swallows; very long swept-back wings, crescent-shaped in a glide; tail short and forked; closed wings cross and overshoot the tail by some 4 cm.
- Plumage — Sooty to brownish-black throughout except a small greyish-white chin patch, hard to make out at any distance or in flight. Sexes alike. Juvenile: darker and less glossy, the white of the throat more extensive and purer, with white feather fringes.
- Bare parts — Bill small, black, slightly downcurved; iris deep brown; feet short, blackish flesh-coloured, all four toes forward with sharp curved claws.
