- In the field — Face looks rounded head-on. A clinging foot only — cannot perch on branch or wire, but clings to trunks, gutters, rough surfaces. Loud screams in two pitches, the higher from the female; screaming parties of 10–20 on summer evenings.
- Similar species —
  - Pallid Swift: Same 16–17 cm; chunkier and browner, large white throat patch often visible at distance, paler flight feathers, underparts and rump giving more contrast, scalier belly.
  - White-rumped Swift: Smaller, 14–15.5 cm, with a narrow white rump band as well as a pale throat patch; slimmer, darker, tail more deeply forked.
  - Alpine Swift: Much larger, 20–23 cm, wingspan 51–58 cm; olive-brown above, white throat and a bold oval white belly patch ringed by a brown breastband; tail fork shallow.

## Common Tern
*Sterna hirundo* · `comter`

- Jizz — 31–37 cm, 97–146 g. Slender tern with forked tail; on a standing bird the tail reaches no further than the folded wingtips; heavier head and body, broader wings and longer legs than Arctic Tern.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: black cap, pale grey upperparts, very pale grey underparts, white rump and tail; outer primaries darker than inner, so the wing looks two-toned, a grey wedge appearing as the shafts wear. Non-breeding: white forehead and underparts, dark carpal bar. Juvenile: brown crown, ginger forehead, brown-scaled back, dark grey secondaries tipped white.
- Bare parts — Bill red with black tip or all black; legs orange-red. Non-breeding: bill black or red-based, legs dark red to black.
- In the field — Call a hard 'kick' or 'kriäh'. In flight the wings look set at mid-body.
- Similar species —
  - Arctic Tern: Tail-streamers project beyond the folded wingtips; slimmer head, wings set further forward and translucent near the tip, shorter legs; dark red bill without a black tip.
  - Black-headed Gull: A gull, 34–39 cm: chocolate-brown hood not a black cap, white leading edge to the outer wing, black primary tips; no long tail-streamers.
  - Ross's Gull: Small gull with wedge-shaped tail, red legs, pink-flushed underparts and a neat black neck ring in summer; never a black cap or forked streamers.
  - Sabine's Gull: Bold wing pattern: black outer primaries, white inner primaries and secondaries, grey coverts; black bill with yellow tip; dark grey hood with black collar.

## Common Waxbill
*Estrilda astrild* · `comwax`

- Jizz — 9.5–13 cm, 6–11 g, wingspan 12–14 cm. Small, slender finch-like bird with short rounded wings and a long graduated tail; bill conical.
- Plumage — Grey-brown throughout, finely barred wavy dark brown; broad scarlet stripe through the eye; cheeks and throat whitish; belly often flushed pink with a reddish stripe down the centre, varying by subspecies; rump brown, tail and vent dark. Female: paler, less red on the belly. Juvenile: duller, little or no red below, barring fainter.
- Bare parts — Bill sealing-wax red in the adult, blackish to brown-black in the juvenile.
- In the field — Various twittering and buzzing calls and a distinctive high-pitched flight-call; song simple, harsh and nasal, descending on the last note, a three-syllable tjre-tjre-srii. Breeds in damp grassy habitats: sedge, reed and reedmace.
- Similar species —
  - Red Avadavat: Rounded black tail, red rump and white spots on red body and wing feathers; breeding male largely red with a black eye-stripe, not barred grey-brown.

## Common Wood-Pigeon
*Columba palumbus* · `cowpig1`

- Jizz — 38–45 cm, wingspan 65–80 cm, 300–615 g. Largest European pigeon; big and strongly built but small-headed, with a proportionately long tail, elongated body and rounded breast.
- Plumage — Mostly grey, paler over the back, with blue-green gloss on head and neck; breast pinkish, paling to grey below. Neck sides carry a green band, a white patch and a glossy purple-red band; broad white wing-bars show in flight; tail and wings boldly marked black. Juvenile lacks the white neck patches until six to eight months old.
- Bare parts — Adult bill yellow with reddish cere, iris pale yellow to orange; juvenile bill, cere and eye grey. Legs short, stout and brownish-pink.
- In the field — Flight fast on regular clipping wingbeats; display flight climbs steeply with loud wing-claps, then glides on stiff wings with the tail spread. Song a muffled five-syllable coo stressed on the first, repeated three to five times.
- Similar species —
  - Stock Dove: Smaller, 28–34 cm; blue-grey with short dark wing-bars and a broad dark tail-band, iridescent neck patch but no white neck spots or white wing-bars; eye dark.
  - Rock Pigeon: Smaller, 30–35 cm; eye orange-red, not pale yellow, and it shows none of the white neck patches or white wing-bars.
  - Oriental Turtle-Dove: Smaller, 30–35 cm; black-and-white striped neck patch, scaly brown wing feathers and a wedge-shaped tail; flight relaxed and direct.
  - Namaqua Dove: Tiny, about 22 cm, with a very long black tapered tail and chestnut primaries in flight; male black-faced with a yellow and red bill.

## Corn Bunting
*Emberiza calandra* · `corbun1`

- Jizz — 16–19 cm, 32–67 g. Largest and bulkiest bunting of the western Palearctic; big-headed, heavy-billed, tail of medium length; male about 20 per cent larger than the female, both alike in plumage.
- Plumage — Lark-like and drab, with no showy head pattern: grey-brown to mid-brown above, forward back and scapulars boldly streaked black-brown, hind back and rump more weakly so; underparts whitish to grey-buff, streaked on breast and flanks, the streaks gathering into a gorget round the throat; lesser coverts dark and white-tipped; tail plain brown.
- Bare parts — Bill heavy and conical, mandibles yellowish-pink at the sides; eye conspicuously dark.
- In the field — Flight in deep undulating bows, legs often left dangling on short flights. Song from a low bush, post or wires: a short, accelerating, jangling metallic phrase like shaken keys; calls an impure tsritt and a hard clicking bitt.
- Similar species —
  - Reed Bunting: Smaller (13.5–15.5 cm); male black-headed and -throated with a white collar; female duller with a streaked brown head and barred underparts; neither is plain drab and lark-like.
  - Yellowhammer: Bright yellow head and underparts, rufous rump, white outer tail feathers; the corn bunting is streaked grey-brown throughout, with a plain brown tail and no yellow.
  - Rustic Bunting: The sources describe only its taxonomy — nothing here separates the two in the field; the corn bunting is 16–19 cm, 32–67 g, plainly streaked and heavy-billed.
  - Cirl Bunting: Smaller (15.5 cm, span 22–25.5 cm); male yellow-headed with black crown, eyestripe and throat and a green breast band; female shows chestnut shoulders.

## Corn Crake
*Crex crex* · `corcra`

- Jizz — 27–30 cm, wingspan 42–53 cm; male averages 165 g, female 145 g. Medium-sized rail, a little larger than a quail; looks long-necked and long-legged when walking.
- Plumage — Crown and upperparts brown-black streaked buff and grey; wing-coverts distinctly chestnut with a few white bars; face, neck and breast blue-grey, cut by a pale brown streak from the bill to behind the eye; belly whitish; flanks and undertail barred chestnut and white. Female: warmer above, eye-streak narrower and duller. Juvenile: yellower above, buff-brown where the adult is grey.
- Bare parts — Bill strong and flesh-coloured; iris pale brown; legs and feet pale grey.
- In the field — Extremely skulking and solitary. In flight the chestnut upperwing shows and the legs dangle rail-fashion. Birds grow paler and greyer towards the east of the range.
- Similar species —
  - Water Rail: Long red bill and red iris; olive-brown black-streaked upperparts, dark slate-blue face and underparts, black-and-white barred flanks, white undertail; 23–28 cm, legs flesh-brown.
  - Eurasian Moorhen: Black and brown, 30–38 cm, with a red frontal shield, yellow-tipped red bill, yellow legs, white undertail and white flank streaks; young browner, without the shield.
  - Western Swamphen: Chicken-sized, with huge feet, bright plumage and a red bill and frontal shield — easily recognised in its Mediterranean range.
  - Spotted Crake: Smaller, 19–22.5 cm; short straight bill, yellow with a red base; brown upperparts with dark barring and white spots, blue-grey breast, green legs.

## Corsican Finch
*Carduelis corsicana* · `corfin1`

- Jizz — 11.5–12.5 cm. Small finch with a short, pointed conical bill.
- Plumage — Male: face and underparts yellow, back rust-brown with dark streaks, two yellow-green bars across the dark wing, uppertail-coverts grey rather than olive; markings diffuse overall. Female: duller.
- In the field — Call a short teh; song goldfinch-like with buzzing redpoll-style trills, longer and more structured than its relatives, recalling a wren.
- Similar species —
  - European Goldfinch: Red face on a black-and-white head, warm brown upperparts, black-and-yellow wings, white underparts with buff flanks; ivory pointed bill.
  - Citril Finch: Greyish above with olive uppertail-coverts, face and underparts yellow-green rather than clean yellow, back less brown and less streaked.
  - Eurasian Wren: Plump rufous-brown bird, barred throughout, with rounded wings and a short cocked tail; only the song invites the comparison.
  - Eurasian Siskin: Male black-capped and black-bibbed with a yellow rump and a bold yellow wing stripe; back greyish-green, flanks striped.

## Cory's Shearwater
*Calonectris borealis* · `corshe1`

- Jizz — 45–56 cm, wingspan 112–126 cm. Large shearwater, about Lesser Black-backed Gull size; long wings characteristically angled — arm forward, hand back — so the carpal stands out, held slightly to clearly bowed; head big and round.
- Plumage — Brownish-grey upperparts, the hand usually darker; head pale grey with no sharp cap; underparts white. Lacks the brown belly patch, dark shoulder markings and black cap of Great Shearwater. No white tongues on the undersides of the outer three primaries.
- Bare parts — Bill proportionately large, pale yellow with a black band near the tip — the pale bill is often visible at very long range.
- In the field — Little behaviour in the sources; picked out at range by size, the bowed angled wings and the conspicuously pale bill.
- Similar species —
  - Scopoli's Shearwater: Smaller, 45–52 cm, with a narrower, greyer bill and paler head; shows white tongues on the outer primaries, which Cory's Shearwater lacks — the safest field criterion.
  - Northern Fulmar: Smaller, grey and white, with a short thick bill and no black wingtips, 44–53 cm against 45–56 cm; flies stiff and straight, not on bowed angled wings.
  - Great Shearwater: Black cap sharply cut from white underparts, brown belly patch, dark shoulder markings, black bill and a white horseshoe at the tail base; smaller, 43–51 cm.
  - Sooty Shearwater: Wholly dark chocolate-brown with a silvery underwing stripe; no white underparts and no pale bill; smaller, 40–51 cm.

## Crested Lark
*Galerida cristata* · `crelar1`

- Jizz — 17–18 cm, 37–55 g; wingspan 29–38 cm. Stocky lark, about Skylark size but shorter and bulkier in head and body; stout curved bill, broad wings, short tail, long pointed erect crest.
- Plumage — Dark grey-streaked brown above, whitish below and greyer than Skylark; outer tail feathers light brown with rusty edges; underwing reddish in flight; no white edges to wing or tail. Sexes alike; young more spotted on the back. Crest conspicuous at all times, raised further in display and song.
- In the field — Call 'trüdritri-eh', also the main motif of the song, delivered from the ground and sometimes in flight; melodious and varied, with mimicry of other birds.
- Similar species —
  - Thekla's Lark: Slightly smaller with a shorter, darker bill; back and rump greyer, streaks on back and breast crisper, uppertail-coverts rustier; underwing grey, not reddish.
  - Eurasian Skylark: Only a short blunt crest, and white edges to tail and rear wing showing as it flies away; browner, slimmer-headed, 16–19 cm.
  - Dupont's Lark: No crest, only a thin pale crown stripe; slim with long neck and legs and a fine slightly curved bill; song a ventriloquial melancholic whistle. 17–18 cm.
  - Horned Lark: Striking black and yellow face pattern, white-faced in the southern European race; tail mostly black against the paler body; summer male has two black head tufts.

## Crested Tit
*Lophophanes cristatus* · `cretit2`

- Jizz — 10.5–12 cm, wingspan 17–20 cm, about 11 g. Small tit with a pointed crest curving slightly forward, which can be laid flat.
- Plumage — Crest patterned black and white. Upperparts grey-brown; sides of head and neck white, enclosed by a black crescent starting behind the eye and by a white collar cut off below by a black band that forms a triangular bib; throat black; underparts dirty white, flanks cream. Sexes alike.
- In the field — Resident in coniferous forest. Commonest call an unmistakable rolling zizigürrrr or burrurret; sharp zit notes; song jingling, ranging over three pitches, delivered at a rapid tempo.
- Similar species —
  - African Blue Tit: No crest; white forehead and supercilium to mid-nape, deep glossy blue crown blackening on the neck, blue above and yellow below.
  - Eurasian Blue Tit: No crest; intensely blue crown, white cheeks ringed dark blue, white forehead and wing-bar, underparts sulphur-yellow with a dark line down the belly.
  - Coal Tit: No crest; glossy blue-black head with a big white nape spot and two white wing-bars; underparts whitish, buff to rufous on the flanks.
  - Great Tit: Larger (12.5–15 cm) and crestless; white cheeks in a black hood, green back, lemon-yellow breast with a broad black stripe from bib to vent.

## Cretzschmar's Bunting
*Emberiza caesia* · `crebun2`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, about 20 g — house sparrow size; smaller than Ortolan, otherwise the same shape and plumage pattern; bill stout.
- Plumage — Breeding male: head bright blue-grey, not olive; throat and moustachial stripe cinnamon-orange, not yellow; upperparts brown and heavily streaked, rump unstreaked warm rust-brown; underparts rusty orange; eye-ring white, not yellow. Female: duller and more heavily streaked, but throat and moustache still cinnamon. In autumn the bright colours are partly veiled. Juvenile very hard to tell from Ortolan.
- Bare parts — Bill stout, pink to reddish-pink.
- In the field — Song built like a southern Ortolan's, two or three short notes closing with one long drawn-out note, but always lacking Ortolan's ringing note.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Bulky and larger, 16–19 cm; lark-like streaked grey-brown with whitish underparts and a gorget of streaking round the throat; no orange head or moustache; yellowish mandibles.
  - Reed Bunting: Male's head and throat black with a white collar; female streaked brown-headed and barred below; neither has a cinnamon throat or a grey head; 13.5–15.5 cm.
  - Yellowhammer: Bright yellow head and underparts with a rufous rump; larger, 15.5–17 cm, 20–36.5 g; look for the grey head, cinnamon throat and white eye-ring.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 13–15 cm against 14–16 cm, and on the grey head, cinnamon throat and moustache and pink bill.

## Crimson-winged Finch
*Rhodopechys sanguineus* · `crwfin2`

- Jizz — 13–18 cm; wingspan about 32 cm. Thickset finch, large-headed and thick-necked, with a heavy conical bill; tail short, wings looking notably long in flight.
- Plumage — Male: broad dark, almost black crown on an ochre-to-sand head; dark rose lores and cheeks; supercilium, ear-coverts and neck sides paler beige; throat and breast sandy-brown, dark-streaked, heaviest on the breast; pink-white wing panels, pink rump, white belly. Female: crown brownish, heavily mixed with paler feathers.
- Bare parts — Bill horn-yellow, heavy and conical; legs pale brown, feet darker.
- In the field — Mountain bird; long-winged look in flight, when the pink-white wing panels and pink rump show.
- Similar species —
  - White-winged Crossbill: Crossed bill and two clean white wing-bars on blackish wings; forked tail; male raspberry-red on head and upperparts, not sandy with a dark crown.
  - Brambling: White rump, orange breast against a white belly and orange scapulars; breeding male's head black; no pink wing panel.
  - Common Chaffinch: Blue-grey crown with black forehead, rust-red face and breast, olive-green rump, white wing panel and white wedges in the outer tail.
  - European Greenfinch: Mainly green with yellow in wing and tail, unstreaked; no black crown or pink rump; 15 cm against 13–18 cm.

## Curlew Sandpiper
*Calidris ferruginea* · `cursan`

- Jizz — 18–23 cm, 40–117 g; wingspan 38–46 cm. Small wader, long-necked and long-legged, with a long evenly decurved bill 32–44 mm; taller-standing than Dunlin.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: brick-red head and underparts, upperparts patterned grey. Non-breeding: grey above, white below with pale grey streaks, dark feather centres mottling crown and upperparts, bold white supercilium, grey-brown lores and ear-coverts. Juvenile: grey-brown above, white belly, peach-washed breast. All plumages a large white rump and a white wing-stripe.
- Bare parts — Bill black; iris dark brown; feet dark grey to black.
- In the field — White rump and wing-stripe obvious in flight; moves more calmly than Dunlin. Call djuerri or dirrit; Dunlin's is deeper and harder. Bill length and curvature vary, so use it with care.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Bigger and dumpier, 23–26 cm and 100–225 g; short neck, small head, straight bill no longer than the head; breeding cinnamon only to the breast, rear belly pale.
  - Dunlin: Breeding birds show a black belly, not red underparts, and in flight only a narrow white wingbar — no white rump. Shorter-legged, shorter-billed, smaller at 16–21 cm.
  - Ruff: Pot-bellied and small-headed with a medium straight bill and pink to orange legs; shows white ovals each side of the tail, not one white rump patch.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Short yellow legs and a yellow-based bill; dark above with a purple gloss, breast smeared grey, rump black not white. 19–22 cm.

## Cyprus Warbler
*Curruca melanothorax* · `cypwar1`

- Jizz — 12.5–13.5 cm. Small, compact Curruca warbler.
- Plumage — Male: black hood, grey back, white malar streaks, and the whole underside coarsely spotted black; flanks grey-beige; undertail-coverts dark-centred; tertials, secondaries and greater coverts broadly white-edged. Female: head grey, back grey-brown, spotting confined to throat and breast, underparts whitish.
- Bare parts — Eye-ring thin and white; bill base yellow-pink.
- In the field — Dry open country, often hill slopes with bushes for nesting. Song fast and rattling but dry and jerky; call a smacking zreck.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male's head grey, not black, and the underparts buff and unspotted.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Sexes alike: grey back, grey head with a dark bandit mask and a white throat; underparts whitish and unspotted.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Red eyes and no thin white eye-ring; underparts whitish and unspotted, tertials and coverts plain; slightly larger at 13–14 cm.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male brick-red below rather than black-spotted, with a grey head and back; female brown above, whitish below with a pink flush.

## Cyprus Wheatear
*Oenanthe cypriaca* · `cypwhe1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, male averaging 15.6 g. Small, compact, rather slim wheatear, shorter-winged and shorter-tailed than pied.
- Plumage — Crown and nape white, in fresh autumn plumage blackish-brown with small white spots, wearing whiter. Lores, face, throat, upper breast, mantle, back, scapulars and wings black, slightly glossy; breast beige. Tail black and white in the usual wheatear pattern but with a broad black terminal band, the white rump not reaching far up the back. Sexes essentially alike.
- In the field — Song unique among wheatears: a three- to ten-second series of harsh buzzing bursts, bizz-bizz-bizz, insect-like. Sung from unusually high perches, 5–10 metres up. The most arboreal wheatear, often breeding in woodland.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: Male grey above with a black face mask but a buff throat, never black-throated and black-backed; white rump and tail with a black inverted T; 14–16.5 cm.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Crown and mantle white or buff, not black; black confined to a mask from ear-coverts to bill; tail much whiter with a narrow tip; throat may be white.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Forehead and crown white, mantle buff, not black; black limited to the face mask; tail mostly white; 13.5–15.5 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Uniformly pale sandy above with no black on face, throat or back; creamy-white over-eye streak, pale brown ear-coverts; larger, 16.5 cm.

## Dalmatian Pelican
*Pelecanus crispus* · `dalpel1`

- Jizz — 160–180 cm, wingspan 270–345 cm, about 9.5 kg. Largest pelican and one of the heaviest flying birds; enormous bill 37–45 cm with a big fishing pouch; shaggy curled crest on the nape.
- Plumage — Silvery-white, greyer or creamier in winter; head and neck feathers long, soft and curled, forming a shaggy nape crest that gives an unkempt look and is longest when breeding. In flight the underwing looks almost wholly pale, with only the wingtips black.
- Bare parts — Bill grey-black, upper edge yellow, nail orange; pouch orange-red when breeding, otherwise yellow to orange; iris whitish to pale blue; legs dark to mid grey.
- In the field — Generally quiet; deep guttural grunts, hisses and barking calls only in the breeding season. Voice poorly known, like Great White Pelican's but higher-pitched.
- Similar species —
  - Great White Pelican: Smooth-headed, without the curly nape crest; whiter, pinker plumage, pink legs not grey, and a pink and yellow bill with a dull pale-yellow pouch; 140–180 cm.

## Dartford Warbler
*Curruca undata* · `darwar1`

- Jizz — 13 cm. Small warbler with a long tail for the family; muted, unobtrusive tones matching dry dead plants and greyish wood.
- Plumage — Male: grey head and back — bluish-grey or brownish-grey in some populations; underparts reddish, throat red spotted white; sides dull grey, clearer on the belly. Female: paler below, especially on the throat, browner grey above, underparts browner, throat spots smaller and fainter. Juvenile: as female.
- Bare parts — Iris red.
- In the field — Song a marked rattling trill, fast short phrases mixed with whistled notes; faster and thinner than Sardinian Warbler but lower pitched. Call a harsh tjäähr; alarm a smattering trry-tr'r'r'r.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male grey-headed with clean white throat; no red underparts or red iris.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Sexes alike: grey back, whitish underparts, grey head with a darker bandit mask through the eye and white throat; nothing red below.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's head wholly black, not grey, throat white and unspotted, underparts whitish. Female brown above with a grey head.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male shows white malar streaks and brick-red underparts with grey head and back, and a naked eye-ring. Female brown above with a pink flush below.

## Dead Sea Sparrow
*Passer moabiticus* · `desspa1`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm. Very small sparrow, some 4 cm shorter than House Sparrow, and small-billed.
- Plumage — Male: crown, nape and cheeks grey; black bib edged by a pale yellow malar stripe running onto yellow neck sides; supercilium white before the eye, buff behind; back reddish-brown, heavily black-streaked; coverts rufous against a blackish wing. Female: like a paler House Sparrow, head plain pale brown with a broad supercilium behind the eye. Race yatii sandier and yellow-washed below.
- In the field — Often silent. Song a soft chirping like House and Spanish Sparrows but thinner, or a rolling trirp-trirp-trirp; flight call a high two-syllable chi-wit.
- Similar species —
  - House Sparrow: Larger, 14–18 cm against 12–13, with a stouter bill; male lacks the yellow neck sides and pale malar stripe, female is darker and greyer.
  - Eurasian Tree Sparrow: Chestnut crown and a black ear-patch on a pure white cheek, sexes alike; no grey head and no yellow neck sides. Two narrow white wingbars.
  - Spanish Sparrow: Larger, 14–16 cm; male chestnut-crowned with heavily black-streaked underparts, not a grey crown with a small bib and yellow neck sides.
  - Italian Sparrow: Larger, 14–16 cm; male's crown, nape and head sides chestnut with white cheeks and plain grey underparts, and no yellow on the neck sides.

## Delicate Prinia
*Prinia lepida* · `delpri1`

- Jizz — 10–13 cm. Tiny warbler with a long, strongly rounded tail and short rounded wings; bill and legs short.
- Plumage — Grey-brown above with broad dark streaks and generally dark-toned; head plain, without eyestripe or supercilium; underparts pale with clear marks on the breast sides; each rounded tail feather black-and-white tipped, obvious from below; upper tail strongly barred. Race akyildizi darkest and most heavily streaked, with the brightest buff flanks.
- In the field — Song a monotonous, persistent srliip srliip srliip running together into a series; calls an explosive tlipp or a rattling srrrrrrrt.
- Similar species —
  - Graceful Prinia: Longer-billed and longer-legged but shorter-tailed; paler and less broadly streaked above, with bolder dark spots below the tail; song in clearly separated short phrases.
  - Zitting Cisticola: Brown above, heavily streaked black, with a broad white-tipped tail flicked constantly; lacks the long graduated tail with black-and-white feather tips.

## Desert Finch
*Rhodospiza obsoleta* · `desfin2`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm; wingspan about 26 cm. Slender, Greenfinch-sized finch with a stout, conical, pointed bill.
- Plumage — Predominantly pale sandy, greyer on head, back and breast, warmer ochre on face and forehead; uppertail-coverts warm red-brown; belly and undertail-coverts whitish. Wing boldly black and white with a broad rosy-pink slash across the coverts, obvious in flight; tail black centrally, white-edged. Male: black lores, black bill, narrowly white-edged tertials. Female: duller, these parts grey-brown.
- Bare parts — Bill stout, black in the male; legs and feet dark flesh-coloured.
- In the field — Calls a purring drrr'r, a rosefinch-like tvoi and a whistled dveyyt recalling Twite and Rock Sparrow; song a quiet, jerky rigmarole with buzzing notes woven in.
- Similar species —
  - Common Rosefinch: Male carmine on head, breast and rump, not sandy with a pink wing slash; female dull yellowish-brown, wings plain with two faint bars.
  - Twite: Streaked brown and black above and below, with no plain sandy tone and no pink wing panel; bill yellow in winter; call a nasal twaa-it.
  - Brambling: Orange breast and scapulars against a white belly, and a white rump; breeding male black-headed. No sandy plumage, no pink in the wing.
  - Common Chaffinch: Male has a blue-grey crown, rust-red face and breast and an olive-green rump; wing marked white and buff, never rosy-pink.

## Desert Wheatear
*Oenanthe deserti* · `deswhe1`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, 15–34 g. Compact wheatear, large-headed, with a short neck and short tail.
- Plumage — Male: crown, nape and mantle warm sandy-grey; rump pale buff to white; tail almost wholly black, white only at the extreme base-sides; chin, throat, lores and ear-coverts black; supercilium and scapulars white; breast sandy-buff, belly creamy-white. Female: rump sandier, lores, chin and throat pale buff, tail's dark brownish-black. First-winter male and female drab pale grey-brown. Juvenile: upperparts pale-centred, brown-tipped, speckled.
- In the field — On the perched bird the black cheek runs into the black of the folded wing — a key mark. Flight feathers pale grey-brown, contrasting with black white-tipped axillaries and under wing-coverts.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: White rump and tail with a black inverted T at the tip, never an almost wholly black tail; summer male grey above with a black face mask, not sandy-crowned.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Tail mostly white, only the central pair dark; back and upper tail-coverts white; crown and mantle white or near-white, and the black mask reaches the bill.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Same largely white tail with dark central feathers; forehead and crown white, mantle buff; black mask from ear-coverts to bill, throat black or white.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Plain pale sandy, sexes alike; white rump and a large white tail base, over half the outer feathers; chin and throat pale cream, never black.

## Diamond Dove
*Geopelia cuneata* · `diadov1`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm. Tiny, slender dove, about lark-sized; tail very long in proportion to the body.
- Plumage — Male: head, neck and breast pale blue-grey; upperwing-coverts fawn-brown with fine round white spots edged black; mantle and tail grey-brown; belly and undertail-coverts creamy. Central four tail feathers grey-brown, dark-tipped, outer ones white at the base. Female: duller, more brown on hindneck and mantle. Juvenile: no white wing spots, breast grey.
- Bare parts — Bill dark grey; iris orange-red; bare eye-ring bright red, browner in female; legs and feet pink. Juvenile: bill pale grey, eye-ring fawn, legs grey-black.
- In the field — Voice soft, cooing; male often a short gru-gru twice over, female answering a rough gruuh; slow, plaintive. Displaying male cocks and fans the tail. Tolerates great heat.
- Similar species —
  - Laughing Dove: Much bigger, 25–27 cm against 19–21 cm; pinkish-brown below with a chequered rufous-and-grey neck patch and a grey wing band, no white wing spots.
  - Namaqua Dove: Wings unspotted, chestnut primaries flashing in flight; tail long, black and tapered; male has black face, throat and breast and a yellow-and-red bill.

## Dovekie
*Alle alle* · `doveki`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm, 130–200 g; wingspan 34–38 cm. Only Atlantic auk of this size, half the size of a puffin; squat, spindle-shaped in flight, head proportionally large and round; bill very short and stubby; tail short and rounded.
- Plumage — Breeding: head, neck, upper breast, back and upperwing black; underparts and undertail-coverts white; underwing grey; white trailing edge to the secondaries and white fringes to the scapulars. Winter: throat, fore-neck, breast and sides of head white up to the black cheeks. Sexes alike; first-winter often brownish.
- Bare parts — Bill and iris black in all plumages; most birds show a small but distinct white eye-spot; feet grey and webbed.
- In the field — Flight straight but slightly unsteady, on very fast whirring beats, low over the sea. Between dives lies very low in the water with drooping wings and neck drawn in. Twitters and cackles at colonies; silent at sea.
- Similar species —
  - Atlantic Puffin: Twice the size, 28–30 cm; stout deep bill, glossy black crown and back with a broad black collar and large pale grey lozenge-shaped face patches.
  - Black Guillemot: Larger, 30–32 cm; summer plumage wholly black but for a large white patch on the upperwing; legs and feet bright coral-red.

## Dunlin
*Calidris alpina* · `dunlin`

- Jizz — 16–21 cm, 34–77 g. Small dumpy wader, starling-sized but chunkier; long black bill, slightly decurved in the outer quarter and blunt-tipped in life.
- Plumage — Breeding: crown and back dark with rufous fringes, throat and breast whitish finely dark-streaked, flanks spotted, belly solid black — unique among waders of this size. Non-breeding: plain grey-brown above, pale grey breast band against white below. Juvenile: buff-brown above with two weak whitish V's on the back, black marks on flanks or belly.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs black to blackish-olive; iris brown-black.
- In the field — Narrow white wingbar in flight at all ages. Probes mud and sand with the soft, nerve-rich bill-tip. Call a sandpiper chreep or buzzing krrrry; song a harsh trill in display flight.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Red knot is much bigger, 23–26 cm and 100–225 g, with a straight bill no longer than its head; breeding birds cinnamon-faced, never black-bellied.
  - Ruff: Ruff is pot-bellied and small-headed with long pale legs, a deeper slower wingbeat, only a thin indistinct wingbar and white ovals at the tail sides.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Purple sandpiper has short yellow legs and a yellow-based bill, dark purple-glossed upperparts and a grey-smeared breast; bigger, 19–22 cm.
  - Temminck's Stint: Temminck's stint is far smaller, 13.5–15 cm, plain brown above with dull yellowish legs and white outer tail feathers; bill short and straight.

## Dunnock
*Prunella modularis* · `dunnoc1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, about 20 g. Robin- to sparrow-sized but slighter and drabber; bill thin, dark, finely pointed — an insect-eater's, not conical.
- Plumage — Crown, supercilium, nape, throat and breast lead- to slate-grey; back and wings rich dark brown streaked black; underparts brownish and plain. Sexes alike, male averaging a little more grey on head and neck. Juvenile: head browner, feathering loose and fluffy, streaking heavier and reaching the throat and breast.
- Bare parts — Bill dark and thin; legs red-brown.
- In the field — Keeps hidden in scrub and low cover, showing best when singing from an open perch. Flicks its wings frequently, unlike any similar small brown European bird. Call a thin high tseep or tih; song rapid, thin, silvery tinkling.
- Similar species —
  - Alpine Accentor: Larger (15–17.5 cm), head all grey and underparts spotted red-brown; the dunnock's grey is confined to head and breast, its underparts plain.
  - Radde's Accentor: Same size (13–14 cm) but dark-capped and dark-cheeked, with a broad dirty-white supercilium and warm orange-buff breast; no grey head or breast.
  - House Sparrow: Bulkier and big-headed (14–18 cm) with a full chest and stout conical seed-eater's bill; the dunnock's bill is thin and its head and breast grey.

## Dupont's Lark
*Chersophilus duponti* · `duplar1`

- Jizz — 17–18 cm. Slim, graceful lark, skylark-sized; neck long, legs long, bill long, fine and slightly downcurved.
- Plumage — Cryptically marked in brown; thin pale crown stripe, pale eye-stripe, dark-streaked breast, white outer tail feathers, and no white trailing edge to the wing. North-western birds mainly brown-grey above and pale below; south-eastern birds rufous above. Sexes alike. Race margaritae longer-billed and more rufous.
- In the field — Song a repeated thin, melancholy, nasal whistling phrase at dawn, dusk and by night, often in high song-flight; strongly ventriloquial and surprisingly hard to locate.
- Similar species —
  - Crested Lark: Same length, 17–18 cm, but bulkier about head and body with a conspicuous crest and a short stubby bill, not long, fine and downcurved.
  - Eurasian Skylark: Short blunt crest, short broad wings with a white trailing edge as well as white tail sides, bill short; 16–19 cm.
  - Horned Lark: Striking black-and-yellow face pattern, black horns on the summer male, and a mostly black tail contrasting with the paler body.
  - Calandra Lark: Larger and robust, 17.5–20 cm, with big black patches on the breast sides, a white supercilium and short broad dark underwings.

## Dusky Warbler
*Phylloscopus fuscatus* · `duswar`

- Jizz — 10.5–12 cm. Chiffchaff-sized leaf warbler, at some angles wren-like; bill short, fine and pointed; tail slightly rounded, unlike most Phylloscopus.
- Plumage — Upperparts uniformly dark grey-brown, crown concolorous; long pale supercilium over a dark eye-stripe, and a thin white eye-ring. Underparts dirty white to buff, breast a shade darker, flanks and undertail-coverts cream, vent white. Sexes alike; juveniles more olive above.
- Bare parts — Bill dark, with a little red-brown at the base; legs dark.
- In the field — Skulking, usually first detected by voice; breeds in taiga bogs and wet meadows. Song a monotonous whistle; call a hard check or teck, recalling Lesser Whitethroat.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Greenish-brown above and yellowish below, not dark grey-brown; legs pale pinkish-yellow, primary projection long; call a disyllabic hoo-eet.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Upperparts dull green washed brown, flanks yellowish, not grey-brown and cream; supercilium short and dull, not the long whitish stripe here.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Greenish above with bold double yellowish-white wingbars and a long yellow supercilium; smaller, 9.5–11 cm, and arboreal, almost constantly moving.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above with a single pale wingbar; song a high jerky trill, not a monotonous whistle.

## Eared Grebe
*Podiceps nigricollis* · `eargre`

- Jizz — 28–34 cm; wingspan 56–60 cm; 215–600 g. Small, chubby grebe with a peaked crown, steep high forehead, fluffy rear end and a thin, slightly upturned bill.
- Plumage — Breeding: head, neck, breast and upperparts black, with a fan of ochre plumes behind the eye over the ear-coverts, not a closed triangle; flanks tawny rufous to chestnut; belly and underwing white; secondaries and inner primaries white. Non-breeding: crown, hindneck and upperparts grey-black, underparts, throat and face-sides white, the dark mark behind the eye distinct.
- Bare parts — Bill black, thin and slightly upturned; iris vivid red; legs dark greenish grey.
- In the field — In flight the leading edge of the upperwing is wholly dark. Territorial call a soft poo-ii-tjk, with a low hard vibrating trill in display.
- Similar species —
  - Great Crested Grebe: Far larger, 46–51 cm and up to 1.5 kg; head and neck decorations in summer; in winter white above the eye with a pink bill.
  - Red-necked Grebe: Bigger and heavier, 750–1,600 g; rusty-red neck and pale grey cheeks in summer, dark eye, black bill yellow at the base; winter face dusky with a pale rear crescent.
  - Horned Grebe: Flat forehead, straight bill with a white tip, erectile horns forming a closed triangle; in flight the lesser coverts are white, the leading edge not all dark.
  - Little Grebe: Small and dumpy with a bright yellow gape; summer neck and cheeks rich rufous, winter buff with a powder-puff rear; bill pointed, not upturned.

## Eastern Black-eared Wheatear
*Oenanthe melanoleuca* · `bkewhe2`

- Jizz — 13.5–15.5 cm, 12–21 g. Relatively small, slim, long-tailed wheatear.
- Plumage — Breeding male: forehead, crown and mantle white or nearly white; wings blacker than Northern Wheatear's; back, rump and most of the tail white, the black terminal band broader at the corners; black mask from ear-coverts to just above the bill base. Black-throated morph, whole face black, commoner than the white-throated. Female: grey-brown, colder-toned and duller, white lower back.
- In the field — Autumn and winter: head, mantle and underparts distinctly buff, the intensity varying. Tail feathers other than the central pair much whiter than Northern Wheatear's, the white on the inner web often reaching the tip.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: Larger, 14.5–16 cm; summer male grey above with a buff throat and no white crown or mantle; tail has an even black inverted T and much less white.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Male's upperparts more buff-tinged, less starkly black-and-white; mask stops at the bill base rather than above it; black throat less extensive, ending less abruptly; female warmer brown.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Sexes alike, pale sandy-isabelline throughout with no black mask; creamy-white supercilium, pale brown ear-coverts, less white on the tail; larger, about 16.5 cm.
  - Black Wheatear: All black, female dark brown, except white rump and mainly white tail with a black inverted T; larger, 16–18 cm.

## Eastern Bonelli's Warbler
*Phylloscopus orientalis* · `eabwar1`

- Jizz — 10.5–12 cm. Small leaf warbler, slightly larger and longer-winged than Western Bonelli's Warbler; bill small and pointed.
- Plumage — Plain grey-green above with a dull grey crown and mantle; rump yellow-green; upperwing and tail green-toned with blackish centres, tertials pale-edged; underwing-coverts and axillaries pale yellow; throat, breast, belly and undertail-coverts white. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Legs brown to pale grey-brown.
- In the field — Call diagnostic: a hard monosyllabic tschip or chup, like a young House Sparrow or a crossbill, never the disyllabic hu-it of Western Bonelli's. Song a fast monotone trill, drier and more mechanical.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Greenish-brown above, off-white to yellowish below, no yellow-green rump; pale pinkish-yellow legs, longer paler bill; call a disyllabic hoo-eet; 11–12.5 cm.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Brown-washed dull green above, yellowish on the flanks, no yellow-green rump; dark legs, short primary projection, short whitish supercilium; 10–12 cm.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Two bold yellowish-white wing bars and a long yellow supercilium against Eastern Bonelli's plain wings and plain face; smaller, 9.5–11 cm.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green with a single pale wing bar and no yellow-green rump; song a high jerky trill rather than a fast monotone one.

## Eastern Olivaceous Warbler
*Iduna pallida* · `eaowar1`

- Jizz — 11.5–14 cm. Small, slim warbler recalling a pale Reed Warbler; flat forehead and pointed profile; long thin bill with straight or slightly concave sides and a faintly decurved tip; tail square-cut.
- Plumage — Upperparts plain grey-brown with an olive tinge, back and uppertail-coverts a shade yellower-brown; below whitish, breast-sides and flanks warm greyish-buff; undertail-coverts cream-white; narrow cream supercilium from bill-base to eye, dark lores, thin pale eye-ring; faint pale wing panel; tail dark grey-brown with grey-white tips and edges; never rufous on the rump. Juvenile: buffier belly.
- Bare parts — Bill dark horn above, pale pink-yellow to yellow-brown below; legs dark grey-brown with a pinkish cast.
- In the field — Flicks the tail downward while moving through vegetation — characteristic, and unlike Reed Warbler. Song a fast, monotonous nasal babble, poorly articulated, no mimicry; call a nasal chack between Blackcap's and Garden Warbler's.
- Similar species —
  - Booted Warbler: Smaller, chiffchaff-sized, short-winged and pot-bellied with a square-tipped tail; bill short with a dark tip; supercilium clear and pale.
  - Western Olivaceous Warbler: Larger, 13–14.5 cm, browner above and without contrasting pale edges to the secondaries; bill bigger — long, broad and swollen-looking.
  - Icterine Warbler: Uniformly light yellow below, greyish-green above; pale lores, yellowish supercilium, bold pale panel on secondaries and tertials; bigger head, broad-based bill, long wings.
  - Moustached Warbler: Back finely streaked, not plain; supercilium prominent and whitish, ear-coverts grey; song softer and more melodious, with nightingale-like phrases.

## Eastern Orphean Warbler
*Curruca crassirostris* · `eaowar2`

- Jizz — 15–16.6 cm. One of the largest typical warblers, somewhat bigger than Blackcap; bill long, pointed and rather heavy; tail long and black with white outer feathers.
- Plumage — Male: head dark grey to black as a neatly demarcated cap, with a black eye-mask; back and upperwing grey; throat white, breast dirty white, flanks faintly buff; undertail-coverts always dark in the centre, giving a blotched look. Female and immature: head paler, underparts reddish, back grey with a brownish tinge.
- Bare parts — Legs black; iris white in adults, dark in young birds; bill long, in males with more clearly marked blue-white at the base.
- In the field — Open deciduous woodland; takes nectar. Song a series of warbling liroo-liroo and scolding notes, more varied than Western Orphean's and approaching Nightingale in richness.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male grey-headed with a white throat but no black mask and no white iris; song scratchy and scolding.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Much smaller and slighter, grey above with a dark bandit mask and a short fine bill; iris dark; song a fast rattle of tet notes, not warbling liroo-liroo.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's black covers the whole head and the eyes are red, not a white iris; underparts whitish, often with a reddish hue; smaller-billed.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male's underparts brick-red with white malar stripes and a naked eye-ring; head and back grey without a black mask; smaller, song a fast rattle.

## Eastern Subalpine Warbler
*Curruca cantillans* · `easwar1`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm. Small warbler, about Lesser Whitethroat's size but slimmer and shorter-tailed; wing feathers and greater coverts pale-fringed.
- Plumage — Male: head and mantle lead-grey; throat and breast intense cinnamon to brick-red, cut off by a broad white malar streak and contrasting with the paler belly. Female: head paler grey, back grey-olive, throat and flanks washed pink, white below. First-winter: olive-brown above, white below. Both sexes: a white pointed wedge on the second-outermost tail feather — hard to see, diagnostic.
- Bare parts — Male: eye-ring and orbital ring brick-red, iris dark; legs brown-pink; bill beige, dark on tip and culmen. Female: eye-ring whitish.
- In the field — Song fast and rattling, recalling Lesser Whitethroat; chattering and hoarse like Sardinian Warbler's but higher-pitched and longer. Call a trep or tjep, doubled to tjeret in albistriata.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male grey-headed with a plain white throat, so no brick-red breast and no white malar streak.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Sexes almost identical: grey above, whitish below, grey head with a darker bandit mask through the eye and a white throat; no red breast, no red eye-ring.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's whole head is black with a white throat and red eyes, underparts whitish, never brick-red on the breast; female brown above and buff below with a grey head.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: The provided texts describe the two identically — grey head and back, brick-red underparts, white malar streaks — and give no measurements; nothing here separates them.

## Egyptian Goose
*Alopochen aegyptiaca* · `egygoo`

- Jizz — 63–73 cm, wingspan 134–154 cm, 1,500–2,348 g. Goose-like duck on notably long legs, heavy in flight; sexes alike, male usually somewhat larger.
- Plumage — Boldly marked, unmistakable: pale head with a brown eye-mask, red-brown upperparts, beige-grey underparts and a dark red-brown belly patch. Large white wing panels are hidden by the coverts at rest and blaze out when the bird is alarmed or in flight. Tone varies individually, greyer to warmer brown. Juvenile lacks eye and breast patches until four to five months old.
- Bare parts — Bill varies from pale red to deep red, reddest on escaped birds; dark patch round the eye.
- In the field — Swims well; flies heavily, more goose than duck. Male a hoarse subdued quack, seldom heard, plus a louder breathy steam-engine sequence; female a far noisier raucous quack.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: True grey-brown goose: short bill bright pink with black base and tip, pink feet, white rump and vent; no eye-mask, no white wing panel.
  - Snow Goose: White with black wingtips, or a blue-grey morph; rose-red legs and pink bill with a black grin patch.
  - Ruddy Shelduck: Orange-brown throughout with a paler head, male with a narrow black collar; bill black and legs dark grey, not red.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Larger, 64–81 cm; greyish brown with a white facial blaze, black belly bars, pink bill and bright orange legs.

## Egyptian Vulture
*Neophron percnopterus* · `egyvul1`

- Jizz — 60–70 cm, 1.5–2.2 kg, wingspan about 1.65 m. Small vulture but medium-sized raptor; slender long bill with hooked tip, long neck feathers forming hackles, wedge-shaped tail; wings pointed, third primary longest.
- Plumage — Adult white, wild birds usually soiled rusty or brown from mud and iron-rich soil; flight feathers black; throat feathers yellowish; head bare, wrinkled, bright yellow, sometimes orange-tinged. Sexes alike, but breeding male's facial skin deeper orange; female averages larger, 10–15% heavier. Juvenile: blackish to chocolate-brown, patched black and white, whitening moult by moult, adult at about five years.
- Bare parts — Bill black in nominate, pale or yellowish in Indian ginginianus; base yellow. Legs pink in adults, grey in juveniles. Iris reddish-brown, black in juveniles.
- In the field — Flight silhouette characteristic: broad, well-fingered wings and wedge-shaped tail; the black flight feathers against the white body are most conspicuous in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Black-winged Kite: Grey and white with black shoulder patches, black wing tips and a black eye stripe; tail short and square, not wedge-shaped; head feathered, no bare yellow face.
  - Black Kite: Generally dark brown throughout, head and neck paler, no white body; tail forked, not wedge-shaped; body feathers dark-shafted, giving a streaked look.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Look for the white body, black flight feathers and bare yellow face; the candidate text gives only size and form, 55–74 cm and 1.4–2.5 kg against 1.5–2.2 kg.
  - Booted Eagle: Pale morph is light grey with a darker head, dark morph mid-brown; flight feathers dark in both, but head feathered and smaller, 40–51 cm, wingspan 110–132 cm.

## Eleonora's Falcon
*Falco eleonorae* · `elefal1`

- Jizz — 36–42 cm, wingspan about 85–105 cm. Slim falcon with long pointed wings and long tail, between Hobby and Peregrine in size; shaped like a big Hobby or a small slender Peregrine.
- Plumage — Two morphs. Light: dark grey to grey-brown above with ochre feather edges, whitish lower cheek, throat and neck, bold black moustachial stripe; rufous-brown underparts boldly black-marked, trousers finely streaked; long rufous tail finely barred, subterminal band broadest. Dark: uniformly sooty grey-brown, underparts a shade paler, underwing-coverts black against paler flight-feather bases. Juvenile: pale below, wingtips and coverts darker.
- In the field — Call a typical falcon kek-kek-kek. About 70 per cent of birds belong to the light morph.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Kestrel: Smaller, 32–39 cm, light chestnut-brown spotted black above; male blue-grey capped and grey-tailed; lacks the long-winged, long-tailed slim outline.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Eleonora's is slimmer with longer wings and tail and looks like a small Peregrine; sources otherwise give only size, 34–58 cm against 36–42 cm.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Smaller, 29–36 cm; slate-grey above, whitish and black-streaked below with sharply cut rufous trousers and vent; Eleonora's moustache unbroken, underparts brown.
  - Merlin: Much smaller, 24–33 cm, wingspan 50–73 cm, and robust and heavily built rather than long-winged and slim; sources give little beyond size and build.

## Eurasian Bittern
*Botaurus stellaris* · `grebit1`

- Jizz — 69–81 cm, wingspan 100–130 cm, 870–1,940 g. Thickset heron, only slightly smaller than grey heron; short thick neck, short legs, short powerful bill; loose plumage, erectile on crown, neck and breast.
- Plumage — Pale buffy-brown, streaked and barred black throughout; crown and nape black; sides of head and neck tawny-buff barred black; yellowish-buff supercilium, brownish-black moustachial stripe; chin and throat cream-buff with a rusty-brown central stripe; breast and belly yellowish-buff with broad brown side stripes; wings and tail rusty-buff mottled black. Male larger and more strongly marked.
- Bare parts — Bill green-yellow with a darker tip to the upper mandible, finely serrated near the tip; feet green-yellow.
- In the field — Skulks in old reed; when approached freezes upright, bill to the sky, swaying with the reeds. Flight owl- or raptor-like on broad wings, front-heavy, feet trailing, upperside evenly dark. Booming song carries five kilometres.
- Similar species —
  - Little Bittern: Far smaller, 33–38 cm, wingspan 52–58 cm; both sexes show pale forewing panels, male black above with a buff-pink oval wing panel, female duller brownish-black above.
  - Purple Heron: Larger but slender, 78–97 cm, wingspan 120–152 cm; dark reddish-brown with a darker grey back, black crown with a dangling crest, buffish-chestnut head and neck sides.
  - Little Egret: Entirely white, 55–65 cm, wingspan 88–106 cm; breeding birds carry two long nape plumes and loose scapular plumes; bill long and slender.
  - Gray Heron: Bigger, 84–102 cm, wingspan 155–195 cm; ashy-grey above, greyish-white below, white head and neck with a broad black supercilium ending in a dangling crest.

## Eurasian Blackbird
*Turdus merula* · `eurbla`

- Jizz — 23.5–29 cm (24–27 cm in more recent treatments), wingspan 35–38 cm, 85–105 g. One of Europe's largest thrushes; long-tailed — noticeably longer-tailed than a starling.
- Plumage — Male uniformly black, faintly grey- or bronze-fringed below in good light; flight feathers bleached by spring. Female dark olive-brown to olive-grey above with paler forehead, weak supercilium and dark lores, throat pale grey and streaked, breast brown-grey to rufous, dark-spotted. Juvenile like the female but pale-spotted above; first-year male black with a brown folded wing.
- Bare parts — Male bill orange-yellow, darkening in late summer, with bright orange-yellow orbital ring; iris dark brown. Female bill pale horn-yellow, dark-ridged; eye-ring weak. Legs dark brown.
- In the field — Nothing on flight, gait or voice in the sources; separation from a starling rests on the much longer tail.
- Similar species —
  - Redwing: Smaller, 20–24 cm against 23.5–29 cm; brown-backed with dark-spotted white underparts, red flanks and underwing and a creamy supercilium — never all black.
  - Fieldfare: Slate-grey head, nape and rump with dark brown back, blackish tail and boldly speckled breast; white underwing-coverts flash in flight; harsh 'tsak tsak'.
  - Song Thrush: Plain brown above, cream to buff underparts neatly black-spotted; smaller, 20–23.5 cm against 23.5–29 cm; yellowish bill, pink legs.
  - Mistle Thrush: Pale grey-brown above with round black spots on a buff breast, white underwing and white tips to the outer tail; stocky and upright on the ground.

## Eurasian Blackcap
*Sylvia atricapilla* · `blackc1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, 15–25 g; wingspan 20–23 cm, wing 7–8 cm. Small, plain grey warbler; tail dark and plain, paler at the base, without white.
- Plumage — Male: olive-grey to dark grey above, nape paler grey, neat black cap; light grey below, silvery white on chin, throat and upper breast; flanks faintly brownish; tail dark grey, olive-edged. Female: cap reddish-brown, upperparts browner. Juvenile: as female, rufous tinge above, olive breast and flanks; first-winter males brown, black or mixed cap.
- Bare parts — Bill and long legs grey; iris reddish-brown in the English source, always dark in the German.
- In the field — Song loud and strong, opening with a garden-warbler-like chatter then turning flute-like and melodic, sometimes mimicking; call a hard smacking täck, repeated in long series when alarmed, with hoarse drawn-out schrää.
- Similar species —
  - Garden Warbler: No cap at all; plain unstreaked olive-brown above, dull white below, whitish eyering and faint pale supercilium, buff wash on throat and flanks; legs bluish-grey.
  - Dartford Warbler: Long-tailed for a warbler; grey head and back with reddish, white-spotted underparts and a red eye — no clean-cut cap, no silvery white throat.
  - Western Orphean Warbler: Larger, 15–16 cm; whole head dark grey with a black eye mask rather than a small cap; white iris, white throat, black legs, white tail edges.
  - Eastern Orphean Warbler: Larger, 15–16.6 cm; dark grey head with black eye mask, not a neat cap; white iris and white throat, black legs, white tail edges.

## Eurasian Blue Tit
*Cyanistes caeruleus* · `blutit`

- Jizz — 10.5–12 cm, wingspan 17.5–20 cm, 11–12.1 g. Small tit with a round head set low on the body, looking neckless; bill short and deep for a tit.
- Plumage — White forehead into a pale blue crown; narrow black eyestripe to a dark blue nape band, set off by a white line; white cheeks bounded by a black chin-spot and black-blue collar. Back dull green, rump grey-blue, wings and tail blue, one white bar; underparts bright yellow with a dark line down the belly. Juvenile: cheeks and supercilium yellow, duller.
- Bare parts — Bill dark horn-brown; iris brown; feet dark blue-grey, claws grey.
- In the field — Crown feathers can be raised into a low blunt crest. Pale blue head and upperparts are shown by no other Central European passerine, so identification is straightforward. Sexes alike to the eye.
- Similar species —
  - African Blue Tit: Similar size, 11–12 cm, but crown deep glossy blue becoming blackish on the neck, and the white forehead runs back as a supercilium to mid-nape.
  - Coal Tit: No blue anywhere: glossy blue-black head and throat, big white nape spot, off-white cheeks, two white wingbars, whitish underparts buff to rufous on the flanks.
  - Willow Tit: Dull black cap running onto the mantle and a black bib; back grey-brown, underparts buff; large head, thin bill. No blue, no yellow.
  - Gray-headed Chickadee: Larger, 12.5–14 cm; dark brown head with white cheeks, cinnamon-brown mantle, whitish underparts washed rusty; no blue or yellow.

## Eurasian Bullfinch
*Pyrrhula pyrrhula* · `eurbul`

- Jizz — 15–19 cm, wingspan 22–26 cm, about 26 g. Bulky, bull-headed, short-necked, with thin feet; bill short, thick and conical, upper mandible strongly curved.
- Plumage — Male: crown, face and chin black, wings and tail black glossed blue, back blue-grey, rump and undertail white, broad white wing-bar; cheeks, breast, flanks and upper belly bright rose-red. Female: back brown-grey, underparts pale grey-brown with a faint reddish-lilac cast. Juvenile: browner still, head pale with no black cap, bill without black.
- Bare parts — Bill short, thick, black; iris deep brown; feet black-brown, blackish in the female.
- In the field — Unobtrusive. Call a quiet, low, mournful whistled peeu or pew, given perched and in flight; song weak, audible only at close range — fluted whistles alternating with scratchy warbling and creaking notes. Good mimics, chiefly juveniles.
- Similar species —
  - African Chaffinch: Male blue-grey-headed with a moss-green mantle, pale pinkish underparts, two bold white wing-stripes and white tail-sides; no black cap, white rump or rose-red breast.
  - Brambling: White-rumped like the bullfinch, but breast and scapulars orange against a white belly and the breeding male black-headed; no black cap on a grey back, no red.
  - Citril Finch: Small (12 cm, 12.5 g), grey above with a brown black-streaked back, yellow face, underparts and double wing-bars; no black cap, white rump or stubby black bill.
  - Island Canary: Much smaller (10–12 cm), yellow-green and dark-streaked with a dull yellow rump; lacks the black cap, white rump, white wing-bar and red underparts.

## Eurasian Collared-Dove
*Streptopelia decaocto* · `eucdov`

- Jizz — 29–33 cm, 125–240 g; wingspan 47–55 cm. Slim, long-bodied dove, about as long as a feral pigeon but lighter and longer-tailed, so slighter and more delicate-looking.
- Plumage — Uniform pale grey-buff to pinkish-grey, slightly darker above than below; back and lesser coverts browner, greater coverts pale blue-grey, flight feathers dark grey. Black white-edged half-collar on the nape is the key mark, missing on autumn juveniles. Long tail broadly white-edged, dark grey and white-tipped below; blue-grey underwing patch.
- Bare parts — Legs short and red; bill black to dark grey; iris red with a narrow white eye-ring, looking black at any distance.
- In the field — Climbs steeply, hangs a moment hovering like a falcon, then glides down almost without wingbeats; the wings make a whirring sound in flight. Song a monotonous three-syllable goo-GOO-goo, stress on the second; a harsh screeching chrrai-chrrai before landing.
- Similar species —
  - European Turtle-Dove: Smaller and slimmer, 26–28 cm; brown, with a black-and-white striped neck patch and a wedge-shaped, dark-centred, white-bordered tail; bare skin around the eye.
  - Oriental Turtle-Dove: About the same size but browner, with dark feather centres giving a scaly back, the striped neck patch, and a wedge-shaped tail; flight relaxed and direct.
  - Stock Dove: Blue-toned grey with no collar; short dark wingbars, broad dark tail-band, iridescent neck patch; eye dark, bill pink with a yellow tip; heavier, 300–365 g.
  - Rock Pigeon: Darker blue-grey with a white lower back, two black wingbars and a black tail-band; bulkier, shorter-tailed and heavier, 238–380 g.

## Eurasian Coot
*Fulica atra* · `eurcoo`

- Jizz — 36–42 cm, wingspan 70–80 cm, 750–900 g. Medium-sized rounded rail with small round head, short tail, strong legs and long lobed toes; swims duck-like and lies high in the water.
- Plumage — Slate-grey to blackish above with a brownish to olive sheen, head and neck velvety black, throat and lower neck darker; whitish trailing edge to the wing. Sexes alike. Juvenile: grey-brown, paler on face-sides and breast, no shield, iris dark. First winter: shield smaller, a dark spot inside the white bill-tip.
- Bare parts — Bill white to faintly pink, about 30 mm, running into a bright white frontal shield 14–30 mm long; iris red to dark red-brown; legs green.
- In the field — Noisy, with a wide range of crackling, explosive or trumpeting calls, often given at night. Toes lobed, not webbed.
- Similar species —
  - Red-knobbed Coot: Also black with a white shield and needs a good view; sources separate them only on measurements, 35–42 cm and span 75–85 cm against 36–42 cm and 70–80 cm.
  - Eurasian Moorhen: Frontal shield red and bill red with yellow tip, not white; white undertail and white flank streaks, yellow legs; browner and smaller, 30–38 cm.
  - Western Swamphen: Chicken-sized with bright plumage, large feet and a red bill and frontal shield — easily told from the black Coot with its white bill and shield.
  - Spotted Crake: Small crake, 19–22.5 cm: brown above with white spots and dark bars, blue-grey breast, short straight yellow bill with red base, green legs.

## Eurasian Crag-Martin
*Ptyonoprogne rupestris* · `eurcrm1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, wingspan 32–34.5 cm, about 23 g. Compact martin, broad-necked, wings broad but pointed; body, wings and tail broader than any other European swallow; tail short and square.
- Plumage — Ash-brown to mid-grey above with grey-brown tones, flight feathers evenly dark grey; underparts paler grey with a dark vent. Underwing- and undertail-coverts blackish, contrasting strongly with the rest of the underside. No breast band. Spread tail shows white square spots near the tips of all but the central and outermost feather pairs. Juvenile: buff-brown tips to head, upperparts and wing-coverts.
- Bare parts — Eyes brown; small bill mainly black; legs brownish-pink.
- In the field — Flight slow for a swallow: rapid wingbeats broken by flat-winged glides, long flexible primaries giving agility along cliff faces; often flies high. Voice quiet — a hurried rough twitter, call a hard prrit or pritprit.
- Similar species —
  - Pale Crag-Martin: Smaller and paler: light brown above and paler on the lower back, throat, upper breast and underwing-coverts pale grey, underparts dirty white; tail spots smaller.
  - Bank Swallow: Smaller (12–13 cm), brown above and white below with a narrow brown breast band and white throat; no white tail spots; flight quick and jerky.
  - Barn Swallow: Larger (17–19 cm), steel-blue above with rufous forehead and throat, a broad dark blue breast band and long forked tail streamers.
  - Western House-Martin: Steel-blue above with a clean white rump, white underparts and white underwings, legs feathered white; no white tail spots, no blackish undertail-coverts.

## Eurasian Curlew
*Numenius arquata* · `eurcur`

- Jizz — 50–60 cm, wingspan 89–106 cm, 410–1360 g. Largest wader in range; long legs and a very long, smoothly downcurved bill, longest in the adult female.
- Plumage — Mainly greyish brown; head, neck, breast and upperparts pale beige-brown streaked and spotted dark; cheeks finely streaked and contrasting with the pale chin and throat; breast more strongly streaked, paling towards the belly. White rump and back form a white wedge in flight. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Legs greyish blue.
- In the field — Feet project well beyond the tail in flight, forming a conspicuous point. Call a loud curloo-oo; song a strong rhythmic trill opening with drawn-out mournful notes.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Whimbrel: Whimbrel is smaller, 37–47 cm against 50–60 cm, its shorter bill kinked rather than smoothly curved; call a rippling whistle, trilled in song.
  - Bar-tailed Godwit: Bar-tailed godwit is smaller-bodied with a slightly upturned bi-coloured bill, and its legs do not extend beyond the tail tip in flight.
  - Black-tailed Godwit: Black-tailed godwit has a long straight bill and bold black-and-white wings with a white rump in flight; legs dark grey, brown or black.
  - Common Greenshank: Greenshank is much smaller, with long green legs and a shortish grey-based bill looking slightly upturned; it too shows a white wedge on the back.

## Eurasian Dotterel
*Eudromias morinellus* · `eurdot`

- Jizz — 21–24 cm. Medium-sized plover, smaller and more compact than European Golden-Plover, well larger than Little Ringed Plover but smaller than Lapwing; fairly long yellow legs make it look bigger; short bill, gentle expression.
- Plumage — All plumages: broad whitish supercilia meeting in a V on the nape, and a pale breast-band. Summer adult unmistakable; black crown, streaked head, slate-grey neck and breast above the white line, chestnut breast to black belly, white vent, warm brown pale-edged back; female brighter. Winter and juvenile washed out, buff-yellow below, grey vermiculated above, juvenile white-fringed.
- Bare parts — Bill short and black to grey-black; legs yellow to green-yellow.
- In the field — Plain wings in flight: upperwing uniform grey-black with no wingbar, only a pale shaft on the outermost primary. Flight call a soft pyurr; the female's song a simple repeated whistle.
- Similar species —
  - Black-bellied Plover: Larger, 25–30 cm; summer black-and-white spangled above with black face and belly and white rump; no white V on the nape; bill and legs black.
  - Common Ringed Plover: One black breast-band, white forehead and black mask, orange-and-black bill, orange legs; smaller, 17–20 cm, and no nape V.
  - European Golden-Plover: Larger, 25–29 cm, thickset with wings only just longer than the tail; white S-shaped band from forehead down the flanks.
  - Kentish Plover: Much smaller and paler, around 40 g; broken dark patches at the breast sides, rufous crown and nape on summer males, black bill and dark legs.

## Eurasian Eagle-Owl
*Bubo bubo* · `eueowl1`

- Jizz — 59–73 cm, wingspan 131–188 cm; female clearly larger (males 1.2–3.2 kg, females 1.75–4.6 kg). Huge, bulky owl; big head with long ear-tufts; loose full plumage; heavy tarsi and claws.
- Plumage — Upperparts dark brown, coarsely blotched and barred black; breast heavily black-streaked on a yellow-brown ground; belly rusty-buff with fine black streaks; underparts and underwing paler than the back; black-and-white band across the wing; throat white, shown only when calling. Juvenile: pale grey down, head still downy, tufts small.
- Bare parts — Iris orange-yellow; tarsus and claws heavy.
- In the field — Ear-tufts held low at the sides when relaxed, raised when calling or agitated, invisible in flight. Flight powerful and steady, recalling a big buzzard but wings more cupped in the glide. Facial disc weaker than Long-eared or Barn Owl.
- Similar species —
  - Snowy Owl: Mostly pure white and looks earless — tufts vestigial, 20–25 mm; head small, facial disc shallow; eyes bright yellow.
  - Ural Owl: No ear-tufts; broad round facial disc; plain pale greyish-brown, underparts streaked without crossbars; exceptionally long wedge-tipped tail; smaller, wingspan 103–134 cm.
  - Long-eared Owl: Far smaller (31–40 cm, wingspan 86–98 cm) and slim, long-winged; ear-tufts set nearer the centre of the crown; ochre-tawny with whitish scapular marks.
  - Boreal Owl: Tiny beside it, 21–26 cm; no ear-tufts; brown above with pearly white spots, whitish below with rust streaks; yellow eyes.

## Eurasian Golden Oriole
*Oriolus oriolus* · `eugori2`

- Jizz — About 24 cm. Slim; blackbird-sized but slimmer-bodied, with a shorter tail and longer wings.
- Plumage — Male brilliant yellow with black-violet wings and tail; yellow wing patch, yellow tips and edges to the tail, central tail feathers black-violet narrowly edged yellow and the outer ones yellow; black lore from bill base to eye. Female: olive-green above, yellower towards the rump, dirty white below with fine dark streaks, tail dark green to black, lore fainter and greyer.
- Bare parts — Bill pink to rust-red; iris brownish, tending to reddish; legs dark blackish-violet.
- In the field — Adult male all but unmistakable, yet shy and remarkably hard to see in the dappled yellow-green canopy. Flight thrush-like, strong and direct with shallow dips. Song a fluting weela-wee-ooo; call a harsh kweeaahk.

## Eurasian Goshawk
*Astur gentilis* · `norgos1`

- Jizz — 46–64 cm, 357–1200 g, wingspan 89–122 cm. Powerful raptor, short broad rounded wings, long boldly barred tail; broad hips, deep belly, very thick yellow tarsi. Female much larger.
- Plumage — Male: blue-grey above with darker crown and ear-coverts, underparts white and closely grey-barred; broad white supercilium. Female: brown-grey back and crown, coarsely barred below, fluffy white undertail-coverts. Juvenile: coarsely mottled brown above, buff to white below with drop-shaped dark brown lengthwise streaks; tail with three to five dark bars. Darker ear-coverts are the best adult character.
- Bare parts — Legs and cere yellow; iris bright yellow in juveniles, turning red-orange in adults.
- In the field — A forest bird: short wings and long tail give agility in tight cover rather than speed. Looks short-tailed, broad-hipped and pot-bellied in flight; spread tail has rounded corners.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Sparrowhawk: Much smaller with pencil-thin legs and a far longer tail with pointed corners; barred underparts in every plumage, juveniles included; male finely red-barred below.
  - European Honey-buzzard: Longer, narrower wings and a small projecting head; soars on flat wings; tail long with only two narrow bars and a broad dark subterminal band.
  - Long-legged Buzzard: Buteo build — large bill on a small head, long wings, long legs; sluggish, perching openly and upright and walking about on the ground.
  - Western Marsh Harrier: Bulky harrier with broad wings; male reddish-brown with a pale greyish-yellow head, grey tail and secondaries and black wingtips; no white supercilium.

## Eurasian Green Woodpecker
*Picus viridis* · `eugwoo2`

- Jizz — 30–36 cm, wingspan 45–51 cm, 138–201 g. Large woodpecker with a long, pointed bill; males and females the same size and weight.
- Plumage — Dark green above, pale yellowish to grey-green below, rump bright green-yellow; crown and nape red; black mask from bill to behind the eye; ear-coverts, chin and throat whitish; wings brown-black spotted yellow-white; tail green-grey, blackish-barred. Male: moustache red-centred, black-bordered. Female: moustache solid black. Juvenile: much duller, heavily dark-spotted and barred below, red of head dull and grey-flecked, upperparts white-spotted.
- Bare parts — Iris bluish-white; bill and feet lead-grey.
- In the field — Shy and wary; usually detected by loud yaffling calls. Drums rarely, a soft fast roll; noisy kyü-kyü-kyück in flight; song 10–18 klü notes, slightly accelerating and falling. Flight undulating: 3–4 beats, then a glide with wings closed.
- Similar species —
  - Gray-headed Woodpecker: Smaller, 25–26 cm; head grey with red forecrown in the male only, black loral line and narrow moustache; underparts pale grey, not yellowish-green.
  - Iberian Green Woodpecker: Little black on the head and a dusky, not black, ring round each eye; males show only a lower black border to the moustache; call sibilant, hiss-like.
  - Levaillant's Woodpecker: Crown crimson in the male, grey in the female; black moustache with a pale upper border; rump chrome yellow; primaries barred black and white; 30–33 cm.
  - White-backed Woodpecker: A pied woodpecker, not green: white wing-bars and white lower back, male red-crowned, female black-crowned; smaller, 24–26 cm; drumming very loud.

## Eurasian Griffon
*Gyps fulvus* · `eurgri1`

- Jizz — 93–122 cm, wingspan 234–270 cm. Very large vulture; very broad, long wings with long primaries, short tail, small head.
- Plumage — Sexes alike. Body, leg feathering and lesser and median coverts pale brown to light rufous with pale buff streaking, contrasting with near-uniform blackish-grey flight and tail feathers; greater coverts and tertials blackish-brown broadly edged pale brown, forming a pale band on the upperwing. Head and neck densely white-downed; ruff loose, white. Juvenile: ruff narrow, lanceolate, pale brown.
- Bare parts — Bill stout, yellowish horn to green-yellow, pale grey at the base; cere, bare parts of legs and toes grey.
- In the field — Soars with the wings raised in a shallow V.
- Similar species —
  - Bearded Vulture: Bearded vulture has long narrow wings and a long wedge-shaped tail, longer than the wing is wide; griffon is broad-winged and short-tailed.
  - Golden Eagle: Golden eagle is smaller, 66–102 cm with a 1.8–2.34 m span, and lacks the white downy head and white ruff and the pale body against blackish flight feathers.
  - Black-winged Kite: Black-winged kite is a small grey-and-white raptor with black shoulder patches, black wingtips and eye-stripe, and long falcon-like wings over a short square tail.
  - Black Kite: Black kite is brown throughout with a forked tail and a 130–155 cm span; no white head or ruff, no pale body against dark flight feathers.

## Eurasian Hobby
*Falco subbuteo* · `eurhob`

- Jizz — 29–36 cm, wingspan 70–85 cm, 140–350 g; female larger than male. Like an oversized swift: narrow, pointed, sickle-swept wings and a relatively short, narrow tail.
- Plumage — Slate-grey above with a dark crown; white cheeks and unstreaked white throat set against a strong dark face mask with a clear but short moustache — two short black stripes. Underparts whitish, boldly black-streaked; thighs and undertail-coverts unstreaked rufous, the red trousers. Sexes alike. Juvenile: much browner, scaled above, thighs and undertail-coverts buff and streaked.
- Bare parts — Cere and feet yellow.
- In the field — At any distance in flight the swift-like outline is the first impression. Distinct first-summer plumage.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Larger, 36–42 cm, span 87–104 cm; dark morph all sooty brown; light morph hobby-like but buff below, with black underwing-coverts contrasting against paler flight-feather bases.
  - Eurasian Kestrel: 32–39 cm, span 65–82 cm; light chestnut-brown above with blackish spots, buff below with narrow streaks, and a distinctively long tail; no red trousers.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Bigger and bulkier, 34–58 cm, span 74–120 cm, 330–1500 g, female up to 30% larger than male; the hobby is the slighter, shorter-tailed bird with rufous vent.
  - Merlin: 24–33 cm, span 50–73 cm; small but more robust and heavily built than other small falcons; lacks the rufous thighs and undertail-coverts.

## Eurasian Jackdaw
*Coloeus monedula* · `eurjac`

- Jizz — 33–39 cm, 174–300 g. Medium corvid; squat and compact beside crows; stout heavy bill, short legs; tail medium-length, slightly rounded; wings rounded, weakly fingered, falling just short of the tail tip at rest.
- Plumage — Shiny black; purple or blue sheen to crown, forehead and secondaries, green-blue to throat, primaries and tail; cheeks, nape and neck light grey to silvery-grey; underparts slate-grey. Sexes alike. Juvenile: duller and less sharply demarcated, head sooty black, neck sides dark grey, underparts greyish to sooty black.
- Bare parts — Bill short, stout, black, about 75% the length of the rest of the head; legs black; adult iris greyish to silvery-white, juvenile light blue.
- In the field — Subspecies separable only in adults: soemmerringii shows a broad white neck band and strongest contrast; monedula a narrower, variable pale collar and palest underparts; clinal intergrades not identifiable in field.
- Similar species —
  - Rook: Larger, 44–46 cm; head and neck all black and silky; bare whitish skin in front of the eye and around the bill base; iris dark brown.
  - Yellow-billed Chough: Glossy all-black, 37–39 cm, with short yellow bill and red legs, iris dark brown; no pale grey nape or neck.
  - Northern Nutcracker: Chocolate-brown, heavily white-spotted on face, neck, mantle and underparts; all-white vent and white tail corners; broad wings, short tail, undulating flight.
  - Eurasian Jay: Same size, 34–35 cm, but pinkish-brown body, black moustache on white throat, streaked whitish crown, white rump, blue-barred wing patch, black tail.

## Eurasian Jay
*Garrulus glandarius* · `eurjay1`

- Jizz — 32–37 cm, about 170 g; wingspan 52–58 cm. Smallish corvid, about Jackdaw-sized; wings short and blunt, tail long and rounded; bill short and stout, hooked at the tip; plumage loose and silky.
- Plumage — Body light rufous-brown to pinkish-buff. Forehead and crown whitish streaked black, raisable into a crest; white throat set off by a bold black moustachial stripe about the length of the bill. Rump, belly-centre and undertail-coverts white, the rump conspicuous against the black tail. Wing barred black and white with a bright azure panel finely barred black.
- Bare parts — Bill grey-black to black; feet grey-brown to brownish flesh with yellowish soles; iris bluish-grey with reddish rings and fine speckling.
- In the field — Flies quietly and floatingly, moving easily among the trees; on the ground clumsy and hopping. Commonest call a harsh rasping screech, at predators and as advertisement; a fine mimic, often exactly copying Buzzard mew or Goshawk cackle.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Jackdaw: Glossy black with light grey nape, neck and cheeks and slate-grey underparts; short stout black bill. No white rump, no azure wing panel.
  - Northern Nutcracker: Chocolate-brown, heavily white-spotted on face, neck, mantle and underparts; all-white vent, dark tail with white corners; broad wings, short tail, undulating flight.
  - Yellow-billed Chough: Glossy black throughout, with a short yellow bill, red legs and dark brown irises; proportionally longer tail, shorter wings, buoyant easy flight. 34–39 cm.
  - Red-billed Chough: Velvet-black, green-glossed, with a long curved red bill and red legs; larger at 38–41 cm. Juvenile bill orange, legs pink.

## Eurasian Kestrel
*Falco tinnunculus* · `eurkes`

- Jizz — 32–39 cm; wingspan 65–82 cm, male 136–252 g. Medium-small falcon, wings long and narrow at the base but fairly blunt when spread, tail long and rounded; female clearly larger.
- Plumage — Male: blue-grey head, rump and tail, the tail plain with a broad black end band; back light chestnut with small black diamond spots; underparts pale cream, lightly marked, underwing-coverts near white.
