Female: head uniformly rufous, back barred dark, tail brown with several bars and a broad end band, underside darker and more spotted. Both show a prominent black malar stripe.
- Bare parts — Cere, feet and a narrow eye-ring bright yellow, paler in juveniles; bill yellow at the base, dark towards the tip; iris and claws dark.
- In the field — Long-winged, long-tailed outline recalling a Sparrowhawk; adult's wingtips reach the tail-tip at rest, juvenile's wings shorter and rounder.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Larger and slimmer, 36–42 cm, wings and tail longer; dark morph sooty brown, light morph buff below with black underwing-coverts — never chestnut and blue-grey.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Far bigger and heavier, 34–58 cm and 330–1300 g against 136–252 g; sources give nothing else, so separate on size and bulk.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Slate-grey above with dark crown and two short moustaches, whitish and black-streaked below with unstreaked rufous trousers and vent; no chestnut back or grey tail.
  - Merlin: Smaller and stockier, 24–33 cm with wingspan 50–73 cm against 65–82 cm, more robust and heavily built; sources give little beyond size and build.

## Eurasian Linnet
*Linaria cannabina* · `eurlin1`

- Jizz — 12.5–14 cm, wingspan 21–26 cm, up to 18 g. Slim, small finch with a short neck, thin feet, a long deeply forked tail and a short conical bill.
- Plumage — Male: grey head and nape, crimson forehead and breast, chestnut back and coverts, whitish brown-streaked throat, yellowish-brown streaked underparts, grey-white streaked rump. White outer webs of the primaries make a pale patch at rest and in flight; tail brown-black, white-edged. Red duller in autumn, veiled by grey fringes. Female and juvenile: no red at all, streaked brown above and below.
- Bare parts — Bill short, conical, grey.
- In the field — Call a short repeated tett with whistles and trills; song varied, twittering and melodic, mixing chattering call-like syllables with whistles.
- Similar species —
  - Twite: Same size, 12.5–14 cm; lacks the red head patch and breast, brown streaked black above, male with a pink rump, bill yellow in winter. Nasal twaa-it call.
  - African Chaffinch: Larger, 13.8–18.5 cm; male blue-grey head, moss-green mantle, black wings with two bold white stripes, blue-grey white-sided tail, pale pinkish underparts.
  - Brambling: Larger, 14–16 cm; white rump, orange breast against a white belly, orange scapulars; breeding male black-headed with dark upperparts.
  - Citril Finch: Smaller, 12–12.5 cm; grey above with a brown-tinged streaked back, yellow underparts, yellow double wing bars and a yellow face mask.

## Eurasian Magpie
*Pica pica* · `eurmag1`

- Jizz — 44–46 cm, wingspan 48–62 cm; male 210–272 g, female 182–214 g. Graduated wedge tail 12–28 cm, over half the total length, as long as the rest of the bird and longer than the short broad wings.
- Plumage — Head, neck, breast and vent glossy black with green and violet sheen; belly, flanks and scapulars pure white; primaries mostly white on the inner webs, a conspicuous flash in the open wing; tail black glossed green and reddish purple; rump black with a variable white band. Juvenile sootier and matt, tail shorter, outer scapulars greyish.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black and fairly stout; iris dark brown. Juvenile has a pink malar region and somewhat clear eyes.
- In the field — Gloss brightest just after the autumn moult, especially in older males, and dulls by spring, almost vanishing on the outer webs of the primaries; white primary flash shows only in the open wing.
- Similar species —
  - Carrion Crow: Wholly black with a green or purple sheen, no white anywhere; broad-winged, 84–104 cm span, and short-tailed against the magpie's 48–62 cm span and long wedge tail.
  - Red-billed Chough: Velvet-black, green-glossed, with a long curved red bill and red legs; 38–41 cm on 73–90 cm wings, no white in the plumage and no long tail.
  - Hooded Crow: Ash-grey body with black head, throat, wings, tail and thighs; 48–54 cm, broad-winged, flight slow, heavy and straight; no white, no long graduated tail.
  - Yellow-billed Chough: Glossy black with a short yellow bill and red legs; 34–39 cm, 65–85 cm span, buoyant easy flight; lacks white belly and scapulars.

## Eurasian Moorhen
*Gallinula chloropus* · `commoo3`

- Jizz — 30–38 cm, wingspan 50–62 cm, 192–500 g. Mid-sized to large rail, a little smaller than a partridge; short tail carried cocked; toes long, edged with a narrow skin flange.
- Plumage — Dark overall: upperparts dark olive-brown, head, neck and underparts dark slate-grey to blackish; broad white streaks along the flanks; undertail-coverts white with a black centre. Juvenile: grey-brown above, grey-white below, chin and throat dirty white, neck-sides faintly rusty, no red shield.
- Bare parts — Bill red with yellow tip; frontal shield red, rounded top, parallel sides, reaching well up; iris red; legs yellow-green, red band above the joint.
- In the field — Swims flicking the cocked tail and nodding the head rhythmically; jerky walk, tail held high. Voice a gargling kjorrrl, sharp ki-keck and cracked kreck-kreck-kreck repeated at length, also at night in flight; hisses when threatened.
- Similar species —
  - Red-knobbed Coot: Larger (35–42 cm), all black with a white bill and frontal shield; no red on the bill, no white flank streak or white undertail sides.
  - Eurasian Coot: Rounder and bigger (36–38 cm), wholly black with a white bill and shield; lacks the red bill, white flank line and white undertail-coverts.
  - Corn Crake: Buff-and-black streaked above with chestnut wing-coverts, blue-grey face and breast, flesh-coloured bill and pale grey legs; 27–30 cm, never dark with a red shield.
  - Water Rail: Smaller (23–28 cm), olive-brown streaked black above, with a long red bill, red iris and flanks barred black and white; no frontal shield.

## Eurasian Nightjar
*Caprimulgus europaeus* · `eurnig1`

- Jizz — 24.5–28 cm, 51–101 g. Elongated, thrush-sized; large flat head, tiny bill with a very wide gape ringed by long bristles; long pointed wings, long tail, very short legs.
- Plumage — Bark-patterned grey-brown, vermiculated black with buff bars and spots; pale buff hindneck collar; white moustachial line and pale throat; underparts greyish-brown barred brown. Male: white patch across three outer primaries and white tips to the two outer tail feathers. Female: those marks rust-buff, throat cinnamon. Juvenile: as female, paler.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish; iris dark brown; legs and feet brown.
- In the field — Flight noiseless and very buoyant on long pointed wings; looks much larger and falcon-like aloft. Sexes told in flight by the male's white wing and tail flashes. Feet hardly ever visible.
- Similar species —
  - Red-necked Nightjar: Larger and longer-tailed, 30–34 cm against 24.5–28 cm; rufous neck-collar, and both sexes show the white wing spots, tail sides and throat.

## Eurasian Nuthatch
*Sitta europaea* · `eurnut2`

- Jizz — 14 cm, wingspan 22.5–27 cm, 17–28 g. Squat and big-headed with a very short neck and short square tail; bill long, straight, pointed, about as long as the head; wings broad and blunt.
- Plumage — Blue-grey above, black eye-stripe, whitish throat and cheeks; flanks and lower belly orange-red, undertail-coverts mottled white, upper tail-coverts red-brown with large white spots. Tail black but for the grey central feathers. Caesia, the widespread western form, is orange-buff below with white throat and cheeks; arctica large and pale, white-fronted, eye-stripe reduced, more white in wings and tail.
- Bare parts — Bill stout, dark grey, paler at the base of the lower mandible; iris dark brown; legs and feet pale brown or greyish.
- In the field — Climbs trunks in short leaps and never props on the tail. Flight fast and brief, wings closed between beats, the shape pointed-headed, round-winged, square-tailed. Very vocal; song a loud, penetrating series of whistles.
- Similar species —
  - Western Rock Nuthatch: A bird of bare rock and mountains, feeding on the ground and wedging food into crevices to hammer open; the sources give no plumage character to separate the two.
  - Krüper's Nuthatch: Smaller, 11.5–12.5 cm and 10–14.3 g against 14 cm and 17–28 g; blue-grey above with a black front crown in both sexes.

## Eurasian Oystercatcher
*Haematopus ostralegus* · `euroys1`

- Jizz — Wingspan 72–85 cm; length 39–45 cm across the sources, of which the bill is 6.5–9 cm. Large, compact, plover-like wader; bill long, laterally flattened and blunt, the female's averaging longer.
- Plumage — Head, breast and upperparts black, underparts white, lower back white, tail white with a broad black terminal band; in winter the black is duller and a white throat collar shows, briefly in adults, longer in immatures. Juvenile: mantle browner with pale washed feather edges, white collar, whitish flecks on throat and neck-sides.
- Bare parts — Bill orange-red to red, dark-tipped in juveniles and first-summer birds; legs pink, matt grey-pink in juveniles; eyes red.
- In the field — Unmistakable in flight: white lower back, broad white wingbars and a broad black tail band; flies straight on fast beats, almost duck-like. Noisy — shrill biik or kubik repeated, accelerating kip-kip-kip alarm, bubbling trill, slow plii-ah in display flight.

## Eurasian Penduline-Tit
*Remiz pendulinus* · `euptit1`

- Jizz — 10–12 cm, to 20 g. Very small, with a finely pointed conical bill and a relatively long tail.
- Plumage — Head light grey with a broad black mask through the eye; mantle and back chestnut-brown. Male: mask broader and blacker, back more rufous, boundary between brown mantle and grey nape sharp. Female duller, colours more diffuse. Juvenile in late summer and early autumn plain, without the mask: yellow-brown above, greyish-white below.
- In the field — Call a drawn-out, very thin, high ziiiyh falling slightly at the end; song quiet, often given from the nest entrance.

## Eurasian Pygmy-Owl
*Glaucidium passerinum* · `eupowl1`

- Jizz — 15–19 cm, 50–65 g; wingspan 32–39 cm. Tiny owl, starling-sized; small, short, flat-fronted head with no ear tufts; wings short and rounded; tail long and conspicuous. Female larger than male.
- Plumage — Greyish-brown, red-tinged, dotted white above; tail darker with five narrow whitish bars; white to grey "eyebrows", dark facial area; white half-collar on the hindneck; dark occipital marks on the nape that can look like a second face; underparts white to pale, brown-speckled and streaked. Juvenile: as adult but lacking most white spots.
- Bare parts — Bill greyish-yellow, hooked; legs and toes brownish-yellow, talons black; iris yellow.
- In the field — Feet disproportionately large. Tail often swung sideways or cocked wren-fashion. Flight noisy, fast and agile, undulating in woodpecker-like bounds. Song evenly spaced fluted whistles, carrying 500 m to 1 km.
- Similar species —
  - Little Owl: Bigger, 22–27.5 cm, flat-topped head with frowning face; brown speckled white above, underparts streaked rather than spotted; no white nape half-collar.
  - Boreal Owl: Larger, 21–26 cm, big-headed; back and wings boldly pearl-spotted white, underparts rust-streaked, crown olive-brown with small white droplets.
  - Eurasian Scops-Owl: Slightly larger, 19–21 cm; shows small ear-tufts and perches upright; grey-brown with paler face and pale shoulder line; long narrow wings, strong direct flight.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, wingspan 32–39 cm against 131–188 cm.

## Eurasian Scops-Owl
*Otus scops* · `eursco1`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm, wingspan 47–54 cm; male 75–80 g, female 90–95 g. Small owl, somewhat smaller than Little Owl; wings long and narrow. Shape shifts with the ear-tufts: flattened it looks squat and big-headed, raised slim and larger.
- Plumage — Bark-coloured, predominantly grey-brown, from grey to chestnut, with variable white flecking; face, underparts and shoulder line paler. Bold black longitudinal streaks below; white spots on back and forehead; shoulder bands white.
- Bare parts — Iris yellow-orange.
- In the field — Perches upright with small ear-tufts showing; strong direct flight on long narrow wings, reflecting its migratory habits; in flight distinctly shorter-tailed and less broad-winged than Little Owl. Call a deep whistle from both sexes, like a midwife toad.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Pygmy-Owl: Smaller, 15–19 cm; no ear-tufts, white half-collar on the hindneck, dotted back, darker tail with five narrow whitish bars, disproportionately large feet.
  - Boreal Owl: Larger, 21–26 cm; no ear-tufts, big head with a dark-framed white facial disc and startled expression; brown above with conspicuous pearly white spots, rust-streaked below.
  - Little Owl: Bigger, 22–27.5 cm; flat-topped head, no ear-tufts, frowning face, white-speckled brown above, long legs; longer-tailed and broader-winged in flight.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the candidate text: separated here on size, wingspan 47–54 cm against 131–188 cm.

## Eurasian Siskin
*Spinus spinus* · `eursis`

- Jizz — 11–13 cm, wingspan 20–23 cm, 12–18 g. Small short-tailed finch; tail short and forked; bill small, conical and sharp, slender but strong.
- Plumage — Male: black cap and chin-bib, rest of head yellow with green cheeks; back greyish-green, rump yellow; wings black with a bold yellow wing stripe; tail sides yellow, tip black; breast yellowish, whiter and streaked towards the vent. Female: olive and streaked, cap and ear-coverts greenish, bib white, rump whitish-yellow. Juvenile as female, duller.
- Bare parts — Legs and feet dark brown; eyes black.
- In the field — Flight rapid and bounding. Reaches between the scales of spruce cones and alder fruit for seeds. Male's song a hurried twittering and grating, from treetops or in flight.
- Similar species —
  - European Greenfinch: Greenfinch is bigger, 14–16 cm with a 24.5–28 cm span, mainly green with yellow only in wings and tail, bill thick and conical; no black cap or bib.
  - European Serin: Serin is dark-streaked greyish green above with a yellow rump, and heavily streaked on yellow breast and white belly; no black cap or bib.
  - Citril Finch: Citril finch is greyish above with a brown black-streaked back, unstreaked yellow underparts, yellow face mask and double yellow wing bars; no black cap.
  - Corsican Finch: Corsican finch has a brown dark-streaked back and yellow face and underparts with little green tinge; sexes alike, no black cap or bib.

## Eurasian Skylark
*Alauda arvensis* · `skylar`

- Jizz — 16–19 cm, wingspan 30–36 cm, 30–45 g. Medium-sized slender lark; rather long wings, fairly long tail, stout bill, short blunt raisable crest, long hindclaw.
- Plumage — Upperparts beige to reddish-brown, crown, back and rump finely streaked blackish-brown. Upper breast and flanks clearly dark-streaked on yellowish-white, ending abruptly against the unstreaked white belly. Broad yellowish-white supercilium ending behind the rufous, pale-rimmed ear-coverts; broad white malar, white throat. Wings dark grey-brown with a whitish trailing edge; outer tail feathers white.
- Bare parts — Bill horn-coloured, lower mandible paler at the base; legs light brown.
- In the field — Male sings a long unbroken clear bubbling warble in hovering flight at 50–100 m, two to three minutes and far longer late in the season. Flying away it shows white on tail and wing edges.
- Similar species —
  - Crested Lark: Crested lark carries a conspicuous crest at all times, is shorter and bulkier about head and body, and has light brown, not white, outer tail feathers.
  - Dupont's Lark: Dupont's lark is slimmer with a long neck, long legs and a fine slightly curved bill, and a thin pale crown stripe; song a thin ventriloquial whistle.
  - Horned Lark: Horned lark has a striking black-and-yellow (white in southern Europe) face pattern and a mostly black tail contrasting with the paler body; summer male has black horns.
  - Calandra Lark: Calandra lark is larger and more robust, 17.5–20 cm, with large black patches on the breast sides, a white supercilium and dark underwings.

## Eurasian Sparrowhawk
*Accipiter nisus* · `eurspa1`

- Jizz — Male 29–34 cm, wingspan 58–65 cm; female 35–41 cm, span 67–80 cm and up to twice his mass. Short broad wings with rounded tips, long tail, long thin legs, elongated middle toe.
- Plumage — Male: upperparts slate-grey, sometimes bluish; underparts finely barred rufous, looking plain orange at distance. Female: dark brown to greyish-brown above, barred brown below. Juvenile: warm brown above with rusty fringes, coarsely barred or spotted below, throat streaked but with no central stripe. Tail with four or five dark bars in both sexes.
- Bare parts — Iris orange in the male, yellow in the female, pale yellow in juveniles; cere and legs yellow, claws black; bill black, blue-grey at base.
- In the field — Flight a few quick beats then a glide, giving a slightly arcing course; female steadier than male. Woodland hunter, manoeuvring among trees after small fast songbirds.
- Similar species —
  - Merlin: Merlin is a falcon, more robust and heavily built than other small falcons, 24–33 cm with a 50–73 cm span; no short rounded wings and long tail.
  - Levant Sparrowhawk: Levant sparrowhawk has a shorter tail and more pointed, falcon-like wings; male's white throat is bisected by a dark central stripe. 32–38 cm.
  - Eurasian Goshawk: Goshawk is far bigger, 46–64 cm and 357–1200 g, with a sizeable bill, longer wings, shorter tail and thick toes on short robust legs.
  - Black-winged Kite: Black-winged kite is grey and white with black shoulder patches, wingtips and eye-stripe; its long wings project beyond the short square tail when perched.

## Eurasian Spoonbill
*Platalea leucorodia* · `eurspo1`

- Jizz — 80–93 cm; wingspan 120–135 cm. Large white waterbird with a long, flattened, spoon-tipped bill; neck held outstretched in flight.
- Plumage — All white with a faint yellowish cast, head washed orange-brown. Breeding: long nape crest, spread into a fan when excited, and a yellow breast patch. Non-breeders lack crest and breast patch. Immature: black tips to the primaries.
- Bare parts — Adult bill black with a yellow tip, legs black; juvenile bill and legs pink.
- In the field — Flies with the neck outstretched, unlike herons. Essentially silent; at colonies only bill-snapping, occasional deep grunting and trumpeting.
- Similar species —
  - Glossy Ibis: Dark: reddish-brown body and iridescent green-to-purple wings, with a thin decurved bill, not a white bird with a spoon; smaller, 48–66 cm.
  - African Sacred Ibis: Also white-bodied, but bald black head and neck, thick curved black bill, dark plumes on the rump and a black rear border to the wing in flight.

## Eurasian Thick-knee
*Burhinus oedicnemus* · `eutkne1`

- Jizz — 38–46 cm; wingspan 76–88 cm, 290–535 g. Compact, high-legged wader; big head, huge eyes, thickened heel joints, long wings.
- Plumage — Sandy ground colour, cryptic, with dark streaking heaviest on neck and breast; underparts pale, at times nearly white. Sexes alike; juvenile as adult; no seasonal change. In flight very pale from below, blackish primaries with white inclusions above, striking black-and-white wing markings.
- Bare parts — Bill stout, yellow with a black tip; iris large and yellow; legs strong, yellow-green, joints swollen.
- In the field — Hunch-backed in profile, flying on shallow, stiff beats. Powerful wailing, far-carrying song delivered at night, some calls recalling Curlew. Hardly confusable.

## Eurasian Three-toed Woodpecker
*Picoides tridactylus* · `ettwoo1`

- Jizz — 21–24 cm, wingspan 32–38 cm, 46–76 g. Just smaller than Great Spotted Woodpecker; hind toe wholly reduced, leaving three toes. Sexes alike in size.
- Plumage — Black and white, no red or pink anywhere; dark-looking. Dark cheeks, yellowish malar stripe, white supercilium running V-shaped into the nape, meeting the white black-barred back or ending on the black upper back in alpinus. Wing coverts black, no white shoulder patch; primaries and outer tail feathers barred, flanks white barred black. Male: crown yellow, female black-grey, juvenile yellow.
- In the field — Coniferous forest across the Palearctic, plus the Alps and Carpathians; forages on conifers for wood-boring beetle larvae and other insects, also fruit and sap; moves into insect-infested stands after fire or flooding. Call a kik or chik.
- Similar species —
  - Great Spotted Woodpecker: Look for the large white shoulder patch, scarlet lower belly and undertail and the male's crimson nape; three-toed shows no red at all and no shoulder patch.
  - Middle Spotted Woodpecker: Red crown, pink vent, no black moustachial stripe, white oval wing patches; short slender bill and rounded pale head make it look smaller, 20–22 cm.
  - Syrian Woodpecker: Glossy black above with a large white shoulder patch, white forehead, reddish belly and undertail-coverts, the male crimson-naped; bill long and slate-black; 23–25 cm.
  - White-backed Woodpecker: Largest of the spotted woodpeckers, 24–26 cm; white bars across the wings rather than spots, white lower back; male red-crowned, female black-crowned.

## Eurasian Tree Sparrow
*Passer montanus* · `eutspa`

- Jizz — 12.5–14 cm, wingspan about 21 cm, 24 g — roughly 10% smaller than House Sparrow and slimmer; bill conical and fairly stout, with obvious bristles at the gape.
- Plumage — Crown and nape wholly rich chestnut; cheeks pure white with a kidney-shaped black ear-patch; chin, throat and bill-base black; pale collar almost closed on the nape. Upperparts light brown streaked black, heaviest on mantle and scapulars; rump yellow-brown; breast and belly brown-grey; two narrow white wing-bars. Sexes identical. Juvenile: duller, crown greyer-brown, cheek and throat patches sooty grey.
- Bare parts — Bill lead-blue in summer, almost black in winter; legs pale brown.
- In the field — Contrasting face pattern makes it identifiable in every plumage. No true song; unpaired or courting males give an excited series of tschip notes, other chirps social, flight call a harsh teck.
- Similar species —
  - House Sparrow: Larger, about 16 cm; male has a grey crown and grey cheeks with no black ear-patch on white, and the sexes differ in plumage.
  - Spanish Sparrow: Larger, 15–16 cm, bill longer and stouter; male chestnut-crowned with white cheeks but underparts heavily streaked black and no black cheek-spot; female house-sparrow-like with cream supercilium.
  - Italian Sparrow: Larger, 14–16 cm; male chestnut on crown, nape and head-sides with white cheeks but no black ear-spot, upperparts bright chestnut, underparts plain pale grey, black throat bib.
  - Dead Sea Sparrow: Male has grey crown, rear neck and cheeks, pale supercilium, yellow neck-sides and only a small black bib; sexes differ; smaller, 12–13 cm.

## Eurasian Treecreeper
*Certhia familiaris* · `eurtre1`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm, 7.0–12.9 g; one of Europe's smallest birds. Bill long, fine and decurved, 12–14 mm; tail feathers long and stiff, to 5 cm, used as a prop.
- Plumage — Upperparts warm brown, intricately patterned black, buff and white, the mantle spotting white and contrasty; rump rufous; tail plain brown. Underparts clean white; belly, flanks and vent tinged buff; supercilium white. Sexes alike. Juvenile: duller above, underparts dull white with fine dark spotting on the flanks.
- In the field — Creeps up trunks after insects, propped on the stiff tail. Contact call a very quiet thin sit; also a penetrating tsree with vibrato, sometimes in series. Song srrih, srrih, twitters, a descending ripple, then a whistle falling and rising.
- Similar species —
  - Short-toed Treecreeper: Duller grey-brown above, weak off-white supercilium, dingy underparts against a white throat; slightly longer bill. Often inseparable by sight — go on voice, a repeated shrill tyt.

## Eurasian Whimbrel
*Numenius phaeopus* · `whimbr5`

- Jizz — 37–47 cm, wingspan 75–90 cm, 270–493 g. Fairly large wader, mid-sized for a curlew; legs relatively short, bill 6–9 cm, decurved with a kink rather than a smooth curve.
- Plumage — Mainly greyish brown; crown strikingly dark brown, split by a pale cream central stripe; broad dark eye-stripe, narrow dark loral streak; back, breast and flanks densely spotted. Back and rump white in phaeopus and alboaxillaris; tail grey-brown, barred; dark 'hand' to the wing.
- Bare parts — Bill long and curved, longest in adult females.
- In the field — Legs do not project beyond the tail in flight. Call a rippling whistle; song the whistle prolonged into a trill.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Curlew: Much larger, 50–60 cm against 37–47; bill far longer and evenly curved; lacks the pale central crown-stripe and heavy dark lores; legs greyish-blue.
  - Bar-tailed Godwit: Bill straight to slightly upturned, pink-based with black tip, never decurved; brick-red neck and underparts when breeding; smaller, 33–41 cm.
  - Eurasian Woodcock: Woodland bird, cryptic reddish-brown above and buff below; head barred black, not striped; bill straight, 6–8 cm; wings rounded, eyes large and high-set.
  - Black-tailed Godwit: Bill long and straight, yellowish or pink at the base; bold black-and-white wings and white rump in flight; uniform brown-grey above in winter.

## Eurasian Wigeon
*Mareca penelope* · `eurwig`

- Jizz — 41–52 cm, wingspan 75–87 cm, 500–1,073 g. Medium dabbler; short neck, fairly large round head, small bill, pointed tail.
- Plumage — Male: chestnut head with creamy-buff forehead blaze, pink-grey breast, vermiculated grey back and flanks, black stern, white belly, brilliant white forewing patch and dark green speculum. Female: brown head, vermiculated grey-brown back, rusty breast and flanks, white belly, upperwing grey with no white. Eclipse male female-like; juvenile as female.
- Bare parts — Bill small, pale grey-blue with black nail and black lower edge; male's tip black.
- In the field — Noisy: male a clear rising whistle ending in a short dry trill, female a low growling karr-karr-karr. White forewing band shows on swimming drakes.
- Similar species —
  - Gadwall: Larger, 47–58 cm; drake patterned grey with a black stern and a brilliant white speculum, no white forewing patch and no chestnut head.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: White cheeks and forehead under an iridescent green-blue cap, black nape, breast barred black and white, grey legs; drake's flanks rust.
  - Northern Shoveler: Huge spatulate bill; drake dark green-headed, white-breasted, chestnut-flanked, and the forewing is pale blue, not white.
  - Red-crested Pochard: Diving duck; drake with rounded orange head, red bill, black breast and white flanks; female pale brown with darker back and crown.

## Eurasian Woodcock
*Scolopax rusticola* · `eurwoo`

- Jizz — 33–38 cm, wingspan 56–65 cm, up to 440 g. Round, heavy-bodied wader with very short legs, rounded wings and a long straight bill of 6–8 cm; male much larger, but sexes not separable in the field.
- Plumage — Cryptic: upperparts intricately patterned reddish-brown, black and grey; underparts buff-white with a rusty wash and brown wavy barring. Head and nape barred crosswise with black, not striped as in the snipe. Primaries black-brown with red-brown and rusty cross-spots; tail black with red-brown bars and grey tips. Sexes alike in colour.
- Bare parts — Bill pale flesh with a dark rounded tip; legs grey to pinkish, feathered to the foot; eyes large and black, set high and far back.
- In the field — Shy and solitary, hidden by day and active from dusk; zigzag flight when flushed. Roding song three or four muffled croaking notes ending in a sharp explosive pissp; also a high sharp ziwitz and a drawn-out frog-like quorr.
- Similar species —
  - Bar-tailed Godwit: Long blue-grey legs and a long tapering, slightly upturned bill, pink at the base and black-tipped; brick-red neck and breast when breeding. Wingspan 70–80 cm.
  - Spotted Redshank: Smaller, 29–33 cm, with red legs and bill, black in breeding plumage and very pale in winter, and a white oval on the back in flight.
  - Eurasian Whimbrel: Greyish-brown with a white back and rump and a long downcurved bill kinked rather than smoothly curved; larger, 37–47 cm. Call a rippling whistle.
  - Great Snipe: Smaller, 26–30 cm; dark eye-stripe, pale wingbar and conspicuous white tail sides, bill only 6–7 cm, though its bulk in flight recalls a woodcock.

## Eurasian Wren
*Troglodytes troglodytes* · `winwre4`

- Jizz — Wingspan 13–17 cm. Tiny, plump, round-shaped; short neck, very short tail habitually cocked; wings short and rounded; bill proportionately long, fine, pointed, slightly decurved; legs long, claws strong, hind toe large.
- Plumage — Rufous-brown above, greyer buff-white below; fine dark barring throughout, on wings, tail and flanks too; indistinct cream supercilium ending at dark ear-coverts; dark lores; primaries dark brown with pale spots on the outer webs. Sexes alike. Juvenile: barring weaker, underparts mottled.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish-brown above, yellowish below; iris nut-brown; legs pale brown to orange-brown or flesh.
- In the field — Mouse-like in low cover; climbs trunks vertically on long toes, but cannot come down head-first. Song enormously loud for the size, long, complex, tinkling trills, heard year-round; call a sharp repeated tic-tic-tic running into a churr.
- Similar species —
  - Dunnock: Larger (13–14.5 cm), sparrow-like: brown back streaked blackish, dull grey head, plain brown underparts, tail not cocked; flicks its wings constantly.

## Eurasian Wryneck
*Jynx torquilla* · `eurwry`

- Jizz — 16–19 cm, 23–52 g. Slim, elongated; more like a small thrush than a woodpecker, with a slender pointed bill, long rounded tail and short legs.
- Plumage — Bark-patterned grey-brown, finely vermiculated rufous, buff and black; broad blackish band from crown or nape down the middle of the back, and a dark lore band running well behind the eye; throat and foreneck buff barred brown; underparts creamy white with brown arrowheads becoming spots on belly; tail grey, faintly barred. Juvenile: duller, less contrasting.
- Bare parts — Bill brown to grey, sometimes greenish-tinged; legs pale brown or pale grey; iris hazel.
- In the field — Flight undulating, wings closed to the body in the troughs. Often on the ground, unlike most woodpeckers. Raises the crown feathers into a barred crest when excited. Call a shrill repeated ty-ty-ty-ty.
- Similar species —
  - Lesser Spotted Woodpecker: Boldly barred wings and lower back, male with a crimson crown and black superciliary stripe; much smaller, 14–16.5 cm, and usually high in tall treetops.
  - Middle Spotted Woodpecker: Black above with white oval wing patches, underparts white, red crown and pink vent; larger, 20–22 cm.
  - Eurasian Three-toed Woodpecker: Black-and-white; white back barred black, black wings and rump, male crowned yellow. Larger, 21–24 cm, and a bird of conifer forest.
  - Great Spotted Woodpecker: Glossy blue-black above with big white shoulder patches and white face-sides, underparts white with scarlet vent; larger, 20–25 cm; male red-naped.

## European Bee-eater
*Merops apiaster* · `eubeat1`

- Jizz — 25–29 cm including the two elongated central tail feathers, wingspan 36–40 cm, 44–78 g (mean 52). Slender; wings sharply triangular.
- Plumage — Crown, nape, mantle and inner secondaries chestnut, wings green; throat bright yellow, cut from the turquoise belly and vent by a black line; rump and outer primaries blue-green; scapulars yellow-white; black face mask, white forehead. Female tends greener rather than gold on the shoulders. Non-breeding much duller, back blue-green, tail streamers absent; juvenile like non-breeding but plainer.
- Bare parts — Bill black, long and slightly decurved; iris red.
- In the field — Flies with the tail spread; underwing beige with ochre tones and a clear black trailing edge, broader on the secondaries. Call a trilling sound. One of Europe's least mistakable birds.
- Similar species —
  - Blue-cheeked Bee-eater: Predominantly green, not chestnut-crowned and chestnut-backed; face blue at the sides with a black eye-stripe and a yellow-and-brown throat; longer, to 31 cm plus 7 cm of streamers.

## European Golden-Plover
*Pluvialis apricaria* · `eugplo`

- Jizz — 25–29 cm, 150–220 g. Thickset, rather plump wader with a round head and compact body; fairly long-legged; wings only slightly longer than the tail.
- Plumage — Upperparts grey-brown spangled gold, buff-yellow and white. Breeding male: black throat and belly framed by a white S-shaped band running from forehead to flanks — the most distinctive feature. Female also dark below but far less contrasting; the black is highly variable, so sexing is not always possible. Northern birds blacker and more contrasting, southern paler. Underwing and axillaries white.
- In the field — Voice a mournful, monotonous drawn-out trüüüüt repeated at regular intervals; males give conspicuous song-flights in spring when claiming a breeding territory.
- Similar species —
  - Black-bellied Plover: Lacks all yellow tones, spangled black and white above with a white rump and black-barred white tail; in flight broader, less pointed wings; bill and legs black.
  - Northern Lapwing: Long crest, rounded wings, black-and-white plumage with a green-tinted back; white face against black crown, throat and breast. Larger, 28–33 cm against 25–29 cm.
  - Eurasian Dotterel: Smaller and more compact, with a striking whitish supercilium in all plumages and plain wings; summer bird has a chestnut breast over a white breast-line, and yellow legs.
  - Kentish Plover: Far smaller, around 40 g against 150–220 g; pale, with black bill and dark legs, and at most incomplete dark breast-bands — never a solid black belly.

## European Goldfinch
*Carduelis carduelis* · `eurgol1`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm, wingspan 21–25.5 cm, 14–19 g. Slim finch with short neck and thin legs; bill long, pointed, laterally compressed with a sharp ridge and no notch; tail slightly forked.
- Plumage — Red face-mask, black crown and nape, white head-sides; back pale warm brown, rump white; underparts white with buff-brown breast patches and flanks; wings black with a broad bright yellow band; tail black, white spots in the outer third. Female: mask smaller, not past the eye, lesser coverts brown not black. Juvenile: head plain, back greyer, yellow wing-band still obvious.
- Bare parts — Bill ivory, long and pointed; pure white when breeding, otherwise with a grey or black mark at the tip.
- In the field — Song a silvery tinkling medley of trills and twitters, always including the three-syllable call phrase; call a melodic tickeLIT, tick-tickeli-tickelitt. Adults moult after breeding, July into November, duller until the new feather tips wear.
- Similar species —
  - Citril Finch: Face mask yellow, not red; grey above with a brown black-streaked back, yellow underparts and double yellow wing-bars — no white head or broad single yellow band.
  - Corsican Finch: Yellow-faced and yellow below, back brown with dark streaks; 11.5–12.5 cm; lacks the red mask, black-and-white head and broad yellow wing-band.
  - African Chaffinch: Larger (13.8–18.5 cm, 21–24 g); male blue-grey-headed, green-mantled, with two bold white wing-stripes and white tail-sides; no red mask, no yellow band.
  - Brambling: Orange breast and scapulars against a white belly, white rump, breeding male black-headed; never shows a red face or a yellow wing-band.

## European Greenfinch
*Chloris chloris* · `eurgre1`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, wingspan 24.5–28 cm, about 30 g. House Sparrow-sized but compact, with strikingly heavy body, head and bill; bill thick, conical, pale horn.
- Plumage — Male grey-green above, yellow-green below, rump paler green; cheeks, neck, nape and greater coverts grey, flanks pale grey, throat dirty yellow; outer webs of the primaries yellow, making a yellow-green wing panel and a yellow edge to the closed wing, and the tail-bases yellow too. Female duller, browner above, faintly streaked throughout. Juvenile whitish below with grey streaks.
- Bare parts — Bill pale horn; legs flesh-coloured, dark sandy in female and juvenile; iris dark in all.
- In the field — Song full of trilling and twittering broken by wheezes; drawn-out knarr among the earliest spring song in the north; male performs a butterfly display flight.
- Similar species —
  - House Sparrow: Little else in the sources: separated here on shape and size, a compact full-chested bird with large round head, stout conical bill and short tail, 14–18 cm, 24–39.5 g.
  - Brambling: White rump, orange breast against a white belly, orange scapulars; breeding male black-headed with dark upperparts — no green, no yellow wing panel.
  - Common Chaffinch: Blue-grey crown and nape, brown saddle, rust-red face and breast, white wing panel and bar, white wedges in the outer tail; only the rump is olive-green.
  - Crimson-winged Finch: Sources give only this: a pale-coloured thickset finch, 13–18 cm, with a heavy dull yellowish bill; no green or yellow plumage is described.

## European Herring Gull
*Larus argentatus* · `euhgul1`

- Jizz — 55–67 cm, wingspan 125–155 cm. Large gull, buzzard-sized; bulky body, heavy clubbed bill 44–65 mm; crown peaks behind the eye for a fierce look; scapulars form a step on the back; wings project 3–6 cm past the tail at rest.
- Plumage — Sexes alike in plumage; males larger, bulkier-billed, flatter-fronted. Adult breeding: light grey mantle and upperwings; head, neck, underparts, rump and tail white; black wingtips with white mirrors. Non-breeding: brown streaking on head and neck. Juvenile and first-winter brown with darker streaks; second-winter whiter-headed with grey back; third-winter near-adult but brown in the wing; adult at four years.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow, red gonys spot confined to the lower mandible; iris sulphur-yellow, orbital ring yellow, orange or red; legs flesh-pink, yellowish in some Baltic birds.
- In the field — Little behaviour in the sources; a common large gull, roughly buzzard-sized, and one of several grey-mantled large gulls needing care.
- Similar species —
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Much larger and bulkier, 61–79 cm against 55–67 cm, with blackish rather than light grey upperwings and a far heavier bill.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Smaller, 49–64 cm against 55–67 cm; thin red orbital ring where Herring Gull shows a dark yellow one.
  - Iceland Gull: Slimmer, lighter and usually smaller than Herring Gull, 50–66 cm against 55–67 cm, with a thinner bill; the sources offer nothing else.
  - Common Gull: Noticeably smaller, 40–46 cm against 55–67 cm; narrower wings, darker eye, slimmer tapered greenish-yellow bill, unmarked when breeding, and yellow legs.

## European Honey-buzzard
*Pernis apivorus* · `euhbuz1`

- Jizz — 46–60 cm, wingspan 113–144 cm, 440–1050 g. Long-winged and long-tailed, the tail about as long as the wing is broad; long-necked with a small, pigeon-like head.
- Plumage — Adult upperparts almost uniform brown. Male has a blue-grey head, female's brown, and she is slightly larger and darker. Tail with a broad dark terminal band and two narrower dark bars — fewer than in the Buteo buzzards — the barring repeated on the wings. Highly variable: dark, intermediate, pale and rufous morphs, as juvenile and adult.
- Bare parts — Bill small, slender, blackish-grey; cere dark grey; iris yellow in adults, dark in juveniles; legs yellow, claws black.
- In the field — Soars on straight, flat wings with the tips level or slightly drooped, head protruding with a slight downward kink, chest sometimes looking angular; glides on softly bowed wings. Call a clear plaintive peee-lu.
- Similar species —
  - Western Marsh Harrier: Male cryptic reddish-brown, streaked yellowish, with a pale greyish-yellow head and shoulders and pure grey tail and secondaries against black wingtips.
  - Eurasian Goshawk: Short, broad, rounded wings and no projecting head; underparts closely dark-barred, upperparts blue-grey, white supercilium, tarsi thick and yellow.
  - Common Buzzard: Compact and distinctly round-headed with shorter wings, a fairly short tail carrying more bars, and a heavier body; bill somewhat slender.
  - Long-legged Buzzard: Large-billed on a small head, long-legged and sluggish: perches openly and upright on rocks or poles and walks about on the ground; no blue-grey head.

## European Pied Flycatcher
*Ficedula hypoleuca* · `eupfly1`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, wingspan 22–24 cm, 12–18 g. Small, rather round flycatcher; bill short and broad but pointed, typical of an aerial insectivore.
- Plumage — Male: pitch-black above, in some areas dark brown, white below, with a large white wing patch, white tail sides and a small white forehead patch. Female: pale brown above, no forehead patch, white below. Non-breeding males and juveniles have the black replaced by pale brown, and are then very hard to tell from other Ficedula.
- Bare parts — Bill black.
- In the field — Deciduous woodland, parks and gardens, favouring oak; nests in tree holes, readily in open-fronted boxes. Takes caterpillars from oak foliage as well as insects in flight, and berries. Song loud, rhythmic, melodious; call a dry whit, whit, whit.
- Similar species —
  - Collared Flycatcher: Male shows a white collar, a larger white forehead patch, a pale rump and usually a black tail. Females near-identical; the two hybridise. Both 12–13.5 cm.
  - Red-breasted Flycatcher: Smaller, 11–12 cm; brown above, grey head and orange throat on breeding males, noticeable white eyering, black tail with white bases to the outer feathers.
  - Semicollared Flycatcher: Same 12–13.5 cm, intermediate in appearance between collared and pied flycatcher; the sources give nothing further to separate it in the field.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: Large white patches on wing and forehead; breeds only in north-west Africa. The sources offer no further field character.

## European Red-rumped Swallow
*Cecropis rufula* · `rerswa8`

- Jizz — 14–19 cm, 18–21 g. Broad pointed wings and a deeply forked tail — much more forked than house martin, slightly less than barn swallow.
- Plumage — Adult: crown and upperparts blue-glossed black, wings dark above; rump, nape collar and sides of head rusty-red; face, throat, diffusely streaked breast and belly buffy, cut off sharply by black undertail-coverts and tail, so the tail looks dipped in ink; no breast band, no white tail spots. Juvenile: duller greyish above, rump whitish-buff, streamers shorter.
- In the field — Fast flier, swooping on insects on the wing. Female's tail streamers slightly shorter and broader, hard to judge in the field.
- Similar species —
  - Barn Swallow: Barn swallow has a broad dark blue breast band, rufous forehead and throat, no rusty rump or nape collar, and a line of white spots across the tail.
  - Pale Crag-Martin: Pale crag martin is light brown above and dirty white below with a pale grey throat, and a short square tail with small white patches; 12–14 cm.
  - Bank Swallow: Sand martin is small, 12–13 cm, brown above and white below with a narrow brown breast band and quick jerky flight; no rusty rump, no streamers.
  - Western House-Martin: House martin has a white rump, wholly white underparts and underwings, and a far shallower tail fork — but beware pale-rumped juvenile red-rumps at distance.

## European Robin
*Erithacus rubecula* · `eurrob1`

- Jizz — 12.5–14 cm, 16–22 g, wingspan 20–22 cm. Small and round-bodied, especially when fluffed; head looks large and often neckless; legs long and thin; tail 5.7–6.2 cm.
- Plumage — Sexes alike. Orange-red forehead, throat and breast, spreading onto head and neck sides and edged bluish-grey along neck and breast. Upperparts olive-brown, greyer by spring through wear; flanks grey to olive-brown, belly greyish-white, undertail-coverts cream. Coverts tipped rusty, giving a thin buff wing-bar, clearest on juveniles. Juvenile brown above with yellow mottling, red appearing gradually on the breast.
- Bare parts — Bill black to blackish-grey and pointed, with three or four rictal bristles each side; iris and legs dark brown.
- In the field — Fluting, warbling song through the breeding season, often given in the evening and sometimes on into the night.
- Similar species —
  - Common Nightingale: Larger, 15–17 cm; plain brown above except for the reddish tail, buff to white below — no orange face or breast.
  - European Pied Flycatcher: Breeding male black above and white below with a large white wing patch, white tail sides and a white forehead spot; browner otherwise. Similar length, 12–14 cm.
  - Red-flanked Bluetail: Blue tail and rump with orange-red flanks, white throat and greyish-white underparts; slimmer and slightly smaller; male dark blue above. Flicks its tail from a perch.
  - Whinchat: Brownish above and mottled darker, buff throat and breast, blackish tail with white bases; breeding male has a black mask ringed by a strong white supercilium and malar.

## European Roller
*Coracias garrulus* · `eurrol1`

- Jizz — 29–32 cm, wingspan 52–58 cm, 130–160 g; Jay-sized or a shade smaller. Stocky, big-headed and short-necked, crow-like in shape; bill heavy, clearly rounded, slightly hooked at the tip.
- Plumage — Head, neck and whole underparts turquoise, pale blue in sun and turquoise-green in shade; forehead and chin whitish-grey; upper back and scapulars rusty cinnamon; lower back violet-blue, uppertail-coverts ultramarine; central tail feathers dark olive-green, the rest turquoise-azure with darker bases, outermost slightly elongated and dark-edged; flight-feather ends blue-black. Female paler overall; juvenile a drabber adult.
- Bare parts — Small bare black area behind the eye.
- In the field — Unmistakable in range — the only roller in its Palearctic range. Flight strong, fast and direct, brilliant blue against blue-black wing-ends. Aerial display of twists and turns; call harsh and crow-like, a raucous series when alarmed.

## European Serin
*Serinus serinus* · `eurser1`

- Jizz — 11–12 cm, 11–13 g; wingspan about 20 cm. Tiny round short-necked finch, head proportionately large, bill stubby and blunt-conical, tail short and shallowly forked.
- Plumage — Dark-streaked greyish-green above, breast and flanks heavily streaked, rump bright yellow and contrasting; narrow yellow wing-bar. Male: lemon-yellow face, neck-sides and breast, forehead, throat and central breast unstreaked; long pale supercilium framing a dark cheek with a pale centre. Female: paler, rump greener yellow. Juvenile: no yellow, brown-green.
- Bare parts — Feet reddish to dark brown; iris brown.
- In the field — Active and often conspicuous; flocks outside the breeding season. Flight call a goldfinch-like trilling 'trillililit'; song a buzzing squeaky trill from a treetop, like glass rubbed on glass.
- Similar species —
  - Fire-fronted Serin: Same length, 11–12.5 cm; adults resemble very dark redpolls, without the Serin's streaked green upperparts and bright yellow rump. Sources give little more.
  - Island Canary: Larger and less contrasted, 10–12 cm; yellow-green head and underparts, grey-green streaked upperparts and only a dull yellow rump.
  - Corsican Finch: Brown streaked back, yellow face and underparts with little green tinge, greyish uppertail-coverts; no bright yellow rump. 11.5–12.5 cm.
  - Eurasian Siskin: Male has black cap and black bib, black wings with a bold yellow wing-stripe and yellow tail-sides tipped black; Serin shows none of these.

## European Shag
*Gulosus aristotelis* · `eursha1`

- Jizz — 68–78 cm, wingspan 95–110 cm. Medium-large cormorant, lightly built with a longish tail, narrow neck and thin bill; head peaks well forward on the forehead. Looks pot-bellied in flight.
- Plumage — Glossy greenish-black throughout, with no white anywhere; yellow throat patch. Breeding adult carries a crest of elongated crown feathers curling forward. Juvenile: more red-brown than a young cormorant, with darker underparts and paler feet.
- Bare parts — Bill dark with a yellow base, pale yellow in juveniles; bare skin round the bill yellow, black with yellow spots on the throat; feet black.
- In the field — Usually silent away from the breeding colonies; there both sexes give clicking notes, the male long series of groaning ahrk mixed with throat clicks, the young squeaky weee-ik and begging weeeu.
- Similar species —
  - Great Cormorant: Larger and heavier-built, 70–102 cm against 68–78 cm, with a stouter bill; no crest and no green sheen; juvenile paler below, and 14 tail feathers to the shag's 12.

## European Starling
*Sturnus vulgaris* · `eursta`

- Jizz — 19–23 cm, 58–101 g; wingspan 31–44 cm. Short-tailed and stocky, the wings looking triangular and pointed in flight; bill narrow, conical, sharp-tipped; legs stout.
- Plumage — Iridescent black glossed purple and green, spangled with white and buff feather tips — heavily speckled in winter, plain glossy black by spring as the tips wear away. Flight and tail feathers blackish-brown with pale fringes, secondaries broadly gloss-edged. Female less glossy, keeps her spotting better in breeding dress. Juvenile grey-brown, throat paler, faint pale supercilium, no gloss.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish in winter, yellow in summer — male's base blue-grey, female's whitish-pink; legs pinkish- to greyish-red; iris dark brown, paler in the female.
- In the field — Throat feathers of the male long and loose, used in display; the female's are shorter and pointed. Moults once a year in late summer.
- Similar species —
  - Spotless Starling: Marginally larger, 21–23 cm; darker oily black, wholly spotless in spring and only finely spotted in winter; long shaggy throat beard; legs bright pink.
  - Bohemian Waxwing: Same size but brownish-grey with a crest, black mask and throat, chestnut lower belly, grey rump and a yellow-banded tail above a broad black border.
  - Common Myna: Larger, 23–25 cm; brown body with black hooded head, bare yellow patch behind the eye, bright yellow bill and legs, white primary patch and white tail tip.
  - Rosy Starling: Adult pink-bodied with glossy black head, wings and tail, pale orange bill and legs and a wispy crest; juvenile obviously paler with a short yellow bill.

## European Stonechat
*Saxicola rubicola* · `stonec4`

- Jizz — 11.5–13 cm, 13–17 g; slightly smaller than a European Robin. Small chat with distinctively short wings, shorter than the more migratory Whinchat's.
- Plumage — Summer male: black head and upperparts, orange-red throat and breast, belly and vent white; white half-collar on the neck-sides, small white scapular patch, very small white rump patch, usually brown and dark-streaked. Female: brown above and on the head, duller, no white neck patches or white rump; only the scapular patch white, and often buffy.
- In the field — Song a squeaky or twittering short phrase, more monotonous and with smaller pitch steps than Whinchat's; alarm a sharp whistle and impure smack, 'vist track-track'.
- Similar species —
  - Whinchat: Blackish face mask almost ringed by a strong white supercilium and malar stripe; brown mottled upperparts, buff throat and breast, white bases to the outer tail feathers.
  - Siberian Stonechat: Darker above, paler below, with a white rump, larger white collar and orange restricted to the throat; female's rump pinkish-yellow and unstreaked.
  - Collared Flycatcher: Black above, white below, with white collar, large white wing patch, big white forehead patch and pale rump; never an orange breast. 12–13.5 cm.
  - Semicollared Flycatcher: Black-and-white flycatcher, 12–13.5 cm, intermediate between Collared and Pied; nothing orange on the underparts. Sources give little more.

## European Storm-Petrel
*Hydrobates pelagicus* · `bripet`

- Jizz — 14–18 cm, wingspan 37–41 cm, 20–38 g. One of the smallest seabirds; square-cut tail ending abruptly; wings narrow and pointed, swallow-like at first glance; webbed feet and tubed bill.
- Plumage — All black, greyer at the colony and browner when worn, except a snow-white rump extending onto the sides of the tail base and a broad white band along the underwing; undertail-coverts black. Fresh juveniles may show a narrow white bar on the upperwing. Sexes alike.
- In the field — Fluttering flight in strong wind; patters and 'runs' on the water snatching small crustaceans stirred to the surface. Silent at sea; at colonies a grunting call from the pair and a purring song from the male.
- Similar species —
  - Band-rumped Storm-Petrel: Larger, 19–21 cm with a 43–46 cm span; forked tail and long wings, extensive white rump; flight shearwater-like, not fluttering.
  - Leach's Storm-Petrel: Larger, 18–21 cm, span 43–48 cm; tail forked not square; white rump usually split by a dark central line; pale grey-brown bar across the secondary coverts.

## European Turtle-Dove
*Streptopelia turtur* · `eutdov`

- Jizz — 26–28 cm, wingspan 47–53 cm, 100–156 g. Small, slender dove; tail long and rounded for the body, often fanned in flight, wedge-shaped with dark centre and white borders and tips.
- Plumage — Blue-grey head, neck, flanks and rump; cinnamon wings mottled black, each feather black-centred with rufous fringe; vinaceous throat and breast; belly and undertail-coverts white; black-and-white barred patch on neck sides. From below the spread tail shows a blackish chevron on white. Juvenile: browner, duller, neck bars absent.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs and eye-rings red. Juvenile legs brown.
- In the field — Wedge tail with white tips obvious as it flies away or raises the spread tail when stooping to drink. Call a purring turr turr.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Collared-Dove: Larger, 29–33 cm, grey-buff to pinkish-grey without rufous; plain black white-edged half-collar on the nape instead of a barred neck patch; tail not wedge-shaped.
  - Oriental Turtle-Dove: Larger; breast less pink, wing feathers browner with dark centres giving a scaly look; lacks the bare skin patch round the eye; flight more relaxed and direct.
  - Laughing Dove: Pinkish-brown below with lilac-tinged head; neck sides chequered rufous and grey, not barred black and white; bluish-grey band along the wing.
  - Stock Dove: Larger and blue-grey with no rufous; two short dark wingbars and a broad dark tail-tip; iridescent green-purple neck patch, not barred; bill pink with yellow tip.

## Ferruginous Duck
*Aythya nyroca* · `ferduc`

- Jizz — 38–42 cm, wingspan 60–67 cm, about 560 g; the smallest Aythya. Small, short-bodied diving duck with a slightly long neck, peaked crown and long bill.
- Plumage — Male rich dark chestnut on head, breast and flanks, back darker; undertail-coverts brilliant white, sharply cut off against the black-brown lower back; white belly patch and white underwing show in flight, with a clear white wingbar. Female similar but duller and browner, with less contrast between back and body. Non-breeding male keeps the same pattern, only less bright.
- Bare parts — Male's iris white to yellow, female's dark brown; bill long, dark grey to blue-black with a black nail and often a paler tip.
- In the field — Voice inconspicuous: male a quiet wräijö audible only a few metres off, female a voiceless rasping rerrr rerrr, also given in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Tufted Duck: Male black with white flanks, blue-grey bill, golden eye and a drooping crest; female brown with paler flanks, sometimes white at the bill-base; no white undertail.
  - Common Pochard: Larger, 42–49 cm, plump and short-tailed; male chestnut-headed but black-breasted with a pale grey vermiculated body and a dark bill with a broad blue-grey band.
  - Greater Scaup: Bigger, 39–56 cm, 0.73–1.36 kg; male dark-headed with green-purple sheen, black breast, pale back, white underside; light blue bill, yellow eyes.
  - Ring-necked Duck: Male black-backed with white breast, angular black head and two white rings on the grey bill; female grey-brown with white eye-ring; 39–46 cm.

## Fieldfare
*Turdus pilaris* · `fieldf`

- Jizz — 22–27 cm, wingspan 39–42 cm, 80–140 g. Large, strongly built, long-tailed thrush; only slightly smaller than a blackbird but longer-winged and distinctly heavier; tail rounded.
- Plumage — Slate to ash-grey head, nape, hind neck, lower back and rump; mantle and scapulars dark chestnut-brown; tail blackish-grey. Whitish supercilium, black lores. Chin, throat and upper breast ochre-buff with bold blackish streaks; flanks with dark arrowhead spots; belly, undertail-coverts and underwing-coverts white. Sexes alike. First-winter: white tips to the upper wing-coverts.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow to yellow-orange with a dark tip; legs dark brown to dark grey; iris dark brown.
- In the field — White underwing-coverts and axillaries flash conspicuously in flight. Most calls chattering: a harsh repeated 'tsak tsak' in flight, also a thin high 'giih'; quick to scold cats and owls.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blackbird: Male all glossy black with yellow eye-ring and orange-yellow bill; female sooty-brown, weakly mottled. Neither shows a grey head and rump, chestnut mantle or white underwing.
  - Redwing: Smaller, 20–24 cm; plain brown back, no grey head or rump; red flanks and underwing — Fieldfare's underwing white — and a creamy-white supercilium.
  - Song Thrush: Smaller, 20–23.5 cm; plain brown above throughout, neatly black-spotted cream underparts, warm yellow underwing; pink legs. No grey head or chestnut mantle.
  - Mistle Thrush: Pale grey-brown above, no grey head against a chestnut mantle; round black spots on a buff breast, white tips to the outer tail; blackish bill, yellowish legs.

## Finsch's Wheatear
*Oenanthe finschii* · `finwhe1`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, wingspan 25–27 cm, 21–32 g. Medium-sized elegant wheatear with long wings and long legs; head large and bill and legs stout for the group.
- Plumage — Summer male: white crown, back and belly against black face, throat and wings; rump and tail white with an inverted black T, the terminal band evenly wide; black of the throat runs down into the wing; in flight the black flight feathers look fairly pale. Female: brown-grey above, dirty white below; wing-coverts low in contrast; tail pattern as the male.
- In the field — Call a whistled tsit; song a mix of clear, short, varied notes with whistles and crackling, electric-sounding.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: Summer male grey above, not white-crowned and white-backed, with a buff throat and black restricted to wings and face mask; 14–16.5 cm, wingspan 26–32 cm.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Same tail pattern but the terminal band narrow and uneven; black only a mask from ear-coverts to bill, throat black or white; mantle buff-tinged; smaller-headed, finer bill and legs.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Mantle buff, not white; black a mask from ear-coverts to bill, throat black or white; tail band narrow and uneven; smaller and finer-billed, 13.5–15.5 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Uniformly pale sandy isabelline, sexes alike, with a creamy over-eye streak and pale brown ear-coverts; no black face, throat or wings; 16.5 cm.

## Fire-fronted Serin
*Serinus pusillus* · `fifser1`

- Jizz — 11–12.5 cm, 9–14 g. Tiny serin with a short stubby bill and a forked tail.
- Plumage — Male: head, nape, throat and neck black, with a glowing blood-red forehead; body heavily streaked sooty-black on yellowish, rump orange- to beige-yellow; two orange-yellow wingbars from the pale covert tips. Female: forehead patch smaller, head often sooty-grey and streaked rather than black, rump streaked. Juvenile: cinnamon-brown on forehead, face and throat, upperparts more heavily streaked.
- Bare parts — Bill short and stubby, grey to black; iris dark brown to black; legs blackish to dark brown.
- In the field — High mountains; outside the breeding season in small flocks working through thistle patches. Flight bouncing. Call a rapid shrill titihihihihihi recalling Linnet; song a frenetic twitter, like a fast Goldfinch.
- Similar species —
  - European Serin: Greyish-green above with a yellow face and breast, never a black head or red forehead; song a buzzing trill, not a fast twitter.
  - Island Canary: Yellow-green head and underparts with a yellower face, upperparts grey-green, streaking confined to the sides. No black hood, no red forehead.
  - Redpoll: Brownish-grey with only a small red forehead patch above a black bib, not a wholly black head; bill yellowish with a dark tip.
  - Corsican Finch: Face and underparts yellow, back brown and streaked; no black head, no red forehead, and greyish rather than orange-yellow uppertail-coverts.

## Fulvous Whistling-Duck
*Dendrocygna bicolor* · `fuwduc`

- Jizz — 45–53 cm, wingspan 85–93 cm; male 748–1050 g, female 712–1000 g. Long-legged whistling duck, more goose-like than duck-like; head elongated, bill long and grey.
- Plumage — Head, neck and breast rich buff; back darker brown, mantle feathers buff-tipped; dark brown to black stripe down the crown centre and hind-neck to the mantle; whitish stripes on the flanks; flight feathers and tail dark brown; white crescent on the rump against the black tail. Female slightly smaller and duller; juvenile paler below and duller, especially on the flanks.
- Bare parts — Bill long and grey; legs grey.
- In the field — Wings brown above, black below, without white markings; the white rump band shows well in flight. Noisy: clear whistling kee-wee-ooo on the ground and in the air, often at night; harsh repeated kee when quarrelling; wings make a dull sound.
- Similar species —
  - White-headed Duck: Stifftail: male largely white-headed with black cap and blue bill, female grey-brown with white face and cheek stripe; smaller, 43–48 cm; no buff breast, no long legs.
  - Red-breasted Goose: A true goose, 53–56 cm and 1000–1620 g: sooty black relieved by white, with a red breast that looks dark at range; no long grey bill, no whistling.
  - Lesser White-fronted Goose: Goose with a conspicuous white face, broad black bars across the belly, bright orange legs, yellow eye-ring and mouse-coloured upper wing-coverts; larger, 53–66 cm.
  - Ross's Goose: Rounded head on a short neck, short triangular bill with a warty bluish base, deep red legs, all-white secondaries; larger, 53–66 cm against 45–53 cm.

## Gadwall
*Mareca strepera* · `gadwal`

- Jizz — 44–58 cm, wingspan 78–95 cm, 500–1,300 g. Dabbling duck, slightly smaller and slimmer than Mallard, with narrower wings; male averages a little larger than female.
- Plumage — Male finely grey-vermiculated; crown dark brown, rest of head pale brown; breast, scapulars and flanks dark grey; rear end black above and below; brilliant white speculum, black behind it, chestnut on the coverts; belly whitish. Female: brown, mallard-like, chin and throat white. Eclipse male female-like but keeps the wing pattern. Juvenile: head greyer than the warm brown body.
- Bare parts — Male's bill blackish-grey; female's short and orange-yellow with an unbroken dark ridge; legs orange-yellow to bright yellow; eyes dark.
- In the field — Pale belly and the obvious white speculum identify it in flight or at rest. Quiet except in courtship: female a high-pitched gag-ag-ag-ag, male a grunted mep and a whistle.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Drake chestnut-headed with creamy crown, pink breast, green speculum; white wing-patch confined to the forewing, not a white speculum; smaller, 41–52 cm.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: Head boldly marked — green-blue cap, white forehead, cheeks and auricular patch; breast barred black and white, flanks rusty; bill bluish-grey with black tip, legs grey.
  - Mallard: Larger and heavier, 50–65 cm; drake green-headed with white collar; female lacks the white speculum and whitish belly and shows a differently patterned bill.
  - Wood Duck: Both sexes crested; drake multicoloured with red eyes and white neck-flare, female with white eye-ring and throat; speculum iridescent blue-green, white-edged behind.

## Garden Warbler
*Sylvia borin* · `garwar1`

- Jizz — 13–14.5 cm, 16–22 g; wingspan 21–24 cm. Plain, long-winged and long-tailed; fairly sturdy build; bill short, thickish and blunt; legs strong.
- Plumage — Lack of marks is the mark: unstreaked olive-brown to grey above, dull whitish below; buff wash on throat and flanks; dirty-white rump; uniform wing-coverts; whitish eyering, faint pale supercilium; grey wash on neck sides. Sexes alike. Juvenile: looser plumage, paler and greyer above, buff below, pale fringes to flight and tertial feathers.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey to beige, grey upper mandible, paler lower, darker tip; legs bluish- to light grey; eye black.
- In the field — Shy; seldom seen, and then only briefly. Keeps to low scrub and sings hidden in dense foliage, often in the evening — a long, even babbling of purling notes, without the blackcap's fluting close.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blackcap: Look for the cap: neat black on the male, reddish-brown on the female, over a paler grey nape; greyer, silvery-white throat; no buff wash; iris reddish brown.
  - Booted Warbler: Smaller and more delicately built, chiffchaff-sized; clear pale supercilium, short flesh-coloured bill with dark tip; short-winged, rather pot-bellied, tail square-tipped.
  - Dartford Warbler: Distinctly long-tailed; male grey above with reddish underparts, white-spotted red throat and red eye; female paler below. Never plain olive-brown with buff flanks.
  - Western Orphean Warbler: Bigger at 15–16 cm; plain grey back, whitish underparts, dark grey head with black eye-mask, white throat and white iris; bill long and pointed, legs black.

## Garganey
*Spatula querquedula* · `gargan`

- Jizz — 37–41 cm, wingspan 58–69 cm, 300–440 g. Small dabbler with a fairly long bill and a tail that sticks out, giving a low, elongated profile when swimming.
- Plumage — Male: red-brown head and breast finely vermiculated white, a broad white crescent sweeping from over the eye to the nape, dark crown; loose grey scapulars, white tertial edges, pale grey-blue flanks and belly; pale blue forewing, white border. Female: vermiculated beige-grey-brown, dark cap, dark eye-line, pale lore spot; grey speculum edged white front and rear.
- Bare parts — Bill grey, fairly long, paler grey on the female; legs grey.
- In the field — Feeds mainly by skimming rather than up-ending; the female head-shakes frequently while dabbling. Male's call a dry crackling rattle, like a finger drawn down a comb; female a feeble quack.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Shoveler: Larger, 44–52 cm, with a huge spatulate bill; drake dark green-headed with white breast and chestnut flanks; female's bill still spatulate.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: Larger, 46–56 cm; white cheeks and forehead under a green-blue cap, black nape, breast barred black and white, rust flanks.
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Larger, 42–52 cm, round-headed with a small grey bill; drake chestnut-headed with creamy crown and a brilliant white forewing patch.
  - Gadwall: Larger, 47–58 cm; drake patterned grey with black stern and brilliant white speculum; female with a dark orange-edged bill and white belly.

## Glaucous Gull
*Larus hyperboreus* · `glagul`

- Jizz — 55–77 cm, wingspan 138–160 cm, 960–2700 g. Second largest gull; heavy, bull-necked, sloping forehead and fierce expression, thick bill, tertials stepped and primary projection short.
- Plumage — Very pale in every plumage, no black in wing or tail. Adult: pale grey above, otherwise white, head and neck grey-mottled in winter; primaries translucent from below. Juvenile in a dark and a pale morph: finely vermiculated grey-buff, or nearly all white. Second winter coarsely grey-blotched; grey mantle from the third winter; adult at four years.
- Bare parts — Bill thick, yellow with red gonys spot; iris pale yellow, orbital ring yellow or orange; legs pale pink. Juvenile: pink black-tipped bill, dark iris.
- In the field — Flight rather heavy, wings looking short with long arm and short hand; front-heavy in the air, round-bellied and long-necked. Call a laughing cry as in Herring Gull.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Black wingtips with white mirrors; Glaucous never shows black in wing or tail and is bulkier and thicker-billed, 55–77 cm against 55–67 cm.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Blackish back and upperwings; Glaucous uniformly pale with no black anywhere. Sizes overlap, 61–79 cm against 55–77 cm.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Dark back and smaller, 49–64 cm and 450–1300 g against 55–77 cm and 960–2700 g; Glaucous pale throughout and far thicker-billed.
  - Iceland Gull: Also pale and black-free but smaller, slighter and rounder-headed, thinner-billed, narrower-winged with a more pointed tip and lighter flight; 50–66 cm.

## Glossy Ibis
*Plegadis falcinellus* · `gloibi`

- Jizz — 48–66 cm, wingspan 80–105 cm, 485–970 g. Mid-sized ibis shaped like a big curlew: long slim neck, long legs, long decurved bill (culmen 9.7–14.4 cm).
- Plumage — Breeding adult: reddish-brown to deep purple body, wings strongly iridescent bottle-green, bronze or purple with the light; narrow white facial band above and below the bill. Non-breeding and juvenile: duller and dull brown, dark head and neck finely streaked and spotted white, juvenile paler brown below. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill brownish, grey-based, orange-tipped, pinker when not breeding; facial skin dark, bordered blue-grey to cobalt blue when breeding; legs red-brown.
- In the field — Flies with neck outstretched, unlike herons; legs project well beyond the tail. Flight curlew-like and graceful — quick mechanical beats and short glides, often in V formation or an undulating line. Quiet: croaks and grunts, a hoarse grrrr when breeding.
- Similar species —
  - African Sacred Ibis: White body with black bald head and neck and a black rear border to the wing; much larger, 65–75 cm against 48–66 cm.
  - Eurasian Spoonbill: All white with a black spatulate bill tipped yellow and a yellow breast patch; it too flies neck outstretched, so go on colour and bill shape.

## Goldcrest
*Regulus regulus* · `goldcr1`

- Jizz — 8.5–9.5 cm, wingspan 13.5–15.5 cm, 4.5–7.0 g — Europe's smallest bird. Warbler-like but round and puffed, the lengthened nape feathers blurring head into body; bill tiny and thin.
- Plumage — Olive-green to yellow-green above, buff-white to grey-white below; two whitish wingbars from pale covert tips; face plain and pale with no white stripe over the eye, the dark iris standing out. Crown stripe edged by broad black: male yellow with an orange centre, usually covered by the yellow fringing feathers, female wholly yellow. Juvenile duller, no coloured crown.
- Bare parts — Bill small, thin and black, the nostrils covered by two bristle feathers; legs dark flesh-brown; iris dark.
- In the field — Flight whirring with sudden changes of direction; feeding flights a mix of dashing and fluttering with frequent hovering; restless among foliage, creeping along branches and up and down trunks.
- Similar species —
  - Common Firecrest: Striking head: long white supercilium and black eye stripe where Goldcrest's face is plain; bronze patch on each shoulder; male's crest mainly orange.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Larger, 9.5–11 cm; greenish above, white below, with a long yellow supercilium, yellow-margined tertials and two bold wingbars, but no black-bordered crown stripe.

## Golden Eagle
*Aquila chrysaetos* · `goleag`

- Jizz — 66–102 cm, 2500–7000 g; wingspan 180–234 cm. Very large eagle, broad-winged, tail rounded; female markedly larger than male.
- Plumage — Adult uniform dark to blackish brown with a golden-brown crown and nape; upper wing-coverts paler, flight feathers nearly black; tail brown, faintly darker-banded and paler at the base. Juvenile: darker, with white panels on inner primaries and outer secondaries and a white tail cut off by a broad black terminal band. Fully adult only at five to seven years.
- Bare parts — Bill dark grey; iris dark brown; legs feathered to the very strong yellow toes.
- In the field — White tail base contrasts with black tail tip in every plumage; subadults keep a white carpal patch that shrinks with each moult.
- Similar species —
  - Spanish Eagle: Sources separate them on size only: somewhat smaller, 72–85 cm and 2500–4800 g against 66–102 cm and 2500–7000 g here.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Again size alone: a medium-sized, comparatively small-bodied eagle at 55–74 cm, span 142–175 cm, 1400–2500 g.
  - Imperial Eagle: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 68–90 cm and a 176–220 cm span against 66–102 cm and 180–234 cm.
  - Steppe Eagle: Bulky and thick-necked with a small head, strong bill and long gape-line; often on the ground or on low open perches, standing upright. 60–89 cm.

## Graceful Prinia
*Prinia gracilis* · `grapri1`

- Jizz — 10–11 cm. Tiny, slim warbler with short rounded wings and a long, strongly graduated tail; head small, round and plain; bill short.
- Plumage — Upperparts pale grey-brown, crown and back finely dark-streaked; underparts dirty white with buff flanks. No supercilium and no eye-stripe. Each tail feather carries a black subterminal band and a white tip, showing clearly from below. Winter adults brighter sandy above, streaking weaker, more buff on the sides. Sexes similar.
- Bare parts — Bill black in the breeding male, brown in the female, paler in winter.
- In the field — Active in shrubs, tall grass, tamarisks and thick undergrowth; the long tail often cocked, flight weak. Insectivorous. Call an explosive tlipp or a rolling trilled breep; song a monotonous, persistent zerlip or srliip in clearly separated short phrases.
- Similar species —
  - Delicate Prinia: Smaller, with shorter bill and legs but a longer tail; upperparts darker with broader streaks, and the song runs together into a series rather than separate phrases.
  - Zitting Cisticola: Heavily black-streaked above rather than finely; tail broad and white-tipped without the black subterminal band, and flicked rather than cocked.

## Gray Heron
*Ardea cinerea* · `graher1`

- Jizz — 85–102 cm, 1000–2080 g; wingspan 155–195 cm, standing up to 100 cm. Big, heavy heron with a long S-curved neck, long wings and very long legs, and a long dagger-shaped bill.
- Plumage — Ashy-grey above with white banding on the back and elongated pale grey scapulars; greyish-white below with some black on the flanks; white head and neck with a broad black supercilium ending in a slender dangling crest, and three rows of black streaks down the foreneck; primaries grey-black. Immature duller, head stripe grey, face paler, crest small.
- Bare parts — Bill long, straight, powerful, pinkish- to yellowish, brighter in breeding adults; iris yellow; legs very long, brown in one source, yellow in another.
- In the field — In flight the head is drawn back onto the body, giving a deep-chested profile. Call a loud croaking fraaank; harsh schaah when driving birds from the nest, soft gogogo when anxious.
- Similar species —
  - Great Egret: All-white and only slightly smaller, 131–170 cm span; yellow bill with black legs and feet, ornamental back plumes when breeding — never grey with a black head stripe.
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Much smaller, 46–56 cm, 200–600 g, 80–100 cm span; stocky and hunched with a short thick neck; white, with orange-buff crown, breast and back plumes when breeding.
  - Purple Heron: Slighter and smaller, 78–97 cm, 0.5–1.35 kg; darker reddish-brown, buffish-chestnut dark-streaked head and neck, darker grey back, crest shorter than Grey's.
  - Eurasian Bittern: Thickset, 69–81 cm on 100–130 cm wings; pale buffy-brown streaked and barred throughout, black crown and nape, tawny-buff barred head and neck — no grey, no white head.

## Gray Partridge
*Perdix perdix* · `grypar`

- Jizz — 28–32 cm; wingspan 45–48 cm; 320–455 g. Rotund, dumpy gamebird; short legs, short rounded wings, short rounded tail.
- Plumage — Orange-brown face and throat; pale grey breast finely vermiculated black; back barred rust-brown; white belly with large chestnut horseshoe; broad rufous bars on flanks; tail rufous, central feathers mottled grey. Female: duller, back barred black, breast patch small or absent. Juvenile: yellow-brown, pale shaft-streaks, lacking face and belly marks.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow, brown or grey at the base; legs pale grey, yellowish in juveniles.
- In the field — Runs fast, sits tight and hides well even in the open; flushes noisily on rounded wings, low and short, calling rick rick rick. Song a harsh high kieerr-ik, often at night. Cereal-field margins; seeds, young take insects.
- Similar species —
  - Chukar: Larger, 32–36 cm; white face ringed by a sharp black gorget, coral-red bill and red legs; no chestnut belly horseshoe.
  - Rock Ptarmigan: White wings and underparts always; wholly white in winter but for black tail-sides and eye-line; higher, barren ground. Larger, 31–36 cm.
  - Black Francolin: Male black with white cheek patch, chestnut collar and white-spotted flanks; female brown with chestnut hindneck and black throat. Larger, 33–36 cm.
  - Hazel Grouse: Crested; male shows a white-bordered black throat, underparts chestnut-flecked white, tail grey with a black tip in flight. Larger, 34–39 cm, woodland.

## Gray Wagtail
*Motacilla cinerea* · `grywag`

- Jizz — 17–20 cm. Slim; the longest-tailed European wagtail, tail 9–10.5 cm; legs long; bill 15–17 mm.
- Plumage — Male: upperparts cool grey, rump and uppertail-coverts greenish-yellow; narrow white supercilium, dark lores, broken white eye-ring; breeding throat black, edged by whitish moustachial stripes; underparts yellow, deepest on the undertail-coverts, whiter mid-flank. Tail black, yellow beneath, outer feathers white; white wing-band shows in flight. Other plumages lack the black throat.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs and feet flesh to brownish horn; iris dark brown, eye ringed white.
- In the field — Flight even and undulating. Forages singly or in pairs on meadows and shallow marshes, on rocks in water; often perches in trees. Call a hard metallic tsitsitt; song trills.
- Similar species —
  - White Wagtail: White wagtail is grey and white without yellow underparts or greenish-yellow rump, and shorter-tailed; 16.5–19 cm.
  - Western Yellow Wagtail: Yellow wagtail is olive to grey-green above, not grey, and the shortest-tailed European wagtail; smaller, 15–16 cm.
  - Citrine Wagtail: Breeding male citrine has the entire head yellow but for a black nape and white in the remiges; smaller, 15.5–17 cm.
  - Richard's Pipit: Richard's pipit is a big brown streaked pipit standing very upright, long yellow-brown legs, long dark bill; brown above, pale below, no yellow.

## Gray-headed Chickadee
*Poecile cinctus* · `gyhchi`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, 11–14.3 g. Fairly large, rather long-tailed tit, between willow tit and great tit in size; plumage loose and downy; bill small, pointed, black.
- Plumage — Cap dark grey-brown, not black, darkest next to eye and bill; throat black; cheeks and area below the eye white; mantle warm cinnamon-brown, back paler grey; flanks rusty-brown, belly whitish-grey. Wing and tail dark above but with clear pale fringes giving a paler grey cast; underwing pale. Whole pattern looks washed-out.
- Bare parts — Bill black; eye black.
- In the field — Conifer specialist; resident, most birds not migrating. Voice like willow tit's, including a drawn-out dih repeated four or five times.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Tit: Willow tit has a dull black cap running down onto the mantle and a black bib, grey-brown back and buff underparts; smaller, 11–14 cm, and neater, not fluffy.
