  - Marsh Tit: Marsh tit's cap is glossy black with a blue sheen and its bib small; upperparts greyish-brown, 11.5–12 cm and 11–12 g. No brown cap, no rusty flanks.
  - Sombre Tit: Sombre tit's dark cap spreads far onto the sides of the head, squeezing the off-white cheek to a narrow triangle above a larger bib; head often peaked.
  - Great Tit: Great tit is yellow below with a broad black mid-line stripe, black head with white cheeks, green mantle and blue-grey wing with a white bar.

## Gray-headed Woodpecker
*Picus canus* · `gyfwoo1`

- Jizz — 25–32 cm, wingspan 38–40 cm, 130–180 g — about collared-dove size. Slighter and shorter-billed than green woodpecker; tail rather long and wedge-shaped.
- Plumage — Head and nape plain light grey; male with a small red patch on the forecrown, female without but with fine black crown streaks. Narrow black line across the lores and a small black moustache like a downturned mouth-corner. Back, scapulars and coverts olive-green; rump yellow-green; underparts pale grey to grey-green; folded primaries barred brown-black on grey-white.
- Bare parts — Bill dark and very slightly upcurved; iris dark, sometimes glinting reddish depending on the light.
- In the field — Face and crown markings are small and unobtrusive — the plain-headed impression is the character. In flight looks neckless and snub-nosed.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Green Woodpecker: Bigger at 30–36 cm; red crown and nape, not just a forecrown spot; black lores and face round a white eye; broad moustache, red-centred in the male.
  - Iberian Green Woodpecker: A green-woodpecker type: little black on the head and a dusky rather than black ring round each eye; sound sibilant, hiss-like. Not grey-headed.
  - Levaillant's Woodpecker: 30–33 cm, span 45–51 cm; crimson nape, male crimson-crowned; black moustache with a pale border above; chrome-yellow rump, primaries barred black and white; bill and feet slate grey.
  - White-backed Woodpecker: 24–28 cm and pied, not green: white bars across the wings, white lower back, male red-crowned. Calls a soft kiuk; drums very loudly.

## Graylag Goose
*Anser anser* · `gragoo`

- Jizz — 74–91 cm, 2,000–4,560 g; wingspan 147–180 cm. Largest and bulkiest grey goose; rotund body, thick long neck, large head and a big clumsy bill 6–7 cm long. Male larger than female.
- Plumage — Grey-brown and paler than the other grey geese; head darker, breast and belly pale with variable black spotting; neck faintly lined by the rows of feathers. Pale grey forewing and rump obvious in flight or on a stretched wing; white line along the upper flanks, vent white. No white on the head. Juvenile browner, belly unspotted.
- Bare parts — Bill large, wholly pale, orange-pink to red-orange with a white or brown nail; legs and feet dull pink.
- In the field — In flight look for the pale head, dark back and pale steely-grey forewing, near-white against dark flight feathers; underwing leading edge pale too. Very vocal: the familiar ga-ga-ga plus a dozen more calls, individually variable.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Smaller, 60–75 cm; short bill black with a pink band, feet pink, head and neck a darker richer brown, rump and vent white; underwing all dark.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Bill black with an orange band across the middle, legs and feet bright orange; upper wing-coverts dark brown with narrow white fringes, not pale bluish-grey.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Smaller, 64–81 cm; bright orange legs, pinkish bill, conspicuous white face blaze and broad black belly bars; upper wing-coverts mouse-coloured, not bluish-grey.
  - Tundra Bean-Goose: As Taiga Bean-Goose: orange-banded black bill, orange legs, dark brown white-fringed coverts. Voice higher-pitched in the smaller races.

## Great Black-backed Gull
*Larus marinus* · `gbbgul`

- Jizz — 61–79 cm, wingspan 144–166 cm, 750–2300 g. World's largest gull; bulky, thick-necked and big-headed with a massive bill and stout legs; wings short and broad, barely projecting past the tail at rest.
- Plumage — Adult greyish-black mantle and upperwings — no other very large black-backed gull in the North Atlantic — with bold white mirrors and a white trailing edge; head, neck, underparts, rump and tail white. Juvenile boldly black-and-white chequered, not the dusky grey of Herring Gull; whitish head finely barred, white rump lightly spotted, narrow dark tail-band. Adult at four years.
- Bare parts — Bill pale yellow with a red gonys spot; iris pale yellow to amber, orbital ring red or orange; legs grey-pink in all plumages.
- In the field — Flight compact, with strong slow wingbeats; the short tail and far-outstretched head recall a white-tailed eagle. Sexes alike in plumage, but males heavy-cheeked and brutal-looking, females often only Herring Gull size.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Smaller, 55–67 cm against 61–79 cm; mantle and upperwings light grey, not blackish, and the bill far less massive.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Smaller, 49–64 cm against 61–79 cm, and adults show yellow legs where Great Black-backed Gull keeps grey-pink ones in every plumage.
  - Iceland Gull: Much slighter and thinner-billed, 50–66 cm against 61–79 cm; mantle a light grey, never the blackish upperwing of Great Black-backed Gull.
  - Common Gull: Far smaller, 40–46 cm against 61–79 cm; grey above with black wingtips, a slim tapered greenish-yellow bill and yellow legs.

## Great Bustard
*Otis tarda* · `grebus1`

- Jizz — Male about 115 cm long, 90–105 cm tall, wingspan 2.1–2.7 m, 5.8–18 kg — among the heaviest flying birds. Massive body on thick strong legs; female much smaller, to 5.3 kg.
- Plumage — Head and neck pale ash-grey; on the male the neck is white above, shading to rust-brown below. Upperparts, back and scapulars barred rust-brown and black; secondaries white with black-brown tips; underparts pale grey. Male's rust tones far more intense than female's; older males carry long whisker-like feathers from each side of the bill-base, hanging back and down; female lacks these.
- In the field — Bulk carried on correspondingly powerful legs; the male is among the largest birds of central Europe.
- Similar species —
  - Little Bustard: Far smaller, 40–45 cm, wingspan 83–91 cm, 500–900 g; breeding male grey-headed with a black white-bordered neck, brown above, white below; wings extensively white in flight.

## Great Cormorant
*Phalacrocorax carbo* · `grecor`

- Jizz — 70–102 cm, wingspan 121–160 cm. Big, nearly goose-sized; stout hook-tipped bill, longish stiff rounded tail of 14 feathers, proportionately short wings, short legs set far back, all four toes webbed.
- Plumage — Largely black; upperwing-coverts bronze with glossy black fringes, giving a scaled look, body glossed purple, wings and tail bronze to green. Breeding: broad white throat patch, white-flecked crown and nape with a loose crest, white thigh patch. Non-breeding: no thigh patch, duller, nape dark. Juvenile: brown-black with white breast.
- Bare parts — Bill stout and grey, dark at the hooked tip, paler at the base; gular skin yellow, edged white; iris deep sea-green, duller grey in juveniles.
- In the field — Perches upright, propped on the stiff tail like a third leg. Silent except at breeding colonies, where it gives guttural calls.
- Similar species —
  - European Shag: Smaller and lighter-built, 68–78 cm, bill thinner; breeding adult crested with a green-tinged metallic sheen; juvenile Shag darker below where young Cormorant is white-breasted.

## Great Crested Grebe
*Podiceps cristatus* · `grcgre1`

- Jizz — 46–51 cm, wingspan 59–73 cm, 600–1500 g. Largest Old World grebe; slim, long-bodied, neck held upright or angled back in a shallow S; long, narrow, dagger bill.
- Plumage — Summer adult unmistakable: black forehead, crown and nape with erectile black tufts, white cheeks, black lores, pale yellow between forehead and bill; elongated chestnut ear- and cheek-feathers form a black-edged ruff. Foreneck, breast and belly white; hindneck, back and rump blackish-brown; flanks pale grey-buff. Winter: whiter, plumes gone, white above the eye. Chicks boldly black-and-white striped.
- Bare parts — Bill red with a brown ridge and pale tip, paler in winter; iris dark red with a pale orange ring; legs and lobes greenish grey.
- In the field — Excellent swimmer and diver, pursuing fish underwater. In flight slim and rangy on fast whirring beats, big dark feet projecting behind. Call a loud barking rah-rah-rah, also a clicking kek and deep growls.
- Similar species —
  - Red-necked Grebe: Smaller; rusty red neck, very pale grey cheeks and throat, black cap running below the eye, black bill with a yellow base. Winter head duskier, cap ill-defined.
  - Horned Grebe: Much smaller, 31–38 cm against 46–51 cm; chestnut neck, flanks and upper chest, black crown and back, golden erectable horns, white-tipped bill.
  - Eared Grebe: Much smaller, 28–34 cm; head, neck, breast and upperparts black with an ochre fan of feathers behind the eye; eye vivid red.
  - Little Grebe: Small and dumpy with a powder-puff rear; rufous neck, cheeks and flanks and bright yellow gape in summer, dirty brownish-grey in winter.

## Great Egret
*Ardea alba* · `greegr`

- Jizz — Wingspan 131–170 cm; length 80–104 cm, standing to 1 m, 700–1,500 g. Large all-white heron, about a grey heron's length but slimmer and longer-necked; gape extends well behind the eye.
- Plumage — Wholly white. No plumes on the hindcrown; instead long, loose scapular and back plumes (aigrettes) grown for breeding, spread wheel-like in display. Sexes identical; juvenile like a non-breeding adult.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow to orange-yellow, often dark-tipped, blackening with a yellow base when breeding; lores and orbital ring greenish-yellow, emerald when breeding; legs black; iris yellow.
- In the field — Flies slowly with the neck retracted, as herons do — unlike storks, cranes, ibises and spoonbills, which fly neck extended. Walks neck extended, wings held close. Not vocal: a low hoarse croaking krrrk.
- Similar species —
  - Gray Heron: Ashy-grey above and greyish-white below, not white; white head with broad black supercilium ending in a dangling crest; bulkier, wingspan 155–195 cm.
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Much smaller, 46–56 cm, stocky and hunched, neck short and thick, bill sturdy; legs greyish-yellow; breeding birds orange-buff on crown, back and breast.
  - Purple Heron: Dark reddish-brown, never white; black crown with dangling crest, buff-chestnut dark-streaked neck; slighter, 78–97 cm, wingspan 120–152 cm.
  - Black-crowned Night Heron: Black crown and back, grey wings, white underparts, red eyes, short yellow legs; stocky and hunched, with shorter bill, legs and neck.

## Great Gray Owl
*Strix nebulosa* · `grgowl`

- Jizz — 60–84 cm, wingspan 135–160 cm, 580–1900 g. Huge grey owl with an outsized round head and the largest facial disc of any raptor; no ear tufts; long tail tapering to a rounded tip.
- Plumage — Low-contrast grey-brown. Facial disc near-circular, grey-white, up to nine concentric dark rings; two white crescents about the small yellow eyes widen beside the bill into a bold X, with a black median line above and black beard below; white collar or bow-tie beneath. Upperparts grey, dark-streaked, pale-barred; underparts paler, streaked blackish-brown. Tail five- or six-banded. Female larger and whiter below.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow; iris yellow, ringed darker.
- In the field — Call a series of very deep, rhythmic hoots; young chatter, shriek and hiss. Given a decent view, not confusable with any other owl.
- Similar species —
  - Tawny Owl: Far smaller, 37–46 cm, wingspan 81–105 cm; eyes dark brown in a plain disc with no concentric rings or white X; rufous or grey-brown.
  - Ural Owl: Smaller, 50–64 cm; facial disc plain but for a tiny V, no ringing; underparts boldly streaked without crossbars; tail long with a wedge-shaped tip.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Sources offer only size: eagle-owl far heavier, to 4.6 kg against at most 1.9 kg, though averaging slightly shorter; wingspan 131–188 cm.
  - Snowy Owl: Mostly white, not grey-brown; head relatively small with a shallow facial disc and no concentric rings; very short tufts can be raised.

## Great Gray Shrike
*Lanius excubitor* · `norshr1`

- Jizz — 22–26 cm, wingspan 30–36 cm, 48–81 g. Big-thrush or blackbird size; long tail, broad rounded wings, strong dark hooked bill about 23 mm.
- Plumage — Pearl to ash grey above, always darker than the white underparts; deep black mask from the bill through the eye, broadening on the ear-coverts, edged above by a narrow white supercilium; scapulars white; black wings with one or two white primary-base patches, secondaries white-fringed; long tail black, outer feathers white, near-white below. Female: greyer below, faintly vermiculated. Juvenile: grey-brown, vermiculated.
- Bare parts — Bill nearly black and hooked, base of the lower mandible paler; legs black-grey.
- In the field — Looks grey, black and white in flight; a perched bird always shows at least one small white wing patch. Eastern birds browner, breast often darker than the rest of the underparts.
- Similar species —
  - Red-backed Shrike: Much smaller, 16–18 cm; male reddish above with a grey head and pink-tinged underparts; female and young brown and vermiculated above and below.
  - Woodchat Shrike: 17–19 cm; black and white above with a chestnut crown and pure white underparts; western Mediterranean birds lack the big white wing patches; female and young brown, vermiculated.
  - Iberian Gray Shrike: Much darker grey above, especially crown, nape and mantle, greyish-pink below; mask wider, supercilium clearer white; white on the primaries only, none on the secondaries.
  - Lesser Gray Shrike: Black covers the forecrown as well as the mask; hind crown and back pale bluish-grey, rump paler; lower breast and belly pink; primaries white-based, secondaries not.

## Great Reed Warbler
*Acrocephalus arundinaceus* · `grrwar1`

- Jizz — 16–21 cm, 22–38 g; wingspan 25–30 cm, wing 9 cm. Thrush-sized — the largest of the reed warblers; forehead flattened, bill strong and pointed.
- Plumage — Unstreaked brown above, chin and underparts dull buffish- to yellowish-white, throat whitish; supercilium whitish and not sharply marked, but stronger than Common Reed Warbler's. Sexes identical. Juvenile: richer buff below, back more rufous, supercilium buff-brown.
- In the field — Song very loud and far-carrying: a chattering, croaking carr-carr-cree-cree-cree-jet-jet in a coarse rasping voice, alternating with whining falsetto whistles and mimicry.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Warbler: Much smaller, 11–14 g against 22–38 g; told by the male's highly imitative song and by avoiding the pure reed stands, no other Acrocephalus mimicking to that extent.
  - Sedge Warbler: Far smaller, 11.5–13 cm; back and wings streaked brown, crown streaked black, prominent whitish supercilium, warm unstreaked rump.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Smaller, 12.5–14 cm against 16–21 cm, and with a weaker supercilium; otherwise the same unstreaked brown above and buff below. Song a slow chattering jit-jit-jit.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Smaller, 12–14 cm, slim, plain olive-brown with short rounded wings and a long bill; short whitish supercilium only between bill and eye, dusky eye-stripe.

## Great Shearwater
*Ardenna gravis* · `greshe`

- Jizz — 43–51 cm, wingspan 105–122 cm. Large shearwater, slightly smaller than Cory's Shearwater.
- Plumage — Dark brown above with a white nape band and a white horseshoe of uppertail-coverts; blackish-brown cap sharply cut from the white underparts; dark neck-side patch. Underwing white, dark-edged, with dark marks on the secondary coverts; dingy dark belly patch and undertail-coverts. Moult in July–August gives white bars across the upperwing. Juvenile greyer, paler-fringed.
- Bare parts — Bill black and relatively slender; feet pink.
- In the field — Powerful, purposeful flight; typical shearing, canting side to side on stiff wings with few rapid beats, wingtips almost dipping the water. Gregarious; follows boats and trawlers, noisy and aggressive; a piercing 'eeyah' from rafts.
- Similar species —
  - Sooty Shearwater: Wholly dark chocolate-brown with a silvery underwing stripe; no white underparts, no black cap, no white tail horseshoe.
  - Northern Fulmar: Grey and white with a short thick yellowish bill and no black wingtips; lacks the dark cap, belly patch and white horseshoe, and flies stiff and straight.
  - Scopoli's Shearwater: Brownish-grey above with a pale yellow dark-tipped bill; underparts wholly white — no black cap, belly patch or white horseshoe; larger, 45–52 cm.
  - Cory's Shearwater: Larger, 45–56 cm; lacks the brown belly patch, dark shoulder markings and black cap, and shows a yellowish bill against Great Shearwater's black one.

## Great Skua
*Stercorarius skua* · `gresku1`

- Jizz — 50–58 cm, wingspan 125–140 cm, 1170–1500 g. Herring Gull size, heavy and barrel-chested; short blunt tail with the two central feathers slightly elongated and rounded.
- Plumage — Adult streaked greyish-brown with rusty-buff shaft-streaks above and a black cap; underparts dull grey-toned; wings blackish-brown with a bold white flash; tail black-brown. Juvenile warmer brown and unstreaked below.
- Bare parts — Bill and feet almost black.
- In the field — White wing flashes, gull bulk and barrel chest carry at long range; sometimes suggests a common buzzard. Powerful flight. Silent away from the colony; there a nasal repeated 'gock' and a squeezed 'tjirr' in display, otherwise a harsh hah-hah-hah-hah.
- Similar species —
  - Parasitic Jaeger: Much smaller and slighter, 37–48 cm; light morphs white below with a black cap and yellowish-white neck, and a pointed central tail projection.
  - Long-tailed Jaeger: Smaller, grey-backed with a black cap and a very long tail, and dark primaries without a white flash; slimmer, longer-winged and more tern-like.
  - Pomarine Jaeger: Smaller, 42–67 cm including a 10 cm tail streamer; bulky and broad-winged but only larger than a common gull, not Herring Gull size; flight more measured.

## Great Snipe
*Gallinago media* · `gresni1`

- Jizz — 26–30 cm, wingspan 42–50 cm, 150–260 g. Bulky, heavily built snipe, the bulk most obvious in flight, more woodcock-like than Common Snipe; bill stout but short, 6–7 cm; wings broad.
- Plumage — Mottled brown above, underparts paler and barred right across, with no clean white belly; dark stripe from the bill base through the eye. Greater and median coverts white-tipped, making two wing bars; outer tail feathers white, showing as broad white triangles at the tail sides. Juvenile: less white on the tail, wing bars more diffuse. Sexes alike, female averaging larger.
- In the field — Pale wingbar shows in flight; the tail is fanned to flash the white sides in display. Display at dusk like bouncing ping-pong balls running into twittering, carrying 200 m and more; otherwise a faint yeah.
- Similar species —
  - Common Snipe: Belly white and unbarred; slimmer, with a longer bill, pointed wings and a single pale wingbar; smaller and much lighter, 25–27 cm, 80–140 g.
  - Common Redshank: A shank: marbled brown with red legs and a black-tipped red bill, white up the back and on the wings in flight; no snipe head stripes; 24–30 cm.
  - Spotted Redshank: Black in breeding plumage and very pale in winter, with long red legs, a long bill and a white oval on the back in flight; 29–31 cm.
  - Red Knot: Short dark legs and a straight dark bill no longer than the head; plain pale grey in winter, cinnamon face and breast in summer; 23–26 cm.

## Great Spotted Cuckoo
*Clamator glandarius* · `grscuc1`

- Jizz — 35–39 cm; wingspan 58–66 cm; 140–170 g. Slender, long-tailed, broad blunt-winged; tail graduated; looks much larger than a Common Cuckoo. Strong legs.
- Plumage — Dark grey above spotted white on tertials and coverts; silver-grey crest, crown and ear-coverts; white tips to flight feathers and graduated tail; face and upper breast yellowish, underparts white. Sexes alike. Juvenile: blackish above with black cap and chestnut primaries, turning sepia after the first moult.
- Bare parts — Bill black, grey at the base; legs grey; eye-ring bright orange-red at all ages.
- In the field — Magpie-like flight on broad blunt wings. Unlike Common Cuckoo hops freely on the ground with tail cocked. Warm open country with trees; takes insects, spiders, small reptiles, hairy caterpillars. Call loud cher-cher-kri-kri.
- Similar species —
  - Common Cuckoo: Smaller, 32–34 cm; plain slate-grey without white spotting or crest; sparrowhawk-like in flight on narrower wings, short-legged, and does not hop on the ground.

## Great Spotted Woodpecker
*Dendrocopos major* · `grswoo`

- Jizz — 20–25 cm, wingspan 34–39 cm, 70–98 g. Sturdy pied woodpecker; bill broad-based and chisel-like, tail stiff and used as a prop, feet with two toes forward and two back, claws sharp and curved.
- Plumage — Glossy blue-black above with a large white oval shoulder patch each side; face and neck-sides white; black malar stripe and a black line from shoulder to nape reaching half across the breast; flight feathers and tail barred black and white; underparts white with scarlet lower belly and vent. Male: crimson nape patch. Female: none. Juvenile: red crown, belly pink.
- Bare parts — Bill slate-black; legs greenish-grey; eye deep red.
- In the field — Climbs upright, braced on the tail and held steady by strong tail muscles; thick skin against insect bites, feathered nostrils against wood dust, sprung bill base absorbing the shock of chiselling. Flies in clear undulating bounds.
- Similar species —
  - White-backed Woodpecker: Larger (24–26 cm, span 38–40 cm), with white bars across the wings in place of the oval shoulder patches and a white lower back; male red-crowned, female black-crowned.
  - Syrian Woodpecker: Very similar; white on the forehead, wing spots forming three bars, outer tail feathers with only a few white spots rather than boldly barred; underparts buffish-white.
  - Eurasian Three-toed Woodpecker: 21–22 cm, black and white with no red at all, male yellow-crowned; back white barred black, flanks barred; no white shoulder ovals, no scarlet vent.
  - Middle Spotted Woodpecker: Red crown at all ages, no black moustachial stripe, vent pink not scarlet, flanks dark-streaked; looks smaller, with a short slender bill and rounded pale head.

## Great Tit
*Parus major* · `gretit1`

- Jizz — 12.5–15 cm, 14–22 g. Large, robust tit — the largest in Europe; bill fairly stout, conical, about twice as long as deep; claws curved and sharp.
- Plumage — Male: crown, nape, throat and bib glossy blue-black; cheeks and ear-coverts pure white, cleanly framed by black; breast lemon-yellow split by a broad black mid-line from bib to vent, widening between the legs. Mantle olive-green, wings blue-grey with one white bar, tail blue-grey with white outer tips. Female: duller, stripe narrower, sometimes broken. Juvenile: nape olive-brown, rump grey.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish horn with paler edges; iris bright reddish-brown to blackish-brown; legs and feet blue-grey to slate.
- In the field — Sexes separable in the field by the black belly stripe: broad and complete on the male, narrow and often broken on the female.
- Similar species —
  - Sombre Tit: Slightly smaller, similar proportions; brownish to grey-black cap spreading onto the head sides, large bib reducing the off-white cheek to a narrow triangle; no yellow, no belly stripe.
  - Gray-headed Chickadee: Same length but lighter, 11–14.3 g; dark brown head, cinnamon-brown mantle, whitish underparts washed rusty — no yellow breast, no black mid-line.
  - Willow Tit: Smaller, 11.5 cm and about 11 g; dull black cap onto the mantle, black bib, grey-brown back, buff underparts; no yellow and no belly stripe.
  - Eurasian Blue Tit: Smaller, 10.5–12 cm; blue crown and white cheeks ringed by a dark blue line through the eye; lacks the black central belly stripe.

## Great White Pelican
*Pelecanus onocrotalus* · `grwpel1`

- Jizz — 140–180 cm, wingspan 226–360 cm; male about 175 cm and 9–15 kg, female about 148 cm and only 5.4–9 kg. Huge pelican with an enormous bill, 28.9–47.1 cm.
- Plumage — White throughout, flushed pink when freshly moulted in October, the flush soon fading. At the start of breeding the crown feathers lengthen into a crest up to 14 cm, shorter in the female, its base tapering wedge-like onto the forehead; the eye region is bare.
- Bare parts — Bill pink and yellow, bluish grey on culmen and base, nail red; pouch pale to yolk-yellow, chrome-yellow before breeding; iris red- to dark brown.
- In the field — Noticeably larger than a mute swan. Bare whitish, pink or flesh-coloured skin round the eye gives a clownish face; a frontal knob grows at the start of breeding.
- Similar species —
  - Dalmatian Pelican: Larger on average, 160–180 cm with a 270–345 cm span, and a huge bill of 37–45 cm; great white's bill is 28.9–47.1 cm over a pale yellow pouch.

## Greater Flamingo
*Phoenicopterus roseus* · `grefla3`

- Jizz — 120–150 cm, 2–4 kg; largest flamingo. Legs alone 40–50 cm. Male averages larger and heavier than female (wing 43 cm, 2.7 kg against 40 cm, 2.1 kg).
- Plumage — Adult mostly pinkish-white; wing-coverts red, primaries and secondaries black. Subadult paler with dark legs; adults feeding chicks also pale but keeping bright pink legs. Chick in short dense grey down, light grey above and whitish below, lores bare; a second, darker grey down at about four weeks. Colour comes from carotenoids in the food.
- Bare parts — Bill pink with a sharply defined, restricted black tip; legs entirely pink; orbital ring pink.
- In the field — Call a goose-like honking, with a trumpeting recalling Greylag or Bean Goose. Feeds on small water animals, shrimps and plankton, on wide shallow muddy shores of salt lakes and sea bays; breeds in large colonies.
- Similar species —
  - Chilean Flamingo: Slightly smaller and pinker, with greyish legs showing pink joints, and more than half the bill black against Greater's restricted black tip.

## Greater Scaup
*Aythya marila* · `gresca`

- Jizz — 39–56 cm, wingspan 71–84 cm, 900–1250 g. Medium-sized diving duck with a large rounded head and no crest; bill long and broad with a small black nail.
- Plumage — Male: head black glossed green, breast and tail black; back pale silver-grey vermiculated, darkening rearwards; flanks and belly bright white; white wing-stripe from the speculum along the flight feathers. Eclipse duller, mottled. Female: dull cinnamon-brown, darker-backed, flanks vermiculated, broad white band round the bill base, often a pale cheek patch. Juvenile like female, less white at the bill base.
- Bare parts — Bill light blue to lead-grey with a small black nail; iris bright yellow, brown-yellow in juveniles; legs and feet grey.
- In the field — Very rarely seen on land. Male mostly silent, murmuring high notes in display and sometimes an eider-like pyoh; female's grating call long, deep and hard.
- Similar species —
  - Tufted Duck: Drake has a drooping crest and a black back, not pale grey; female tufted, brown-backed, with at most a narrow white surround to the bill; bill shorter.
  - Common Pochard: Drake chestnut-headed with a sloping forehead, body pale grey throughout with no white flank block; bill dark grey with a broad blue-grey band.
  - Ferruginous Duck: Dark chestnut head, breast and flanks with sharply white undertail-coverts and white belly and underwing patch in flight; no pale grey back.
  - Ring-necked Duck: Angular peaked head and black back, not pale grey; grey bill with two white rings; a white line on the wing rather than a long white stripe.

## Greater Short-toed Lark
*Calandrella brachydactyla* · `gstlar1`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm. Small, pale lark, smaller than Skylark; bill short, conical and pointed; tertials very long, barely leaving the primary tips exposed.
- Plumage — Sandy to greyish-brown above, finely dark-streaked on crown and upper mantle, rump plain sandy; crown rufous on some western birds. Broad dirty-white supercilium bordered below by a fine dark eye-stripe, cheeks and ear-coverts plain grey-brown. Dark patch each side of the neck. Two clear wingbars from pale-tipped coverts. Underparts unstreaked white. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill pinkish with a grey culmen.
- In the field — Winter flocks large and compact, wheeling in unison; flies to favoured drinking spots in the morning. Song a dry stuttering twitter accelerating at the end, or a long ecstatic flight-song with mimicry; call a dry dritt.
- Similar species —
  - Mediterranean Short-toed Lark: Duller, with fine streaking across the breast and no dark neck patches; bill short and stubby, head shape differs; smaller, 13–14 cm.
  - Calandra Lark: Much larger and more robust, 17.5–20 cm, with big black patches on the breast sides; in flight short broad wings dark below and a white-edged tail.
  - Crested Lark: Bigger, 17–18 cm and bulkier about head and body, with a crest conspicuous at all times and light brown outer tail feathers.
  - Dupont's Lark: Larger, 17–18 cm; slim and long-necked with long legs, a fine slightly curved bill, a thin pale crown stripe and a dark-streaked breast.

## Greater Spotted Eagle
*Clanga clanga* · `grseag1`

- Jizz — 59–74 cm, wingspan 155–179 cm; male 1.7–1.9 kg, female 1.8–2.5 kg. Large compact eagle; short neck, big shaggy-naped head, strong beak, short gape-line; wings broad and long, reaching the tail tip; tail short and rounded, near vulture-like in silhouette.
- Plumage — Adult almost uniform dark to blackish brown, sometimes purplish and glossy; upperwing-coverts often a shade paler; two contrasts only — a pale beak and a narrow white U above the tail, usually hidden at rest; flight and tail feathers slightly paler brown-grey. Rare pale fulvescens morph: head, body and all coverts pale beige to gold. Juvenile white-spotted on the wings.
- Bare parts — Beak pale yellow, contrasting.
- In the field — Perches in the open, on treetops at a forest edge or on an isolated bush, pole or steep riverbank; often forages on the ground and rests there hunched. Primaries strongly fingered. At times very vocal, a dog-like yip.
- Similar species —
  - Lesser Spotted Eagle: Head and wing-coverts pale brown against darker plumage; clean white rump V and a white upperwing patch; juvenile lacks covert spots but shows a cream neck patch; 55–67 cm.
  - Imperial Eagle: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 68–90 cm and wingspan 176–220 cm against 59–74 cm and 155–179 cm.
  - Steppe Eagle: Longish very thick neck, small head with a long gape-line — this bird's is short — and dishevelled leg feathering; perches upright, walks horizontally on the ground; 60–89 cm.
  - Red Kite: Rufous body and coverts, long forked tail twisting in flight, wings held in a dihedral, white primary patches against black tips; 60–70 cm, wingspan 175–195 cm.

## Greater White-fronted Goose
*Anser albifrons* · `gwfgoo`

- Jizz — 64–81 cm, wingspan 130–165 cm, 1.93–3.31 kg. Medium-sized grey goose, smaller than Greylag; males typically larger, sexes alike in plumage.
- Plumage — Greyish brown, paler below; light grey breast dappled with dark brown to black blotches and bars; white undertail-coverts and a broad white band on the outer tail; upper wing-coverts mouse-grey, flight feathers dark. Adult has a white blaze from bill-base to forehead, not reaching the crown. Juvenile: no blaze, no belly bars, black bill-nail.
- Bare parts — Bill pink in European birds, yellow-orange in Greenland flavirostris; legs bright orange.
- In the field — Voice higher and more musical than Greylag or Taiga Bean Goose: a two- or three-syllable kjy-jy or kjo-jo-jo, and a high cackling he-he broken distinctly between the notes.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Bill short, black with a bright pink mid-band; feet pink, not orange; head and neck richer dark brown; forewing pale bluish-grey; no white blaze, no belly bars.
  - Graylag Goose: Larger and bulkier, 74–91 cm, big head and heavy orange or pink bill, pink legs; no white blaze and no black belly bars; calls deeper.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Larger, 65–90 cm; head and neck dark brown with no white blaze; bill black with an orange mid-band; wing-coverts dark brown, white-fringed; loud honking.
  - Tundra Bean-Goose: Bill black with an orange band, no white blaze; wing-coverts dark brown with white fringes; larger, 66–90 cm; honking, higher-pitched in the smaller races.

## Greater Whitethroat
*Curruca communis* · `grewhi1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, 12–17 g. One of the larger Curruca warblers; tail relatively long for the genus.
- Plumage — Male: grey head, white throat, pink-tinged breast, broad white eye-ring. Female: head tinged brown, breast pale buff, throat duller; first-winter birds like the female. Both grey-brown above and whitish below with light brown flanks; wing and covert feathers black with broad rufous fringes; tail grey-brown with white outer edges.
- Bare parts — Legs yellow-brown to buff; iris red-ochre in the male, brown in the female.
- In the field — Song a short brisk scratchy phrase in a rough scolding voice, eager and jerky in rhythm; longer and more chattering in song flight. Call wed-wed or void-void; alarm a long rough tschehr.
- Similar species —
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Lesser whitethroat is slightly smaller and grey-backed, with a darker bandit mask through the eye and no chestnut in the wings; song a fast rattle.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Sardinian male has a black head and red eyes above the white throat; female brown above with a grey head. Neither shows rufous wing fringes.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Subalpine male has grey head and back, brick-red underparts and white malar stripes; female brown above, whitish below with a pink flush.
  - Dartford Warbler: Dartford warbler is dark and notably long-tailed, 13 cm; male grey above with reddish, white-spotted throat and underparts and a red eye.

## Green Sandpiper
*Tringa ochropus* · `grnsan`

- Jizz — 21–24 cm, 50–80 g. Plump, thickset wader; short bill, rather short legs — toes barely project beyond the tail in flight. Slightly larger and heavier-bodied than Wood Sandpiper.
- Plumage — Upperparts dark greenish-brown spotted white, boldest on breeding adults and faint in winter and on young birds; head and breast grey-brown, finely streaked, cut off sharply from the white underparts. Broad white tail base, tail tip barred black.
- Bare parts — Legs and short bill dark green.
- In the field — Conspicuous in flight: wings dark above and below against a brilliant white rump, underwing dark. Call a three-note whistle, plyitt-vitt-vitt; alarm tlipp-tlipp-tlipp; song in display flight tluii-tyi tluii-tyi.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Sandpiper: Slighter and longer-legged with a brown back, finer bill and longer yellowish legs; rump patch smaller and far less contrasting.
  - Common Greenshank: Larger, grey-brown with long greenish legs and a long grey-based bill looking upturned; shows a white wedge up the back. Call teu-teu-teu.
  - Common Redshank: Red legs and a black-tipped red bill; marbled brown, with white up the back and on the wings, not just a white rump. 24–30 cm.
  - Spotted Redshank: Larger, 29–31 cm with a 61–67 cm span; red legs and bill, black in breeding plumage, very pale in winter, white oval on the back. Call a creaking teu-it.

## Green Warbler
*Phylloscopus nitidus* · `grnwar1`

- Jizz — 10–11 cm. Small leaf warbler, brighter and greener than its relatives.
- Plumage — Moss-green above, whitish below, with pale yellow cheeks, throat and forebreast and a clear yellow supercilium running to the bill base; one strong pale wing bar and often a short, fainter second, best marked on young birds.
- Bare parts — Lower mandible pale orange.
- In the field — Call loud, fast and three-syllabled, chi-zu-wi; song rapid sharp trills carrying one to three dry rolling tsi-tserr notes in almost every phrase.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Greenish-brown above with plain wings and no wing bar, off-white to yellowish below; larger at 11–12.5 cm; call a disyllabic hoo-eet.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Dull brown-washed green above with plain wings and a short whitish, not yellow, supercilium; dark legs, short primary projection.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Two bold wing-bars, yellow-edged tertials and a very long yellow supercilium; smaller at 9.5–11 cm.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyer-green above and much less yellow below, with one wing bar only; song lacks the dry rolling notes.

## Green-winged Teal
*Anas crecca* · `gnwtea`

- Jizz — 34–43 cm, wingspan 53–59 cm, 250–400 g. One of the smallest dabbling ducks; small, short-billed and compact, with short legs.
- Plumage — Male: chestnut head with a broad iridescent green crescent running from before the eye to the nape, thinly edged creamy-white; buff breast spotted brown; finely vermiculated grey body, white belly, white scapular side-stripe; butter-yellow triangle each side of the black stern. Female: dark brown above with rufous feather edges, buff-brown below with dark spots. Speculum green above, black below.
- Bare parts — Bill sides orange to greenish in both sexes.
- In the field — Fast, tight flocks can recall waders despite the short legs; quick and nimble on land compared with other ducks. Male repeats a characteristic whistled kriick right through spring.
- Similar species —
  - Mallard: Far larger, 50–65 cm and heavily built; drake bottle-green headed with white collar and brown breast; speculum purple-blue, not green.
  - Northern Pintail: Much larger, 59–76 cm, slender and elongated with a long neck and long tail; drake chocolate-headed with a white stripe up the neck-side.
  - Marbled Duck: 39–42 cm, pale sandy-brown blotched off-white, with a dark eye-patch and shaggy head; wings pale and unpatterned, no speculum.
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Larger, 42–52 cm; drake chestnut-headed with creamy crown, pink breast and a brilliant white forewing patch; female plainer light brown.

## Greenish Warbler
*Phylloscopus trochiloides* · `grewar3`

- Jizz — 10–11 cm, 15–21 cm span, about 8 g. Small, compact leaf warbler with a relatively large head and a thin bill.
- Plumage — Upperparts grey-green to olive-green, underparts grey-white to off-white; long pale to yellow supercilium reaching almost to the hindcrown, often broadened and upturned at the rear; dark eye-stripe, not reaching the bill base; one narrow white wing-bar, often barely visible. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Legs brown; lower mandible paler than the upper, with no dark tip.
- In the field — Lowland deciduous or mixed forest; insectivorous, nests on the ground in low shrub. Song high, jerky and penetrating, sometimes ending in a Wren- or Coal Tit-like trill; call disyllabic, t'slii or zli-wit.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Plain greenish-brown wings with no wing-bar; pale pinkish-yellow legs, yellower below; song a simple descending whistle, call hoo-eet. 11–12.5 cm.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Dumpy, brown-washed dull green with only a short whitish supercilium and no wing-bar; dark legs, short primary projection, call a monosyllabic hweet. 10–12 cm.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Prominent double wing-bars, yellow-margined tertials and a long yellow supercilium; smaller still at 9.5–11 cm, 4–9 g, and almost constantly in motion.
  - Western Bonelli's Warbler: Plain grey-green back with a green-toned rump and wings and whitish underparts; small pointed bill, brown legs, and no wing-bar described. 10.5–12 cm.

## Gull-billed Tern
*Gelochelidon nilotica* · `gubter2`

- Jizz — 33–42 cm, wingspan 76–91 cm, 150–292 g. Large powerful tern, Sandwich Tern-sized; short thick gull-like bill set high on the head, broad wings, long legs, robust body; tail barely forked, wing-tips projecting well past it at rest.
- Plumage — Summer adult: black cap from forehead to nape, pale grey upperparts to the tail, white underparts. Winter: cap lost, forehead white, back a whiter grey, and a dark patch through the eye. Juvenile: much like the winter adult but with a fainter mask and often brown-fringed feathers.
- Bare parts — Bill black, short and thick; legs black.
- In the field — Call a characteristic ker-wik.
- Similar species —
  - Sandwich Tern: Bill thin and sharp with a yellow tip, not short, thick and wholly black; slimmer, 36–41 cm; juveniles are short-billed and the usual cause of error.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: A huge gull, 61–79 cm with 1.44–1.66 m wingspan; dark back, no black cap, no forked tail.
  - Glaucous Gull: Large, very pale gull, 55–77 cm, thick yellow bill, no black at all in wingtips or tail; never capped.
  - Pallas's Gull: Very large gull, 55–72 cm, wingspan 1.4–1.7 m; summer adult wears a full black hood, not a cap ending at the nape.

## Gyrfalcon
*Falco rusticolus* · `gyrfal`

- Jizz — 48–63 cm, wingspan 105–131 cm, 805–1350 g. Largest falcon; broad-based wings with rather blunt tips, long broad tail, heavy build. Female clearly larger.
- Plumage — Polymorphic: white, silver, brown and black morphs. Grey birds grey to grey-brown above with ash-grey barring, white below finely spotted, flanks and trousers barred. Brown morph cream-streaked on crown and nape. Juvenile: cold dark brown-grey above, coarsely streaked below. Northern birds paler; Greenland birds white with dark spots.
- Bare parts — Cere and feet yellow in adults, blue-grey in juveniles; iris dark brown in a yellow eye-ring; bill blue-grey with a darker tip.
- In the field — In flight the arm is long and broad-based, the hand full and blunter-tipped than other falcons; tail long and broad.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Half the size, slim, with long pointed wings; dark morph all sooty brown, light morph buff below with black underwing-coverts; 36–42 cm against 48–63.
  - Eurasian Kestrel: Far smaller, span 65–82 cm against 105–131; light chestnut above with blackish spots, buff and narrowly streaked below; long tail.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Smaller, narrower-winged and shorter-tailed; brown-morph Gyr shows cream streaking on crown and nape where Peregrine is plain dark-hooded.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Much smaller; slate-grey above with two short black moustaches, white unstreaked throat, black-streaked underparts and rufous thighs and vent.

## Harlequin Duck
*Histrionicus histrionicus* · `harduc`

- Jizz — 38–45 cm, wingspan 60–70 cm, 550–750 g. Small compact duck; short small bill, high forehead, rounded head, thick short neck, wedge-pointed tail — shape alone identifies it.
- Plumage — Male: slate-blue head and neck with a large white crescent before the eye running to the crest, a round white dot behind it and an oval white streak down the neck-side; black crown stripe with chestnut sides; white black-bordered collar and breast bar; chestnut flanks; white inner secondaries over the folded wing. Female: brown-grey with three white head patches.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey; eye reddish.
- In the field — Eclipse male mostly grey-brown but keeps the white head marks; first-year male already adult-patterned, less intense. Mostly silent; displaying male a nasal squeaky vii-ah vii-ah, or vih.
- Similar species —
  - Common Scoter: Bulkier and much larger-billed; drake wholly black with a yellow bill patch, female plain brown with pale cheeks and no white head marks.
  - Common Eider: Much larger, 50–71 cm, with a long wedge-shaped bill; drake white-backed and black-bellied with a green nape.
  - Common Goldeneye: Larger, 40–50 cm; drake has a single round white patch between bill and eye, white neck and belly, and a golden iris.
  - Common Merganser: Far larger, 58–72 cm, long-necked with a long thin red serrated bill; drake white-bodied with a dark green head.

## Hawfinch
*Coccothraustes coccothraustes* · `hawfin`

- Jizz — 18–19 cm, wingspan 29–33 cm, 46–70 g. Squat and bull-necked, with a big round head, a huge conical bill of metallic appearance, and a short tail.
- Plumage — Light brown, head orange- to red-brown joined to the dark brown back by a broad grey nape band; underparts reddish-brown to brownish-white. Black mask round the eye, and a black throat patch. Wings blue-black with a white greater-covert band, crescent-shaped in flight; tail black, broadly white-tipped. Female paler, grey panel along the secondaries. Juvenile: grey-yellow breast, coarsely spotted belly.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-black in summer, bone-white to pale yellow-brown in winter; legs and toes short and pinkish flesh; eyes brown.
- In the field — White wing-band shows on both surfaces in flight. Call an explosive clicking zick or pix, plus a muffled blackbird-like srri; song quiet, chiefly in spring, zick notes among creaking phrases.
- Similar species —
  - Parrot Crossbill: 16–18 cm, 44–58 g; mandible tips crossed, male red or orange, female green or yellow. Large-headed and thick-billed but with no white wing-band and no black face mask.
  - Red Crossbill: The crossed mandibles decide it; male red or orange, female green or yellow, juvenile greyish-green and streaked. No white greater-covert band, no black mask, no bull neck.
  - African Chaffinch: 13.8–18.5 cm, 21–24 g and slim; male blue-grey headed with moss-green mantle, black wings with two bold white stripes, pale pink underparts and white-sided tail.
  - White-winged Crossbill: 14.5–17 cm, 25–40 g; crossed bill, forked tail and two prominent white wing-bars; male raspberry-red, female greenish-yellow — never black-masked with a conical bill.

## Hazel Grouse
*Tetrastes bonasia* · `hazgro1`

- Jizz — 34–39 cm, wingspan 48–54 cm. Small plump grouse, about partridge-sized; tail relatively long and slightly rounded; head feathers raised into a crest when excited.
- Plumage — Upperparts grey washed rufous, blackish barring on head, nape and back; rump greyest; underparts mottled brown, black and white, strengthening towards the throat. Tail grey vermiculated brown, all but the central feathers with a broad black band inside a grey-white tip. Male: black white-bordered throat patch, prominent crest, fine red supercilium. Female: throat pale rufous, crest barely visible.
- In the field — Shy, in dense woodland, usually detected only by voice or the burr of the wings in flight. Male calls a high-pitched ti-ti-ti-ti-ti, female a liquid tettettettet; whistling song rhythmic and as high as a Goldcrest's.
- Similar species —
  - Black Francolin: Male black with a white cheek patch, chestnut collar and white-spotted flanks, back scalloped golden-brown, legs reddish; female brown with a chestnut hind-neck. No crest.
  - Chukar: White face sharply outlined by a black gorget; breast grey, belly buff, flanks rufous-streaked; bill coral-red and legs red; no crest or black throat patch.
  - Rock Ptarmigan: White wings and underparts, wholly white in winter but for black outer tail feathers and eye-line; higher, more barren ground.
  - Willow Ptarmigan: Larger (35–44 cm), deep-chested and broad-billed with feathered legs; marbled brown with white wings and underparts, black tail and red combs over the eyes.

## Hen Harrier
*Circus cyaneus* · `norhar1`

- Jizz — 41–55 cm, 97–122 cm span; male 290–400 g, female 390–750 g. Buzzard-sized but slim and light; long, fairly narrow wings, broad at the base, and a long tail.
- Plumage — Male: pale blue-grey above and across the upper breast, white below, black wingtips, dark trailing edge to the wing, white rump. Female: brown above with white unstreaked uppertail-coverts — a 'ringtail' — underparts buff barred brown. Juvenile: as female but barring less distinct, secondaries dark brown, belly less barred.
- Bare parts — Iris pale in young birds, dark in adults.
- In the field — Quarters low over fields on buoyant, tern-like flight with wings held in a V; fast fluttering broken by buzzard-like soaring. Male chek-chek-chek and a bouncing chuk-uk-uk-uk in display; female whistles piih-eh, alarm chit-it-it-it-et-it.
- Similar species —
  - Western Marsh Harrier: Bigger and bulkier, 48–56 cm and 115–130 cm span, broader-winged; male three-toned brown-grey-black with no white rump, female chocolate-brown with a pale crown and throat.
  - Montagu's Harrier: Narrower-winged with a longer, thinner hand of four fingered primaries; male shows black bands along the secondaries and rusty flank streaks. 39–50 cm.
  - Pallid Harrier: Smaller, narrower-winged and paler with a different wingtip pattern and four fingered primaries; male whitish grey with narrow black tips. 40–48 cm.
  - Common Buzzard: Round-headed, fairly short-tailed and compact with broader wings and no white rump; soars rather than quarters. 40–58 cm, 109–140 cm span.

## Hooded Crow
*Corvus cornix* · `hoocro1`

- Jizz — 48–54 cm, wingspan 84–105 cm, 400–600 g, average 510 g. Typical crow build with a stout bill; male averages larger, sexes otherwise alike.
- Plumage — Ash-grey body, dark feather shafts giving a streaked look; head, throat, upper breast, wings, tail and thigh feathers black and mostly glossy; black bib ragged and variable; black on the wing takes in the flight feathers, primary coverts and greater coverts. Newly hatched young much blacker; juvenile duller.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black; iris dark brown. Juvenile: iris blue-grey, gape red at first.
- In the field — Flight slow, heavy and usually straight; walks with a rolling gait. Call a kraa, all but indistinguishable from Carrion Crow's.
- Similar species —
  - Carrion Crow: Same size (48–52 cm) and same voice: separate on plumage — wholly black with a green or purple sheen, never grey-bodied.
  - Rook: Smaller (44–46 cm, 280–340 g), all black with a blue-purple sheen and a bare whitish face at the bill base making the head look domed.
  - Common Raven: Much bigger (54–71 cm, span 116–153 cm) with a large heavy bill; entirely black, no grey body.
  - Brown-necked Raven: Larger (52–56 cm), black glossed purple-blue with a brownish-black head and throat, deep bill and more pointed wings; never grey.

## Hooded Merganser
*Lophodytes cucullatus* · `hoomer`

- Jizz — 40–49 cm, wingspan 60–66 cm, 453–879 g. Small merganser with a large fan-shaped crest that makes the head look long and oversized.
- Plumage — Male: head, neck, breast and upperparts black with white markings, a large white patch on each side of the crest; flanks chestnut, breast and underparts largely white with white stripes across the crop and breast. Female: grey-brown, upperparts nearly black, crest light reddish-brown. Both sexes show narrow white stripes along the tertials. First-winter: neck and upperparts grey-brown, tertial edges narrower.
- Bare parts — Iris yellow in the male, brown in females of all ages; male's eye already pale in the first winter; female's bill yellowish grey.
- In the field — Raises the crest in courtship, when the white head patches are most conspicuous. Non-breeding male like the female but yellow-eyed and white-marked on the breast; moults from June, back in breeding plumage from October.
- Similar species —
  - Bar-headed Goose: A pale grey goose, far larger at 71–76 cm, with black bars across an otherwise white head; no crest and no chestnut flanks.
  - Barnacle Goose: A goose: white face, black head, neck and breast, silver-grey barred back and white belly; 55–70 cm, wingspan 120–145 cm; crestless.
  - Common Scoter: Bulky all-black seaduck with a bulbous bill yellow around the nostrils; female brown with pale cheeks; no crest and no white head patch.
  - Black Swan: A swan, 110–142 cm and 3.7–9 kg, black with white flight feathers, a long S-curved neck and a bright red bill.

## Horned Grebe
*Podiceps auritus* · `horgre`

- Jizz — 31–38 cm, wingspan 46–74 cm, 300–570 g. Small grebe with a moderately long neck, flat forehead and a straight, pointed, pale-tipped bill; legs set far back.
- Plumage — Breeding: black head with erectile golden-red ear tufts and black fan-shaped cheek feathers; neck, flanks, lores and upper breast chestnut; crown and back black; belly dull white. Winter: white neck, chest and cheeks against a dull black-grey crown and back, the border running in a straight line behind the eye. Juvenile: duller, back brown-tinged, crown line less distinct, bill paler.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey to black, base flesh, tip near-white; iris intense red when breeding, pink at rest, narrow silver-white ring; legs grey, feet blue-green.
- In the field — Flight unsteady, recalling an auk, with a small white shoulder patch and white showing on the secondaries only.
- Similar species —
  - Great Crested Grebe: Much larger (46–51 cm); summer head-and-neck frills, not golden ear-tufts; winter whiter than other grebes, white above the eye and bill pink.
  - Red-necked Grebe: Larger; black cap runs below the eye over pale grey cheeks, neck rusty red, bill black with a yellow base; winter face dusky with a pale rear crescent.
  - Eared Grebe: Neck and breast black in summer with an ochre fan behind the eye; winter crown blurs into the cheeks; white in the wing reaches the primaries.
  - Little Grebe: Smaller, dumpier and shorter-necked; rufous cheeks, neck and flanks with a bright yellow gape in summer, buff with a powder-puff rear in winter.

## Horned Lark
*Eremophila alpestris* · `horlar`

- Jizz — 16–20 cm, 28–48 g, wingspan 30–34 cm; slightly smaller than Skylark. Summer male carries two black, pointed feather horns on the crown, best developed in northern European flava.
- Plumage — Face pattern striking black and yellow (black and white in Asian and north-west arctic birds); upperparts mainly brown-grey, variably rufous-toned by subspecies; underparts pale; tail mostly black except the central feathers, contrasting strongly with the paler body, especially in flight. Female: horns shorter, mask paler and less contrasting. Southern European penicillata greyer above, white replacing the yellow.
- Bare parts — Bill short and dark; legs dark grey.
- In the field — Distinctive on the ground, unlike most larks. Calls high-pitched, lisping or tinkling, weak; song given in flight, a few chips then a warbling ascending trill. Regular winter visitor to central European coasts.
- Similar species —
  - Crested Lark: Conspicuous crest at all times and no black-and-yellow face pattern; brown with light brown outer tail feathers, not black-tailed; 17–18 cm, bulkier about head and body.
  - Dupont's Lark: Slim, long-necked and long-legged with a fine slightly curved bill, thin pale crown-stripe and dark-streaked breast; no horns, no face mask; 17–18 cm.
  - Eurasian Skylark: Slightly larger, 16–19 cm; dull brown above, paler below, with a short blunt raisable crest; tail and rear edge of wing white-edged, not blackish-tailed.
  - Calandra Lark: Large and robust, 17.5–20 cm; streaked greyish brown above, white below, with big black patches on the breast sides and a white supercilium; wings dark below.

## House Bunting
*Emberiza sahari* · `houbun3`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm. Small, slight bunting of dry country.
- Plumage — Male: head grey with lengthwise black-and-white streaking, eye- and malar stripes weak; breast grey and spotted, cut off sharply from the throat; back rust-brown and unstreaked; belly and wings cinnamon-rufous; no white in the tail. Female: grey of the head tinged brown, streaking more diffuse, colours paler throughout.
- Bare parts — Lower mandible yellow.
- In the field — Breeds around houses and walls and is very tame, freely entering buildings. Song from a perch, light and jerky, weaker than a chaffinch's; calls a cheery dvuit and a falling tjyyo.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Larger and bulky, lark-like, streaked grey-brown above and whitish below with a streaked gorget across the throat; 16–19 cm against 13–14.
  - Reed Bunting: Male black-headed and black-throated with a white neck collar; female with a streaked brown head; back heavily barred, not plain rust-brown.
  - Yellowhammer: Bright yellow head and underparts, rufous rump, white outer tail feathers; larger at 16–16.5 cm.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 13–15 cm against 13–14 cm.

## House Sparrow
*Passer domesticus* · `houspa`

- Jizz — 14–18 cm, average 16 cm; wingspan 19–26 cm, 24–39.5 g. Compact, stocky passerine with a full chest, large rounded head, stout conical seed bill and short tail, 5.2–6.5 cm.
- Plumage — Male: lead-grey crown flanked by chestnut from eye to hind neck; cheeks pale grey to whitish; black bib from bill over throat to upper breast, veiled by pale fringes in autumn and clean only by spring; back brown, black-streaked; one clear white wing-bar; underparts ash-grey. Female plain grey-brown, finely marked, pale supercilium strongest behind the eye; no chestnut, no bib.
- In the field — Winter birds notably paler with much less black on the breast; town-centre and industrial birds far less contrasting through grime. Part-albino individuals fairly frequent.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Tree Sparrow: About 10% smaller, 12.5–14 cm; sexes identical, chestnut crown and nape, black kidney-shaped patch on a pure white cheek, two white wing-bars.
  - Spanish Sparrow: Male has a chestnut, not grey, crown, white rather than grey cheeks and heavily black-streaked underparts; slightly larger and stouter-billed. Females effectively inseparable.
  - Italian Sparrow: Male has chestnut crown, nape and head sides with white cheeks, bright chestnut upperparts and pale grey unstreaked underparts. Female nearly identical to House Sparrow.
  - Dead Sea Sparrow: Smaller, 12–13 cm, some 4 cm shorter; male grey-crowned with only a small black bib, pale supercilium and yellow neck sides. Female paler and smaller-billed.

## Hume's Warbler
*Phylloscopus humei* · `humwar1`

- Jizz — 9–11 cm. One of the smallest leaf warblers; slim, restless, fine-billed, barely larger than a Firecrest.
- Plumage — Greenish above with a grey-green cast, crown browner with at most a hint of a pale central stripe; long beige supercilium not reaching far into the nape, dark eyestripe; cream-white below, washed beige on throat, cheeks, breast and flanks. One clear pale wing bar on the greater coverts, the median-covert bar a faint vestige; tertials cream to white-tipped. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill dark horn-brown, base flesh or pale horn, lower mandible dark; iris brown; legs greyish olive-brown to dark horn, sometimes orange-tinged.
- In the field — Voice the safest character: song a drawn-out high buzzing bzzzzzzzyu falling slightly at the end; call more disyllabic than Yellow-browed Warbler's.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Plain greenish-brown wings with no wing bars and no bold supercilium; pale pinkish-yellow legs, longer paler bill, long primary projection; larger, 11–12.5 cm.
  - Common Chiffchaff: No wing bars, only a short whitish supercilium; dumpy, with short primary projection and dark legs; larger, 10–12 cm.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Two prominent wing bars rather than one, brighter and more strongly marked, with a long yellow supercilium; pale lower mandible and legs; call less clearly disyllabic.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green with a single wing bar, but off-white below without the beige wash, and its thin bill has no dark tip to the lower mandible; song a high jerky trill.

## Iberian Chiffchaff
*Phylloscopus ibericus* · `ibechi2`

- Jizz — 10–12 cm; males 7.0–8.3 g. Small, compact, short-winged leaf warbler with a rather large head and no bold markings.
- Plumage — Cleaner, warmer olive-green above with almost no brown, in autumn only on the head-side; underparts three-toned: throat and breast yellow-streaked on olive-brown, belly white, undertail-coverts bright lemon-yellow; more yellow on neck-sides and flanks than Common Chiffchaff. Vivid yellow supercilium, strongest before the eye, and a distinctly dark eye-stripe.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs paler than Common Chiffchaff's.
- In the field — Dips the tail like a Chiffchaff. Song in three parts: a fast chiffchaff-like opening, dry rattling notes, then long drawn-out whistles, chiff-chiff-chiff-dr-dr-dr-swit-swit-swit. Call a downward-inflected fiy.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Longer primary projection, more elegant shape, pinkish-yellow legs and a longer paler bill. Song a simple repetitive descending whistle; call a disyllabic hoo-eet.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Brown-washed dull green above, off-white below, supercilium short and whitish, legs dark, bill fine and dark. Often inseparable in the field: go by song.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Two prominent yellowish-white wing bars and yellow-fringed tertials; smaller, 9.5–11 cm and 4–9 g; restlessly active in the canopy.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above and off-white below with a single pale wing bar; bill thin, lower mandible without a dark tip. Song a high jerky trill.

## Iberian Gray Shrike
*Lanius meridionalis* · `ibgshr1`

- Jizz — 23–25 cm, 48–93 g. Starling-sized grey, white and black shrike, looking somewhat long-tailed; head slightly larger and tail thinner than Great Grey Shrike. Adult males biggest, yearling females smallest.
- Plumage — Head, nape, mantle, back and rump lead-grey, crown and nape darkest; broad black mask from the bill base over the eye to behind the ear-coverts, edged above by a fine white supercilium; cheeks and throat white; breast and belly pink-flushed, strongest on the flanks; scapulars white against black flight feathers; moderate white patch on the primaries. Juvenile: less distinctive.
- In the field — Perched, the white scapulars against black flight feathers make a marked black-and-white contrast. Song and calls appear like Great Grey Shrike's, but no comparison has been made.
- Similar species —
  - Red-backed Shrike: Much smaller, 16–18 cm; male reddish above with a grey head and pink-tinged underparts; female and young brown and vermiculated above and below.
  - Woodchat Shrike: Smaller, 17–19 cm; male black and white above with a chestnut crown and pure white underparts; female and young brown, white and vermiculated.
  - Great Gray Shrike: Same size, 22–26 cm, but much paler grey above, a narrower black mask, no clear white supercilium and no pinkish-grey wash below.
  - Lesser Gray Shrike: Black covers the forecrown as well as the mask; hindcrown and back pale bluish-grey, rump paler still; white below flushed pink on lower breast and belly.

## Iberian Green Woodpecker
*Picus sharpei* · `grnwoo3`

- Jizz — 31–33 cm. Medium-sized green woodpecker; bill medium-long and wedge-shaped at the tip.
- Plumage — Green above, rump and uppertail-coverts yellowish; crown and nape red; head almost free of black, the eye ringed dusky grey rather than black. Male: moustache red, bordered black below only, not all round. Female: moustache pale-bordered above, red crown feathers often white-tipped.
- Bare parts — Bill dark grey; iris white or pink; legs olive-grey.
- In the field — Voice described as sibilant, hiss-like. Endemic to the Iberian peninsula.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Green Woodpecker: Black lores and a black ring round the white eye; male's red-centred moustache framed black all round; 30–36 cm, wingspan 45–51 cm; juveniles spotted and streaked all over.
  - Gray-headed Woodpecker: Smaller, 25–26 cm; head grey with red confined to the male's forecrown, female none; black line across the lores, narrow black moustache; underparts pale grey.
  - Levaillant's Woodpecker: 30–33 cm, dark green above, yellowish green below with a chrome-yellow rump; male's crown crimson, female's grey and without red; moustache pale-bordered above.
  - Black Woodpecker: Far bigger and all black, 45–55 cm, wingspan 64–84 cm, 250–400 g, with a very long 5–6.7 cm bill.

## Iberian Magpie
*Cyanopica cooki* · `azwmag3`

- Jizz — 34–36 cm, 65–76 g; Swedish source gives 31–35 cm. Slighter than a magpie, with proportionately smaller bill and legs; large head, short broad wings, long broad strongly graduated tail, 16–20 cm.
- Plumage — Glossy black cap reaching below the eye; white throat, standing out sharply in shade against a grey-brown bird. Back pinkish-brown, breast and belly buff; wings and long tail pale azure-blue, obvious in sunlight.
- Bare parts — Iris dark brown to black; bill, legs and feet black. Sexes alike.
- In the field — Flapping, magpie-like flight but lighter and more agile. Commonest call a light nasal upslurred vrryiih, also kui and a whining vih-e; alarm a drawn-out chattering krrrrr.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Magpie: Eurasian magpie is far bigger, 44–46 cm, black and white with pure white belly and scapulars and green-purple gloss; no blue wings and tail, no white throat.
  - Brown-necked Raven: Brown-necked raven is an all-dark 52–56 cm corvid, head and throat brownish-black, body glossed purple-blue; no blue wings or tail, no black cap on grey-brown.
  - Common Raven: Common raven is far larger, 54–71 cm with a 116–153 cm span, wholly black with a large bill; nothing blue or grey-brown.
  - Eurasian Jackdaw: Jackdaw is black with a grey nape and neck and slate-grey underparts, short stout bill, 34–39 cm; no blue wings and no long graduated tail.

## Iceland Gull
*Larus glaucoides* · `y00478`

- Jizz — 50–66 cm, wingspan 115–150 cm, 480–1100 g. Medium-large gull, slender and light; head small, round, delicate; wings slim, hand long. Wing-tips project well past the tail at rest, often laid flat rather than crossed.
- Plumage — Very pale in every plumage; no black anywhere in wing or tail. Adult pale grey above with white trailing edge, white head, neck, breast, underparts, rump and tail; winter head streaked brownish onto the breast. Juvenile and first-winter grey-buff, upperwing paler than the body; second-winter near-white with grey vermiculation on head, nape and breast-sides.
- Bare parts — Adult bill yellow to yellow-green with red gonys spot, red orbital ring, pale yellow iris; legs pink to greyish flesh. Juvenile bill dark, eye black.
- In the field — Elegant in flight on slim wings. Laughing call as Herring Gull's but higher-pitched. Four years to maturity; kumlieni averages larger, coarser-billed, with darker grey-black wing-tip markings.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Larger, 55–67 cm on 125–155 cm wings, heavier-billed; darker grey back and solid black wingtips with white mirrors — Iceland shows no black at all.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Far bigger — the world's largest gull, 61–79 cm on 144–166 cm wings and up to 2.3 kg; sources describe no plumage for it, so separate on bulk.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Little else in the sources: sizes nearly overlap, 49–64 cm and 118–158 cm span against 50–66 cm and 115–150 cm. No plumage separation available.
  - Common Gull: Much smaller, 40–46 cm; grey above with black wingtips carrying large white mirrors, yellow legs and unmarked greenish-yellow bill; Iceland never shows black.

## Icterine Warbler
*Hippolais icterina* · `ictwar1`

- Jizz — 12–13.5 cm, 11–20 g. Fairly big slim warbler; large head, long broad-based bill, long wings with primary projection equalling the tertials, short square-ended tail.
- Plumage — Uniform brownish olive-green to grey-green above, evenly light yellow below, the yellow deepest on throat and fore-breast; breast sides and flanks washed brownish. Pale lores, short pale yellow supercilium, pale eye-ring. Secondaries and tertials broadly pale-edged, forming a pale panel on the closed wing. Sexes alike. Autumn juvenile slightly paler.
- Bare parts — Iris dark brown; bill two-toned, upper mandible dark brown, lower yellow to orange; legs lead-grey, sometimes bluish.
- In the field — Song loud and fast, weaving in mimicry, with nasal whining gie gie gie notes; call tä-tä-hyi, alarm short series of tä tä tä.
- Similar species —
  - Melodious Warbler: Melodious warbler is browner above and yellower below, with brown legs, much shorter primary projection and no pale wing panel; song fluent, sustained, unvaried, no mimicry.
  - Olive-tree Warbler: Olive-tree warbler is much bigger, 16–18 cm, pale olive-grey above and whitish below, with a long thick orange-yellow-based bill and long dark grey tail.
  - Upcher's Warbler: Upcher's warbler is greyer, 14–15 cm, with a longer bill, shorter wings and a longer tail that it moves constantly like a chat.
  - Eastern Olivaceous Warbler: Eastern olivaceous is plain pale brown above and whitish below, not yellow, with grey legs and a characteristic downward flick of the tail.

## Imperial Eagle
*Aquila heliaca* · `impeag1`

- Jizz — 68–90 cm, wingspan 176–220 cm; males 2.45–2.72 kg, females 3.26–4.54 kg. Large heavy Aquila, stockier than golden eagle; wings long and rather narrow, tips strongly fingered; tail relatively long, almost square-ended; legs feathered to the toes.
- Plumage — Adult almost uniformly blackish-brown; crown and nape pale beige to golden; clear white spots on the scapulars. Upper- and underwing-coverts dark, contrasting only weakly with slightly paler, uniform dark grey flight feathers. Tail silvery grey and finely dark-barred at the base, with a broad black subterminal band and a narrow white tip.
- Similar species —
  - Golden Eagle: Underwing-coverts paler, making a band along the underwing; no white scapular spots and the crown darker; wings broader; 66–102 cm, span 180–234 cm.
  - Spanish Eagle: Near-identical in size, 72–85 cm and span 177–220 cm; the sources separate them only by range, this one being the Iberian bird.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Distinctly smaller and lighter, 55–74 cm with a 142–175 cm span against 176–220 cm; sources give no plumage marks to go on.
  - Steppe Eagle: Mainly dark brown throughout, the sources noting no white scapular spots or pale nape; long gape-line, small head on a thick neck, tail rounded; 60–89 cm.

## Indian Peafowl
*Pavo cristatus* · `compea`

- Jizz — Male 100–125 cm bill to tail, 4.1–5.2 kg, and 200–230 cm to the end of a full train; female about 95 cm. Very large, heavily built gamebird; small head, short rounded wings, flat 18-feathered tail.
- Plumage — Male: metallic blue crown with a fan-shaped crest of bare shafts tipped blue-green; bare white stripe above and white crescent below the eye; neck and breast purple-blue, mantle scaly bronze-green, wing with chestnut primaries and black secondaries, belly blackish; train of bronze-green upper tail-coverts bearing purple-black, blue and copper eye-spots. Female mostly brown-grey, no train.
- In the field — Raises and fans the train, and rattles the quills loudly if the display alone fails to drive off an attacker. Male has spurs; adult plumage gained in the third year.
- Similar species —
  - Barbary Partridge: A rotund partridge — grey-brown back, grey breast, buff belly, light grey face with broad reddish-brown gorget, red legs; runs rather than flies; nothing peafowl-sized about it.
  - Red-legged Partridge: Small rotund partridge, sandy-brown above, black-streaked gorget, bold rufous-and-black flank bars, red bill and eye-ring; no crest, no train.
  - Western Capercaillie: Also huge and strongly size-dimorphic, cock 74–85 cm and about 4.1 kg, but a grouse: no train, no fanned crest, no eye-spots.

## Isabelline Wheatear
*Oenanthe isabellina* · `isawhe1`

- Jizz — 16.5 cm, 27–31 g; wing 9.4–9.7 cm. Long-legged, upright-standing wheatear with a bill longer than Northern Wheatear's.
- Plumage — Sexes alike; male averages blacker lores. Upperparts pale sandy brown, isabelline-tinged; rump and uppertail-coverts white; tail with broad black terminal band, black central feathers and large white base; creamy-white supercilium, pale brown ear-coverts; throat pale buff, breast sandy buff, belly creamy white; black alula contrasting with the pale wing. Juvenile: grey-brown above with yellowish-white fringes and spots.
- Bare parts — Bill, legs and feet black; iris brown.
- In the field — Stands markedly upright on long tarsi. Call a chirp, sometimes a loud whistle; song lark-like, beginning with a croaking noise.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: Smaller, 14–16.5 cm, and darker; summer male grey above with black face-mask and wings; narrower black tail-band; bill shorter, ear-coverts darker, stance less upright.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Breeding male near-white on forehead, crown and mantle with a black mask from bill to ear-coverts and blacker wings; tail much whiter, white often reaching the tips.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Smaller, 13.5–15.5 cm; breeding male white-crowned with buff mantle, black mask to the bill and blacker wings; tail whiter.
  - Black Wheatear: Larger, 16–18 cm, and all black (female dark brown) with white rump and mainly white tail carrying a black inverted T.

## Island Canary
*Serinus canaria* · `comcan`

- Jizz — 10–12 cm, wingspan 21–23.7 cm, 8.4–24.3 g. Typical finch shape: rounded head, short neck, short stout conical bill; tail a little longer and less deeply forked than a siskin's.
- Plumage — Male: head and underparts yellow-green, forehead, face and supercilium yellower; ear patch framed by a dark malar stripe; upperparts grey-green with dark streaks, rump dull yellow; lower belly and undertail-coverts whitish; dark streaks along the sides. Female: duller, head and breast greyer. Juvenile: largely brown, dark-streaked.
- Bare parts — Upper mandible flesh to pale lead-grey, blackish towards the tip, lower horn-coloured; iris grey-black; legs and toes grey-brown.
- In the field — Song a silvery twittering; calls include a greenfinch-like djuit, a ringing tvi-vi-vi and an odd tremulous ti-ti-tyrr.
- Similar species —
  - European Serin: Shorter-tailed and more contrasted, with a brighter yellow face and breast, yellow rump and heavily streaked breast; relatively longer wings.
  - Fire-fronted Serin: Adults very dark and redpoll-like, a high-mountain bird; 11–12.5 cm against 10–12 cm.
  - Citril Finch: Clean yellow underparts and yellow double wing-bars, greyish above with a yellow face mask; canary is streaked along the sides and greener.
  - Corsican Finch: Rust-brown, dark-streaked back and greyish uppertail-coverts, face and underparts yellow rather than yellow-green.

## Italian Sparrow
*Passer italiae* · `itaspa1`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm; 26 g in summer to 30 g in winter. Small, chunky sparrow, about house sparrow size; tail 5.3–6 cm, male wing 7.3–8.2 cm.
- Plumage — Male: crown, nape and sides of head chestnut; cheeks near-pure white; fine white supercilium usually clear; throat and breast bib pure black, more black-flecked than house sparrow; upperparts bright chestnut; lower back and rump brownish, not grey; underparts pale grey, unstreaked. Bib dull when fresh, brightened by wear. Female: as female house sparrow.
- In the field — Male gives a chreep call like Spanish Sparrow to proclaim nest ownership; voice like both parent species, but carries better in natural surroundings than house sparrow's.
- Similar species —
  - House Sparrow: Cheeks dirty grey-white, not near-pure white; lower back and rump grey, not brownish; bib less black-flecked. Females effectively inseparable in the field.
  - Eurasian Tree Sparrow: Smaller, 12.5–14 cm, wingspan 21 cm; black kidney-shaped ear patch on a pure white cheek, black chin and throat, two narrow white wing-bars, sexes alike.
  - Spanish Sparrow: Underparts heavily streaked black, not plain pale grey; slightly larger with a longer, stouter bill; female shows cream supercilium and broad cream back streaks.
  - Dead Sea Sparrow: Much smaller, 12–13 cm; male has grey crown, rear neck and cheeks, only a small black bib, yellow neck sides and dark-streaked reddish-brown upperparts.

## Ivory Gull
*Pagophila eburnea* · `ivogul`

- Jizz — 40–44 cm, wingspan 108–120 cm, 450–690 g. Pigeon-shaped rather than Larus-like: compact and thick-bodied, with broad-based wings, short neck and very short legs.
- Plumage — Adult wholly pure white, without the grey saddle of other gulls. Juvenile and first-winter: white, spotted regularly with black-brown on wings and tail, face dirty and dusky. Second-winter: intermediate, spots smaller, face less dusky, some already adult-like; full adult dress at two years.
- Bare parts — Bill thick, blue-grey with a yellow tip, occasionally reddish; iris dark with a narrow dark red orbital ring; legs short and black.
- In the field — Flies with strong, springy, elastic beats recalling a skua's power. Flight call a harsh tern-like keeeer or kriääh; also a warbling fox-call warning of arctic fox, polar bear, Glaucous Gull or human near the nest.
- Similar species —
  - Arctic Tern: A tern: 33–36 cm, grey above with black cap and white cheeks, deep-forked tail, dark red bill and legs; the ivory gull is wholly white and black-legged.
  - Armenian Gull: Large grey-backed gull (52–62 cm, span 120–145 cm) with a short black-banded bill and extensive black wingtips; the ivory gull is smaller and white throughout.
  - Audouin's Gull: Sources describe none of its appearance: go on the ivory gull's own marks — pure white plumage, black legs, blue yellow-tipped bill, pigeon-like shape.
  - Black-headed Gull: 34–39 cm, chocolate-brown hood in summer, pale grey back, black primary tips and a white leading edge to the outer wing; the ivory gull shows no grey or black.

## Jack Snipe
*Lymnocryptes minimus* · `jacsni`

- Jizz — 17–25 cm, wingspan 30–42 cm, 33–73 g. Smallest true snipe, two-thirds the size of Common Snipe, about skylark-sized; bill relatively short, 36–43 mm; wings narrow with blunt tips; tail pointed.
- Plumage — Mottled brown above with yellow back stripes showing in flight, pale below. Crown glossy blackish-brown, no central crown-stripe: two pale lateral crown-stripes separated from the broad pale yellow supercilium by dark. Black eyestripe broad before the eye, narrower behind, ending in a dark ear-covert patch. Neck, breast and fore-flanks washed brown with heavy dark streaks; belly and undertail white.
- In the field — Bobs constantly when feeding, as if on springs — almost hypnotic. Flight less jerky than Common Snipe's; tail shows no white.
- Similar species —
  - Common Snipe: Bigger, 25–27 cm, with a very long straight bill 5.5–7 cm; crown shows a central pale stripe and light stripes above and below the eye-stripe; flight jerkier.
  - Red Phalarope: About 21 cm with lobed toes and a straight bill; unstreaked — grey above, white below in winter with a black eye-patch; red below when breeding.
  - Pectoral Sandpiper: Sandpiper build: grey-brown back, grey breast sharply cut off from the white belly, yellowish legs, olive dark-tipped bill; no striped crown, no long bill.
  - Curlew Sandpiper: 18–23 cm; black bill, dark grey to black feet; large white rump patch and a white wing-stripe in flight — Jack Snipe shows yellow back stripes and no white.

## Kentish Plover
*Anarhynchus alexandrinus* · `kenplo1`

- Jizz — 15–20 cm, wingspan 35–45 cm, 30–55 g (about 40 g). Small, pale shorebird; medium-length legs, short fine pointed bill.
- Plumage — Grey-brown to sandy above, white below, with a white hind-collar and whitish supercilium. Summer male: black frontal bar, black stripe through the eye, black ear-coverts, rufous crown and nape, and two dark breast-side patches forming a broken collar. Female paler, without the black and rufous. In flight: white wingbar, white rump, black tail with white sides.
- Bare parts — Bill black, short and fine; eye black; legs dark to black in summer, grey in winter and in juveniles.
- In the field — Call about bip or drip. Males have longer tarsi and longer flank feathers than females; ornament varies with season, rainfall and breeding system.
- Similar species —
