  - Black-bellied Plover: Far bigger, 25–30 cm and 170–320 g; summer spangled black and white above with black face and belly, winter plain grey with speckled breast; bill and legs black.
  - Common Ringed Plover: One complete black breast-band, not two side patches; white forehead, black mask, short orange-and-black bill and orange legs; 17–20 cm.
  - European Golden-Plover: Much larger, 25–29 cm, thickset, wings barely longer than the tail; white S-shaped band running from forehead down the flanks.
  - Eurasian Dotterel: Striking whitish supercilium in all plumages and plain wings in flight; yellow legs; summer bird chestnut-breasted with a white breast-line and black belly.

## King Eider
*Somateria spectabilis* · `kineid`

- Jizz — 50–70 cm, wingspan 86–102 cm, 1.5–2.01 kg. Large sea duck, slightly smaller and more compact than Common Eider, and distinctly smaller-billed.
- Plumage — Male: black body, white to salmon breast, powder-blue crown and nape, sea-green cheeks, white chin and throat; white wing-covert patches, white patches at the black rump sides, two triangular tertial 'sails' over the black scapulars. Female: warm rust-brown with blackish chevrons, fine dark head streaks, buff spot at the bill base and a buff eye-ring drawn back downcurved.
- Bare parts — Bill coral-red with a large orange-yellow frontal knob set off by a thin black line; female's bill black or grey and shorter; legs greenish grey.
- In the field — Eclipse male keeps a shrunken orange knob and the white covert patches, otherwise mottled brown-grey and dark. Female's upcurved gape gives a gentle expression. Juvenile greyish brown.
- Similar species —
  - Common Eider: Larger, 50–71 cm, longer-billed, no knob; drake has a black crown over a white back; female colder brown and barred rather than chevroned.
  - Red-breasted Merganser: Slim sawbill with spiky crest and long thin red serrated bill; male green-glossed dark head, white collar, rusty breast.
  - Common Merganser: Slim, with a long red bill and smooth crest; male white-bodied with black green-glossed head; female's red-brown head cut sharply off the white breast.
  - Velvet Scoter: All glossy black, female dull brown; thick neck, long broad bill, pointed tail; white wing patch and small white mark behind the eye.

## Krüper's Nuthatch
*Sitta krueperi* · `krunut1`

- Jizz — 11.5–12.5 cm, 10–14.3 g. Small nuthatch with a relatively small head and short bill; wing 71–81 mm in the male, 68–74 mm in the female; tail 33–40 mm.
- Plumage — Upperparts blue-grey, flight feathers grey-brown; black fore-crown; long white supercilium underlined by a black eyestripe that fades behind the eye; throat white, rest of the underparts dirty white to pale grey with a large rusty-red patch on the crescent-shaped breast; undertail rufous with white tips; thin white eye-ring. Female: black crown smaller and less sharply cut off behind.
- Bare parts — Bill dark horn-grey, cutting edge and lower mandible base blue-grey; iris dark cinnamon to brown; legs grey-brown to dark grey.
- In the field — Song a series of shrill nasal notes likened to a bicycle horn or a toy trumpet; call a soft dvuii.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Nuthatch: Larger, 14 cm; no black fore-crown and no rusty breast patch – instead orange-red flanks and lower belly; head plainer, with a black eye-stripe but no bold supercilium.
  - Western Rock Nuthatch: Bird of bare rock and mountain crags, feeding on the ground and hammering food wedged into crevices; the sources give it no plumage marks, so separate on habitat.

## Lanceolated Warbler
*Locustella lanceolata* · `lanwar`

- Jizz — 11.5–12.5 cm. Small, compact warbler, short-necked and short-tailed.
- Plumage — Dull brown above with black streaking, whitish-grey below with small lance-head streaks that carry onto the undertail-coverts; dark marks sharply defined though variable; near-black tertials with even, sharply edged pale fringes. Sexes alike; juvenile yellower below.
- In the field — Skulks, creeping through grass and low foliage and hard to see except when singing; grassland with thicker scrub or trees, often near water. Song a monotonous mechanical insect-like reeling, mostly at dusk.
- Similar species —
  - Common Grasshopper Warbler: Larger and longer-tailed at 12.5–13.5 cm, cream-buff below with only a few diffuse breast and flank spots; reeling lower, slower, in phrases of 5–10 minutes.
  - River Warbler: Larger, with an unstreaked grey-brown back and a darker undertail patterned by white feather tips; breast streaking limited.
  - Savi's Warbler: Uniform dark reddish-brown above and unstreaked below, around 14 cm; trill lower-pitched and less prolonged, preceded by low ticks.

## Lapland Longspur
*Calcarius lapponicus* · `laplon`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, wingspan 22–29 cm, 22–33 g. Robust bunting, near yellowhammer size; rather short tail, long primary projection reaching half the tail; hind claw longer than the hind toe.
- Plumage — Male: crown, cheeks, throat and upper breast black; white supercilium behind the black ear-coverts; nape chestnut; back streaked black and white; median coverts white-tipped; underparts white, flanks black-spotted. Female: no black on head or throat, white throat patch under a grey collar, buff supercilium, black comma on the cheek. Other plumages plainer, orange-brown head, chestnut nape and wing panels.
- Bare parts — Bill thick, straw-yellow with a black tip, paler yellow in the female; legs dark.
- In the field — Call on migration and at flush a dry pr'r'r'rt, harder and drier than snow bunting's; also a whistled tju and rough tjyb. Song a short bleak strophe, sometimes in a hovering song-flight with the tail spread.
- Similar species —
  - Snow Bunting: Larger and longer-winged, 15–18 cm, wingspan 32–38 cm, 30–40 g; breeding male white with black back and wingtips, female's black back feathers brown-fringed.

## Laughing Dove
*Spilopelia senegalensis* · `laudov1`

- Jizz — 25–27 cm, wingspan 40–45 cm, 80–120 g. Long-tailed slim pigeon; tail graduated.
- Plumage — Head and underparts pinkish, head and neck tinged lilac, shading buff on the lower belly; chequered rufous and grey patch of split feathers on the neck sides; upperparts brownish with a blue-grey band along the wing; outer tail feathers tipped white. Sexes alike in the field; young lack the chequered neck patch. Arid-zone birds paler.
- Bare parts — Legs red.
- In the field — Flies fast with regular wingbeats in a way characteristic of doves. Chuckling call a low rolling croo-doo-doo-doo-doo, rising and falling in amplitude.
- Similar species —
  - European Turtle-Dove: Neck patch black-and-white striped, not chequered rufous; wedge-shaped tail dark-centred with white borders, showing as a blackish chevron from below; 26–28 cm.
  - Eurasian Collared-Dove: Grey-buff to pinkish-grey with a white-edged black half-collar on the nape and no chequered neck patch; larger, 29–33 cm; black bill, red iris.
  - Stock Dove: Blue-grey with two short dark wingbars and an iridescent green-purple neck patch; far bulkier, 28–34 cm, wingspan 60–68 cm, 300–365 g; bill pink with a yellow tip.
  - Diamond Dove: Tiny at 19–21 cm, light blue-grey head, neck and breast, white spots and black edges on the wing, red eyes in orange eye-rings; dark grey bill.

## Leach's Storm-Petrel
*Hydrobates leucorhous* · `lcspet`

- Jizz — 18–21 cm; wingspan 43–48 cm. Small dark seabird with long wings and a forked tail; tubular nostril along the top of the bill. Clearly larger than European Storm-Petrel.
- Plumage — All black except a paler grey-brown bar across the secondary coverts, a slightly paler greyish face, and usually a white rump with a hint of a dark divide down the middle. The pale greater coverts read as a light half-circle above when the bird banks and shows its back. Dark-rumped birds occur off western North America.
- In the field — Fluttering flight but with deep beats; patters on the surface picking plankton, and often glides low with legs dangling, dipping the feet in the water. On land only a short shuffle to the burrow.
- Similar species —
  - European Storm-Petrel: Smaller, 14–18 cm, wingspan 37–41 cm; square-tailed, face black not greyish, rump snow-white extending onto the tail-base sides, and a broad white band on the underwing.
  - Band-rumped Storm-Petrel: Difficult: Leach's has the more deeply forked tail, a V-shaped or triangular white rump and tern-like flight; Band-rumped's white rump is extensive and its flight shearwater-like.

## Lesser Black-backed Gull
*Larus fuscus* · `lbbgul`

- Jizz — 49–64 cm, wingspan 118–158 cm, 450–1300 g. Slimmer and smaller-bodied than Herring Gull; wings long and rather narrow, especially the inner wing; bill 4.2–5.8 cm, long, narrow, looking pointed. Male larger.
- Plumage — Adult breeding: head, neck, breast, belly, underwing, rump and tail pure white; upperparts slate-grey to velvety black-grey by race; outer primaries black, contrasting little on the darkest birds; narrow white trailing edge running unbroken to the fifth primary; small white mirrors. Winter: head grey-streaked. Juvenile: vermiculated black, white, grey and buff, all primaries wholly dark.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow with a red gonys spot; legs yellow.
- In the field — Four-year gull: adult dress usually in the fourth year, eastern fuscus and heuglini often a year earlier; adult moult begins May–August and is often unfinished into winter.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Bigger, paler: light grey back, white-mirrored black wingtips, pink legs at every age, pale eye in a yellow orbital ring; lesser is dark-backed and yellow-legged.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: Much larger (64–79 cm, span 1.5–1.7 m) and bulkier, with pinkish legs, bigger white mirrors and a head that stays white in winter.
  - Iceland Gull: Sizes overlap (50–64 cm); sources give only build — Iceland is slender, light and thin-billed, while the slate-to-black mantle and yellow legs are the lesser's.
  - Common Gull: Far smaller (40–46 cm, span 100–115 cm), grey above with big white mirrors, and a short tapered greenish-yellow bill with no red spot.

## Lesser Gray Shrike
*Lanius minor* · `legshr2`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm. Starling-sized but far slimmer and longer-tailed; bill high, broad and strongly toothed; tail graduated, shorter than the grey shrikes', wing-points long on the perched bird.
- Plumage — Male: black forehead and forecrown running back through eye and ear-coverts to the nape; crown, mantle and rump pale blue-grey; scapulars, wings and outer tail black; throat white, breast and belly flushed salmon-pink. White primary bases form a patch when perched, a crescent in flight. Female: head dark grey, upperparts browner, less pink. Juvenile: browner, back faintly barred, no pink.
- Bare parts — Bill brownish-black with a paler base to the lower mandible; iris brown; legs and feet black to dark grey-brown.
- In the field — Despite the shared grey, black and white pattern of the grey shrikes, identifiable with certainty given good viewing conditions. Melanistic individuals occur occasionally.
- Similar species —
  - Red-backed Shrike: Reddish upperparts and a grey head with only a black eye-stripe, no black forehead; female and young brown and vermiculated. Smaller, 16–18 cm.
  - Woodchat Shrike: Chestnut crown and nape, black-and-white upperparts, pure white underparts; female and young brown and vermiculated. Smaller, 17–19 cm.
  - Iberian Gray Shrike: Much darker grey above, especially crown, nape and mantle, with a wider black mask, a clear white supercilium, and no black across the forehead.
  - Great Gray Shrike: Bigger, 22–26 cm; longer-tailed with shorter wing-points when perched, and no black over the forehead and forecrown.

## Lesser Kestrel
*Falco naumanni* · `leskes1`

- Jizz — 27–33 cm, wingspan 63–72 cm, 130–200 g. Small falcon, slightly smaller than Common Kestrel, with proportionally shorter wings and tail; tail more wedge-shaped from the elongated central feathers. Female barely larger than male.
- Plumage — Male: head slate-grey, back brick-red without black spotting, no black malar stripe; slate-grey band across the upperwing to the inner secondaries; underparts barred grey. Female and juvenile: paler, very like Common Kestrel but lacking the wing band; belly streaks more spot- or ring-shaped; underside of flight feathers pale, contrasting with the coverts; undertail paler, broad white-edged terminal band.
- Bare parts — Talons whitish-horn in all plumages, not black as in other falcons; conspicuous only at very close range.
- In the field — Call diagnostic: a harsh chay-chay-chay, unlike Common Kestrel's kee-kee-kee. For females and young, call and structure are better guides than plumage.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Larger and longer-winged, 36–42 cm, wingspan 87–104 cm; dark morph all sooty brown, light morph buff below with black underwing-coverts; no brick-red back or grey wing band.
  - Eurasian Kestrel: Larger, 32–39 cm; male black-spotted above, with a black malar stripe and no grey wing band; longer wings and tail; talons black, not whitish-horn.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Much bigger and heavier, 34–58 cm and 330–1,300 g against 27–33 cm and 130–200 g; sexes alike in markings, so no grey-headed, red-backed male.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Slate-grey above with a dark crown and two short black moustache stripes, white throat, black-streaked underparts and rufous thighs and vent; larger, 29–36 cm.

## Lesser Spotted Eagle
*Clanga pomarina* · `leseag1`

- Jizz — 55–67 cm, wingspan 146–168 cm. One of the smallest eagles, but clearly bigger than a buzzard; head and bill small; wings look short and strikingly broad, primaries deeply fingered; tail rounded at the corners; legs feathered to the toes.
- Plumage — Adult brown; body, head and wing-coverts warm pale brown, contrasting with uniformly dark grey-brown flight and tail feathers. Whitish bases to the inner primaries make a pale patch on the upperwing; upper tail-coverts broadly white-edged, adults keeping a white V on the rump. Juvenile: less wing contrast, prominent white spots on the remiges, cream neck patch, no covert spotting.
- Bare parts — Iris orange-yellow; cere and toes yellow; bill black with a grey base.
- In the field — Call a dog-like yip. Males average about 85% of female size, 1.0–1.4 kg against 1.3–2.2 kg.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Spotted Eagle: Larger and more compact, black-brown without pale coverts; broad wings reaching the tail tip and a short rounded tail give a vulture-like silhouette; no wing patch, white V poorly defined.
  - Long-legged Buzzard: A Buteo: large bill on a smallish head, long tail, long legs; three or four highly variable morphs; perches openly and upright, and often walks slowly on the ground.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Candidate text gives only size and form, 55–74 cm and 142–175 cm against 55–67 cm and 146–168 cm; separate on the pale coverts and white rump V.
  - Short-toed Snake-Eagle: Predominantly white below with pale earthy-brown chin to upper breast, 3–4 tail bars, owl-like rounded head, bright yellow eyes; longer-winged, wingspan 162–195 cm.

## Lesser Spotted Woodpecker
*Dryobates minor* · `leswoo1`

- Jizz — 14–16.5 cm, wingspan 24–29 cm, 17–25 g. Smallest European woodpecker, barely bullfinch-sized, but compact, short-necked, short-tailed and round, so it looks bulkier and bigger than it is.
- Plumage — Male: crimson black-bordered crown; face white to cream, framed by black lores, malar and cheek stripe, the last not reaching the black nape band; nape and upper back black; lower back barred black and white, wings broadly barred; outer tail barred; underparts white, flanks streaked. No red on vent or undertail-coverts. Female: crown white. Juvenile: some crimson on the head.
- Bare parts — Bill slate-grey, long for the bird at up to 18 mm; legs slate-grey.
- In the field — Keeps to the tops of tall trees in woods and parks, so easily overlooked; on a trunk told at once by the broad wing bars and narrower bars across the lower back.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Wryneck: Larger, 16–18 cm, slim and thrush-shaped rather than pied: upperparts barred and mottled pale brown, rufous and blackish; cheeks and throat buff barred brown; rounded grey tail.
  - Iberian Green Woodpecker: Medium-sized Iberian endemic, far bigger; little black on the head, dusky rather than black eye-ring, sibilant hiss-like call. No measurements for it in the sources.

## Lesser White-fronted Goose
*Anser erythropus* · `lwfgoo`

- Jizz — 53–66 cm, wingspan 120–135 cm, 1.4–2.2 kg — not much bigger than a Mallard. Small goose with rounded head, steep forehead, short neck and delicate bill; long wing-tips project past the tail on standing birds.
- Plumage — Like Greater White-fronted but smaller and darker, especially on the head; white facial blaze runs up over the forehead, ending between the eyes; belly crossed by broad black bars, weaker than in Greater White-fronted; upper wing-coverts mouse-coloured. Downy young brown above, whitish below, cream face and neck, dark lores, red-brown forehead.
- Bare parts — Orbital ring bright yellow, yellow-green in juveniles until the moult of their first November; legs bright orange.
- In the field — Commonest call a two- or three-syllable jerky nasal trumpeting, like Pink-footed Goose but usually lower-pitched. Hard to name on passage — Anser geese vary greatly in size between individuals.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: No white blaze and no yellow eye-ring; short bill pink in the middle with black base and tip, feet pink, wing-coverts pale bluish-grey; larger, 60–75 cm.
  - Graylag Goose: Much bigger and bulkier, 74–91 cm, thick long neck, large head, flesh-coloured bill and legs, bluish-grey wing-coverts; no white blaze or belly bars.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Larger, 68–90 cm; black bill with an orange band and no white blaze; wing-coverts dark brown with narrow white fringes; legs orange, voice a loud honking.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Larger, 64–81 cm, and paler; no yellow eye-ring, blaze stops short of the crown, belly barring heavier, bill pinkish.

## Lesser Whitethroat
*Curruca curruca* · `leswhi4`

- Jizz — 11.5–13.5 cm, 12–16 g. Small, stockily built warbler with a fairly short tail showing white outer feathers; bill short.
- Plumage — Brown to grey-brown above, crown and short tail greyer, whitish below with faintly buff flanks; throat white. Surest mark the sharply defined grey ear-coverts, a darker bandit mask through the eye. Sexes and ages alike. Race blythi has a paler grey crown cut off from the cheek by a white supercilium; minula paler and browner, althaea larger and darker.
- Bare parts — Bill short and dark with a paler grey-blue base; legs and tarsi dark; iris dark, lower eyelid pale, a thin light grey eye-ring.
- In the field — Open country and cultivation with large bushes and some trees; song about two seconds, a short rough chatter running into a dry rattle, like dried peas shaken in a tin; call a dry tet or tsche.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Slightly larger, with chestnut fringes to the secondaries and a uniform head and face rather than a dark mask; male grey-headed with a white throat; song scratchy and scolding.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male has a black head against a white throat, grey back and red eyes; female brown above with a grey head and buff underparts.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male grey above with brick-red underparts and white malar streaks; female brown above with a greyer head and a pink flush below; naked eye-ring.
  - Dartford Warbler: Long-tailed for a warbler, 13 cm; male grey above with reddish, white-spotted underparts and a red eye; female paler below, especially on the throat.

## Lesser Yellowlegs
*Tringa flavipes* · `lesyel`

- Jizz — 23–25 cm, wingspan 58–64 cm, 60–100 g. Elegant, slim shorebird; neck long and thin, legs and wings long; bill straight, slim, all dark, about head length, 3–4 cm.
- Plumage — Breeding: upperparts mottled grey-brown, black and white; head pale grey-white finely streaked; supercilium diffuse, white eye-ring and white chin clear; breast streaked and spotted dark brown, flanks finely barred; underparts white. Non-breeding: upperparts uniform grey-brown, breast streaked only. Juvenile: throat paler, upperparts browner, breast greyer.
- Bare parts — Legs bright yellow; bill black, in many birds yellowish-brown at the base; iris dark brown.
- In the field — In flight all the flight feathers look uniformly dark, without pale spotting on secondaries or inner primaries. Call soft.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Sandpiper: Supercilium runs well behind the eye, crown darker, bill two-toned, legs at most dull yellow; shows a white rump-patch.
  - Common Greenshank: Larger, with a longer bill grey at the base and looking upturned; legs green, not bright yellow; a white wedge runs up the back in flight.
  - Common Redshank: Legs red and bill red with a black tip, not yellow and all-dark; 24–30 cm; shows white up the back and on the wings in flight.
  - Green Sandpiper: Plumper, 21–24 cm, dark greenish-brown above with white spotting; legs and short bill dark green; brilliant white rump against wings dark above and below.

## Levaillant's Woodpecker
*Picus vaillantii* · `levwoo1`

- Jizz — 30–33 cm, wingspan 45–51 cm, wing 154–170 mm. Medium-large Picus with a faint crest, long stiff tail, and a long broad-based chisel bill slightly decurved along the culmen.
- Plumage — Dark moss-green above, yellowish-green below; nape crimson; rump chrome yellow, brighter than the green woodpecker's; outer webs of the primaries barred black and white, outer tail feathers barred grey-green and white; flanks and lower belly with olive-grey arrowheads. Black moustache pale-bordered above. Male: crown crimson. Female: crown grey, wholly without red. Lores and eye-surround never black.
- Bare parts — Bill slate grey, pale at the base and grey at the tip, slightly shorter than the green woodpecker's; feet slate grey.
- In the field — Flight light and bounding despite the bulk; call a loud ringing laugh, plue, plue, plue, like a green woodpecker's yaffle but faster; drums more often, rolls lasting one to 1.5 seconds.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Green Woodpecker: Averages larger, 30–36 cm; black lores and black round the white eye, male's moustache red-centred, rump duller yellow; yaffle slower.
  - Gray-headed Woodpecker: Grey head with at most a red forecrown in the male and no crimson nape; breast and underbody plain pale grey; smaller, 25–32 cm.
  - Iberian Green Woodpecker: Extremely close: look for its dusky rather than clean-bordered ring round the eye and its sibilant, hiss-like sound; endemic to Iberia.
  - White-backed Woodpecker: A pied woodpecker, 24–28 cm: black above with white bars across the wings and a white lower back; no green and no yellow rump.

## Levant Sparrowhawk
*Tachyspiza brevipes* · `levspa1`

- Jizz — 32–38 cm; wingspan 64–80 cm. Small, slim accipiter; wings short and broad but pointed with black tips, tail shorter than Sparrowhawk's — falcon-like in the air.
- Plumage — Male: blue-grey above, head included, wingtips dark; underparts and leg feathers finely barred rufous on white, looking almost white at range; white throat split by a dark central stripe, sometimes faint. Female: slate-grey above, barred red-brown below, often showing the throat line. Juvenile: dark grey-brown above, breast coarsely streaked, flanks and underwing barred and spotted, throat stripe clear.
- Bare parts — Legs orange-yellow, cere yellow.
- In the field — Short broad wings and a longish tail for manoeuvring through trees. Female larger than male, though less markedly so than in Sparrowhawk; juvenile recalls a young Goshawk rather than a young Sparrowhawk.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Sparrowhawk: Longer-tailed, with blunter, rounder wingtips and no black tips; female far larger than the male, up to 25% bigger. Male's iris orange-yellow to orange-red.
  - Montagu's Harrier: Far longer-winged, span 96–116 cm; male pale grey with black bands across the secondaries, female brown with a white rump. Not an accipiter shape.
  - Pallid Harrier: Long wings held in a shallow V in low quartering flight, span 95–120 cm; male whitish-grey above and white below, female a brown ringtail.
  - Booted Eagle: Much bigger, wingspan 110–132 cm; pale morph light grey with darker head and flight feathers, dark morph mid-brown. Never finely barred rufous below.

## Little Bittern
*Botaurus minutus* · `litbit1`

- Jizz — 27–38 cm, wingspan 40–58 cm, 60–150 g. Smallest of the European herons, about the size of a jay; tiny, with a long sharp bill and a thick neck.
- Plumage — Male: crown, nape, back, scapulars and tail black, faintly green-sheened; a large pinkish-buff oval panel on the inner wing-coverts contrasts with the otherwise black wing; underwing wholly whitish; underparts pale buff. Female: duller, upperparts brownish-black with paler feather margins, underparts streaked dark buff and brown, wing panel less obvious and browner. Juvenile: duller, more rufous, heavily streaked above and below.
- In the field — Flies with the neck drawn into an S. Call a measured grook or kekk-ek.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Bittern: Far larger, 69–81 cm against 27–38 cm; thickset, buffy-brown streaked and barred throughout with a black crown and nape, never black-backed with a pale wing panel.
  - Black-crowned Night Heron: Stockier, black crown and back with pale grey wings and white underparts, red eyes, short yellow legs, long white head plumes; no buff wing panel.
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Larger, 46–56 cm; white, stocky, short thick neck, sturdy yellow bill, hunched posture; breeding adults orange-buff on back, breast and crown.
  - Great Egret: All white and much larger, 80–104 cm with a wingspan of 131–170 cm against 40–58 cm; yellow bill, black legs and feet.

## Little Bunting
*Emberiza pusilla* · `litbun`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, about 15 g. Small bunting, looking stocky and compact in flight.
- Plumage — Chestnut face and cheeks, with a fine dark border behind them and often a small pale spot at the rear of the cheek; white malar stripe; black crown stripes with a pale central band; bold white eye-ring; lores pale rufous. Upperparts brown, heavily streaked; underparts white, finely dark-streaked on breast and sides. Sexes similar; female duller, winter birds plainer.
- Bare parts — Legs always pink, never red- or grey-brown.
- In the field — Call a distinctive sharp, hard zik; song a rolling siroo-sir-sir-siroo, melodic, mixing harsh rasping notes with clear ones.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Much larger and bulkier, 16–19 cm; plain streaked grey-brown with no chestnut cheeks or crown stripes; dark eye, yellowish mandibles, streaked gorget round the throat.
  - Reed Bunting: Male black-headed and black-throated with a white collar. Female's head streaked brown — Little Bunting shows evener chestnut cheeks, bolder eye-ring, pale crown stripe and pink legs.
  - Yellowhammer: Larger, 15.5–17 cm; male bright yellow-headed with rufous rump and white outer tail feathers, female still yellow-toned; never chestnut-cheeked.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 12–14 cm against 13–15 cm.

## Little Bustard
*Tetrax tetrax* · `litbus1`

- Jizz — 40–45 cm, wingspan 83–91 cm, 500–900 g. Smallest Palearctic bustard but still the size of a hen Pheasant; wings medium-long and rounded, primaries somewhat curved.
- Plumage — Breeding male: head and throat lead-grey; neck deep black with two white bands, one above and one below; upperparts sandy brown with dark markings, underparts white. Female and non-breeding male lack the neck pattern, sandy above with black spots and more vermiculated, white on breast and belly; female darker below than male. Juvenile like female.
- In the field — In flight the long wings look extensively white above, with black outer primaries and black primary coverts. Usually silent; male gives a snorting, raspberry-blowing prrt audible up to 500 m.
- Similar species —
  - Great Bustard: Vastly bigger: male around 115 cm long, 90–105 cm tall, wingspan 2.1–2.7 m and 5.8–18 kg — one of the heaviest flying birds.

## Little Crake
*Zapornia parva* · `litcra1`

- Jizz — 17–19 cm, wingspan 34–39 cm; male 30–72 g, female 36–65 g. Very small rail, with Baillon's the smallest of the region; bill short and straight, tail short, toes long.
- Plumage — Male: upperparts brown with blackish and white markings; face and underparts blue-grey, flanks only faintly barred. Female: underparts buff, grey confined to the face. Juvenile: as female but face and breast white. Undertail short and barred black and white. Downy chick black.
- Bare parts — Bill short and straight, yellow to olive-green with a red base; legs green, toes long.
- In the field — Probes mud or shallow water, also picking food by sight; eats insects and aquatic animals. Reed beds. Very secretive when breeding, then mostly heard: male a drawn-out nasal barking kva, slow then accelerating into a stammer, carrying 1–2 km.
- Similar species —
  - Baillon's Crake: Flanks and undertail strongly barred black and white; bill yellow or green with no red base; legs olive-green; a little smaller, 15–18 cm.
  - Spotted Crake: Dark barring and white spots on the flanks, which Little Crake lacks; larger, 19–22.5 cm; bill also yellow with a red base, so go on the flanks.
  - Western Swamphen: Chicken-sized, with huge feet, bright plumage and a red bill and frontal shield — no confusion with a tiny grey-and-brown crake.

## Little Egret
*Egretta garzetta* · `litegr`

- Jizz — 55–65 cm, 88–106 cm span, 280–710 g. Small, slender white heron, much smaller than Grey Heron or Great Egret; bill long, slim, straight and pointed; legs long.
- Plumage — Entirely white, though dark forms are largely bluish-grey. Breeding: two long narrow nape plumes forming a crest, looser-barbed breast plumes and elongated scapulars up to 200 mm; in winter the scapulars are short and normal. Juvenile: as non-breeding adult, sometimes with a few greyish or brownish feathers. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill and lores black, iris yellow, bare facial skin greenish-grey turning red in high courtship; legs black, feet yellow — juveniles greenish-black legs, duller feet.
- In the field — Mostly silent; croaking and bubbling calls at breeding colonies and a harsh alarm call when disturbed.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Bittern: Thickset buff-brown heron streaked and barred dark, with a black crown and nape; never white, and bigger at 69–81 cm and 0.87–1.94 kg.
  - Black-crowned Night Heron: Black crown and back, pale grey wings, white underparts, red eyes and short yellow legs; stocky, with a short bill and neck and a hunched posture.
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Stocky and hunched with a short thick neck, sturdy yellow bill and greyish-yellow legs; breeding birds orange-buff on crown, breast and back. 46–56 cm.
  - Great Egret: Far larger, 80–104 cm with a 131–170 cm span; yellow bill, and black legs and feet — no yellow feet.

## Little Grebe
*Tachybaptus ruficollis* · `litgre1`

- Jizz — 23–29 cm, wingspan 40–45 cm, 130–236 g. Small, compact, round-bodied grebe with a short neck, pointed bill and fluffy tailless powder-puff rear; legs set far back, so it walks badly.
- Plumage — Summer adult unmistakable: blackish-brown above, crown and hindneck black with a faint green sheen, lores and chin black, throat, cheeks, neck-sides and lower neck rich rufous; flanks blackish-brown with pale flecks, underparts glossy white. Winter and juvenile: rufous replaced by dull brownish-grey, buff-toned with darker back and cap; juvenile face streaked black and white.
- Bare parts — Bright yellow gape patch at the base of the bill; juvenile bill yellow with a small black tip, darkening to black with age.
- In the field — Shape varies: at rest the rear feathers are raised, the body looks round and the neck all but disappears; alarmed birds stretch the neck and lie flat. Rises easily, flies fast and low. Whinnying trilled weet-weet-weet.
- Similar species —
  - Eared Grebe: Larger, 28–34 cm; breeding bird black on head, neck, breast and upperparts with an ochre fan behind a vivid red eye — no rufous cheeks, no yellow gape.
  - Great Crested Grebe: Much larger, 46–51 cm on 59–73 cm wings, 0.9–1.5 kg; long-necked, head decorations in summer, whiter than other grebes in winter with white above the eye, bill pink.
  - Horned Grebe: Larger, 31–38 cm; erectable golden horns, black fan-shaped cheeks, chestnut neck and flanks in summer, black and white in winter; bill straight, pointed, white-tipped.
  - Red-necked Grebe: Bigger and much heavier, 750–1600 g; black cap to below the eye, very pale grey cheeks and throat, rusty neck, long pointed black bill with a yellow base.

## Little Gull
*Hydrocoloeus minutus* · `litgul`

- Jizz — 24–28 cm, wingspan 62–69 cm; adult males 82–127 g, females 68–113 g. The world's smallest gull, two-thirds of a common gull; large round head, short slim bill, short legs, near-square tail.
- Plumage — Adult summer: black hood; pale grey back and upperwing, white rump and tail, underparts white with a rosy flush; underwing dark grey with a narrow white trailing edge running to the tip. Winter: white head with black cap and ear-spot. Juvenile: blackish back and head, bold dark W across the upperwing, white underwing; second year near-adult with some black primaries.
- Bare parts — Bill dark red-brown in summer, looking black at range, black in winter; legs bright red, duller pink-red in winter; iris dark.
- In the field — Flies like a marsh tern or a nightjar — lively, jerky, half-bounding search flight low over the water. Adult wing-tip looks rounded, first-year pointed; dark underwing shows at long range.
- Similar species —
  - Black Tern: 22–26 cm, span 56–62 cm; dark grey back with white forewing, black head, neck and belly, white face with a dark triangle before the eye; bill slender, slightly decurved.
  - Little Tern: 21–25 cm, span 41–55 cm; white forehead in breeding plumage, thin sharp yellow bill with a black tip and yellow legs — no hood and no dark underwing.
  - Ross's Gull: 29–31 cm; slightly larger, longer and more pointed in the wing, with a wedge-shaped tail; summer adult has a neat black neck ring, not a hood, plus a pink flush.
  - Sabine's Gull: 27–33 cm, span 81–87 cm; striking upperwing of grey coverts, four black outer primaries and white inner primaries and secondaries; forked white tail; black bill with a yellow tip.

## Little Owl
*Athene noctua* · `litowl1`

- Jizz — 22–27.5 cm, wingspan 50–57 cm, 140–200 g. Song-thrush sized but looks bigger in its loose plumage; plump and compact, head broad, round and flat-topped, no ear tufts; short tail, long legs.
- Plumage — Dark sepia to grey-brown above, spotted, streaked and barred whitish, the spots small and dense on the crown, larger and rounder on the upper back; white V on the nape running into a white nape band; underparts pale, streaked brown; whitish supercilia over a weak facial disc give a frowning, stern look. Juvenile: duller, no white crown spots.
- Bare parts — Iris lemon- to sulphur-yellow; cere strongly swollen; toes only sparsely feathered.
- In the field — Bounding flight like a woodpecker. Song a soft low whistle rising slightly at the end, repeated every 5–10 seconds; commonest call a querulous falling kiew or kilo; yelping and barking near the nest.
- Similar species —
  - Boreal Owl: Large-headed, 21–28 cm, wingspan 54–62 cm; pearly white spots on brown back and wings, whitish underparts with rust-coloured streaks, olive-brown crown with small white droplets.
  - Eurasian Scops-Owl: Smaller, 19–21 cm, wingspan 47–54 cm; perches upright showing small ear-tufts; grey-brown with paler face, underparts and shoulder line; strong direct flight on long narrow wings.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Vastly bigger — wingspan 131–188 cm, largest birds to 4.6 kg, among the largest owls in the world. The sources give no plumage detail for it.
  - Snowy Owl: Mostly white, wingspan 116–166 cm, ear tufts vestigial, eyes bright yellow, head relatively small; on the ground looks like a pale rock or lump of snow.

## Little Ringed Plover
*Thinornis dubius* · `lirplo`

- Jizz — 15–18 cm, wingspan 32–45 cm, 25–55 g. Small, slim plover with a short bill; smaller and slighter than Common Ringed Plover; all three toes webbed.
- Plumage — Back and wings grey-brown, breast and belly white with a single black neckband; cap brown, forehead white, black mask through the eye with white above. Sexes alike. Race dubius: orbital ring distinctly broader, with a large red patch at the bill-base.
- Bare parts — Bill short and dark, red at the base; legs flesh to brown-yellow; iris black with a conspicuous yellow orbital ring.
- In the field — Commonest call a downslurred piu, alarm a rougher prii; song given in slow-winged display flight by day and night — rhythmic pri-pri-pri and rasping krri-a, krri-a.
- Similar species —
  - Common Ringed Plover: Larger, 17–19.5 cm, wingspan 35–41 cm; bill orange with black tip, legs orange, no yellow eye-ring; head pattern differs; only the outer two toes webbed.
  - Eurasian Dotterel: Striking whitish supercilium in every plumage; summer adult chestnut-breasted with white breast-line and black belly; legs yellow, bill black; wings plain in flight.
  - Kentish Plover: Two incomplete dark breast-bands, one each side, never a full black band; male has a black head-bar and ear-coverts and rufous crown; bill and legs black.
  - Sociable Lapwing: Larger, with long black legs, black crown and eye-stripe bordered white above and below, white tail with black terminal band, brown, white and grey wings.

## Little Stint
*Calidris minuta* · `litsti`

- Jizz — 12–15.5 cm, wingspan 27–31 cm, 20–30 g. Tiny Calidris, two-thirds the bulk of a Dunlin; bill short, straight and fine-tipped; long primary projection, toes unwebbed.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: rusty upperparts finely streaked black on head and breast, white throat, strong white V on the back, white belly. Non-breeding: plain grey, difficult. Juvenile: pale crown stripes, pinkish breast, dark feather centres above, whitish or bright rufous coverts, clear white Vs; whiter about head and breast than other small waders.
- Bare parts — Bill fine and dark; legs black.
- In the field — Movements quick and eager; in flight the tail sides are grey, not white. Call a sharp stit.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Far bigger, 23–26 cm and 100–225 g, dumpy with a short neck and a bill as long as the head; winter plumage uniformly pale grey.
  - Dunlin: Half again as long, 16–21 cm, with a longer, slightly decurved bill and a black belly when breeding; juvenile's back Vs weaker than here.
  - Ruff: Pot-bellied and small-headed with a long neck and long pale green to orange legs, white ovals at the tail sides, and far heavier at 130–254 g.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Short yellow legs and a yellow-based bill; dark and purple-glossed above, breast smeared grey, rump black. Larger, 19–22 cm.

## Little Tern
*Sternula albifrons* · `litter1`

- Jizz — 21–25 cm, wingspan 41–55 cm, about 45 g. Smallest tern, the size of a Ringed Plover; large-headed, bill long and thin, about head length; legs short; wings long and narrow; tail short and forked, without long streamers.
- Plumage — Summer adult: black crown and black eye-mask separated by a narrow white wedge on the forehead; upperparts grey, underparts white. Winter: forehead and face much whiter, front of the crown and much of the mask lost. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Summer: bill yellow with a black tip, legs orange-yellow. Winter: bill black, legs duller.
- In the field — Wingbeats very fast. Perched birds look compact and flat-crowned with the bill held horizontal. Call sharp and grating, kr'rit; a fast chattering kerre-kit kerre-kit serves for display and alarm.
- Similar species —
  - Black Tern: Dark grey back with black head, neck and belly, white face and forewing, square grey tail; short weak black bill and dark legs; no white forehead wedge.
  - Little Gull: A gull: pale grey back and upperwings with white primaries against very dark grey underwing, black hood or cap, red legs; larger, 24–28 cm.
  - Ross's Gull: Larger, 29–31 cm; pale grey above, white below with a pink flush and a neat black neck ring; wedge-shaped tail, red legs.
  - Sabine's Gull: Bold wing pattern — grey coverts, four black outer primaries, white inner primaries and secondaries; forked white tail, dark grey hood, black bill with yellow tip.

## Long-billed Dowitcher
*Limnodromus scolopaceus* · `lobdow`

- Jizz — 26–30 cm, wingspan 48–55 cm, 88–130 g. Stocky medium-sized sandpiper; bill very long, straight and about twice the head length, 6–8 cm, the tip thickish and drooped; legs fairly long.
- Plumage — Whitish supercilium, dark loral stripe running past the eye. Breeding: crown dark, neck, breast and belly rufous, breast barred black, flanks barred white. Non-breeding: drab grey, upperparts and breast darker, belly white. Tail barred black and white, black bars near twice the white's width; white wedge up the back to mid-mantle. Juvenile: as breeding adult, paler.
- Bare parts — Bill dark, sometimes paler at the base; legs and feet pale olive to yellow-olive; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Feeds with a steady sewing-machine action. In flight a narrow white wedge on the back and a white trailing edge to the wing. Call a short, whippy kjipp.
- Similar species —
  - Bar-tailed Godwit: Bigger, 33–41 cm; bill bi-coloured, pink-based and slightly upturned, not straight and dark; breeding neck, breast and belly unbroken brick red, unbarred.
  - Black-tailed Godwit: Larger, with much longer bill, neck and legs; bold black-and-white wings and white rump in flight; winter breast and upperparts uniform brown-grey.
  - Broad-billed Sandpiper: Much smaller, 16–18 cm; split double supercilium and pale crown stripe, shorter legs, and a bill distinctly kinked down at the tip.
  - Common Greenshank: Bill long but grey at the base and looking upturned, not straight, dark and twice head-length; legs long and green; call a fluty teu-teu-teu.

## Long-eared Owl
*Asio otus* · `loeowl`

- Jizz — 31–37 cm, 86–98 cm span, 220–400 g. Slim, long-winged owl with relatively narrow wings and long erectile ear tufts set nearer the centre of the head than in most owls; wingtips reach below the tail when perched.
- Plumage — Ochraceous-tawny with a grey or brown wash, overlaid with blackish vertical streaks heaviest on wings and back; scapulars marked whitish; dark carpal patches and broad buff to almost orange panels at the base of the primaries; flight feathers clearly barred. Underside paler, upper breast dusky-streaked, below that a herring-bone pattern. Female darker and rustier.
- Bare parts — Iris bright orange-yellow; tarsus and toes fully feathered.
- In the field — By day sits against a trunk with plumage compressed to a narrow, elongated shape, hard to find; easier on migration over land and near the coast. Male's song a deep oh every 2.5 seconds; young beg pii-eh, audible a kilometre.
- Similar species —
  - Short-eared Owl: Big-headed, short-necked and broad-winged, upper breast heavily streaked, flight floppy and moth-like; yellow-orange eyes in black rings. 34–43 cm, 85–110 cm span.
  - Tawny Owl: Robust, with a large round head, no ear tufts, a plain facial disc and dark brown eyes; much heavier at 37–46 cm and 385–800 g.
  - American Barn Owl: Pale-coloured, long-winged and short square-tailed, with no ear tufts. Similar span, 80–95 cm, at 33–35 cm long.
  - Boreal Owl: Far smaller, 21–28 cm and 54–62 cm span; large head with no ear tufts, brown with pearly-white spots above, whitish rust-streaked below, eyes yellow.

## Long-legged Buzzard
*Buteo rufinus* · `lolbuz1`

- Jizz — 50–66 cm, around 1,035–1,100 g. Large, sturdy Buteo; large bill on a smallish head, long fingered wings, rather long tail, long legs with powerful feet; eagle-like aloft.
- Plumage — Pale and rufous morphs: plain creamy-rufous head with a dark eye-line and darker nape, pale streaked breast, belly, flanks and trousers rufous-brown; tail unbarred light rufous-orange; dark carpal patch and dark rump. Dark morph: head and body dark brown, tail white barred dark with a broad blackish terminal band. Juvenile: pale head, white breast, dirty-white tail.
- In the field — Sluggish; perches openly and upright on rocks, crags and pylons, and often stands on the ground, walking slowly with a waddle. Underwing usually pale. Call like Common Buzzard's but softer and less plaintive.
- Similar species —
  - Common Buzzard: Smaller and more compact, 40–58 cm; round head with a slender bill, short tail, short bare tarsi, narrower wings. Call harder and more plaintive.
  - Rough-legged Hawk: Little in the sources beyond bulk: Long-legged is described only as the larger and heavier of the two, against 46–68 cm and 600–1,660 g here.
  - Lesser Spotted Eagle: Dark overall with pale brown head and wing coverts, a white patch on the upperwing and a clear white V on the rump; longer-winged, 146–168 cm.
  - Eurasian Goshawk: Blue-grey or brownish-grey above, barred or streaked below over grey or white; short broad wings and a long tail for forest manoeuvring, with thick toes.

## Long-tailed Duck
*Clangula hyemalis* · `lotduc`

- Jizz — 39–47 cm, wingspan 65–82 cm, 600–1,000 g; adult male's slender central tail feathers add 10–15 cm. Small, round-headed, short stubby bill; narrow, pointed, all-dark wings.
- Plumage — Complex moult, at least three plumages a year, all pied white, brown and black, with wholly dark wings and white underparts. Male winter: white head and neck, dark cheek patch, dark breast, mostly white body; summer: head, neck and back dark, cheek patch white. Female: brown back, short pointed tail; head white with dark crown in winter, dark in summer.
- Bare parts — Male's bill dark grey crossed by a pink band, often all black outside the breeding season.
- In the field — Wings held rather swept back and beaten in supple, fast strokes mostly below the horizontal, giving a bat-like jizz. Males vocal, a musical yodelling ow, ow, owal-ow.
- Similar species —
  - Common Scoter: Bulky and large-billed; drake all black, female brown with pale cheeks; no long tail and no white body or head.
  - Common Eider: Far bigger, 50–71 cm, bulky, big-headed, wedge-shaped bill; drake white-backed and black-bellied, with no long tail.
  - Common Goldeneye: Larger, 40–50 cm, peak-headed; drake has a round white patch between bill and eye, and both sexes show white in the inner wing.
  - Common Merganser: Much larger, 58–72 cm, long-necked, with a long thin red serrated bill; drake white-bodied with a dark green head.

## Long-tailed Jaeger
*Stercorarius longicaudus* · `lotjae`

- Jizz — Wingspan 88–117 cm, 220–444 g — smallest of the skuas. Slim, small-headed, long narrow wings, tern-like; adult's central tail feathers hugely elongated, streamers to 15 cm; bill stout but shorter than Parasitic's.
- Plumage — Adult: glossy black cap and face, grey to grey-brown back and coverts, contrasting near-black primaries with no white flash; neck-sides, nape and throat white to faintly yellowish, breast white shading gradually into brown-grey belly and vent; underwing lacks a pale primary-base flash. Juveniles in light, intermediate and dark morphs, colder and greyer than Parasitic.
- Bare parts — Bill dark in summer; juveniles show a pale blue-grey bill base contrasting with a dark tip.
- In the field — Hovers over breeding territory. Takes fish at the sea surface, lemmings near arctic nest sites, also insects and birds; kleptoparasitic, chasing terns and small gulls to rob them, chiefly in winter.
- Similar species —
  - Great Skua: Far bulkier, 50–58 cm and 1.17–1.5 kg, herring-gull size with barrel chest and bold white wing flashes; streaked greyish brown, short blunt tail, powerful flight.
  - Parasitic Jaeger: Bulkier, browner-toned, less tern-like; light morph shows a white primary flash and a short pointed tail projection about 7 cm, not long fine streamers.
  - Pomarine Jaeger: Much bulkier and broader-winged, 110–125 cm span; blunt spoon-shaped twisted streamers and a diagnostic double white flash on the underwing that Long-tailed lacks.

## Long-tailed Tit
*Aegithalos caudatus* · `lottit1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, 7–10 g; tail 7–9 cm, more than half the total length, long and narrow. Tiny and round-bodied with a short stubby bill 6–7 mm and a wing of only 6–7 cm.
- Plumage — Mainly black and white with variable grey and pink: black mantle, wine-red to rufous scapulars, black white-edged tail. Head and underparts vary geographically — northern caudatus pure white-headed, sharply black-naped, no breast band, white belly, white or pink flanks; europaeus and rosaceus have a broad dark band through the eye and reddish-tinged underparts; alpinus darker-faced, black-bibbed, shorter-tailed.
- Bare parts — Bill black, short and fine; eye peppercorn-black with a yellow-orange upper lid.
- In the field — Flocks call constantly and are heard before seen: a high single pit, a triple trill eez-eez-eez or sii-sii-siih and a rattling buzzy schnuur or tserr, faster and louder over open ground. Song quiet, twittering.

## Mallard
*Anas platyrhynchos* · `mallar3`

- Jizz — 50–65 cm, wingspan 81–98 cm, 0.7–1.6 kg. Medium-large dabbling duck, heavier than most; large head, long fairly heavy bill, short tail.
- Plumage — Male: glossy bottle-green head, narrow white collar, purple-tinged chestnut breast, pale grey belly, grey-brown wings; black stern with curled black upper tail-coverts, outer tail feathers beige-white. Both sexes: iridescent purple-blue speculum with black then broad white border. Female: mottled buff to dark brown, buff cheeks, supercilium, throat and neck, darker crown and eye-stripe. Juvenile: as female.
- Bare parts — Male's bill plain dull yellow to yellowish-orange, black-tipped; female's orange with dark grey ridge, or mottled brown; legs yellow to yellow-red.
- In the field — White border to the speculum shows in flight in both sexes. Eclipse male (July–August) female-like; told by the plain yellow bill, darker red-brown breast and more contrasting head.
- Similar species —
  - Green-winged Teal: Far smaller, 34–43 cm against 50–65 cm; drake looks grey with a chestnut head, green teardrop patch, white flank stripe and yellowish stern.
  - Northern Pintail: Overlaps in size but slimmer, elongated and gracile, neck longer and male's tail long; drake chocolate-headed with a white stripe up the neck-side.
  - Gadwall: Smaller; drake patterned grey with black stern and brilliant white speculum; female told by dark orange-edged bill, white speculum and white belly.
  - Wood Duck: About three-quarters the length, 47–54 cm; both sexes crested, male multicoloured with a white neck flare, female with a white eye-ring.

## Mandarin Duck
*Aix galericulata* · `manduc`

- Jizz — 41–51 cm, wingspan 71 cm; male 571–693 g, female 428–608 g. Small duck, shorter-bodied than most dabbling ducks and slightly smaller than Wood Duck; head looks large and crested; tail small and pointed.
- Plumage — Male: large white crescent over the eye, reddish face and whiskers, metallic-green crest; purple breast with two vertical white bars; ruddy flanks; two orange sail feathers raised on the back. Female: greyish-brown, slender white eye-ring and stripe back from the eye, paler below, small white flank stripe, weaker crest. Eclipse male: female-like but crestless, breast and flanks clearer yellow-brown.
- Bare parts — Male's bill small and red, dull carmine in eclipse or bright yellow-orange; female's bill dark with a pale tip.
- In the field — Dark green speculum shows in flight in both sexes, but the small pointed tail is the surest mark on flying birds. Flightless for about a month in July while moulting.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Duck: Larger, 47–54 cm; drake has red eyes and a white flare down the neck, no orange sail feathers; female very like female Mandarin, with white eye-ring and whitish throat.
  - Mallard: Much bigger, 50–65 cm; drake bottle-green head, white collar, brown breast, grey body; no crest and no sail feathers. Ducklings' eye-stripe runs right to the bill.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: Longer, 46–56 cm; head black glossed green-blue with white forehead and cheeks and a white ear-patch, breast barred black and white, rust flanks; no sail feathers.
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Drake has a chestnut head with creamy crown, grey flanks, pink breast, black stern and a bold white upper-wing patch; 42–52 cm; crestless, no white eye-crescent.

## Manx Shearwater
*Puffinus puffinus* · `manshe`

- Jizz — 30–38 cm, wingspan 75–89 cm, 350–575 g. Medium-sized shearwater, about Black-headed Gull size.
- Plumage — Slate-black above, pure white below. Head and neck sides mottled grey-and-white but sharply demarcated — white bristly feathers cut a crescent between ear-coverts and hind-neck; white bulge behind the wing on the rump. Underwing mostly white with a broad dark grey trailing edge, widest at the tip, a narrow dark leading edge, and often a dark axillary bar.
- Bare parts — Bill dark brown-grey to blackish, often paler at the base; iris blackish; legs and feet pink or bluish flesh, dark brown along the outer edges.
- In the field — Shearing flight, canting side to side on stiff wings with few beats, wingtips almost touching the water, looking like a flying cross flashing black then white. Silent at sea; raucous croons, howls and screams at night at the colony.
- Similar species —
  - Barolo Shearwater: Pale face with a darkly contrasting eye, a silvery panel in the upperwing, shorter and rounder wings, and blue feet.
  - Balearic Shearwater: Larger, and far less contrasting — dingy brown-white below with brown-smudged axillaries and brownish belly and vent, not clean white.
  - Yelkouan Shearwater: Near-identical: brown-grey rather than blackish above, shorter tail so the feet project, usually a black axillary bar and dark undertail-coverts, and no pale cheek crescent.
  - Bulwer's Petrel: All-brown with a long pointed tail; smaller, 25–29 cm against 30–38 cm; buoyant twisting flight, not stiff-winged shearing.

## Marbled Duck
*Marmaronetta angustirostris* · `martea1`

- Jizz — 39–42 cm, about 540 g, wings long at 63–70 cm. Small, slim duck; a shaggy nape-tuft makes the head look large; tail long, bill long, narrow and dark.
- Plumage — Pale sandy to light grey-brown, diffusely blotched off-white — cream feathers with dark grey-brown edges; underparts barred; dark eye-patch, sometimes running back to the nape; tail white. Sexes alike, the female's crest weaker and she averages smaller; juvenile similar but more heavily blotched off-white. No speculum on the secondaries.
- Bare parts — Bill long, narrow and black; breeding male with a narrow blue band behind the nail, blue bill edges and lower base of the upper mandible.
- In the field — Dabbles and up-ends in shallow water and behaves mostly like a dabbling duck, though its habits place it with the diving ducks; wings look pale and unpatterned in flight, and the flight is characteristically low. Usually silent.
- Similar species —
  - Green-winged Teal: Smaller, 34–43 cm; drake grey with chestnut head and iridescent green teardrop, yellowish stern and white flank stripe; shows a speculum and is never blotched off-white.
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Larger, 42–52 cm; drake chestnut-headed with creamy crown, grey flanks, pink breast, black stern, green speculum and a brilliant white upper-wing patch; no dark eye-patch.
  - Northern Shoveler: Huge spatulate bill; drake green-headed with white breast and chestnut flanks, pale blue forewing in flight; 44–52 cm; female mottled brown, not blotched.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: Bigger, 46–56 cm; head black glossed green with white forehead, cheeks and ear-patch, breast barred black and white, rust flanks; bill blue-grey with black tip.

## Marmora's Warbler
*Curruca sarda* · `marwar1`

- Jizz — 13–16 cm; wingspan 25–30 cm. Small, long-tailed, large-headed warbler with a fine pointed bill.
- Plumage — Uniformly smoke-grey above and below, with no brick-red on the underparts and no white spots on the throat; throat paler than the rest. Male: darker patches on the forehead and between the eye and the bill. Female paler and duller, but likewise without a trace of brown. Juvenile grey below with a whiter throat, belly and breast centre.
- Bare parts — Bill base, iris and legs red; the juvenile's iris dark.
- In the field — Song a fast rattling twitter in a clear voice, often ending in a trill; alarm and contact call a hard tjreck.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes in the wing and a white throat, never uniform grey. Call a nasal wed-wed or woid-woid.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Underparts whitish rather than grey, and a dark bandit mask through the eye on a grey head; throat white. Legs and iris not red.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male black-hooded with a sharply white throat and whitish underparts; female brown above and buff below. This bird is grey throughout.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male has brick-red underparts and a white malar streak; female brown above, whitish below with a pink flush. No red or pink here, and no moustache.

## Marsh Sandpiper
*Tringa stagnatilis* · `marsan`

- Jizz — 20–26 cm, wingspan 40–60 cm, 45–120 g. Like a small, slim, elegant greenshank; bill long, fine and straight; legs very long and thin.
- Plumage — Breeding: upperparts cream to pink-grey or greyish brown, boldly spotted and vermiculated black; breast densely black-spotted, flanks with black chevrons; forehead, cheeks and supercilium whitish, indistinctly streaked. Winter: grey above, white below, head and neck whitish. Juvenile: upperparts brown with cream spots. A diffuse white supercilium and white feathering round the bill base in all plumages.
- Bare parts — Legs long, thin and yellowish.
- In the field — In flight, dark wings, a very long white wedge up the back almost to the nape, and legs projecting well past the tail. Call a clearly diphthongal pjeu or teu-teu-teu, faster and higher than greenshank's.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Sandpiper: Much the same size but shorter-legged and less elegant; brown-backed, with only a small, weakly contrasting white rump-patch, not a long white back wedge.
  - Common Greenshank: Larger, with a heavier bill whose lower edge gives it an upturned look, and greenish legs; call a rapid three-note fluty teu-teu-teu, lower than marsh's.
  - Common Redshank: Red legs and a black-tipped red bill; white shows on the wings as well as up the back; bulkier, 24–30 cm.
  - Green Sandpiper: Plump and dark greenish-brown with a brilliant white rump, not a long back wedge; bill short and dark, legs dark green; 21–24 cm.

## Marsh Tit
*Poecile palustris* · `martit2`

- Jizz — 11.5–13 cm, wingspan 18–20 cm, 11–12 g. Small compact tit with a proportionately large head; bill short, conical and slightly domed.
- Plumage — Cap and nape glossy black with a blue sheen, reaching to mid-eye and back into the nape; bib below the bill small and black; cheeks white, evenly narrow, dusky brown on the ear-coverts. Upperparts, wings and tail grey-brown, rump warmer beige, tertials only slightly pale-fringed. Underparts off-white, buff on flanks and undertail-coverts. Juvenile duller-capped and whiter below.
- Bare parts — Bill black with paler cutting edges; iris dark to blackish-brown; legs dark grey to slate, tinged blue.
- In the field — Call an explosive pitchou or pitjä stressed on the second note, often running into pitchou-bee-bee-bee. Song well defined and varied, over five per bird: ringing schip-schip-schip, liquid tu-tu-tu, sweet tyeu-tyeu-tyeu.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Tit: Cap dull sooty and running onto the mantle; pale panel on the secondaries, larger bib, cheek broadening to the rear, thicker nape; call a nasal zizi tää tää tää.
  - Gray-headed Chickadee: Larger (12.5–14 cm); head dark brown rather than glossy black, mantle cinnamon-brown, wing feathers pale-fringed, flanks washed rusty.
  - Sombre Tit: Larger and proportionately longer-tailed, head often peaked; brown to grey-black cap spreads onto the head-sides and a bigger bib squeezes the cheek to a narrow triangle.
  - African Blue Tit: Deep glossy blue crown with white forehead and supercilium, blue upperparts and yellow underparts; no black cap or bib.

## Marsh Warbler
*Acrocephalus palustris* · `marwar3`

- Jizz — About 13 cm, wingspan 17–21 cm, 11–14 g. Medium-sized Acrocephalus; pointed bill, forehead less flat and bill less sharply pointed than reed warbler.
- Plumage — Upperparts grey-brown, greyer on the back than reed warbler; underparts yellowish-white; throat whitish. Sexes alike.
- In the field — Moves adeptly in dense vegetation; avoids the pure reed stands reed warbler favours. Song loud, very fast, varied, from dusk to dawn; superb mimic, imitations woven into typical reed-warbler phrases.
- Similar species —
  - Sedge Warbler: Streaked brown back and wings, black-streaked crown, prominent whitish supercilium and warm unstreaked rump; marsh warbler is plain grey-brown above with no bold supercilium.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Near-identical; reed warbler browner on the back, forehead flatter, bill more sharply pointed. Favours pure reed; song slower, chattering, far less varied.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Plainer olive-brown, lacking warm tones; short rounded wings, long thick bill; short whitish supercilium between bill and eye, with a dusky eye-stripe.
  - Great Reed Warbler: Thrush-sized, 16–21 cm against about 13 cm, 22–38 g against 11–14 g; stronger supercilium, very loud far-carrying song.

## Masked Shrike
*Lanius nubicus* · `masshr1`

- Jizz — 17–18.5 cm, wingspan 24–26.5 cm, 20–23 g. Smallest and slenderest shrike; head small and flat-fronted, bill relatively small with a tomial tooth, tail long and strongly graduated.
- Plumage — Male: deep black above; crown and nape black, forehead, cheeks, supercilium and neck sides white, split by a narrow black eye-stripe; white scapular patch and white primary bases; outer tail feathers white; underparts white, orange on flanks and breast. Female: duller, brownish-black above, white patches smaller and buff-tinged. Juvenile: grey-brown, barred head to rump, forehead pale grey, underparts barred off-white.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs and toes slate-grey to black; iris brown.
- In the field — Tail never still when perched — wagged, cocked and twisted. In flight the white scapular and primary patches join as a broad white sickle.
- Similar species —
  - Red-backed Shrike: Same length, 16–18 cm, but bulkier, bigger-headed and longer-billed; male rufous-backed with a grey crown, not black above with a white crown; female brown and vermiculated.
  - Woodchat Shrike: Chestnut crown and nape and a white rump — masked has a white crown and dark rump; bulkier, less slender, shorter-tailed; 17–19 cm.
  - Iberian Gray Shrike: Grey above, not black, with a greyish-pink wash below, a broad black mask and clear white supercilium; head larger, bill stronger.
  - Great Gray Shrike: Much larger, 22–26 cm and 60–70 g, and grey above; masked is black-backed, white-crowned and only 17–18.5 cm.

## Meadow Pipit
*Anthus pratensis* · `meapip1`

- Jizz — 14.5–15 cm, 15–22 g. Small slim pipit, House Sparrow-sized but more graceful and slender-looking; bill thin and pointed; hind claw conspicuously long, longer than the rest of the hind toe.
- Plumage — Brown above with an olive to grey-green cast and marked dark back-streaking, buff below; darker barring over most of the plumage, breast finely streaked and little yellow on it; supercilium whitish; tail brown with narrow white outer edges.
- Bare parts — Legs pale pinkish-yellow to reddish.
- In the field — Ground bird of open country, seldom perching in trees. Song high, thin, repetitive, accelerating at the end, given in a short song flight begun before take-off from a low rise and rarely returning to it; call a faint ist.
- Similar species —
  - Rock Pipit: Larger (16.5–17 cm), smoky-olive above and weakly streaked, buff below with heavy diffuse streaks; legs, bill and iris dark brown to blackish, with a pale eye-ring.
  - Tree Pipit: Slightly larger, heavier-billed, shorter hind claw; buff breast contrasts with a white belly, face markings stronger; perches in trees; call a strong spek, song slowing at the end.
  - Red-throated Pipit: Summer adult brick-red on face and throat; at all seasons more striped — heavy streaks on cap, back, rump, flanks and chest, whitish mantle stripes; flight call psii.
  - Water Pipit: Larger (15–17 cm) and greyer, with a grey head and broad white supercilium, weakly streaked upperparts and pink-buff underparts; legs and bill blackish, not pink.

## Mediterranean Gull
*Ichthyaetus melanocephalus* · `medgul1`

- Jizz — 37–40 cm, wingspan 94–102 cm. Relatively small but strongly built gull; large round head, very short neck; short, stout bill; long pointed wings projecting well beyond the fan-shaped tail.
- Plumage — Breeding adult white with very pale grey mantle and wings and pure white primaries, no black tips; black hood reaching the nape, bold white eye-crescents. Non-breeding: hood reduced to a dusky bandit mask through the eye with grey streaking on the hindcrown. Juvenile: scaly mantle, brown secondaries, grey scapulars, blackish-brown primaries and tail-tip, white below.
- Bare parts — Bill short but stout, dark red with a black subterminal band and yellow to orange tip; legs dark red. Juvenile: bill grey-pink, dark-tipped; legs slate-grey.
- In the field — Often joins black-headed gull flocks and appears dominant among them. First-year: narrow black tail band against a white rump, mostly white underwing; second-winter near-adult with only streaks of black in the primaries.
- Similar species —
  - Audouin's Gull: Described only as a large gull; Mediterranean is a relatively small gull at 37–40 cm. Sources give no plumage marks to separate them.
  - Pallas's Gull: Far larger, 55–72 cm with a 142–170 cm span; black-hooded but grey-backed, and no other gull of that size carries a black hood.
  - Arctic Tern: Tern, not gull: 33–36 cm, deeply forked tail, high-aspect wings with translucent tips; black cap ends on the nape leaving white cheeks; dark red bill.
  - Armenian Gull: Much larger, 52–62 cm; no black hood, darker grey back, dark eyes, extensive black wingtips with small white spots; short bill with a black band.

## Mediterranean Short-toed Lark
*Alaudala rufescens* · `mstlar1`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm. Small, pale lark with a short, stubby, finch-like bill and a small, rounded head; about three primary tips show beyond the coverts.
- Plumage — Greyish-brown above, dark-streaked, white below, with fine streaking across the breast and a pale supercilium. No dark neck patches. Sexes alike. Colour varies across the range and is not a reliable mark; overall browner and less rufous than its relatives.
- In the field — Song faster, richer and more varied than Greater Short-toed Lark's, with mimicry and a dry buzzing call woven in; in song flight it circles with tail spread, at times on characteristically slow wingbeats.
- Similar species —
  - Turkestan Short-toed Lark: Larger, greyer and less rufous, with finer breast streaking; song phrases longer, with longer pauses between them.
  - Greater Short-toed Lark: A dark patch on each side of the neck and an unstreaked breast; bill stronger, more conical, pinkish with a grey culmen; only one or two primary tips show.
  - Wood Lark: Bold white supercilia meeting on the nape, a small crest and a very short tail; song a melodious lu-lu-lu. Slightly larger, 13.5–15 cm.
  - Crested Lark: Much bigger, 17–18 cm, with a conspicuous crest raised at all times and a short tail with pale brown, not white, outer feathers.

## Melodious Warbler
*Hippolais polyglotta* · `melwar1`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm, wingspan 17.5–20 cm, 10–13 g. Large-headed, crown feathers often raised; bill broad-based and relatively short; wing clearly short; tail rather short and square-cut.
- Plumage — Plain brown-green above — browner than icterine — and light yellow below. Lores, eyering and sides of the bill pale. Wing uniform: no pale panel formed by tertial and secondary edges. Sexes alike, the male often stronger-coloured; juvenile much paler overall with a faintly indicated pale wing panel.
- Bare parts — Legs brown to brownish; bill strong and pointed.
- In the field — Favours open bushy scrub. Song a pleasant babbling, fluent and sustained but faster and much less varied than icterine and without mimicry; calls a sparrow-like rattling trrrr, a quiet tuk, a chattering chret-chet.
- Similar species —
  - Icterine Warbler: Greyer-green above and evenly light yellow below; pale-edged secondaries and tertials form a wing panel; longer primary projection, grey to bluish legs; song more varied, with mimicry.
  - Olive-tree Warbler: Much bigger at 16–18 cm, 19–23 g; long thick bill with obvious orange-yellow lower mandible; pale olive-grey above with a pale wing panel, whitish below; tail long, dark grey.
  - Upcher's Warbler: 14–15 cm; rather grey plumage, slightly longer bill, shorter wings and a longer tail that it moves constantly like a chat or a Sylvia.
  - House Sparrow: A sparrow at 14–18 cm, 24–39.5 g: compact, full-chested, big rounded head and a stout conical seed-eating bill. Only the rattling trrrr call invites confusion.

## Merlin
*Falco columbarius* · `merlin`

- Jizz — 24–33 cm, 50–73 cm span; male 125–210 g, female 190–300 g. Smallest falcon; compact and robustly built, shorter-tailed and more pointed-winged than a kestrel, the wings fairly broad.
- Plumage — Male: slate blue-grey above with a rusty nape, buff to orange underparts heavily streaked dark, grey tail with a black terminal band. Female and juvenile: grey-brown to dark brown above, whitish buff below spotted and streaked brown, tail strongly barred. Face weakly patterned — faint whitish supercilium, indistinct malar; remiges blackish, tail with three or four wide dark bands.
- Bare parts — Cere yellow; blue-grey in juveniles.
- In the field — In flight recalls a much larger Peregrine. Separated from compact small hawks such as Sparrowhawk by the pointed wings.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Much larger, 36–42 cm with an 87–104 cm span, long-winged and slim; dark morph sooty brown, pale morph with black underwing-coverts against paler flight feathers.
  - Eurasian Kestrel: Longer-tailed and blunter-winged, 32–39 cm and 65–82 cm span; chestnut-brown above with blackish spotting, not slate blue-grey.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Similar in flight but far bigger, 34–58 cm and 330–1,500 g; sexes alike in markings. Merlin's face is the less strongly patterned of the two.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Slate-grey above with two short black moustaches, unstreaked white throat and rufous thighs and vent; longer-winged, 29–36 cm, span 74–84 cm.

## Middle Spotted Woodpecker
*Dendrocoptes medius* · `miswoo1`

- Jizz — 20–22 cm, 50–85 g; wingspan about 34 cm. Only slightly smaller than Great Spotted but looks much smaller: short, slender, light grey bill and a rounded, pale head.
- Plumage — Crown bright red without a black border; face largely dirty white, black head markings weak and no black loral band; upperparts glossy black with white shoulder patches and broad white barring on the coverts; underparts white, breast pale yellowish, flanks boldly streaked dark grey; belly pink, deepening to red at the vent. Female: crown slightly duller.
- Bare parts — Bill short, light grey, not powerful.
- In the field — Crown feathers often raised in aggressive or sexual encounters. The only European woodpecker in which the sexes barely differ in colour.
- Similar species —
  - Great Spotted Woodpecker: Adult crown black, male red only on the nape; black lines run from shoulder to nape and bill; heavier bill; scarlet lower belly, not pink with streaked flanks.
  - Syrian Woodpecker: Larger, 23–25 cm, with a longer bill; adult crown black with a crimson nape spot on the male; juvenile's red crown narrowly edged black, and the malar reaches the bill.
  - Eurasian Three-toed Woodpecker: Male's crown yellow, no red on the bird at all; back white barred black and flanks barred, not streaked; no white shoulder patch.
  - Eurasian Wryneck: Not pied: slim, thrush-shaped, cryptically barred and mottled pale brown, rufous and blackish with wider black streaks; no red anywhere.

## Mistle Thrush
*Turdus viscivorus* · `misthr1`

- Jizz — 26–29 cm, 93–167 g, wingspan 45 cm. Largest European thrush; long-tailed, stocky, standing very upright on the ground.
- Plumage — Pale grey-brown above; chin and throat greyish-white; yellowish-buff breast and off-white belly marked with round black spots, denser on the lower breast; throat centre unspotted, often a dark patch each side of the upper breast. Outer tail feathers white-tipped, underwing-coverts white. Sexes alike. Juvenile: paler above with creamy teardrop centres, smaller spots below.
- Bare parts — Eyes dark brown; bill blackish with a yellowish base to the lower mandible; legs and feet yellowish-brown.
- In the field — Strongly undulating flight, wings closed after each burst of beats; powerful, in longer arcs than song thrush. Call a hard trr or zrr; song short melancholy fluting phrases, often given at midday.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blackbird: Male blackbird is glossy black with yellow bill and eye-ring, female sooty-brown only weakly mottled; neither shows round black spots on a pale breast.
  - Redwing: Redwing is much smaller, 20–24 cm, with a creamy-white supercilium and red flanks and underwing; mistle thrush's underwing is white.
  - Fieldfare: Fieldfare has a slate-grey head, nape and rump, dark chestnut-brown back and blackish tail; underwing also white. Flight call a harsh tsak tsak.
  - Song Thrush: Song thrush is smaller, 20–23.5 cm, warm yellow underwing, neat black spots on cream-buff, and shorter-tailed, darker and less upright.

## Moltoni's Warbler
*Curruca subalpina* · `subwar8`

- Jizz — Small slim Curruca, in size and shape as Western Subalpine Warbler; no measurements in the sources.
- Plumage — Male: upperparts lead-grey, white moustachial stripe, underparts red — but paler than Western Subalpine, salmon or old rose rather than brick-red. Female: paler than the male, back brown-grey, only a faint red tone below, white eye-ring. Juvenile all but identical to Western Subalpine.
- In the field — Moults later in the year, so spring birds show fresh feathers. Song faster and higher, a rapid succession of insect-like notes including the dry trill zerr; call a metallic buzzing zerrr or trrrt, like Wren or Long-tailed Tit.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male grey-headed with a white throat, never salmon-pink below; song scratchy and scolding.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Grey back, whitish underparts and a dark bandit mask through the eye; no pink below and no white moustache; song a fast rattle of tet notes.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's whole head black with red eyes over a white throat and whitish underparts; no white moustachial stripe and no salmon-pink below.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Underparts orange brick-red, not salmon or old rose; moults earlier, so spring birds look worn; song slower, lower, less insect-like. Females and juveniles inseparable.

## Monk Parakeet
*Myiopsitta monachus* · `monpar1`

- Jizz — 28–31 cm, wingspan 48 cm, 100 g. Small green parrot with a long, tapering, pointed tail; female 10–20% smaller.
- Plumage — Upperparts bright green; forehead, throat and breast pale grey with darker scalloping; rest of the underparts pale green to yellow; flight feathers dark blue, the wing blue-toned; tail green. Sexes look alike — the slight difference in crown and covert colour is invisible to the eye.
- Bare parts — Bill orange.
- In the field — Call loud and throaty, chape-yee or quak quaki quak-wi quarr, with screeching skveet notes. The only parrot that builds a large stick nest; feral birds are wild-type green.

## Montagu's Harrier
*Circus pygargus* · `monhar1`

- Jizz — 39–50 cm; wingspan 96–116 cm; male 227–305 g. Slim, light, long narrow-winged harrier; hand pointed with four fingers showing, not five; deceptively small.
- Plumage — Male pale blue-grey with black wingtips; upperwing three-toned, dark grey coverts, paler secondaries, black tips; one black band along the secondaries above, two below; belly and flanks finely rufous-streaked, underwing-coverts barred rufous. Female: dark brown above with white uppertail-coverts, underparts pale yellow-brown streaked, coverts spotted. Juvenile: unspotted rufous belly and underwing-coverts. Melanistic morph regular.
- In the field — Flight graceful and buoyant, tern-like and springy, quartering low with the wings in a marked positive dihedral. Undertail faintly barred.
- Similar species —
  - Hen Harrier: Male evenly grey above without black secondary bands, underwing plain pale with a dark trailing edge; broader wings, five fingers, white below rather than rufous-streaked.
  - Western Marsh Harrier: Much bulkier and broader-winged, 42–62 cm; male reddish-brown with pale greyish-yellow head and shoulders, grey tail and secondaries against a brown forewing.
  - Pallid Harrier: Male paler, whitish-grey above and white below with only narrow black wingtips; lacks the black secondary bands and rufous belly streaks. Female-types near-identical.
  - Booted Eagle: Bulkier eagle, 510–770 g on a 110–132 cm span; pale morph light grey with darker head and flight feathers, dark morph mid-brown with dark grey flight feathers.

## Mountain Chiffchaff
*Phylloscopus sindianus* · `mouchi2`

- Jizz — 10–11.5 cm. Small leaf warbler with a fine, dark bill.
- Plumage — Brown above, cream-white below, flanks buff to dull yellow; race lorenzii warmer and darker brown than the nominate.
- Bare parts — Bill fine and dark; feet black.
- In the field — Song fast, of short syllables and all but identical to Common Chiffchaff's; call a weak psew. Altitudinal migrant, dropping lower in winter.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Greenish-brown above, off-white to yellowish below; paler pinkish-yellow legs, longer paler bill, longer primary projection; call a disyllabic hoo-eet.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Song is no help — take the call, a weak psew rather than hweet; upperparts browner, bill finer and darker, flanks buff.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Bold double yellowish-white wing-bars, yellow-edged tertials and a long yellow supercilium; greener above; smaller at 9.5–11 cm.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above with a single pale wing bar and a thin bill; song a high jerky trill, not short chiffchaff syllables.

## Mourning Wheatear
*Oenanthe lugens* · `mouwhe2`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, 19–25 g. Medium-sized wheatear; bill and legs rather heavy for the group.
- Plumage — Black and white: white crown, nape, breast and belly against a black face, throat and back; wings look wholly black at rest but show white flight-feather bases as a white flag in flight; tail mostly white with an even black terminal band and a central black intrusion forming an inverted T. Nominate rufous-vented. Female only subtly duller.
- In the field — Song short, deep, melancholy clear phrases with a rolling tone; call a straight hiit or a smacking tschack.
- Similar species —
