  - Northern Wheatear: Grey above with a buff throat and a black face mask only, not black-backed; female pale brown with brown, not black, wings.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Forehead, crown and mantle white to buff with a black mask from bill to ear-coverts; tail feathers much whiter, white often reaching the tip.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Buff mantle under a whitish crown, black restricted to a face mask; underparts white washed buff; smaller at 13.5–15.5 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Uniform pale sandy isabelline with no black at all; white rump, brownish-black tail with a large white base; creamy supercilium; 16.5 cm.

## Moussier's Redstart
*Phoenicurus moussieri* · `moured1`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm, 14–15 g. Smallest redstart; short-tailed and short-winged, the female shorter-tailed still.
- Plumage — Male: black head with a broad white band over the eye and down the side of the neck; upperparts black but for a large white wing patch; rump and tail chestnut; underparts rich orange-red. Female: pale brown head and upperparts, underparts paler orange than the male but redder than female Common Redstart; the wing pale where the male shows white.
- In the field — Perches openly, flicks its wings and constantly quivers the rust-red tail; habits between a redstart and a Whinchat. Open woodland in rocky country to 3000 m. Song scratchy with svii-svii notes; call a plaintive hii.
- Similar species —
  - Black Redstart: Male sooty grey to black with a black breast, no white head band and no white wing patch; only rump and tail orange-red; larger, 13–14.5 cm.
  - Common Redstart: Male slate-grey above with a white forehead only, no white neck stripe or wing patch; female greyer and paler orange below, longer-tailed; 13–14.5 cm.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: Black and white flycatcher with large white wing patches and a white forehead, but no chestnut tail and no orange underparts.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Wheatear pattern: white crown, white back and rump, black mask from ear-coverts to bill, blacker wings, underparts white to buff, tail mostly white; 13.5–15.5 cm.

## Moustached Warbler
*Acrocephalus melanopogon* · `mouwar1`

- Jizz — 12–13.5 cm. Medium-small Acrocephalus with a flattened forehead, a strong pointed bill and a relatively short tail.
- Plumage — Crown almost black, contrasting sharply with a prominent white supercilium; ear-coverts dark grey-brown, throat paler. Back finely streaked brown, more rufous on nape and back than Sedge Warbler; underparts white. Sexes alike. Juvenile more heavily streaked, with markings on the breast.
- In the field — Secretive; cocks the relatively short tail, unlike Sedge Warbler. Song fast and chattering, softer and more melodious than its relatives', with mimicry and phrases recalling Thrush Nightingale; no song flight. Call a thick smacking treck.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Warbler: No black crown and no bold white supercilium; told mainly by song — a heavy mimic of other birds, unlike the softer, nightingale-toned phrases here.
  - Sedge Warbler: Crown streaked black rather than near-solid black, and the rump warm unstreaked brown; it sings in flight and does not cock the tail. Legs greyish.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Back plain unstreaked brown, underparts buff, with no real supercilium and no dark crown. Song a slow chattering jit-jit-jit.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Upperparts plain olive-brown, neither streaked nor rufous; supercilium short and confined to before the eye; bill long, with a pale pinkish base below.

## Muscovy Duck
*Cairina moschata* · `musduc`

- Jizz — 66–84 cm, wingspan 137–152 cm, 1.1–4.1 kg. Big heavy duck, male much the larger at 76–84 cm and 3–4 kg against the female's 71–76 cm and about 1.25 kg. Broad flat tail, long claws.
- Plumage — Predominantly black with large white wing patches; back and wing feathers iridescent and glossy in the male, drabber in the female. Male has a short erectile nape crest and a bare face; female smaller, face feathered. Juvenile duller, little or no white on the wing. Domestic birds far more variable, often extensively white.
- Bare parts — Bill black speckled pink; male with a blackish or dark red knob at the bill base matching the bare face skin; eyes yellow-brown; legs blackish.
- In the field — Drake gives a low breathy call, the hen a quiet trilling coo. Domestic birds are heavier and less able to fly far. Tolerates cold down to about −12 °C.
- Similar species —
  - Bar-headed Goose: A goose: pale grey with black bars on an otherwise white head, much paler than other grey geese; 71–76 cm; no white wing patches, no facial knob, honking call.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: A greyish-brown goose, 64–81 cm, with white face blaze, dark-blotched breast, pinkish bill and bright orange legs; no knob and no white wing patches.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: A goose, 68–90 cm; black bill with an orange band, orange legs, dark brown wing-coverts narrowly fringed white; loud honking voice.
  - Tundra Bean-Goose: As given for Taiga Bean-Goose — 68–90 cm, black-and-orange bill, orange legs, white-fringed dark coverts; honking higher-pitched in the smaller subspecies.

## Mute Swan
*Cygnus olor* · `mutswa`

- Jizz — 140–160 cm, wingspan 200–240 cm, 9.2–14.3 kg. Huge; heavy body, long supple neck, small head; male larger, with the bigger bill knob.
- Plumage — Adult wholly white. Juvenile grey-brown-white, moulting into white plumage during the first summer or the second autumn. A rare recessive morph, the 'Polish swan', has white downy young and grey or pink feet as an adult.
- Bare parts — Bill orange with black nail, base and knob; a black wedge runs from the bill base to the hazel eye; legs black-grey.
- In the field — Neck usually carried in an S-curve; a raised display posture is frequent when breeding. Wings make a constant, species-specific throbbing hum in flight; among the heaviest flying birds.
- Similar species —
  - Whooper Swan: Sources give no plumage marks for it: similar in size, 140–165 cm against 140–160 cm. Separate on Mute's orange black-based bill and black knob.
  - Black Swan: Black-feathered with white flight feathers, bright red bill with pale bar and tip; smaller, 110–142 cm, and the longest neck of any swan.
  - Tundra Swan: Smaller, 115–150 cm; bill mostly black with a thin salmon-pink mouthline and some yellow at the base, no knob; iris dark brown.
  - Ruddy Duck: Not a swan: small compact duck, stout scoop-shaped bill, stiff tail cocked upward; male has a blackish cap over bright white cheeks.

## Namaqua Dove
*Oena capensis* · `namdov1`

- Jizz — 22 cm, wingspan 28–33 cm, 40 g — sparrow-sized, one of the smallest doves; much of the length is the very long, tapering black tail, so size and shape recall a budgerigar.
- Plumage — Male: forehead, face, throat and breast black, sharply cut from pale blue-grey head-sides; nape, back and inner coverts very pale brown, belly white; coverts pale grey with black- to red-glossed spots; rump grey-brown with a white, black-edged bar; chestnut primaries flash in flight. Female: no black mask, forecrown and breast dull grey-brown. Juvenile: dark blotched on wings and shoulders.
- Bare parts — Male's bill long, orange-red paling to yellow at the tip; female's duller, grey with a red base; iris dark brown, eye-ring grey.
- In the field — Song a quiet, short double hoo, the longer second note higher — kuh-whooo — mournful and frequently repeated.
- Similar species —
  - Common Wood-Pigeon: Far larger, 38–45 cm, grey with a pink breast and white neck and wing patches; no black face and no long tapering black tail.
  - Diamond Dove: Similar in size, 19–21 cm, but short-tailed; white-spotted black-edged wings, red eye in an orange ring, no black face-mask.
  - Eurasian Collared-Dove: Bigger, 29–33 cm, grey-buff to pinkish, with a black white-edged half-collar on the nape; tail broad, not a long black spike.
  - European Turtle-Dove: Bigger, 26–28 cm, brown with a black-and-white striped neck patch; tail wedge-shaped with white borders and tips, not long and all black.

## Northern Fulmar
*Fulmarus glacialis* · `norful`

- Jizz — 44–53 cm, wingspan 101–117 cm, 450–1000 g. About crow-sized; gull-like but bull-necked, with a short stubby bill and a rounded tail; wings held stiff and straight.
- Plumage — Grey and white: head, neck and underparts white or dark grey; upperwing grey-blue; rump and tail paler grey; pale inner primaries on the upperwing; no black wingtips, unlike gulls. Light, dark ('blue') and intermediate morphs; only the dark morph is more than dark-edged below. Dark eye set in a grey loral smudge.
- Bare parts — Bill short and thick, mostly yellowish, darkening towards the nostrils and often mottled black there; legs bluish to yellowish-green.
- In the field — Strong flier, gliding low over the water on stiff straight wings with a wing action quite unlike a gull's; walks poorly.
- Similar species —
  - Scopoli's Shearwater: Brown-grey above rather than grey; white underparts with a brown border broadest on the trailing edge; pale yellow bill dark near the tip.
  - Great Shearwater: Black cap sharply cut off from white underparts, brown belly patch and a white horseshoe at the tail base; shears side to side rather than gliding straight.
  - Cory's Shearwater: Bigger, 45–56 cm against 44–53 cm; brownish-grey upperparts and a yellowish bill against the fulmar's grey back and short thick bill.
  - Sooty Shearwater: All dark chocolate-brown with a silvery stripe down the centre of the underwing; shears side to side on stiff wings, not gliding straight.

## Northern Gannet
*Morus bassanus* · `norgan`

- Jizz — 87–100 cm, wingspan 165–180 cm, 2300–3600 g. Largest gannet and largest western Palearctic seabird; long narrow pointed wings set mid-body; long strong conical bill, 9–11 cm, slightly downcurved at the tip.
- Plumage — Adult white with dark brown to black primaries, primary coverts and alulae; head and neck washed buff-yellow, strongest when breeding and deeper in males, occasionally absent altogether. Bare black lores and chin give the hard-faced look. Fledglings look white-spotted from pale feather tips; juveniles dark grey-brown speckled, whitening over four to five years into adult plumage.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey with dark edges and a black groove running back to the eye; iris pale blue-grey; bare black facial skin, cobalt orbital ring.
- In the field — Glides over the sea on stiff wings; water-repellent plumage lets it rest swimming for long spells. Unmistakable in range — the largest seabird of the western Palearctic.

## Northern Hawk Owl
*Surnia ulula* · `nohowl`

- Jizz — 35–43 cm, wingspan 69–82 cm, 270–300 g. Medium owl with a hawk-like silhouette: long, wedge-shaped tail and hawk-like wings, but a proportionally larger head than a hawk — small, rounded and flattened. Female heavier.
- Plumage — Dark brown above, spotted off-white on all dorsal parts; head and neck blackish above with white spots; black V on the hindneck; face white with a black border, white eye-surrounds bearing black bristles; underparts white, closely barred dark grey-brown right down to the toes; long tail brown with white bars. Juvenile: greyer-brown, underparts not yet clearly barred.
- Bare parts — Bill curved, pale yellow; iris yellow.
- In the field — Hawk-like in flight and behaviour, likened in North America to a Cooper's Hawk. Pumps the tail up and down and moves jerkily about the trees. Male's song a rolling ho ho hoho y y yyyyyy, starting quietly and swelling.
- Similar species —
  - Short-eared Owl: Much broader-winged, wingspan 85–110 cm; mottled tawny-brown with a heavily streaked upper breast rather than fine barring; floppy, moth- or bat-like flight.
  - Tawny Owl: Bulkier, with a large round head, plain facial disc and dark brown eyes; underparts whitish streaked brown, not barred; rufous or grey-brown morphs.
  - Long-eared Owl: Slim and long-winged with erectile ear-tufts set near the centre of the head; ochraceous-tawny, boldly streaked blackish rather than barred, scapulars marked whitish.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, wingspan 69–82 cm against 131–188 cm.

## Northern Lapwing
*Vanellus vanellus* · `norlap`

- Jizz — 28–33 cm, 128–330 g. Pigeon-sized wader with broad rounded paddle-shaped wings and a long wispy twin-pointed crest; legs fairly long but the shortest of any lapwing.
- Plumage — Black and white, mantle glossed green-grey with a blue-violet shoulder patch; white face crossed by a diffuse black band under the eye; black crown drawn into the crest; black throat and sharply cut black breast-band; belly white, vent rusty-orange; broad white band on the inner wing. Female: shorter crest, less contrast. Winter: chin and foreneck white, face buff, upperparts buff-scaled.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs dark red to brown.
- In the field — Seldom still: loose leisurely beats on broad wings, flapping and wavering. Male tumbles in acrobatic display with a wheezy 'pee-wit, wit wit'; contact call a shrill 'pee-wit'; wings drum audibly close to.
- Similar species —
  - Spur-winged Lapwing: No crest: black crown, chest, foreneck stripe and tail with light brown wings and back, white face and belly, black bill and legs; call did-he-do-it.
  - Sociable Lapwing: No crest; black crown and eyestripe bordered white above and below, brown, white and grey wings, white tail with black terminal band, long black legs.
  - Black-bellied Plover: No crest; summer bird spangled black and white above with black face, breast and belly and white rump; plain grey in winter; 25–30 cm.
  - European Golden-Plover: No crest; thickset, wings only slightly longer than the tail, and a white S-shaped band running from forehead to flanks.

## Northern Nutcracker
*Nucifraga caryocatactes* · `eurnut3`

- Jizz — 32–38 cm, wingspan 49–53 cm, 120–170 g. Jackdaw-sized, broad-winged, short-tailed corvid with a big head and a long, straight, sharply pointed bill.
- Plumage — Mid to dark chocolate brown, heavily white-spotted on face, neck, mantle and underparts, each body feather with a white wedge; blackish-brown cap running onto the nape; large white loral spot and white eye-ring; wings dark with a greenish-blue gloss; vent all white; tail dark with white corners and a white terminal band below. Sexes alike; juveniles barely different.
- Bare parts — Bill black, slender to stout, with white feathering at its base; iris, legs and feet black.
- In the field — Flight undulating and rather laboured; broad wings, white vent and short tail conspicuous. Voice loud and harsh, kraak-kraak-kraak, like a Eurasian Jay but lower and more monotone, given from a treetop.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Jay: Pinkish-brown and unspotted, with black moustache, black-streaked whitish crown, white rump and a bright blue black-barred wing patch; longer tail, smaller head.
  - Eurasian Jackdaw: Glossy black with light grey nape and neck and slate-grey underparts; short stout bill, pale iris; no white spotting and no white vent.
  - Yellow-billed Chough: Glossy all-black with a short yellow bill and red legs; proportionally longer tail and shorter wings, buoyant easy flight; no white spotting.
  - Red-billed Chough: Larger, 38–41 cm, velvet-black and green-glossed, with a long curved red bill and red legs; no white spots or white undertail.

## Northern Pintail
*Anas acuta* · `norpin`

- Jizz — Male 59–76 cm and 450–1,360 g, female 51–64 cm and 454–1,135 g; wingspan 80–95 cm. Slender, elongated, gracile; very long thin neck, pointed tail.
- Plumage — Male: chocolate-brown head, darker crown, near-black stripe down the nape, white breast with a narrow white wedge up the neck-side; grey upperparts and sides, long grey black-striped scapulars draped over the back; yellow vent against the black undertail, central tail feathers to 10 cm. Female: mottled and scalloped light brown, head plainer grey-brown, tail shorter but pointed.
- Bare parts — Bill bluish grey, long and slim; legs blue-grey.
- In the field — Walks and swims well; flight very fast with the wings held slightly swept back, unlike the more right-angled wings of other ducks. Male's speculum black, white trailing edge, pale rufous leading edge.
- Similar species —
  - Mallard: Broadly the same size but bulkier and shorter-necked, 50–65 cm; drake bottle-green headed with white collar and purple-blue speculum.
  - Green-winged Teal: Tiny by comparison, 34–43 cm; chestnut head with a green teardrop patch, white flank stripe and yellowish stern.
  - Gadwall: Shorter and stockier, 47–58 cm; drake patterned grey with a black stern and a brilliant white speculum, no long tail.
  - Garganey: Much smaller; drake brown-headed with a broad white crescent over the eye, and a pale blue forewing with white border.

## Northern Shoveler
*Spatula clypeata* · `norsho`

- Jizz — 44–52 cm, wingspan 70–84 cm, 400–1,100 g. Short-necked dabbler with a huge flat spatulate bill up to 7 cm long, looking front-heavy swimming and in flight.
- Plumage — Male: dark iridescent green head, black at range or in poor light, white breast and back, chestnut belly and flanks, black stern; bright pale blue forewing separated from the green speculum by a white border; white face crescent in early autumn. Female: mottled beige and brown, pale face with darker crown and eye-stripe, forewing grey. Juvenile: much darker, chestnut-toned.
- Bare parts — Male's bill black or very dark grey, iris yellow, legs bright orange-red; female's bill grey with orange cutting edges, eye dark.
- In the field — Underwing pale grey-white with a dark trailing edge. Rather quiet: male a knocking tok-tok…tok-tok, female a Mallard-like quack.
- Similar species —
  - Garganey: Smaller, with a normal bill; drake brown-headed with a broad white crescent over the eye and loose grey scapulars; forewing pale blue too.
  - Mallard: Larger, 50–65 cm, bill normal; drake more intensely green-headed with a white collar and brown breast; female very like this one but bill ordinary.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: Small grey-blue bill; white cheeks and forehead under a green-blue cap, black nape, breast barred black and white, rust flanks.
  - Eurasian Wigeon: Round head, small grey bill; drake chestnut-headed with creamy crown and a brilliant white forewing patch, not pale blue.

## Northern Wheatear
*Oenanthe oenanthe* · `norwhe`

- Jizz — 14–16.5 cm, wingspan 26–32 cm, 17–30 g. Larger than a robin; stands very upright, tail short; bill thin, sharp and straight, with a narrow raised ridge between the nostrils.
- Plumage — White rump and tail with a bold black inverted-T terminal band in all plumages, central feathers wholly black. Spring male: grey crown and mantle, white forehead and supercilium, black lores and ear-coverts forming a mask, black wings, buff throat and breast, whitish vent. Autumn male browner, wings pale-fringed. Female duller, wings brown, lores not black.
- Bare parts — Bill black.
- In the field — Very upright, bobbing body and tail; sings from rocks a whistling, crackly, grating phrase; call a chat-like chack, also used in flight, and a piping hiit.
- Similar species —
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Forehead, crown and mantle white or near-white, wings blacker, black mask reaching the bill; tail much whiter, the white on inner webs often to the tip.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Forehead and crown white, mantle buff, wings blacker, black mask from ear-coverts to bill; tail much whiter than Northern's; 13.5–15.5 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Sexes alike, pale sandy isabelline throughout, creamy supercilium and pale brown ear-coverts, no black mask; tail feathers brownish-black with white only at the base.
  - Black Wheatear: Larger, 16–18 cm, and all black — female dark brown — except white rump and mostly white tail; it too carries a black inverted T on the tail.

## Olive-tree Warbler
*Hippolais olivetorum* · `oltwar1`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm, wingspan 24–26 cm, 19–23 g. Very large warbler, well bigger than Icterine or Melodious; long thick bill, heavy legs, sloping forehead, long wings and a long tail.
- Plumage — Pale olive-grey to grey above, whitish-grey below; obvious pale wing panel, wingtips and tail blackish, the long tail otherwise dark grey; rump pale grey-white; outer webs of the outer tail feathers almost white. Dingy white supercilium, often faint, from bill base to eye, merging with a white upper eye-ring; lower eye-ring faint or absent. No marked difference between the sexes.
- Bare parts — Bill long and strong, the lower mandible clearly orange-yellow; legs unusually stout.
- In the field — Voice deep and thick for a warbler: call a smacking chack, alarm a nasal chatter recalling a coarse-voiced tit; song fast, gruff and loud, a cyclical repetition of chacking notes with softer sounds between.
- Similar species —
  - Icterine Warbler: Underparts uniformly light yellow, upperparts greyish-green, vague yellowish supercilium and pale lores; far smaller, 12–13.5 cm, with a short square tail and grey-blue legs.
  - Melodious Warbler: Plain brown back and wings with yellowish underparts and brown legs; song a pleasant fluent babbling; much smaller, 12–13 cm.
  - Upcher's Warbler: Also grey-plumaged, but far smaller at 14–15 cm, with shorter wings and a longer tail moved constantly, chat-like; semi-desert scrub and tamarisk.
  - Great Reed Warbler: Unstreaked warm brown above, buffish-white below, strong supercilium, flattened forehead; a reed-bed bird with a very loud song; 16–21 cm, 22–38 g.

## Oriental Turtle-Dove
*Streptopelia orientalis* · `ortdov`

- Jizz — 30–35 cm, wingspan 54–62 cm. Bigger and heavier than a turtle dove, by about a third, and roughly collared-dove size; wings distinctly rounded, tail wedge-shaped.
- Plumage — Upper back, head, neck and breast dark brown-pink; rump blue-grey; belly and undertail-coverts whitish grey. Scapulars, tertials and inner coverts black-centred with broad rust-brown fringes, giving a scaly look; median and greater coverts white-tipped, forming two clear pale wing-bars; primaries black. Neck-side patch of fine parallel black stripes on blue-grey. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Legs red, bill dark grey; no bare patch of skin round the eye.
- In the field — Flight relaxed and direct. Song a dull cooing of four syllables, her-her-oo-oo, two hoarse notes then two clear ones.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Collared-Dove: Similar length, 29–33 cm, but plain grey-buff to pinkish-grey with a black white-edged half-collar; no scaly rust-fringed upperparts or striped neck patch.
  - European Turtle-Dove: Smaller, 26–28 cm; neck and breast paler, wing fringes broader and rustier yellow, no pale wing-bars, and bare skin round the eye.
  - Rock Pigeon: Same length, 30–35 cm, but plain grey above, without rust-fringed scaly upperparts and without the striped neck patch.
  - Stock Dove: Blue-grey with only two short blackish bars on tertials and greater coverts and an iridescent green-purple neck patch; bill pink with a yellow tip.

## Ortolan Bunting
*Emberiza hortulana* · `ortbun1`

- Jizz — 15–17 cm, 17–28 g, wingspan 23–29 cm; House Sparrow size. Slim bunting with a rather long, square-cut tail, smallish rounded head and fairly long, faintly curved bill; wings long and narrow.
- Plumage — Male: head, neck and breast dull grey-green; yellow throat, curved yellow submoustachial stripe and yellow-white orbital ring contrast strongly; mantle rust-brown, boldly blackish-streaked; belly cinnamon-brown; tertial fringes red-brown, broadest in the middle. Female: hood lacking or barely hinted, colours paler, crown, throat-sides and breast finely dark-streaked. Juvenile: adult tones but dulled and indistinct.
- In the field — Song like Yellowhammer's but rounder, purer and deeper, with a ringing, echoing tone: sry-sry-sry-sry, dry dry dry, the echoing notes lower. Often in small flocks on autumn passage.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Bigger and bulkier, 16–19 cm, streaked grey-brown above and whitish below with a streaked gorget; no yellow throat or pale orbital ring; tail plain brown.
  - Reed Bunting: Smaller, 13.5–15.5 cm; male black-headed and white-collared, female streak-headed and barred below; neither shows the yellow throat and orbital ring.
  - Yellowhammer: Head bright yellow, rump rufous, outer tail feathers white; tail not square-cut and wings shorter and broader than Ortolan's.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 13–15 cm against 15–17 cm.

## Osprey
*Pandion haliaetus* · `osprey`

- Jizz — 50–66 cm, 127–174 cm span, 0.9–2.1 kg. Medium-large, slim, long-winged raptor with a short tail; four long finger-like primaries and a shorter fifth; bushy crest on the hindcrown.
- Plumage — Upperparts uniform deep glossy brown; underparts and underwing-coverts brilliant white, the breast with a brown band that is broader and darker on the female. Head white with a broad, sharply demarcated dark brown stripe through the eye to the nape. Blackish greater under-coverts form a dark carpal patch. Juvenile: pale-scaled above, crown white streaked dark, tail finely barred.
- Bare parts — Bill black, cere lead-grey blue; iris golden to brown, orange in juveniles; feet white with black talons, toes equal, the outer one reversible.
- In the field — Flies with arched wings and drooped hands, looking gull-like; easily identified on appearance alone. Call a series of sharp whistles, tjiip tjiip or jeok jeok, and a violent cheereek near the nest.

## Paddyfield Warbler
*Acrocephalus agricola* · `padwar1`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, wingspan 15–17.5 cm, 7–14 g. Small Acrocephalus, smaller than a reed warbler; bill short and pointed, tail longer and slightly rounded, primary projection short.
- Plumage — Broad, evenly wide, long pale supercilium, white in breeding adults and often edged darker above and below. Upperparts unstreaked: warm orange-brown to rufous when fresh, dull olive-brown when worn, nape paler; rump warm rusty buff, always contrasting with the mantle. Throat whitish, breast-sides, flanks and underparts warm yellowish-buff. Sexes alike. Juvenile: richer buff below.
- Bare parts — Bill straw-yellow with a dark tip and dark upper mandible; iris pale red-brown in adults, dark in young birds; legs pink to dark grey-brown.
- In the field — Low vegetation — long grass, reeds and rice. Song fast, with much mimicry and acrocephaline whistles, close to marsh warbler but weaker and more rhythmic.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Warbler: Told by song — no other member of the genus mimics so freely; lacks the broad dark-edged supercilium and the rusty rump contrast, and avoids pure reed stands.
  - Sedge Warbler: Back and wings streaked brown, crown streaked black, though the rump is warm brown and unstreaked; 11.5–13 cm, bill strong, legs greyish.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Bigger, 12.5–14 cm, longer-billed and longer-winged; plain brown back with no rusty rump contrast and only a faint supercilium.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Plain olive-brown, without warm tones; supercilium short, between bill and eye only, with a dusky eye-stripe; bill long and thick, wings short and rounded.

## Pale Crag-Martin
*Ptyonoprogne obsoleta* · `465888`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, 14–24.5 g. Small, fairly robust martin; drab, washed-out plumage; short square tail; wing averages 13 cm, tail 4.8 cm.
- Plumage — Sexes alike. Upperparts light brown, paler on the lower back; throat, upper breast and underwing-coverts pale grey, rest of underparts dirty white; no breast band. Short square tail with small white patches near the tips of all but the central and outermost feather pairs. Juvenile: pale buff to rufous fringes above, washed rufous throat and vent.
- Bare parts — Iris brown; small bill mainly black; legs brownish-pink.
- In the field — Flight slow, rapid wing beats broken by flat-winged glides; more acrobatic than the larger Eurasian crag-martin. Quiet; song a muffled twitter, calls a house-martin-like trrt, nasal vick and high twee. Strong desert light makes colour hard to judge.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Crag-Martin: Larger and darker, 13–15 cm against 12–14 cm, broader-bodied; underwing- and undertail-coverts blackish, not pale grey; pale crag-martin greyer with smaller tail spots.
  - Bank Swallow: Narrow brown breast band below a white throat, no white tail spots; slighter build and quick jerky flight. Crag-martin more robust and unbanded.
  - Western House-Martin: Steel-blue above with a white rump, wholly white underparts and underwings, and a forked tail; crag-martin dull brown, square-tailed, white-spotted.
  - European Red-rumped Swallow: Blue-glossed black above with pale orangey rump, buffy face and orangey-red nape collar; tail deeply forked and all black, without white spots.

## Pale Rockfinch
*Carpospiza brachydactyla* · `palroc1`

- Jizz — 14.5–15.5 cm. Slim, small-headed sparrow-finch; bill stout but short.
- Plumage — Pale beige-grey throughout and wholly unstreaked; short, faint pale supercilium; two indistinct paler wing-bars; white spots at the tip of the tail, showing in flight.
- Bare parts — Bill dark grey when breeding, paler beige-grey in winter.
- In the field — Dry shrubland and temperate grassland. Calls a greenfinch-like dvui and a bee-eater-like trry; song a second-long, insect-like note.
- Similar species —
  - Dead Sea Sparrow: Smaller, 12–13 cm, and sexually dimorphic; male grey-crowned with a small black bib and yellow neck-sides, upperparts dark-streaked reddish brown.
  - Eurasian Tree Sparrow: Chestnut crown and nape, black kidney-shaped patch on a pure white cheek, black chin, black-streaked back, two narrow white wing-bars; 12.5–14 cm.
  - House Sparrow: Compact and full-chested with a large rounded head and stout conical bill, 14–18 cm; sexes differ and the back is streaked, never plain pale beige-grey.
  - Italian Sparrow: Chunky; male chestnut-crowned with white cheeks, bright chestnut upperparts and a black bib; female streaked brown, not uniformly pale.

## Palestine Sunbird
*Cinnyris osea* · `palsun2`

- Jizz — 8–12 cm; wingspan 14–16 cm, male 7.6 g, female about 6.8 g. Chunky, short-tailed sunbird with a fine, downcurved, pointed bill.
- Plumage — Breeding male looks black at range; close to, metallic green above, violet-blue on the crown, steel-blue on the uppertail-coverts, blue on the throat; wings and tail black. Yellow to orange-red pectoral tufts, hard to see except at close range. Female and juvenile grey-brown above, whitish below with a faint yellow wash. Non-breeding male as female, with scattered black feathers.
- Bare parts — Bill black, thin, downcurved and pointed.
- In the field — Takes nectar from flowers with the decurved bill. Song high, fast and jingling — whistled notes then a rattling trill; calls include a rising tvyit, a whistled viju and a harsh alarm.

## Pallas's Gull
*Ichthyaetus ichthyaetus* · `gbhgul2`

- Jizz — 55–72 cm, wingspan 140–162 cm, 960–2100 g. Very large gull but slim and long-winged; forehead strikingly flat and sloping.
- Plumage — Summer adult: black hood with narrow white crescents above and below the eye; back and upperwing pale grey; primaries silvery white with conspicuous white mirrors and a narrow black subterminal band; body and tail otherwise white. Winter: hood reduced to a dark eye-mask with dusky crown and hindneck. Juvenile: brown-scaled back and coverts, brown breast-sides, sharp black tail band.
- Bare parts — Bill tricoloured: dark yellow base, narrow black then red bands, yellow tip, the red band lost in winter; iris dark; legs yellow.
- In the field — Grey upperparts come fast, the back even grey by the first winter, but four years to full adult plumage. Call a deep aargh.
- Similar species —
  - Mediterranean Gull: Smaller and slighter, with pure white primaries lacking black tips, a very pale grey mantle and a dark red black-banded bill; two years to maturity.
  - Audouin's Gull: Nothing in the sources to work with: no size or plumage characters are given for Audouin's, only that it is a large Mediterranean gull.
  - Caspian Gull: White-headed all year, never hooded, though the forehead slopes too; bill long and slender, legs pale pink to yellowish, eye small and dark.
  - Glaucous Gull: Very pale in every plumage with no black at all on wings or tail; thick yellow bill; never hooded.

## Pallas's Leaf Warbler
*Phylloscopus proregulus* · `palwar5`

- Jizz — 9–10 cm, 4–7 g. One of the smallest warblers: large-headed, very short-tailed, barely bigger than a Goldcrest and smaller than Yellow-browed Warbler.
- Plumage — Dark olive-green above, whitish below, sometimes pale yellow on the breast; bold lemon-yellow supercilium reaching the nape and running into a pale forehead band, dark loral and eye-stripe, and a clear pale yellow central crown stripe on an otherwise dark crown; two prominent sulphur-yellow wing bars on median and greater coverts; pale yellow rump patch about 10 mm wide.
- Bare parts — Bill short, blackish-brown with yellowish cutting edges and lower-mandible base; iris brown; legs brown tinged green or grey.
- In the field — Almost constantly in motion and often hovering, when the yellow rump patch shows best. Call a weak, soft, slightly rising tsui; song loud and trilling, like a Wren's.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Plain greenish-brown wings without wing bars, no crown stripe and no yellow rump; larger, 11–12.5 cm, with pale pinkish-yellow legs.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Dull brown-green with only a short whitish supercilium, no wing bars, no crown stripe and no yellow rump; larger, 10–12 cm, with dark legs.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Also double-barred, but head stripes and bars yellowish-white rather than lemon, crown stripe faint or absent and rump not yellow; slightly larger, 9.5–11 cm.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green with a single wing bar, plain crown and no yellow rump; song a high jerky trill rather than a loud wren-like one.

## Pallid Harrier
*Circus macrourus* · `palhar1`

- Jizz — 40–50 cm, wingspan 95–120 cm, 250–550 g. Slender harrier, narrow-winged, hand pointed with four fingers showing; tarsus notably long.
- Plumage — Male: pearl-grey above, head and neck paler, white below with only head and upper breast greyish; narrow black wedge-shaped wingtips, outermost primary pale; rump grey centrally, white and faintly barred at the sides; undertail barred. Female: grey-brown above with white uppertail-coverts — a ringtail — underparts buff streaked brown, belly pale, face pattern well defined.
- In the field — Hunts low with the wings held in a shallow V; flight buoyant and light, tern-like.
- Similar species —
  - Hen Harrier: Broader, blunter wings showing five fingers, not four, and shorter legs; adult male's uppertail-coverts pure white, never barred as here. 41–55 cm.
  - Western Marsh Harrier: Much larger and bulkier, 42–62 cm, span 115–140 cm, 400–650 g; male cryptic reddish-brown with pale head and grey tail and secondaries, not pearl-grey.
  - Montagu's Harrier: Male has black bands along the secondaries above and below and rusty streaks on belly and flanks; females very close, but this bird is paler-bellied with a sharper face.
  - Booted Eagle: An eagle, not a harrier: 40–51 cm but 510–770 g on a 110–132 cm span; pale birds light grey with dark head and flight feathers, others all mid-brown.

## Pallid Swift
*Apus pallidus* · `palswi3`

- Jizz — 16–17 cm, 32–44 g. Torpedo-bodied swift with a short forked tail and long swept-back crescent wings; wings a shade broader and blunter, head and neck thicker than Common Swift.
- Plumage — Sooty overall but browner and paler than Common Swift, with a large white throat patch visible at a distance and a dark patch round the eye. Inner primaries and secondaries paler brown, contrasting with the rest; belly finely scaled, best seen in good light.
- In the field — Separable from Common Swift only on good views; flight action subtly different. Call a loud dry scream, lower, drier and more clearly disyllabic, vryyy-e.
- Similar species —
  - Common Swift: Blacker and colder-toned, with only a small pale chin patch invisible at distance, no contrast in the flight feathers and no scaling; slimmer. Same 16–17 cm.
  - White-rumped Swift: Smaller, 14–15.5 cm, slimmer and darker, with a narrow white rump and a more deeply forked tail; throat patch pale but small.
  - Alpine Swift: Much larger, 20–23 cm with a 51–58 cm span; olive-brown above, white throat and a big oval white belly patch ringed by a brown breastband.

## Parasitic Jaeger
*Stercorarius parasiticus* · `parjae`

- Jizz — 37–48 cm, wingspan 107–125 cm, 300–650 g. Small skua, like a dark, sharp-winged common gull; narrow, fairly long wings; breeding adult carries pointed central tail streamers 5–10 cm long.
- Plumage — Three morphs. Light morph: brown back, mainly white underparts, black cap, yellowish-white head and neck sides; dark morph uniformly dark brown; intermediates dark with paler underparts, head and neck. All morphs show a white flash in the black primaries, seen from below as a white wedge. Juvenile: warm brown, sandy to rufous or dark brown.
- Bare parts — Bill bluish at the base, black at the tip; legs black in adults, blue-grey in juveniles.
- In the field — Flight fast and falcon-like. Commonest of the three jaegers seen from shore. Call a nasal mewing 'äh-glå' repeated in display; short 'pjy!' when irritated and 'gack!' in alarm.
- Similar species —
  - Great Skua: Much bigger, 50–58 cm, span 125–140 cm; herring-gull size with a barrel chest, streaked greyish-brown, short blunt tail, powerful flight; no tail streamers.
  - Long-tailed Jaeger: Adult has a grey back, very long streamers and no white primary flash. Juveniles slimmer, longer-winged, more tern-like and colder grey-toned than the browner Parasitic.
  - Pomarine Jaeger: Larger, 46–67 cm; bulkier, broader-winged and less falcon-like, flight more measured; adult streamer about 10 cm. Same three morphs, so judge structure.

## Parrot Crossbill
*Loxia pytyopsittacus* · `parcro2`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm, 44–58.2 g; wingspan 27–31 cm. Bulky, heavy, bull-necked finch with a large head and a massive bill, about as deep as long, with a parrot-like bulge and a blunt, barely obvious crossed tip.
- Plumage — Wings and tail dark to blackish with pale grey fringes in all plumages. Male mainly brick-red to orange, varying individually with grey-green or brown tones. Female: grey-green above, rump yellow-green, underparts pale grey washed green on breast and flanks. Juvenile: greenish yellow-grey, underparts heavily streaked blackish.
- In the field — Bill specialised on pine cones. Vocal; call like Red Crossbill's but on average harder and deeper, without its interposed l: typp-typp-typp. Song trills and low twitter mixed with the call, which is the best indicator.
- Similar species —
  - Red Crossbill: Plumage identical; look for the smaller head, slimmer neck and thinner bill whose crossed tips show clearly, and listen for the softer call with its interposed l.
  - White-winged Crossbill: Two bold white wing-bars, absent in Parrot; smaller and smaller-headed, 14.5–17 cm and 25–40 g, with a forked tail.
  - African Chaffinch: Blue-grey head with a black forehead patch, moss-green mantle, two bold white wing stripes and white tail sides; underparts pale pinkish. Much lighter, 21–24 g.
  - Hawfinch: Bigger-billed still but conical, not crossed; light brown with an orange-toned head, black throat and black round the bill, short pinkish legs. Larger, 18–19 cm.

## Pectoral Sandpiper
*Calidris melanotos* · `pecsan`

- Jizz — 19–24 cm, wingspan 26–46 cm, 31–126 g. Largish calidrid; bill long and slightly decurved at the tip; central tail feathers elongated, showing in flight.
- Plumage — Crown dark brown streaked black; ear-coverts red-brown against paler streaked cheeks; throat white. Neck and breast pale brownish, densely dark-streaked into a broad band cut off sharply against the white underparts. Mantle and back dark with broad chestnut fringes, looking scaly; brownest in summer male, greyest in winter; breeding male blacker-breasted, spotted white. Juvenile: brighter rufous above, white mantle stripes.
- Bare parts — Bill olive with a darker tip; legs yellowish; iris dark brown.
- In the field — The sharp lower edge of the breast band is most conspicuous when the bird turns towards the observer. Rump and uppertail-coverts blackish, sides white.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Bill shorter, straight and dark, legs dark not yellow; no sharp breast band — cinnamon face and breast in summer, uniform pale grey in winter; 23–26 cm.
  - Dunlin: Smaller, 16–20 cm, with black legs and black decurved bill; black belly patch in summer, never a sharply cut breast band; narrow white wingbar.
  - Ruff: Pot-bellied, small-headed and long-necked, legs orange or pink; white ovals either side of the tail and a deeper, slower wing stroke; male 29–32 cm.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Dark above with a purple gloss, breast smeared grey without a sharp lower edge; bill dark with a yellow base, legs short and yellow; 20–22 cm.

## Peregrine Falcon
*Falco peregrinus* · `perfal`

- Jizz — 34–58 cm, wingspan 74–120 cm, 330–1300 g. Powerful falcon; strong body, big head, long triangular pointed wings, medium-length rounded tail. Female up to 30% larger than the male.
- Plumage — Adult bluish-black to slate-grey above with indistinct darker barring and black wingtips; white to rusty below, cleanly barred dark brown, foreneck and upper breast lightly spotted. Crown black; heavy black moustachial stripe set sharply against pale neck sides and a white throat. Tail barred, black-tipped with a white terminal band. Juvenile: much browner, underparts streaked rather than barred.
- Bare parts — Cere, eye-ring and feet yellow; bill grey with a darker tip to nearly black, notched near the tip; claws black; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Best marks at range are the very dark upperparts, the pale barred underparts and the moustachial stripe, which carries a long way.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Slimmer, 36–42 cm; dark morph all sooty brown with black underwing-coverts, light morph buff below with the same dark coverts against paler flight feathers.
  - Eurasian Kestrel: Smaller and much lighter, 32–39 cm and 136–252 g; light chestnut-brown above with blackish spots, buff below with narrow streaks, long tail.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Smaller, 29–36 cm; two short black moustache stripes, unstreaked white throat, boldly black-streaked underparts and unstreaked rufous thighs and undertail-coverts.
  - Merlin: Much smaller, 24–33 cm with a 50–73 cm span and 125–300 g, though robust and heavily built for a small falcon.

## Pied Avocet
*Recurvirostra avosetta* · `pieavo1`

- Jizz — 42–46 cm; wingspan 67–77 cm; 267–400 g. Tall, long-legged wader with a long, slender, strongly upcurved bill.
- Plumage — Pied black and white: black cap running down the nape, black lateral back, shoulders and outer primaries, the rest pure white; secondary coverts dark grey; tail white with a pale grey-brown centre. No seasonal change; sexes alike, though the female's black may look browner. Juvenile: matt brown for black, wing coverts dingy grey, back vermiculated reddish-brown.
- Bare parts — Bill black to dark grey; legs bluish grey; iris red to reddish-brown in males, hazel-brown in females.
- In the field — Unmistakable: in flight boldly pied above, white below but for black wingtips, legs trailing well beyond the tail, neck not fully extended. Call a restless repeated plytt-plytt-plytt.
- Similar species —
  - Black-winged Stilt: Long pink legs and a long thin black bill; black above and white below with a white head and neck. Smaller, 33–36 cm.

## Pied Kingfisher
*Ceryle rudis* · `piekin1`

- Jizz — 25 cm. Medium-sized, black-and-white kingfisher with a shaggy, partly raised crest.
- Plumage — Black and white throughout; black mask through the eye under a white supercilium; upperparts barred black. Male: two black breast bands, the second narrow. Female: a single, often broken band. Race travancoreensis darker with less white; insignis much bigger-billed.
- In the field — Gregarious, unlike most kingfishers, gathering in large flocks in the evening; noisy and easy to find, calling a sharp chirruk chirruk.
- Similar species —
  - White-throated Kingfisher: Bright blue back, wings and tail, chestnut head and flanks, white throat and breast; bill and legs red; big white wing patches in flight.
  - Common Kingfisher: Much smaller at 16 cm, dumpy and long-billed; blue-green above with an azure back, rufous underparts and ear-patch, red feet.

## Pin-tailed Sandgrouse
*Pterocles alchata* · `pitsan1`

- Jizz — 31–39 cm, wingspan to 65 cm. Robust medium-sized sandgrouse; small dove-like head, long pointed wings, central tail feathers drawn out into slate-grey streamers; 16 tail feathers.
- Plumage — Breeding male: crown, neck, back and underwing-coverts yellowish-green with dull yellow shoulder spots; cheeks yellow, narrow black line from bill through eye to nape; black throat patch; chestnut breast-band edged black cuts the white belly from the green neck; rump and tail barred black and brownish-yellow. Non-breeding: crown and upperparts barred, throat whitish. Female: duller, throat white, extra black neck-band.
- Bare parts — Bill slate-grey; iris brown; legs feathered dull white.
- In the field — Flight fast; in the air the white belly, white underwings and elongated central tail feathers show well. General colouring otherwise cryptic — barred and flecked olive green, brown, buff, yellow, grey and black.
- Similar species —
  - Black-bellied Sandgrouse: Belly black against white underwings, not white; head, neck and breast grey, throat chestnut; no tail streamers; stockier, 30–39 cm, 300–615 g.

## Pine Grosbeak
*Pinicola enucleator* · `pingro`

- Jizz — 19–25.5 cm, wingspan 33 cm, 52–78 g. One of the largest finches, thrush-sized and robust; long tail forked at the tip; heavy bill with curved culmen, 1.4–1.65 cm.
- Plumage — Male: head, breast and rump rose-red to pink, flanks grey. Female: head, breast and rump olive-yellow, back and underparts grey. Both: black wings with double white wing-bars and white tertial edges, blackish forked tail. Older females and young males intermediate, orange or brownish; young birds less contrasting and shaggy while moulting the head.
- In the field — Flight fast, powerful and undulating. Flight calls fluting and clear, pui pui pui, plyit or dju-djy; flocks give a quieter bytt-bytt-bytt; song a short, bleak musical warble.
- Similar species —
  - Red Crossbill: Crossed mandible tips settle it; 20 cm, wingspan 27–29 cm, 40–53 g; males red or orange, females green or yellow, juveniles greyish-green and streaked.
  - Hawfinch: Thick neck, large round head, huge metallic-looking conical bill, short tail; light brown with an orange-hued head, black throat and black round the eye; pinkish legs; 18 cm.
  - Parrot Crossbill: Crossed bill tips, often hidden by the bill's thickness; 16–18 cm, wingspan 27–31 cm, 44–58 g; bulky, with head and bill larger than other crossbills.
  - Eurasian Bullfinch: Bull-headed, grey above with black cap, face, flight feathers and short thick bill; white rump striking in flight; male's chest rich red, female grey-buff. Call a low melancholy peeu.

## Pink-footed Goose
*Anser brachyrhynchus* · `pifgoo`

- Jizz — 60–75 cm, wingspan 130–170 cm, 2.5–3.5 kg. Medium-sized grey goose, short-necked and short-billed with a small dark head; smaller than the bean geese.
- Plumage — Body mid grey-brown, head and neck a richer dark brown; back and forewing pale bluish-grey, flight feathers blackish-grey; rump and vent white; tail grey with a narrow pale band inside a broad white tip.
- Bare parts — Bill short, black at base and tip with a bright pink band across the middle; feet pink.
- In the field — Cackling like White-fronted Goose; males and young high-pitched, females lower and more nasal. Wing moult in mid July, flightless about 25 days.
- Similar species —
  - Graylag Goose: Largest and bulkiest grey goose, 74–91 cm, rotund with thick neck and large head; bill wholly orange or pink, without the black-and-pink pattern.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Feet and bill band orange, not pink; larger, 65–90 cm; upperparts uniformly grey-brown, lacking the pale grey back and forewing.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: White facial blaze, pinkish bill and bright orange legs; greyish-brown with dark blotches and bars on the breast, mouse-coloured upperwing-coverts.
  - Tundra Bean-Goose: As Taiga Bean: orange bill-band and orange feet against pink, dark brown coverts with narrow white fringes; larger, 66–90 cm.

## Pomarine Jaeger
*Stercorarius pomarinus* · `pomjae`

- Jizz — 42–67 cm, about 10 cm of it the breeding adult's streamers; wingspan 110–125 cm, 540–920 g. Second-largest holarctic skua: bulky, full-chested, big-headed, strong hooked bill, broad but very pointed wings.
- Plumage — Three morphs. Light adult: black cap running below the bill, yellow neck-sides, white neck crossed by a dark breast band, blackish-brown above, white breast and underparts. Dark morph all dark brown, intermediates paler below and on the head. All morphs show a white primary flash, a diagnostic double flash beneath. Central tail feathers elongated 5.5–11 cm, spoon-shaped, twisted.
- Bare parts — Bill brownish with the strong downward hook of all skuas; feet black.
- In the field — Flight measured, looking very big and broad-winged when gliding, falcon-like at speed. Streamers moult twice a year, so migrants usually lack them. Harsh chattering which-yew, barking calls and rising nasal ringing series.
- Similar species —
  - Great Skua: Bigger and heavier, 50–58 cm and 1.17–1.5 kg, streaked greyish brown with black cap, short blunt tail and no elongated central feathers; barrel chest, herring-gull size.
  - Parasitic Jaeger: Smaller and slimmer, 37–48 cm, more falcon-like in flight; tail projection pointed and about 7 cm, and the primary flash is single, not a double underwing flash.
  - Long-tailed Jaeger: Slimmer and smaller, 88–117 cm span, 220–444 g; grey back, dark primaries without any white flash, very long fine streamers, colder greyer tones.

## Purple Heron
*Ardea purpurea* · `purher1`

- Jizz — 78–97 cm, wingspan 120–152 cm, 0.5–1.35 kg; stands 70–94 cm. Large but slender; smaller than Grey Heron, head smaller and sloping flatter into the bill, bill thinner and less dagger-like, toes much longer.
- Plumage — Darker and more reddish-brown than Grey Heron, adults also darker grey on the back. Forehead and crown black, a dark stripe down the hindneck ending in a slender dangling crest. Head and neck sides buff-chestnut with dark lines down each side; mantle oily brown; breast chestnut-brown, belly and undertail-coverts black. First-winter: upperparts, coverts, neck and head ochre-brown, neck streaks indistinct.
- Bare parts — Bill long, straight, powerful, brownish-yellow and brighter when breeding, culmen dark, upper mandible base pale; iris yellow; legs brown in front, yellowish behind.
- In the field — Flies with the neck kinked in an S and the long toes often spread; wingbeats faster than Grey Heron's. Call a harsh frarnk, quieter and higher-pitched than Grey Heron's, and generally a less noisy bird.
- Similar species —
  - Gray Heron: Larger, 85–102 cm; ashy-grey above, white head and neck with a broad black supercilium into a longer crest; bill heavier and more dagger-like, toes shorter.
  - Great Egret: All-white plumage with a yellow bill and black legs and feet; no chestnut neck, no black belly; wingspan 131–170 cm against 120–152 cm.
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Much smaller, 46–56 cm; white and stocky, short thick neck, sturdy yellow bill, hunched posture; breeding birds orange-buff on crown, back and breast.
  - Eurasian Bittern: Thickset and buffy-brown, streaked and barred throughout, crown and nape black; smaller, 69–81 cm, and lacking the chestnut neck sides and black belly.

## Purple Sandpiper
*Calidris maritima* · `pursan`

- Jizz — 19–22 cm, 42–46 cm across the wings, 50–105 g. Compact, bulky, short-necked sandpiper; bill medium-length and thin.
- Plumage — Summer: dark above with a faint purple gloss, scapulars grey-black turning rufous inwards with white fringes; mostly white below with grey-buff vermiculation, breast smeared grey, rump black. Winter: darker and plainer, head, breast and upperparts uniform grey-brown, no supercilium. Juvenile: paler breast, strongly orange legs, yellow bill base.
- Bare parts — Legs short and yellow, mustard-yellow in winter, bright orange on juveniles; bill dark with a yellow base.
- In the field — In flight looks dark, with whitish belly, axillaries and rump sides and a narrow indistinct pale wingbar; call a short nasal krit-krit, alarm a laughing kryhyhyhy.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Larger, 23–26 cm, with short dark legs and an all-dark bill; winter plumage uniform pale grey, breeding birds cinnamon on face, throat and breast.
  - Dunlin: Slighter and a little smaller, 16–20 cm, longer-necked, with black legs and a black decurved bill; breeding birds show a solid black belly.
  - Ruff: Much bigger and pot-bellied, male 29–32 cm, with a small head, longish neck and long variable legs; deeper, slower wing stroke.
  - Temminck's Stint: Much smaller, 13.5–15 cm; plain brown above, dull yellowish or brown legs, white outer tail feathers, short-legged and long-winged.

## Pygmy Cormorant
*Microcarbo pygmaeus* · `pygcor2`

- Jizz — 45–60 cm, wingspan 75–90 cm, about 700 g. Small cormorant, told from its larger relatives by a proportionally long tail and a short bill.
- Plumage — Dark throughout, black with dark-green and brown gloss; head dark red-brown. Breeding adult: white spots on head and neck. Non-breeding: no white spots, breast paler and brown-toned. Juvenile: duller and browner.
- In the field — Lacking hooks on the flight feathers, it becomes soaked when diving, forcing out the air that would buoy it up, so it is often seen ashore drying its wings. Colony calls croaking and grunting.
- Similar species —
  - European Shag: Much larger, 68–78 cm against 45–60 cm; yellow throat patch, breeding adults crested with a metallic green sheen; bill thinner than a cormorant's but the tail shorter than the pygmy's.
  - Great Cormorant: Far bigger, 70–102 cm and wingspan 121–160 cm against 45–60 cm and 75–90 cm; the pygmy's long tail and short bill are the shape clues.

## Radde's Accentor
*Prunella ocularis* · `radacc2`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm. Small, sparrow-like accentor with a thin bill, the size of a dunnock.
- Plumage — Dark cheeks and dark crown set off a broad dirty-white supercilium; upperparts cold brown; underparts warm beige, the orange tone concentrated on the breast.
- In the field — Lively and restless, scurrying about on the ground and flitting between low bushes. Song a silvery, clear phrase.
- Similar species —
  - Dunnock: Same size but dull grey-headed, back brown streaked blackish; no dark cheeks and no broad white supercilium; flicks its wings frequently.
  - Alpine Accentor: Larger, 15–17.5 cm, with an all-grey head and red-brown spotting on the underparts; no white supercilium and no dark cheeks.

## Razorbill
*Alca torda* · `razorb`

- Jizz — 37–43 cm, wingspan 60–69 cm, 505–890 g. Large, thick-set auk; wings narrow and pointed, folded tips reaching over the tail base; tail long for an auk, longest in the centre; bill deep, laterally compressed, blunt-ended.
- Plumage — Breeding: head, neck, back and feet black, breast and belly white, underwing and axillaries wholly white; thin white line from eye to bill; bill grooved near the tip and crossed by a bold white stripe. Non-breeding: chin, cheeks, face behind eye and neck-sides white, head and bill lines faint or gone. Juvenile: as winter adult, bill shorter, narrower, plain black.
- Bare parts — Bill black, deep and blunt, grooved and white-striped near the tip, slimmer with fainter grooves in winter; feet black.
- In the field — Flight straight on rapid whirring beats, feet not projecting beyond the tail; hunts alone, usually diving only a few metres; carries fish crosswise in the bill for the chick; incubates in a horizontal stance.
- Similar species —
  - Common Murre: Bill thin, dark and pointed, not deep and blunt; head paler, tail small and rounded; winter face white with a dark spur behind the eye; axillaries not clean white.
  - Thick-billed Murre: Larger (40–48 cm, span 64–81 cm) with a long pointed bill and a small rounded black tail; no white bill-stripe and no long tail.
  - Black Guillemot: Smaller (30–32 cm), wholly black with a large white upperwing patch and bright coral-red legs and feet; no white belly, no white bill-stripe.
  - Atlantic Puffin: Smaller (28–30 cm), short-winged and short-tailed, with pale grey lozenge face patches, a broad black collar and a stout deep bill; no white bill-stripe.

## Red Avadavat
*Amandava amandava* · `redava`

- Jizz — 9–10 cm. Tiny finch-like waxbill, about the size of a firecrest; bill short and pointed, tail short, rounded and black.
- Plumage — Breeding male: deep red on face, neck sides and underparts, flanks flecked white; lores dark; back and scapulars brownish red, white-spotted; wings blackish-brown, coverts and tertials white-tipped; rump and uppertail-coverts red, spotted pink to white; lower belly and undertail-coverts blackish, tail white-tipped below. Female and non-breeding male: brown above, beige below, orange on flanks, keeping red bill and red rump.
- Bare parts — Bill bright red in all adult plumages, with a dark line along the upper mandible; iris red, red-brown or orange; feet flesh to brownish.
- In the field — Call a light tsee tsee.
- Similar species —
  - Common Waxbill: Grey-brown finely barred dark throughout, with a red eye-stripe and whitish cheeks and throat; rump brown, not red; tail long and graduated; 9.5–13 cm.

## Red Crossbill
*Loxia curvirostra* · `redcro`

- Jizz — 15–17 cm, 34–40 g, wingspan 27–30 cm. Stocky finch, big round head, thick curved bill longer than deep at the base with crossed tips; tail short and deeply forked.
- Plumage — Male: mostly red to brick-red, strongest on crown, breast and rump, the rump looking pink when fresh; wings and tail dark brown; some birds yellow, orange or showing green. Female: olive to grey-green or yellow-green, rump yellowish, flanks strongly brown-streaked. Juvenile: grey-brown to greenish-grey, heavily dark-streaked, bill straight at first and crossing only later.
- Bare parts — Eyes small and dark brown.
- In the field — Levers conifer cone scales apart with the crossed mandibles to extract seeds. In flight the wings look dark and unbarred and the rump pink.
- Similar species —
  - Parrot Crossbill: Bulkier (16–18 cm, 44–58 g) with a larger head and thicker bill, deeper than long, crossed tips often hidden; plumage identical — tell by call.
  - White-winged Crossbill: Two bold white wing-bars on blackish wings, male raspberry-red with pink-edged black scapulars; Red Crossbill's wings are plain brownish-black.
  - Hawfinch: Bill huge, conical and uncrossed; light brown with an orange-toned head, black round the eye and on the throat; short tail, short pinkish legs.
  - Pine Grosbeak: Much larger (19–25.5 cm, 52–78 g); bill conical, not crossed; black wings with white wing-bars and a long forked black tail; male rose-red.

## Red Kite
*Milvus milvus* · `redkit1`

- Jizz — 60–70 cm, wingspan 170–195 cm; males 800–1200 g, females 1000–1300 g. Elegant; long narrow wings held in a dihedral, long deeply forked tail.
- Plumage — Body, upper tail and wing-coverts rufous; head and neck whitish, brown-streaked; underparts red-brown with black shaft-streaks. White primary patches contrast with deeply fingered black wing-tips and dark secondaries; tail rufous, barred blackish. Female as male. Juvenile: washed-out, breast buff with pale streaks, tail less deeply forked with a dark subterminal band, pale tips to all greater coverts.
- In the field — Soars on long wings held at a dihedral, twisting the forked tail as it changes direction. Call a thin piping, less mewling than common buzzard. Takes small mammals, invertebrates, amphibians, small birds and carrion.
- Similar species —
  - Black Kite: Slightly smaller, tail less deeply forked in flight; brown throughout with no rufous and no white primary patches; head and neck paler, dark patch behind the eye.
  - Common Buzzard: 40–58 cm, span 109–140 cm; compact, round-headed, fairly short unforked tail; no white primary windows. Call more mewling than the kite's thin pipe.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: A medium-sized eagle at 55–74 cm, span 142–175 cm, 1.4–2.5 kg; female larger than male. No forked tail and no white primary windows.
  - Short-toed Snake-Eagle: 59–70 cm, span 162–195 cm; predominantly white below, pale brown chin, throat and upper breast, owl-like rounded head, bright yellow eyes; tail barred, not forked.

## Red Knot
*Calidris canutus* · `redkno`

- Jizz — 23–26 cm, wingspan 47–53 cm, 100–225 g. Squat, thickset, blackbird-sized Calidris; small head and eyes, short neck, short legs, medium-thin straight dark bill no longer than the head.
- Plumage — Breeding: back and coverts mottled grey, orange, white and black; face, throat, breast and underparts cinnamon-red, rear belly paler. Female slightly lighter, eye-line less distinct. Winter: uniformly pale grey above, pale below, white supercilium, grey-spotted breast and flanks. Juvenile: like winter adult but buffier on the breast, back and coverts scaled dark inside pale feather tips.
- Bare parts — Bill short, straight, dark, with a pale almost white ring round the base; legs short, dark grey-green, olive-yellow in juveniles.
- In the field — Feeds more deliberately than dunlin and flocks keep tightly packed, cloud-like in flight; low-slung on the short legs. Flight shows a white wingbar and grey rump and tail. Nasal soft uätt-uätt; low knutt from flocks.
- Similar species —
  - Dunlin: Smaller, 16–21 cm and 34–77 g; breeding bird has a black belly, and it feeds faster and less tightly packed. Bill slightly decurved, legs black.
  - Ruff: Pot-bellied with a small head, longer neck and long pale legs; deeper, slower wingbeats, only a thin indistinct wingbar and white ovals each side of the tail.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Smaller, 19–22 cm; dark above with a purplish gloss, black rump, short yellow legs, dark bill with a yellow base.
  - Temminck's Stint: Tiny, 13.5–15 cm; plain brown above and on the head, white outer tail feathers, dull yellowish or brown legs.

## Red Phalarope
*Phalaropus fulicarius* · `redpha1`

- Jizz — About 21 cm, 40–80 g. Small swimming wader; toes lobed, bill straight and somewhat thicker than Red-necked Phalarope's.
- Plumage — Breeding female: dark brown and black above, red below, white cheek patch, blackish-brown crown. Male a duller version, crown light brown. Non-breeding: pale grey above, white below, face and most of the crown white, black patch behind the eye always present. Juvenile: grey-brown above, buff below, dark eye-patch.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow with black tip when breeding, more yellow on the female; black in winter.
- In the field — Swims habitually. Call a short 'beek'. Moults August to December, after which the sexes are alike.
- Similar species —
  - Red-necked Phalarope: Smaller, 18 cm, with a fine bill; breeding bird dark grey above with chestnut neck and upper breast, black face, white throat, and a white wing stripe.
  - Jack Snipe: Snipe build: mottled brown above, pale below, two pale lateral crown-stripes and no central one, yellow back stripes in flight; bobs as if on springs.
  - Pectoral Sandpiper: Grey-brown back and grey breast sharply cut off at its lower edge; legs yellowish, bill olive with darker tip; larger, 19–24 cm.
  - Curlew Sandpiper: Long black bill, 32–44 mm, dark grey to black feet; large white rump patch and a white stripe across the wing in flight; 18–23 cm.

## Red-backed Shrike
*Lanius collurio* · `rebshr1`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm, 22–47 g. Smallest shrike of central Europe; strong, slightly hooked bill. Wing about 93 mm, tail 68–90 mm.
- Plumage — Male: chestnut back and scapulars, pale blue-grey crown and nape, narrow black mask through the eye, swallowing the eye at distance, sometimes edged by a faint whitish supercilium; grey rump; underparts tinged pink; black-and-white tail like a wheatear's. Female: brown and vermiculated above, greyer nape and rump, darker mask, underparts buff and vermiculated. Juvenile: as female, vermiculated above as well.
- Bare parts — Iris dark; bill strong and slightly hooked.
- In the field — Hunts from prominent perches and impales prey — large insects, small birds, frogs, rodents, lizards — on thorns or barbed wire as a larder. Song a quiet, tuneful twittering with harsh notes and mimicry.
- Similar species —
  - Woodchat Shrike: Slightly larger, 17–19 cm; male black and white above with a chestnut crown and pure white underparts. Female and young brown and white above, vermiculated as in red-backed.
  - Iberian Gray Shrike: Grey, never chestnut, above and darkest on crown, nape and mantle; greyish-pink below, mask wider, clear white supercilium, white patch on the primaries.
  - Great Gray Shrike: Much larger, thrush-sized, 22–26 cm against 16–18 cm, and 60–70 g; grey above rather than rusty-backed.
  - Lesser Gray Shrike: Black covers the forecrown as well as mask, nape and cheeks; hindcrown and back pale bluish-grey, underparts white flushed pink on lower breast and belly.

## Red-billed Chough
*Pyrrhocorax pyrrhocorax* · `rebcho1`

- Jizz — 38–41 cm, wingspan 73–90 cm, average 310 g. Medium-large corvid, slim and long-legged, with a long, narrow, curved bill; wings rectangular, tail square-ended.
- Plumage — Adult velvet-black, glossed green on the body, the metallic sheen varying between populations; sexes alike in colour. Juvenile less glossy. Legs lack the scaling shown by other corvids; nasal bristles very short, barely covering the nostrils.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs red in adults; juvenile has an orange to brown-yellow bill and pink legs until its first autumn.
- In the field — Flight buoyant and easy. Call a loud, ringing chee-ow, clearer and louder than the jackdaw's; small races call at higher frequency than large ones.
- Similar species —
  - Yellow-billed Chough: Bill short and yellow, not long, curved and red; slightly smaller, 34–39 cm, with proportionately longer, less square-ended tail and shorter, less rectangular wings; calls preep and sweeeooo.
  - Eurasian Jackdaw: Smaller, 33–39 cm; unglossed plumage with light grey nape and neck and slate-grey underparts; bill short, stout and black; legs black.
  - Northern Nutcracker: Chocolate-brown heavily spotted white, white vent, white tail corners; broad wings, short tail, undulating flight; bill black, 32–38 cm.
  - Eurasian Jay: Pinkish-brown body, white rump, black moustache, barred blue wing patch, black tail; bill not red; 34–35 cm.

## Red-breasted Flycatcher
*Ficedula parva* · `rebfly`

- Jizz — 11–12 cm. Very small flycatcher, only slightly larger than a wren; bill small, broad-based but pointed, as in aerial insectivores.
- Plumage — Head and upperparts dull dark brown, unmarked. Male: throat orange-red, diffuse against the buff-white breast, sharply bordered by slate-grey neck sides, head and nape. Female: no red throat, no grey, whitish below with a yellowish flank wash. Tail black with white bases to the outer feathers, wheatear-like; upper tail-coverts paler than the tail. Noticeable white orbital ring in all plumages.
- Bare parts — Bill black with a pale pinkish base to the lower mandible, extensive and obvious from below; eye large and dark.
- In the field — Restless, moving quickly through the foliage; perched birds constantly flick and fan the tail, flashing the white tail sides. Song three to four seconds of far-carrying, repetitive whistles; migrants give a short, muffled dry trill, serrrt.
- Similar species —
  - European Pied Flycatcher: Larger, 12–13.5 cm; breeding male black above and white below with a large white wing patch, white tail sides and small white forehead spot; no orange throat.
  - Collared Flycatcher: Larger, 12–13.5 cm; breeding male black and white with a white collar, large white forehead patch, big white wing patch and pale rump; no orange throat.
  - Semicollared Flycatcher: Larger, 12–13.5 cm; a black-and-white flycatcher intermediate between Collared and Pied — the candidate text gives no further marks.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: Black and white with large white wing and forehead patches; candidate text gives no measurements and no other marks.

## Red-breasted Goose
*Branta ruficollis* · `rebgoo1`

- Jizz — 53–56 cm, 1000–1620 g; wingspan 116–135 cm. Small, dark Branta with a short bill and relatively thick neck; strikingly narrow-winged in flight.
- Plumage — Black, sharply drawn with white contour lines; breast and neck panels rusty-red, each outlined white; white flank stripe; back and belly black, upper- and undertail white. Two pale wingbars in flight. Juvenile less contrastingly marked.
- Bare parts — Feet black, webbed.
- In the field — Unmistakable seen well, but at range the red breast looks dark and birds are surprisingly hard to pick out of mixed flocks, especially Brent; in flight the short neck and black belly show.
- Similar species —
  - Canada Goose: Far larger, 75–110 cm against 53–56; black head and neck with a white chinstrap and brownish body, never a red breast or white flank lines.
  - Barnacle Goose: White face, black head, neck and upper breast, silver-grey barred back, white belly; no red. Larger, 55–70 cm, with a V-shaped white rump in flight.
  - Brant: Uniformly dark grey-brown with only a small white patch on each side of a black neck; no red breast, no white flank stripe. Longer, 55–66 cm.
  - Cackling Goose: White chinstrap on a black head and neck, no red anywhere; larger, 60–65 cm and 1400–2380 g against 53–56 cm and 1000–1620 g.

## Red-breasted Merganser
*Mergus serrator* · `rebmer`

- Jizz — 51–64 cm, wingspan 66–82 cm; male 950–1,350 g, female 800–1,100 g. Medium sawbill with a pointed look; long thin serrated bill, shaggy divided crest.
- Plumage — Male: black head and upper neck glossed green, ragged two-part crest, broad white collar, pale rusty breast spotted black, black back with a white stripe, white vent and speculum crossed by black bars. Female: red-brown head and upper neck merging into greyish lower neck and breast-sides, white below, crested. Juvenile: as female, shorter crest, smaller white wing patches.
- Bare parts — Bill long, narrow, straight, red with a black upper ridge; feet red.
- In the field — Prefers saltwater. Female a rasping prrak prrak and a harsh gruk in flight; male a catlike meow, or a rhythmic tjika… pi-tjiih; otherwise largely silent.
- Similar species —
  - Common Merganser: Larger, 58–72 cm; crest smooth and rounded, not spiky; drake's neck all white; female's brown head cut sharply off the white breast, with white chin.
  - King Eider: Bulky sea duck, no crest and no thin bill; male black with white breast, multicoloured head and red knobbed bill; female warm brown.
  - Common Eider: Bulky, big-headed, with a large wedge-shaped bill running into the forehead; male black and white with green nape; female barred brown.
  - Velvet Scoter: Thick neck, long broad bill, pointed tail; male glossy black with a large white wing patch and a small white mark behind the eye.

## Red-crested Pochard
*Netta rufina* · `recpoc`

- Jizz — 45–55 cm, wingspan to 90 cm, 800–1500 g. Large diving duck; head disproportionately big and rounded, thickened further by a loose crest.
- Plumage — Male: rounded orange to red-brown head with loose crest; black breast, crop, belly and undertail-coverts; white flanks; brown back and wings; black tail. Female: pale brown, back darker, crown dark brown from eye level down, cheeks and lower neck whitish-grey, face whitish. Eclipse male: female-like but with a more contrasting head pattern.
- Bare parts — Male's bill scarlet, iris red; female's bill dark brown with a light red band at the tip, iris dark; legs brown.
- In the field — White leading edge and a broad white wing-bar in flight; swimming females show white underwing-coverts as a pale patch near the tail. Gregarious in winter with other diving ducks; upends more than most. Male a wheezing veht, female hoarse vrah-vrah-vrah.
- Similar species —
  - Chiloe Wigeon: Head black glossed green-blue with white forehead and cheeks and a white ear-patch, breast barred black and white; bill blue-grey with a black tip, not red; 46–56 cm.
  - Wood Duck: Smaller perching duck, 47–54 cm; drake multicoloured with red eyes and a white flare down the neck; both sexes crested; blue-green speculum white-edged behind.
  - Gadwall: Drake patterned grey with a black stern and a brilliant white speculum, no orange head or red bill; female mallard-like with white belly and orange-edged bill; 47–58 cm.
  - Northern Shoveler: Huge spatulate bill; drake dark green head, white breast, chestnut belly and flanks, pale blue forewing in flight; 44–52 cm.

## Red-flanked Bluetail
*Tarsiger cyanurus* · `refblu1`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm, 10–18 g. Small chat, the size and weight of Common Redstart, slimmer and slightly smaller than European Robin; small thin bill, slender legs.
- Plumage — Male: deep blue above, brighter cobalt on rump, tail sides, shoulder and crown sides, darkest on cheeks and neck sides; white throat, creamy-white underparts, bright orange flanks; white streak over forehead and eye. Female and immature male: olive-brown above with blue rump and tail, dusky breast, whitish throat and eye-ring, orange flanks. Juvenile spotted, like a young robin.
- Bare parts — Bill small, thin and black; legs slender and black.
- In the field — Flicks tail and wings constantly, redstart-fashion; flies from a perch to take insects in the air or on the ground. Male sings a brisk, short, melancholy trill from treetops, often at dawn; alarm a straight viht or muffled track.
- Similar species —
  - European Robin: Orange breast and face lined blue-grey, brown above, whitish belly — no blue tail or rump and no orange flanks; slightly stockier, 12.5–14 cm.
  - Common Redstart: Tail orange-red, not blue; male slate-grey above with orange-chestnut rump, flanks and underparts. Same length, 13–14.5 cm, and quivers its tail the same way.
  - Cyprus Wheatear: Sources give no plumage for it: separated here on size alone, 14–15 cm against 13–14 cm.
  - Spotted Flycatcher: Dull grey-brown above, off-white below with streaked crown and breast, sexes alike; no blue tail, no orange flanks; 13–15 cm.

## Red-footed Falcon
*Falco vespertinus* · `reffal1`

- Jizz — 28–34 cm; wingspan 65–76 cm; 110–190 g. Medium-small, long-winged falcon.
- Plumage — Male all sooty blue-grey, darkest about the head, with paler silvery secondaries and primaries above contrasting with dark coverts; lower belly, trousers and undertail-coverts deep rufous-red; underwing uniformly grey. Female: grey barred above, crown and whole underside bright orange, face white with black eye-mask and short moustache. Juvenile: brown above, buff below streaked dark, face as female.
- Bare parts — Male: cere, eye-ring and feet red to deep orange; bill blue-grey, dark-tipped. Juvenile: cere and feet yellow.
- Similar species —
  - Eleonora's Falcon: Larger, 36–42 cm on an 87–104 cm span; dark morph all sooty brown, light morph buff below with black underwing-coverts against paler flight feathers.
  - Eurasian Kestrel: Chestnut-brown above spotted black, buff below finely streaked, with a long tail; larger at 32–39 cm. Male never all blue-grey with red trousers.
  - Peregrine Falcon: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 28–34 cm against 34–58 cm, and 110–190 g against 330–1,300 g.
  - Eurasian Hobby: Sexes alike: slate-grey above with two black moustaches, whitish underparts heavily black-streaked and rufous trousers. Slightly larger, 29–36 cm; no orange-headed female.

## Red-knobbed Coot
*Fulica cristata* · `rekcoo1`

- Jizz — 35–42 cm; wingspan 75–85 cm, male 770–910 g, female 455–790 g. Rounded, thin-necked waterbird; head small and angular, tail very short and wedge-shaped, wings broad.
- Plumage — Glossy black on head, neck and fore-body, somewhat paler behind; white frontal shield with two red knobs above it, swollen and visible only when breeding, and hard to see at any distance. Feathering between shield and bill rounded, not pointed. Secondaries with no white trailing edge. Juvenile: dark brown, throat and foreneck pale, shield barely developed.
- Bare parts — Bill white with a blue-grey tinge; iris red; legs yellow-grey to yellow-green, toes blue-grey. Juvenile: bill yellow-orange, legs orange-red, eyes black.
- In the field — Swims with the fore-body sinking relatively deep; toes long, lobed and partly webbed. Needs a good view — the knobs show only at close range and regress fast.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Coot: Bill and shield cream-white, not blue-grey; feathering between shield and bill pointed; no red knobs; and a white trailing edge to the secondaries in flight.
  - Eurasian Moorhen: Red frontal shield and a red, yellow-tipped bill; white undertail and white flank streaks; legs yellow. Smaller, 30–38 cm.
  - Western Swamphen: Chicken-sized, with brightly coloured plumage, huge feet and a red bill and frontal shield, not a white shield on a black bird.
  - Spotted Crake: Far smaller, 19–22.5 cm; brown above with white flank spots, a blue-grey breast, green legs and a short yellow, red-based bill.

## Red-legged Partridge
*Alectoris rufa* · `relpar1`

- Jizz — 32–36 cm. Squat, rotund partridge with a pale face, a face mask and a dark necklace; wings rounded.
- Plumage — Sandy-brown above; crown warm pinkish-brown, forecrown and crown edges pale blue-grey, narrow off-white supercilium. Black lores broaden behind the eye and run round the creamy-white throat into a black gorget that breaks into broad black streaks over the pale blue-grey breast. Belly pinkish-buff; flanks with eight to ten bold rufous bars edged black. Outer tail feathers rufous, hind neck browner.
- Bare parts — Bill red; bright red eye-ring above a patch of pinkish-red bare skin on the lores; legs pink to red.
- In the field — Sociable but wary, preferring to run from disturbance; flight low on fast beats and stiff glides, showing bright rufous tail feathers. Calls a long-repeated tsche or tset psiä tok; song builds from wät-wät through tek-tek to tok-tok-korährähräh.
- Similar species —
  - Chukar: Gorget sharply defined and unbroken, not dissolving into black breast streaks; back browner-grey, foreneck with a yellowish tinge, face white. 32–35 cm.
  - Barbary Partridge: Face light grey with a broad reddish-brown gorget — no white throat patch and no black necklace; back grey-brown, flanks white streaked rufous.
  - Black Grouse: Far larger, 45–60 cm against 32–36 cm; cock glossy black with blue sheen, white wing-line and undertail, red skin over the eye; hen drab and cryptic.
  - Black Francolin: Male black with a white cheek patch, chestnut collar and white flank spots, tail black barred white; female brown with a chestnut hind neck. 33–36 cm.

## Red-necked Grebe
*Podiceps grisegena* · `rengre`

- Jizz — 40–50 cm; 692–925 g in the nominate race, up to 1,600 g in the larger North American holbollii. Medium-large grebe, smaller and shorter-necked than Great Crested Grebe; bill long and pointed.
- Plumage — Breeding: black cap running below the eye, pale grey cheeks and throat, rusty red neck, dark grey back and flanks, white underparts.
