Winter: duskier than other grebes — grey cap ill-defined, merging into a grey face, a pale crescent curving behind the ear-coverts; foreneck whitish, hindneck darker. Juvenile: striped face, diffuse blackish cap, pale red neck.
- Bare parts — Bill long, pointed, black with a yellow base — yellow duller in winter, extensive on juveniles; eye dark brown.
- In the field — Unmistakable in breeding plumage; far less distinctive in winter, when it can be confused with other grebes.
- Similar species —
  - Great Crested Grebe: Larger, 46–51 cm, longer-necked with a more contrasting head pattern and always white above the eye; whiter in winter, with a pink bill.
  - Horned Grebe: Smaller, 31–38 cm, with a relatively smaller bill and a white rather than grey face; breeding bird chestnut-necked with erectable golden 'horns'.
  - Eared Grebe: Smaller, 28–34 cm; breeding bird has black head, neck and breast with an ochre fan behind a bright red eye — no rusty red neck.
  - Little Grebe: Small and dumpy, short pointed bill, bright yellow gape, rufous only on neck, cheeks and flanks, and a fluffy 'powder puff' rear in winter.

## Red-necked Nightjar
*Caprimulgus ruficollis* · `rennig1`

- Jizz — 30–34 cm; wingspan 60–65 cm. Larger and longer-tailed than Eurasian Nightjar; long narrow wings, small bill with a wide gape, soft downy plumage.
- Plumage — Lichen-grey, barred and streaked buff, chestnut and black; underparts barred; rufous neck-collar, and throat and upper breast finely barred yellow-red; four evenly broad pale bands across the upperwing, coverts pale-tipped. Both sexes show white wing spots, white tail sides and white throat; the male's whiter.
- In the field — By day lies flat and silent on the ground, like a lichened twig or bark flake. Song a mechanical two-note kyok-kyok, rising and falling as the head turns, given from a post or in flight; wing-clapping in display.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Nightjar: Smaller and shorter-tailed, 24.5–28 cm; hindneck collar buff, not rufous; only males show white in wing and tail. Churring song, not a knocking kyok-kyok.

## Red-necked Phalarope
*Phalaropus lobatus* · `renpha`

- Jizz — About 18 cm, wingspan 30–38 cm, 24–48 g. Small, slight wader; straight, very fine needle bill; lobed toes.
- Plumage — Breeding male: grey-brown above, black face, white throat, orange-brown neck patch smaller and duller than the female's. Female: slate-grey above, orange-red patch on the foreneck reaching the cheeks. White wing stripe. Juvenile: grey and brown above with yellow bands, black crown, black patch behind the eye. Winter grey above, white below; black eyepatch always present.
- In the field — Swims habitually, lobed toes aiding it. Call a sharp whit or twit; also a short nasal tjepp, a longer near-disyllabic tjerre, a harder tjick; display a chittering kik-kik-kikik-tree-kik.
- Similar species —
  - Red Phalarope: Larger, 21 cm against 18 cm, bill somewhat thicker; breeding bird red below with white cheek patches and a yellow black-tipped bill, black in winter.
  - Dunlin: Breeding bird shows a black belly no similar-sized wader has; slightly decurved black bill, black legs, narrow white wingbar. Winter greyish-brown above, white below.
  - Broad-billed Sandpiper: Pale crown stripe and a distinctive split supercilium; bill longer and straighter with the tip kinked down; shorter legs. 16–18 cm.
  - Sanderling: Winter bird very pale, almost white but for a dark shoulder patch; face and throat brick-red late summer; lacks a hind toe. 18–20 cm.

## Red-throated Loon
*Gavia stellata* · `retloo`

- Jizz — 53–69 cm, 1–2.7 kg; wingspan 91–110 cm. Smallest and lightest loon; long-bodied, short-necked and streamlined, legs set far back, bill sharply pointed; feet large, front toes fully webbed, tarsus flattened.
- Plumage — Breeding adult unmistakable: dark grey head and neck-sides with narrow black-and-white stripes down the hindneck, a triangular rust-red throat patch, white underparts and a plain dark grey-brown mantle — the only loon with an all-dark back. Non-breeding: chin, foreneck and much of the face white, crown and hindneck grey, mantle speckled white. Juvenile as winter but duller, neck greyer.
- Bare parts — Bill dark grey, pale grey in winter; iris carmine-red to burgundy; legs black outside, pale inside, webs pinkish-brown with darker margins.
- In the field — Swims with head and bill tilted noticeably upward. Wingbeats faster than other loons; feet barely project beyond the tail; head raised and lowered repeatedly in flight, otherwise carried low. Paired territorial calls, male a'roo, female a longer aaarooo.
- Similar species —
  - Arctic Loon: Larger, 58–75 cm; breeding bird has a black throat and large black foreneck patch with white parallel lines on throat-sides and chest, and a white-marked, not all-dark, back.
  - Common Loon: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 53–69 cm and 1–2.7 kg against 66–91 cm and 2.2–7.6 kg, wingspan 91–110 cm against 118–148 cm.
  - Yellow-billed Loon: The largest loon, 76–97 cm and 4–6.4 kg, wingspan 135–160 cm; breeding adult black and white with a purple gloss on head and neck.

## Red-throated Pipit
*Anthus cervinus* · `retpip`

- Jizz — 14–15 cm. Small, fairly slim pipit, about the size of a Meadow Pipit.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: brick-red face and throat. Otherwise dull brown above, crown and upper mantle boldly dark-streaked, rump and uppertail-coverts streaked too; whitish mantle stripes; underparts yellowish-white, breast and flanks heavily black-streaked. Female: red confined to throat and upper breast, lost in winter. First-winter: throat white, but strong white supercilium, narrow white eye-ring, pale tertial edges.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow, as in Meadow Pipit.
- In the field — Altogether more striped than the other pipits — more streaks on cap, back, flanks, rump and chest. Flight strong and direct, with a drawn-out psii call; song given in a short song-flight.
- Similar species —
  - Meadow Pipit: Plainer: brown above, buff below with darker barring and an unstreaked rump; no red face or throat. Hind claw conspicuously long.
  - Rock Pipit: Larger, 15.5–17 cm; smoky-olive above, weakly streaked, buff below with ill-defined brown streaks; dark bill, legs and iris, pale eye-ring; no red throat.
  - Tree Pipit: Heavier bill, greater contrast between buff breast and white belly, less striped overall; call a strong spek; perches in trees, parachuting song-flight.
  - Water Pipit: Greyish-brown above and weakly streaked, pink-buff below; grey head with broad white supercilium, white outer tail feathers; no red on face or throat.

## Redpoll
*Acanthis flammea* · `redpol1`

- Jizz — 11.5–14 cm, 12–16 g; wingspan 19–25 cm. Small finch, in size and shape like a Linnet but with a more forked tail; bill short, triangular and conical.
- Plumage — Brownish-grey to whitish above with dark streaks; bright red patch on forehead and crown, black bib, black feathering round the bill-base and a very fine black loral line. Two pale wingbars. Adult male's breast rose-red in spring, white or barely tinged in winter; female's yellow-grey. Rump streaked; broad dark brown streak across the vent.
- Bare parts — Bill yellowish with a dark tip; legs brown; iris dark brown.
- In the field — In flight the two white wingbars stand out on otherwise dark upperwings, against pale underwings; the pale underbody and vent contrast with the dark face in front and the dark tail behind.
- Similar species —
  - Citril Finch: Greyish above with a brown black-streaked back; yellow underparts, yellow double wingbars and a bright yellow face mask — no red forehead, no black bib. 12–12.5 cm.
  - Twite: Lacks the red head patch and red breast; brown streaked black above, buff to whitish streaked below; adult male has a pink rump; call a nasal twaa-it.
  - Corsican Finch: Like Citril Finch but back brown with dark streaks; yellow underparts and face, greyish uppertail-coverts; no red forehead, no black bib. 11.5–12.5 cm.
  - Eurasian Siskin: Male greenish above with yellow rump, yellow wing-stripe and yellow tail-sides, black cap and bib, short tail; no red on the head, which is diagnostic here.

## Redwing
*Turdus iliacus* · `redwin`

- Jizz — 20–24 cm, wingspan 33–34.5 cm, 50–75 g. Small, compact thrush; short-tailed with a proportionately large head.
- Plumage — Sexes alike: plain brown above, white underparts streaked and spotted dark brown, belly almost wholly white. Best marks the creamy-white supercilium and the rusty-red flanks and underwing-coverts — the red shows in flight and still on the flanks of perched birds. Subspecies coburni darker overall and rather larger.
- In the field — Song a short, varied falling scale of fluted notes ending in a low creak, sometimes rising instead; alarm a rattling 'tret-tret-tret'; flight call a drawn-out, harsh, sucking 'styyyf'.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blackbird: Much bigger, 23.5–29 cm against 20–24 cm; male all black with orange-yellow bill and eye-ring, female sooty-brown and barely mottled — no red flanks, no supercilium.
  - Fieldfare: Slate-grey head, nape and rump, dark brown back and blackish tail; underwing-coverts white, not rusty; harsh 'tsak tsak'. Larger, 22–27 cm.
  - Song Thrush: Very similar; Redwing is shorter-tailed and larger-headed with the creamy supercilium and rusty flanks — Song Thrush's underwing is warm yellow, bill yellowish, legs pink.
  - Mistle Thrush: Larger, 26–29 cm against 20–24 cm; pale grey-brown above, round black spots on a buff breast, white underwing and white tips to the outer tail.

## Reed Bunting
*Emberiza schoeniclus* · `reebun`

- Jizz — 13.5–15.5 cm, 10–28 g. Medium-sized bunting with a small but powerful seed-eating bill; bill stout and bulbous in south-east European, Caspian and Black Sea birds.
- Plumage — Male breeding: black head and throat, broad white neck collar and white moustachial stripe, dark-streaked brown back, greyish rump, pale underparts washed and streaked brown, heaviest on the flanks; white tail-sides. Grey feather fringes veil this after the autumn moult, wearing off through winter. Female: streaked brown head, buff supercilium, black-and-white malar, chestnut lesser coverts.
- Bare parts — Bill dark and usually small; legs dark.
- In the field — Song a monotonous repeated zripp zripp zryy; calls a soft siy and an impure buzzing bzy. Hard to identify on migration, especially a lone bird.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Larger and bulkier, 16–19 cm, lark-like; sexes alike, streaked grey-brown with breast streaking forming a gorget, dark eye, yellowish mandibles, plain brown tail.
  - Yellowhammer: Larger, 15.5–17 cm; male's head bright yellow with rufous rump and yellow underparts, female duller but still yellowish — never a black hood and white collar.
  - Rustic Bunting: Sources describe no plumage for it and the sizes almost coincide, 13.5–15.5 cm against 13–15 cm: nothing here separates the two.
  - Cirl Bunting: 15–16.5 cm; male has a yellow head with black crown, eyestripe and throat and a greenish breast band; female shows streaked grey-brown rump and chestnut shoulders.

## Richard's Pipit
*Anthus richardi* · `ricpip1`

- Jizz — 17–20 cm, 25–36 g, wingspan 29–33 cm. Large, slender pipit; long yellow-brown legs and toes, long tail, long bill, hindclaw long and fairly straight.
- Plumage — Brown above with dark streaking, pale below; breast streaked, belly plain; throat and flanks warm buff to rusty, the throat clearly streaked; face strongly marked with pale lores and supercilium, dark eyestripe, moustachial and malar stripes; two pale wing-bars; outer tail feathers white.
- Bare parts — Legs long and yellow-brown; bill long and dark with a yellowish base to the lower mandible.
- In the field — Stands very upright; flight strong and undulating with an explosive shreep call like a house sparrow's chirp; song monotonous buzzy notes in an undulating song-flight.
- Similar species —
  - Meadow Pipit: Far smaller at 14.5–15 cm, thin-billed with pale pinkish-yellow legs; call a faint tsi-tsi, never an explosive shreep.
  - Rock Pipit: Smaller, smoky-olive above and heavily but diffusely streaked buff below; legs, bill and iris dark, with a pale eye-ring.
  - Tree Pipit: Smaller, buff breast contrasting with a white belly, heavier bill; perches in trees; call a strong spek and a parachuting song-flight.
  - Red-throated Pipit: Smaller at 14–15 cm; brick-red face and throat when breeding, otherwise streaked all over including cap, rump and flanks; call a psii.

## Ring Ouzel
*Turdus torquatus* · `rinouz1`

- Jizz — 23–29 cm, 90–138 g. Blackbird-sized and blackbird-shaped, but slimmer, with a slightly longer neck and tail and narrower wings.
- Plumage — Male black, hooded look from the unmarked dark head, throat and nape, broken by a broad white breast crescent; wing feathers pale-edged, giving a pale wing panel in flight; belly feathers white-fringed, scaled. Female: browner, breast band duller grey. Juvenile: spotted, crescent faint or absent, but wings still paler than a Blackbird's.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow to orange with a dark tip; legs greyish brown.
- In the field — Song simple repeated series in a desolate tone at a calm tempo, tack…tack or trüh…trüh; call a hard smacking tyck.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blackbird: No white breast crescent, wings uniformly dark without the pale panel; male glossy black with a yellow eye-ring; female sooty-brown with a pale throat.
  - Redwing: Smaller, 20–24 cm; brown above with white underparts spotted dark, red flanks and underwing, and a creamy supercilium; no breast crescent.
  - Fieldfare: Grey head, nape and rump against a chestnut-brown back and black tail; breast boldly speckled, underwing white; harsh tsak tsak in flight.
  - Song Thrush: Smaller, 20–23.5 cm; plain brown above, underparts cream to buff neatly black-spotted, underwing warm yellow, legs pink. Never black with a crescent.

## Ring-necked Duck
*Aythya collaris* · `rinduc`

- Jizz — 37–46 cm, wingspan 62–72 cm, 490–910 g. Small, short-bodied diving duck; big angular head with a high steep forehead, the peak set well back and a hint of a tuft making a corner between head and nape.
- Plumage — Male: head glossy purple-black, breast, back and stern black, flanks grey with a white wedge at the front; belly white; cinnamon neck ring usually impossible to see. Female: grey-brown with dark brown back, pale throat, pale surround to the bill base, pale eye-ring and often a pale line back from the eye.
- Bare parts — Bill grey, white band before a black tip, white line round the base; iris yellow in the male, brown in the female.
- In the field — Wing bar grey in flight, not white. Female gives a trrr.
- Similar species —
  - Tufted Duck: Drooping thin crest, flanks pure white and a white wing bar in flight; Ring-necked has grey flanks with a white front wedge, a banded bill and a grey wing bar.
  - Common Pochard: Chestnut head and neck, black breast, pale grey vermiculated body, sloping forehead and a long bill with a broad blue-grey band; larger, 42–49 cm.
  - Greater Scaup: Back pale grey, not black – the essential difference; head rounded with a green or purple sheen, bill plain light blue with a small black nail; 39–56 cm.
  - Ferruginous Duck: Rich dark chestnut head, breast and flanks with sharply contrasting pure white undertail-coverts; white belly and underwing patch in flight; male yellow-eyed.

## Ring-necked Pheasant
*Phasianus colchicus* · `rinphe1`

- Jizz — 53–90 cm, wingspan 56–90 cm, 500–3000 g. Medium-large gamebird; the male's narrow wedge-shaped tail alone accounts for almost 50 cm; female smaller, 50–63 cm, with a much shorter tail.
- Plumage — Male: body barred bright gold to fiery copper-red and chestnut, glossed green and purple, rump sometimes blue; wing coverts white or cream; head bottle-green with a small crest, elongated nape feathers forming ear-tufts, and bare scarlet face wattles. Nominate colchicus and some other races lack the white neck ring. Female and juvenile dull mottled brown throughout.
- Bare parts — Bill greenish horn in males, dark brownish horn in females; iris pale orange in males, orange to amber in females; legs unfeathered, male spurred.
- In the field — Nothing on flight, gait or voice in the sources; note the very long wedge-shaped tail and bare red face on any large ground bird.
- Similar species —
  - Barbary Partridge: Small and rotund: grey-brown back, grey breast, buff belly, light grey face with a broad reddish-brown gorget, rufous-streaked white flanks, red legs; prefers to run.
  - Red-legged Partridge: Rotund partridge: sandy-brown above, pale grey breast with a bold gorget of black streaking, rufous and black flank-bars, cream throat, red bill and eye-ring, pink legs.
  - Western Capercaillie: Little else in the sources: separated here on size — cock 74–85 cm and about 4.1 kg against the pheasant's 53–90 cm and 0.5–3 kg.

## Ringed Teal
*Callonetta leucophrys* · `rintea1`

- Jizz — 35–38 cm, wingspan 70 cm, 190–360 g. Small duck; toes webbed but with long pointed claws, so it perches on branches.
- Plumage — Drake: back rich chestnut, flanks pale grey, breast salmon speckled black, a black band from the top of the head down the nape. Female: back olive-brown, head blotched and streaked white, breast and belly pale with fine pencilled barring. Both sexes: dark tail, contrasting pale rump, distinctive white patch on the wing. Same plumage all year; no eclipse.
- Bare parts — Bill grey; legs and feet pink in both sexes.
- In the field — Sits on tree branches. Contact calls a cat-like mee-oowing in the duck, a lingering peewoo in the drake. Pairs bond easily.
- Similar species —
  - Green-winged Teal: Drake grey with a chestnut head and an iridescent green teardrop through the eye, white flank stripe and yellowish rear; no chestnut back or salmon breast; 34–43 cm.
  - Marbled Duck: Pale sandy-brown blotched off-white, with a dark eye-patch and shaggy head; wings plain and pale in flight with no white patch and no speculum; 39–42 cm.
  - Smew: Drake black-and-white, cracked-ice or panda patterned; female grey with chestnut crown, white cheeks and black lores; oval white wing patches; 38–44 cm, wingspan 56–69 cm.
  - Ring-necked Duck: A diving duck: drake with angular black head, black back, white breast, yellow eyes and a grey bill ringed white; 37–46 cm, wingspan 62–72 cm.

## River Warbler
*Locustella fluviatilis* · `eurwar2`

- Jizz — 14.5–16 cm. Largish, sturdy Locustella with a broad, rounded tail.
- Plumage — Upperparts unstreaked olive- to grey-brown; throat and breast with diffuse dark streaks; underparts dirty white, flanks olive-brown. Indistinct dirty-white supercilium, pale eye-ring. Olive-brown undertail-coverts broadly tipped whitish, giving a contrasting pattern. Juvenile: yellower below.
- Bare parts — Bill fairly dark; legs pink.
- In the field — Skulking; creeps through grass and low foliage, hard to see unless singing. Song a monotonous mechanical reeling, sewing-machine quality, dze-dze-dze, at dusk and in darkness; male sings 5–8 m up a tree, then drops like a stone.
- Similar species —
  - Common Grasshopper Warbler: Upperparts streaked, each feather dark-centred, not plain; cream-buff below with a few dark spots on breast and flanks; smaller, 12.5–13.5 cm.
  - Savi's Warbler: Plain reddish-brown above with unstreaked breast and undertail matching the belly; this bird olive-toned, breast streaked, undertail-coverts broadly white-tipped.
  - Lanceolated Warbler: Much smaller, 11.5–12.5 cm, and streaked: the spear-shaped breast streaks that give it its name, against diffuse smudging here.

## Rock Bunting
*Emberiza cia* · `rocbun1`

- Jizz — 16 cm. Fairly large bunting, short-winged and long-tailed.
- Plumage — Male: head pale grey, strongly marked black — a black stripe down each side of the crown, another through the eye, and a long moustachial line framing the cheek; upperparts chestnut; underparts unmarked deep buff. Female: washed-out, paler below, back grey-brown, head pattern less contrasted. Juvenile: as female but head streaked. The European race alone shows white wingbars.
- In the field — Rocky ground in the Mediterranean, sunny hill country, Alpine valleys and steep vineyards. Song a clear, high-pitched strophe, hesitant at the start and uneven in rhythm; call a short, pointed tsi.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Same length but plain: streaked grey-brown above, whitish below with streaking forming a gorget; no grey black-striped head, no chestnut upperparts; dark eye, yellowish mandibles.
  - Reed Bunting: Smaller, 13.5–15.5 cm; male's head and throat solid black with a white collar, female streaked brown-headed and barred brown above; neither shows deep buff underparts.
  - Yellowhammer: Yellow head and yellow underparts with rufous rump and white outer tail feathers; female duller but still yellow-toned, never grey with black head stripes.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 16 cm against 13–15 cm.

## Rock Pigeon
*Columba livia* · `rocpig`

- Jizz — 30–35 cm, 238–380 g; wingspan 60–68 cm. Compact grey pigeon, clearly smaller than Wood Pigeon but larger than Collared Dove; bill short, about 1.8 cm.
- Plumage — Head, neck and breast dark blue-grey with glossy yellowish, greenish and red-violet iridescence along the neck. Best mark is the white lower back; the two black bars across the pale grey wings are also distinctive. Tail with a black terminal band, outer webs edged white; underwing pale. Female's neck gloss weaker; juvenile duller. Feral birds endlessly variable.
- Bare parts — Bill grey-black with a conspicuous off-white cere; iris orange, red or golden-yellow with a paler inner ring; bare eye-surround bluish-grey; feet pink-red.
- In the field — Strong, fast flier, bursting out of sea caves and skimming low over the water with the paler grey rump showing from above; glides often, wings held in a marked V.
- Similar species —
  - Common Wood-Pigeon: Much larger at 38–45 cm, with white on the neck-side and a white wing patch, and a pinkish breast; no white lower back.
  - Stock Dove: Uniform blue-toned grey with no white back; wingbars short, on tertials and greater coverts only; broad dark tail-band; eye dark, bill pink with a yellow tip.
  - Oriental Turtle-Dove: Brown and scaly-backed, with a black-and-white striped patch on the neck-side and a wedge-shaped tail; flight relaxed and direct.
  - Eurasian Collared-Dove: Pale grey-buff and longer-tailed, with a black white-edged half-collar on the nape, red legs, black bill and red iris; lighter, 125–240 g.

## Rock Pipit
*Anthus petrosus* · `rocpip1`

- Jizz — 15.5–17 cm, 18–32.5 g. A big, robust pipit; bill fairly long and dark.
- Plumage — Smoky-olive above, weakly streaked darker; underparts buff, heavily but poorly defined streaked brown — the whole bird looks dark and blurred. Pale eye-ring; tail-sides grey-white. Sexes alike, males average brighter but overlap completely. Immature browner and more streaked above. Eastern littoralis in summer can show pinkish underparts and a pale supercilium.
- Bare parts — Legs, bill and iris dark brown or blackish.
- In the field — Call like Meadow Pipit's viisst but sharper, more explosive, and given more sparingly and singly. Song also Meadow Pipit-like but thinner and more metallic, delivered in a climbing then descending song-flight on stiff wings.
- Similar species —
  - Meadow Pipit: Smaller at 14.5–15 cm, browner with darker barring and narrow white tail edges; thin bill and pale pinkish-yellow legs, never dark; call a faint tsi-tsi.
  - Tree Pipit: White below with black markings and a buff breast contrasting sharply with the white belly; perches in trees; call a strong spek; parachutes down from a tree in song-flight.
  - Red-throated Pipit: Breeding adult has a brick-red face and throat; otherwise altogether more striped, with whitish mantle stripes and black marks on white below; call a characteristic psii.
  - Water Pipit: Greyer above, pink-buff below, with a broad white supercilium and white outer tail feathers. Winter birds separate readily; summer littoralis Rock Pipits are the hard ones.

## Rock Ptarmigan
*Lagopus muta* · `rocpta1`

- Jizz — 31–36 cm, wingspan 54–60 cm; 400–640 g across sources. Bulky round body, fairly small head, slender bill; tail 8 cm. About ten per cent smaller than willow ptarmigan.
- Plumage — Winter: all white but for the black tail; male also black lores and a red comb over the eye. Breeding male: head, neck, upper breast, back, coverts and flanks mottled grey-brown, black, beige and white; wings and underparts white. Female less contrasted. Autumn: grey-speckled, only the primaries white.
- Bare parts — Bill black and slender; red comb above the eye, largest on the breeding male; tarsi feathered white.
- In the field — White wings and black tail show in flight in every plumage. Moults three times a year to match the ground. Prefers higher, more barren ground than willow ptarmigan.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Ptarmigan: Larger, 35–44 cm, wingspan 55–66 cm; deep-chested, longer-necked, broad-billed; summer male marbled brown with reddish neck and breast; no black lores. Also lower, less barren ground.
  - Chukar: Rotund partridge, 32–35 cm; light brown back, grey breast, buff belly, white face with a sharply defined black gorget, rufous-streaked flanks, coral-red bill and red legs.
  - Black Francolin: Male black with a white cheek patch, chestnut collar and white-spotted flanks; female brown with a chestnut hind neck and black throat; legs reddish; 33–36 cm.
  - Hazel Grouse: Plump woodland grouse, 35–39 cm; grey upperparts, brown wings, chestnut-flecked white underparts; male has an erectile crest and white-bordered black throat; tail grey, black-tipped.

## Rock Sparrow
*Petronia petronia* · `rocpet1`

- Jizz — 15–17 cm. Large, stout sparrow-finch with a conspicuously big, conical bill; tail feathers tipped white on the inner web, largest on the central pair.
- Plumage — Strong whitish supercilium over a dark brown eye-stripe; paler grey-brown median crown stripe; cheeks, ear-coverts and neck-sides pale grey-brown; mantle grey-brown streaked dark; rump and uppertail-coverts grey-brown; breast and flanks diffusely streaked grey-brown; lemon-yellow spot on the lower throat, diagnostic but hard to see. Sexes alike; female's throat spot smaller and paler.
- Bare parts — Upper mandible brown, lower yellowish with a dark brown tip; eyes pale grey-brown; legs pale flesh to horn.
- In the field — Song loud and wheezy. Yellow breast patch carried by both sexes, signalling status in the breeding season.
- Similar species —
  - Italian Sparrow: Male chestnut-crowned with white cheeks, bright chestnut upperparts and a black throat-and-chest bib; underparts plain pale grey; no supercilium or yellow throat spot.
  - Spanish Sparrow: Male chestnut-crowned and white-cheeked with heavy black streaking below; female has a cream supercilium and broad cream back streaks; neither shows a yellow throat spot.
  - White-winged Snowfinch: Bigger (16.5–19 cm), grey-headed with unstreaked white underparts and huge white wing panels; black bib in summer, bill black then, yellow in winter.
  - Eurasian Tree Sparrow: Smaller (12.5–14 cm); rich chestnut crown, black ear-patch on a pure white cheek, black chin, two white wing-bars; underparts unstreaked.

## Rook
*Corvus frugilegus* · `rook1`

- Jizz — 44–46 cm, 280–340 g, wingspan 81–99 cm. A shade smaller than Carrion Crow; bill pointed and dagger-like, slimmer and less curved above; wings proportionately longer, narrower and more deeply fingered; tail less wedge-shaped than a raven's.
- Plumage — Black throughout with a blue to purple sheen, faintly reddish in some lights, crown and nape flashing green or violet; head, neck and shoulder feathers dense and silky with no visible shafts; thigh feathering shaggy and lax. Juvenile: black with a faint green gloss, hindneck, back and underparts brown-black, face still feathered.
- Bare parts — Bill grey-black, pointed, its base bare, scurfy and whitish to pale yellow in adults from February–May of the second year; legs black; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Flight lighter than Carrion Crow's, wingbeats a little quicker, with longer glides; on the ground walks or hops purposefully. Very social and talkative: usual call kah or krah, longer and higher krhäääh in aggression, with soft chattering notes in spring.
- Similar species —
  - Carrion Crow: All black with a greener gloss, face feathered to the bill base, bill stouter and more curved, wings shorter and broader; young rooks show a thin dagger bill.
  - Hooded Crow: Ash-grey body against a black head, throat, wings and tail; face fully feathered, bill stout and black; the rook is wholly black with a bare pale bill base.
  - Common Raven: Much larger (54–71 cm, span 116–153 cm), with a large deep bill and a wedge-shaped tail; face feathered, never showing a bare whitish patch.
  - Brown-necked Raven: Larger (52–56 cm), head and throat brownish-black, rest black glossed purple-blue; bill deeper, wings more pointed; no bare face patch.

## Rose-ringed Parakeet
*Psittacula krameri* · `rorpar`

- Jizz — 37–43 cm, much of it tail, 16–29 cm; 51–93 g in African, 104–143 g in Asian birds. Slender, narrow-winged, tail long and sharply pointed.
- Plumage — Grass-green throughout, flight feathers dark, tail blue with the two longest feathers yellow-tipped, undertail pale yellow. Adult male: black chin drawn back as a thin line to a rose-pink nape ring, and a dark lore-stripe. Female and immature lack the ring or show a shadowy pale to dark grey one, and are duller.
- Bare parts — Upper mandible red, lower mostly black; iris pale yellow with a red orbital ring; legs greyish with dark claws.
- In the field — Noisy, with an unmistakable squawking call; herbivorous and non-migratory.
- Similar species —
  - Alexandrine Parakeet: Much larger, 50–62 cm and 200–300 g, with a massive red bill and a red patch on each shoulder; nape band pink as here, cheeks and nape blue-grey.

## Ross's Goose
*Anser rossii* · `rosgoo`

- Jizz — 53–66 cm, 1198–1567 g, wingspan 113–116 cm. Small, rounded white goose; short neck, rounded head, short triangular bill; body carried slightly upright.
- Plumage — Pure white but for the black flight feathers; adults with all-white secondaries, juveniles dark-centred. Juvenile: largely grey, paler and sometimes yellowish on the belly, flight feathers brownish. A dark morph occurs but is extremely rare.
- Bare parts — Bill pink, the bluish base carrying warty growths that increase with age, weaker on females; no dark lips; eyes dark brown; legs deep pink.
- In the field — Stands slightly upright with a high, conspicuous tarsal joint; goslings' legs olive-grey, turning deep red with age.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Grey-brown, not white, with a darker head and neck, a short pink-and-black bill and pink feet; larger at 60–75 cm.
  - Graylag Goose: Far larger and bulkier at 74–91 cm, grey-brown with a thick long neck and a big orange or pink bill.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Larger at 68–90 cm, dark brown with white feather fringes above; bill black with an orange band, legs orange.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Greyish-brown with a white facial blaze and black belly blotches, pinkish bill and orange legs; larger at 64–81 cm.

## Ross's Gull
*Rhodostethia rosea* · `rosgul`

- Jizz — 29–31 cm, wingspan 90–100 cm, 140–250 g. Small gull with a round head, short thin bill, strongly sloping back, pointed wings and a wedge-shaped tail.
- Plumage — Summer adult: pale silver-grey above, white below, body flushed pink, a neat black ring behind the head. Winter: collar gone but for a grey stripe each side of the neck; small dark crescent behind the eye; nape and breast grey. Juvenile: white-grey, a dark W across the wings in flight and a big dark carpal triangle, no pink.
- Bare parts — Bill black in every season; legs red to red-orange, grey-brown to orange in juveniles.
- In the field — Two years to full adult plumage. The shape itself — round head, short bill, sloping back, long pointed wings, wedge tail — is unlike any other gull.
- Similar species —
  - Little Gull: Smaller, 24–28 cm, wingspan 62–69 cm, tail square; breeding adult has a full black hood and dark red bill; all plumages show very dark grey underwing.
  - Sabine's Gull: 27–33 cm; striking wing pattern of grey coverts, four black outer primaries and white inner primaries and secondaries; bill black with a yellow tip; tail slightly forked.
  - Common Tern: A tern: 31–35 cm with a 6–9 cm tail fork, black cap, narrow red black-tipped bill, orange-red legs; pale grey upperwing develops a grey wedge late in summer.
  - Black Tern: A tern, 25 cm, wingspan 61 cm; dark grey back, black head, neck and belly, white face and forewing, square light grey-brown tail, weak black bill.

## Rosy Starling
*Pastor roseus* · `rossta2`

- Jizz — 19–22 cm, 59–89 g. Same size and build as Common Starling; short strong tail, pointed triangular wings; fast, capable flier.
- Plumage — Male: body matt pink, contrasting glossy black head, wings and tail; crest reaching the nape, glossed metallic green like the wings. Female: duller, crest shorter, wings browner, pink and black not sharply divided. Winter: crest shorter and black feathers pale-fringed, plumage dull until the fringes wear. Juvenile: paler than young Starling, dark wings against a pale body and pale rump.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs pale orange; juvenile bill short, yellow, yellowish at the base.
- In the field — Crest fluffed out and most prominent when the bird is excited. Females in adult plumage at about one year, males at about two; second-year males like adult females but longer-crested and with pale feather edges.
- Similar species —
  - European Starling: Iridescent black spangled with white, never pink-bodied; juveniles darker than young Rosy with a longer, darker bill; same length, 19–23 cm.
  - Common Myna: Brown body, black hooded head and a bare yellow patch behind the eye; yellow bill and legs, white primary patch and white tail tip; 23–25 cm.
  - Spotless Starling: Oily black and spotless, never pink; shaggy long throat feathers and bright pink legs; 21–23 cm, 70–100 g.

## Rough-legged Hawk
*Buteo lagopus* · `rolhaw`

- Jizz — 46–68 cm, 600–1,660 g, wingspan 120–153 cm. Fairly large buzzard, bigger and longer-winged than Common Buzzard; head strikingly round; bill small and slight; tarsi feathered to the toes. Females larger.
- Plumage — Upperparts nearly uniform cinnamon-brown; round head and nape pale brown to grey; narrow dark line from eye to yellow bill-base; underparts mottled, with a broad brown chest-band and belly almost always black against a paler breast; square black carpal patch against white underwing in pale morphs; tail white with a sharp black terminal band. Juvenile: very pale below, tail-band diffuse.
- Bare parts — Bill small, yellow at the base; legs feathered to the toes, the feathering pale grey to white with dark shaft-streaks.
- In the field — White rump and tail-base with black terminal band show in every plumage. Vocal at the breeding site: a buzzard-like mewing, but shriller and falling in pitch.
- Similar species —
  - Common Buzzard: Smaller, 40–58 cm, wingspan 109–140 cm; tarsi bare, not feathered; plumage far more variable; lacks the clean white black-tipped tail and black belly.
  - Long-legged Buzzard: Sturdier, 50–66 cm, with a large bill on a small head, long tail and long legs; perches upright and prominent, walks on the ground; several morphs.
  - Black-winged Kite: Grey and white with black shoulder patches, wing-tips and eye-stripe; long falcon-like wings extend past the short square tail when perched.
  - Black Kite: Dark brown throughout with paler head and neck and dark-shafted streaking below; tail forked in flight; no white tail-base or black carpal patch.

## Ruddy Duck
*Oxyura jamaicensis* · `rudduc`

- Jizz — Male 37–43 cm, female 35–41 cm; female about 500 g, male some 100 g heavier. Small, compact stifftail; head thick and slightly peaked, neck short and thick, bill stout, broad and scoop-shaped; tail long, stiff, usually cocked.
- Plumage — Breeding male: blackish cap over bright white cheeks, body rich chestnut. Non-breeding male: dull grey-brown above, paler below, crown blackish-grey, white cheeks and white undertail-coverts kept. Female and first-year male brownish like the winter male but with a blurry stripe across the pale cheek patch. South American birds: males wholly black-headed.
- Bare parts — Male's bill bright light blue in summer, dull dark grey in winter; female's bill darker.
- In the field — Legs set far back: powerful swimmer and diver, clumsy ashore; takes off only after a long run-up and heavy wing-beats, then flies fast and low over the water. Wings solidly dark above. Complex moult makes ageing in the field hard.
- Similar species —
  - White-headed Duck: The other stifftail: male's whole head white below a black cap, not just the cheeks, and the body greyer and reddish; female white-faced; 43–48 cm, 580–750 g.
  - Bar-headed Goose: A pale grey goose, 71–76 cm, with black bars across an otherwise white head and a typical goose honking; nothing like a small cock-tailed diving duck.
  - Barnacle Goose: A goose, 55–70 cm with 120–145 cm wingspan: white face, black head, neck and upper breast, silver-grey barred back, white belly, black bill and legs.
  - Black Swan: Huge black swan, 110–142 cm, with white flight feathers, bright red pale-tipped bill and a very long S-curved neck; greyish-black legs.

## Ruddy Shelduck
*Tadorna ferruginea* · `rudshe`

- Jizz — 58–70 cm, wingspan 110–135 cm; male just under 1.4 kg, female 1.2 kg. Large, goose-like duck — recalls a goose more than most ducks in flight.
- Plumage — Male: orange-brown body, paler orange-brown head and neck cut off by a narrow black collar; rump, flight feathers, tail-coverts and tail black; green speculum on the inner wing; wing-coverts white above and below. Female: whitish head and neck, no collar. Juvenile: as female but darker brown. Colour variable, fading with wear; collar lost after breeding, restored by April.
- Bare parts — Bill black; legs dark grey.
- In the field — White fore-wing against black flight feathers and tail very striking in flight, but hardly visible at rest; sometimes a white flank line on swimming birds. Agile flier, even in alpine terrain; often perches on lookouts. Call a loud nasal honking.
- Similar species —
  - Common Shelduck: Dark green head and neck, white body with chestnut patches and a black belly, pinkish-red bill and pink feet; body never orange-brown; 55–65 cm.
  - Barnacle Goose: A goose: white face, black head, neck and upper breast, silver-grey barred back, white belly, small black bill and black legs; white V-shaped rump patch in flight.
  - Cackling Goose: Black head and neck with a white chinstrap over a brown body; a goose in build, with no white fore-wing and no green speculum; 60–65 cm.
  - Brant: Small dark goose: black head and neck with a small white neck patch, uniformly dark grey-brown body, pure white under-tail and a very short black tail.

## Ruddy Turnstone
*Arenaria interpres* · `rudtur`

- Jizz — 22–24 cm, wingspan 43–57 cm, 80–190 g. Small, stocky, squat wader; short neck, unusually short legs, and a short dark wedge-shaped bill 2–2.5 cm long and slightly upturned.
- Plumage — Harlequin pattern of brown, black and white in all seasons. Breeding: reddish-brown above with black marks, head mainly white with black crown streaks and a black face pattern, breast black with a white patch at the sides, rest white. Female duller, head browner and more streaked. Non-breeding: grey-brown above, black-mottled, head dark. Juvenile: pale brown head, pale fringes, scaly.
- Bare parts — Legs short, about 3.5 cm, and bright orange, duller yellow-brown on juveniles; bill dark.
- In the field — Strikingly pied in flight — white wingbar, white patch near the wing base, and white lower back, rump and tail with dark bands. Call a staccato rattling tuck-a-tuck-tuck; alarm chattering and accelerating.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Sandpiper: Slim and elegant, with very long yellowish legs and a long fine bill; plain greyish-brown, paler in winter, with a white wedge up the back in flight.
  - Green Sandpiper: Dark greenish-brown above, spotted white, greyish head and breast, white below; wings dark above and below against a brilliant white rump; legs and short bill dark green.
  - Terek Sandpiper: Long upcurved bill with a yellowish base; grey back, face and breast in every plumage, whitish belly and yellow feet; no harlequin pattern.
  - Lesser Yellowlegs: Longer-legged with yellow legs; upperparts mottled grey-brown, black and white, breast and neck streaked brown; bill slim, straight, dark, about head length.

## Ruff
*Calidris pugnax* · `ruff`

- Jizz — Male 29–32 cm, wingspan 54–60 cm, 130–254 g; female far smaller, 20–25 cm, span 47–52 cm, 70–150 g. Pot-bellied, small-headed, longish-necked, long-legged; bill medium and faintly decurved.
- Plumage — Back grey-brown and scaled, underparts white. Breeding male unmistakable: head tufts and a neck ruff, black, chestnut or white, solid, barred or irregular, with extensive black on the breast. Female and non-breeding birds plain and scaled, without ornaments. Juvenile: broad cinnamon to cream feather edges, coarsely scaled above, crown streaked black.
- Bare parts — Legs dark greenish in juveniles, pink to orange in adults, red on breeding males; bill dark, orange-red or pink; eye small and dark.
- In the field — Deeper, slower wing stroke than waders of similar size; thin indistinct white wingbar and white ovals at the sides of the tail. Usually mute. Males display communally.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Smaller and evenly built, 23–26 cm, not pot-bellied; winter plumage uniform pale grey, breeding birds cinnamon on face, throat and breast; short dark legs.
  - Dunlin: Much smaller, 16–20 cm; breeding birds show a solid black belly, all ages a narrow white wingbar; bill slightly decurved and black, legs black.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Smaller and compact, 20–22 cm; dark above with a purplish gloss, black rump, short yellow legs and a yellow-based bill.
  - Temminck's Stint: Tiny beside it, 13.5–15 cm; plain brown above, white outer tail feathers, dull yellowish or brown legs.

## Rufous-tailed Rock-Thrush
*Monticola saxatilis* · `rtrthr1`

- Jizz — 17–20 cm. Medium-sized but stocky and compact, thrush-like; longer-winged and shorter-tailed than a true thrush, and slightly smaller than Song Thrush.
- Plumage — Summer male unmistakable: blue-grey head, orange-red breast and underparts, white back, dark brown wings, rufous outer tail feathers. Female and immature much plainer — dark brown scaly above, paler scaly orange-buff below, tail rufous as in the male. Juvenile more spotted than the female. Both sexes show a dark brown tail centre flanked by rufous.
- In the field — Quivers the tail when agitated. Song a clear, melancholy, melodic phrase, often falling in pitch, recalling Redwing or a distant Blackbird, sometimes given in a descending song-flight; calls a squeaky viiht and a smacking tjack. Long-distance migrant to African savanna.
- Similar species —
  - Blue Rock-Thrush: Larger, 21–23 cm, with a long slim bill; male all blue-grey with darker wings, no orange underparts or white back; both sexes lack the rufous outer tail feathers.
  - Black Wheatear: All black (female dark brown) with white rump and largely white tail carrying a black inverted T — no rufous in the tail; 16–18 cm.
  - Thrush Nightingale: Uniform dark olive-brown above, breast mottled brown on buff; tail feathers dark rufous-brown throughout, not rufous sides against a dark centre; 16–18 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Pale sandy above, creamy supercilium, white rump, black tail with a big white base; no rufous tail, no blue head or orange underparts; 16.5 cm.

## Rufous-tailed Scrub-Robin
*Cercotrichas galactotes* · `rutscr1`

- Jizz — 15 cm. Slim, long-legged, thrush-like chat; large rounded tail, often spread as a fan.
- Plumage — Upperparts rich brownish chestnut, rump and uppertail-coverts more rufous; broad creamy-white curved supercilium from nostrils to behind eye, dark brown line through lores and eye; ear-coverts pale brown. Underparts buffish white, chin, central belly and undertail-coverts palest. Secondaries white-tipped. Tail rufous-chestnut, outer feathers broadly black-banded and white-tipped. Juvenile: paler sandy-brown. Western birds paler, warmer above.
- Bare parts — Iris and bill brown, lower mandible grey at the base; legs and feet pale brown.
- In the field — Often fans and spreads the long tail, showing the black and white edges. Song lark- or thrush-like, disjointed, now loud now soft, with a sad tone; given from a treetop, pole or wire.
- Similar species —
  - Desert Wheatear: Sandy-grey above with black chin, throat, lores and ear-coverts; tail black with only the basal third white — not rufous with black-and-white edges; 14–16 cm.
  - Finsch's Wheatear: Black and white: white crown, central back and belly against black face, throat and wings; rump and tail white with a black inverted T; 14 cm.
  - Mourning Wheatear: Black head, neck and back against white crown, nape and underparts; white tail with a black inverted T; white flash in the black wing in flight; 14–16 cm.
  - Northern Wheatear: White rump and tail with a black inverted T, never a rufous fanned tail; grey (male) or pale brown above, wings and face mask black; 14–16.5 cm.

## Rustic Bunting
*Emberiza rustica* · `rusbun`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, 16–21 g. Reed-bunting-sized; crown feathers form a small crest, unique among the buntings; bill relatively long.
- Plumage — Male summer: black head, white supercilium and throat; rufous breast band and nape, rufous-spotted flanks, rufous rump; upperparts brown streaked dark, belly white. Female: crown and cheeks brown-speckled, supercilium dirty white, less rufous on the nape. Autumn: sexes alike, black and rufous hidden under buff-brown fringes. All plumages: white, black-framed spot at the rear of the ear-coverts.
- Bare parts — Legs pink; lower mandible pink.
- In the field — Breeds in wet coniferous woodland; feeds on seeds, insects when raising young. Song pleasant and fast, a jumble of high notes ending in a churr; call a thrush-like zit.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: Larger (16–19 cm) and lark-like; streaked grey-brown above, whitish below with a streaked gorget; no supercilium, black head or rufous; bill yellowish, eye dark.
  - Reed Bunting: Male's throat black, not white, with a white neck collar and no rufous breast band; supercilium fainter and no white black-framed cheek spot.
  - Yellowhammer: Larger (15.5–17 cm); head and underparts bright yellow, rump rufous, outer tail feathers white; never black-headed with a white supercilium.
  - Cirl Bunting: Male yellow-headed with black crown, eyestripe and throat and a greenish breast band; female grey-brown-rumped with chestnut shoulders; song a rattling trill.

## Rüppell's Warbler
*Curruca ruppeli* · `ruewar1`

- Jizz — 12.5–13.5 cm. Typical Curruca, about the length of Sardinian Warbler but slimmer; bill fine and pointed, culmen slightly decurved.
- Plumage — Male: forehead, head and throat black, split by a clean white malar streak; back grey, rump paler grey, underparts pale grey with a paler wash down the centre. Wing feathers black with broad pale fringes, secondaries and tertials white-edged; tail black, outer feathers white-edged. Female: head lead-grey, forehead black-speckled, throat pale and dark-spotted, back grey with a brown tinge.
- Bare parts — Male: broad, bright red eye-ring, iris reddish-brown; legs ochre-brown; lower mandible pale beige. Female: eye-ring narrow and dark red within a thin white orbital ring.
- In the field — Call like Sardinian Warbler's, but the alarm a rattling, House Sparrow-like series. Song also like Sardinian Warbler's but lower-pitched and bouncing, a slower, deeper rattle.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male grey-headed with a white throat, never black-hooded with a white malar streak.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Sexes almost identical: grey above, whitish below, grey head with a darker bandit mask and white throat; no black hood, no red eyes or red eye-ring.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's throat is white below the black head, not black with a white malar streak; Rüppell's is slimmer, with white-edged secondaries and tertials and a slower, deeper song.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male grey-backed and grey-headed with brick-red underparts and white malar streaks — never black-hooded; female brown above, whitish below with a pink flush.

## Sabine's Gull
*Xema sabini* · `sabgul`

- Jizz — 27–33 cm, 135–225 g. Small gull; wings long, thin and pointed, span 81–87 cm, proportionally very large against a small body; small head, rather slight bill; tail forked.
- Plumage — Striking tricoloured wing: pale grey back and coverts, four black outer primaries, white inner primaries and secondaries, forming a broad white triangle. Breeding adult hood slate-grey, cut off from the white neck by a narrow black collar; winter head white with dark grey nape. Juvenile: grey replaced by brown, black terminal tail band, grey-brown head running down the breast-sides.
- Bare parts — Bill black with a yellow tip, about 2.5 cm long; legs grey.
- In the field — Flight tern-like, the body lifted slightly at each wingbeat. Call very high-pitched and squeaking, a screeching tern-like cry.
- Similar species —
  - Black-legged Kittiwake: Larger, 37–41 cm, yellow bill, black legs; grey wings tipped solid black, no white triangle. Immatures show a dark covert bar and a less deeply forked tail.
  - Ross's Gull: Wedge-shaped tail, not forked; upperparts uniform pale grey without the black-and-white wing triangle; summer adult pink-flushed with a neat black neck ring; legs red.
  - Common Tern: Tern: black cap only, deeply forked tail with a 6–9 cm fork, pale grey upperwing without a white triangle; red-and-black or all-black bill, orange-red legs.
  - Little Gull: Smaller, 24–28 cm; adult has white primaries above and very dark grey underwing, no black-and-white triangle; juvenile shows a dark W across the upperwing.

## Sanderling
*Calidris alba* · `sander`

- Jizz — 18–21 cm, 40–100 g; wingspan 40–45 cm. Small plump sandpiper, about Dunlin-sized but with a shorter, rather thick bill; three toes only, the hind toe lacking.
- Plumage — Winter: very pale — light grey above and on the crown, face and underparts white, a dark shoulder patch and dark primaries. Breeding: face, throat and neck brick-red, upperparts brownish mottled, increasingly rufous; belly white. Spring birds greyer, more speckled on head and neck. Juvenile: spangled black and white, far more contrasted than the adult.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black.
- In the field — Runs after the retreating waves along the tideline of flat sandy beaches, picking crustaceans and worms. In flight silver-grey with a broad white wingbar and pale grey inner wing; central tail feathers black. Call a snappy plitt.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Larger, 23–26 cm; winter plumage uniformly pale grey with no dark shoulder patch; breeding birds cinnamon on face, throat and breast; small head, slightly tapering bill.
  - Dunlin: Breeding birds show a black belly; longer, slightly decurved bill; narrower white wingbar and grey-brown, not pale grey, inner wing; no dark shoulder patch in winter.
  - Ruff: Pot-bellied, small-headed and long-necked, with long legs that are green, pink or orange, never black; deeper slower wingbeats, faint wingbar, white ovals at the tail sides.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Dark above with a purplish gloss and grey-smeared breast; short yellow legs and a dark bill with a yellow base — Sanderling's bill and legs are all black.

## Sandwich Tern
*Thalasseus sandvicensis* · `santer1`

- Jizz — 36–43 cm, wingspan about 95–110 cm, 200–300 g. Medium-large tern looking very pale in flight; bill thin and sharp, legs short, tail short and forked.
- Plumage — Upperwing pale grey, underparts and neck white; primaries darken through the summer. Breeding: crown wholly black with a shaggy black nape crest. Winter: forehead and crown extensively white, crest worn. Sexes alike. Juvenile: back and wings scaly, tail dark-tipped, bill all-blackish, sometimes yellowish at the base only.
- Bare parts — Bill long, thin and black with a pale yellow tip; legs short and black.
- In the field — Voice a grating, creaking kärrik. Black forehead lost in the post-breeding moult of July–August and regained in February–March.
- Similar species —
  - Black-legged Kittiwake: A gull, 37–42 cm on 91–105 cm wings: white head and body, grey back, wings tipped solid black, plain yellow bill, black legs; no black cap or crest.
  - Slender-billed Gull: A gull, 37–40 cm: pale grey body, white head and breast, black primary tips, long tapering dark red bill, dark red legs, yellow iris.
  - Black-headed Gull: A gull, 34–39 cm: chocolate-brown summer hood, white leading edge to the outer wing and dark undersides to the inner primaries.
  - Common Gull: A gull, 40–46 cm: grey above, white below, black wingtips with large white mirrors, yellow legs and an unmarked greenish-yellow bill.

## Sardinian Warbler
*Curruca melanocephala* · `sarwar1`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm, about the size of a lesser whitethroat. Compact, rather large-headed warbler; tail dark, outer feathers white-tipped.
- Plumage — Male: head black, merging diffusely into the dark grey nape and back; throat white; breast and belly paler grey to whitish; undertail-coverts white; a reddish hue on upper- or underparts varies from absent to pronounced. Female: head grey, back brown, flanks grey-brown, underparts buff to pale brown.
- Bare parts — Eye red; orbital ring bright red in the male, duller red in the female.
- In the field — Call a fast rattle; song fast, chattering and rattling with sudden whistled notes and that call woven in — a characteristic sound of the Mediterranean where it breeds.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above, buff below, chestnut fringes to the secondaries; male's head grey, never black; song fast and scratchy with a scolding tone, call wed-wed.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Sexes alike: grey back, whitish underparts, grey head with a darker bandit mask through the eye, white throat; no red orbital ring; song a fast rattle of tet notes.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male's head grey like its back, not black, with brick-red underparts and a white malar streak; female brown above, whitish below with a pink flush.
  - Dartford Warbler: Long-tailed for a warbler, 13 cm; male grey above with reddish underparts and a white-spotted red throat; muted overall tones, dull greyish sides. Red eye shared.

## Savi's Warbler
*Locustella luscinioides* · `savwar1`

- Jizz — About 14 cm, 12–24 g. Medium-sized Locustella; tail broad, rounded and distinctly graduated.
- Plumage — Uniform dark reddish-brown above, sometimes faintly greenish, largely unpatterned; indistinct short buff eye-stripe, dark lores, pale brown ear-coverts. Chin, throat and belly whitish-buff, remaining underparts sandy brown, undertail the same colour as the belly. Both surfaces paler when breeding. Juvenile: yellower below.
- Bare parts — Bill slender, brown; iris brown; legs brown.
- In the field — Not shy, but hard to pick out in reeds unless singing. Sings from high on a reed head, bill open and throat vibrating, both sexes; song a trill often preceded by low ticks that merge into it, at dawn.
- Similar species —
  - Common Grasshopper Warbler: Streaked: pale olive-brown above with dark feather centres, dark spots on breast and flanks, against plain reddish-brown here; smaller, 12.5–13.5 cm; song higher and longer.
  - River Warbler: Grey-brown, not reddish, above, with a streaked breast and dark undertail-coverts broadly tipped white; this bird's undertail matches its belly.
  - Lanceolated Warbler: Smaller, 11.5–12.5 cm, and streaked — spear-shaped breast streaks give it its name — where this bird is plain.

## Scopoli's Shearwater
*Calonectris diomedea* · `scoshe1`

- Jizz — 45–52 cm, wingspan 100–125 cm. Large shearwater, about lesser black-backed gull size; long wings held angled — arm forward, hand swept back — carpal well marked, wing held slightly bowed; head large and round.
- Plumage — Upperparts brownish-grey, feathers pale-fringed, hand darker; head pale grey without contrast beyond a dark eye-patch; upper tail-coverts whitish-tipped, a variable pale band before the dark tail. Underparts white, breast-sides grey; underwing and undertail framed by clear dark edges, with a pale bar from elbow to carpal; more white on the outer primaries than Cory's, especially P10.
- Bare parts — Bill proportionally large, pale yellow with a dark band near the tip, visible at long range; legs and feet pale flesh.
- In the field — Long-range field mark: the big pale yellow bill, often visible far off.
- Similar species —
  - Cory's Shearwater: Larger, 45–56 cm, with a heavier, yellower bill and darker head; less white on the underside of the hand — overlap occurs and some birds are not safely told.
  - Northern Fulmar: Grey and white, bull-necked, with a thick pale yellow bill and bluish legs; stiff-winged flight quite unlike a gull's; pale inner primaries above.
  - Great Shearwater: Black cap, black bill, brown belly patch, dark shoulder markings and a crisp white horseshoe at the tail base; hand proportionally smaller. Stiff flight.
  - Sooty Shearwater: All dark chocolate-brown, looking black at distance, with a silvery strip down the centre of the underwing; no white underparts, no pale bill.

## Sedge Warbler
*Acrocephalus schoenobaenus* · `sedwar1`

- Jizz — 11.5–13 cm, wingspan 17–21 cm, 10–15 g. Medium-sized Acrocephalus; forehead flattened, bill strong and pointed.
- Plumage — Back and wings streaked brown; crown streaked black with dark side bands and a dark brown central band; prominent whitish to buff-yellow supercilium; rump warm brown and unstreaked, contrasting with the duller wings; throat whitish, underparts pale and yellowish. Sexes identical. Juvenile: dark spots on the breast and an apparent pale central crown stripe against darker edges.
- Bare parts — Legs greyish.
- In the field — Song varied, rushed and chattering — jirra-jirra-jirra broken by sweeter whistles and mimicry, the phrases in random order and never twice the same; calls tscheck and schrrr.
- Similar species —
  - Marsh Warbler: Told by song rather than plumage: the only member of the genus that mimics other birds heavily, and it avoids the pure reed stands.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Back plain unstreaked brown and underparts buff, with no streaked crown; 13 cm, wingspan 17–21 cm; song a slow chattering jit-jit-jit.
  - Blyth's Reed Warbler: Plain olive-brown above with no streaking and no warm tones; short whitish supercilium only in front of the eye; long thick bill, short rounded wings.
  - Great Reed Warbler: Much larger, 16–21 cm and 22–38 g, thrush-sized; upperparts unstreaked brown with a strong supercilium; song very loud and far-carrying.

## Semicollared Flycatcher
*Ficedula semitorquata* · `semfly1`

- Jizz — 12–13.5 cm, wingspan 23–24 cm. Small black-and-white flycatcher, intermediate in size and pattern between collared and pied; bill black, broad-based but pointed.
- Plumage — Breeding male: black above, white below; large white forehead patch; white half-collar reaching further back than pied's; large white wing patch and white primary bases; white-tipped median coverts, unique in both sexes; most white of the three at the tail base; rump often pale grey. Female, non-breeding male and juvenile: black replaced by pale brown, hard to separate from collared.
- Bare parts — Bill black.
- In the field — Takes insects in flight, only rarely gleaning caterpillars in foliage as pied does. Call a short straight tyyp, lower-pitched than collared's. Breeds in montane oak and hornbeam woods, to 2,000 m.
- Similar species —
  - European Pied Flycatcher: Forehead patch small, no white half-collar and no white bar on the median coverts; less white at the tail base; gleans caterpillars in the foliage.
  - Collared Flycatcher: Full white collar right round the neck, tail usually all black, and no white bar on the median coverts; call higher-pitched.
  - Red-breasted Flycatcher: Smaller, 11–12 cm; brown above with a grey head and orange throat, bold white eye-ring, black tail with white bases to the outer feathers.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: Large white wing and forehead patches too — the sources separate them only by range, this being the north-west African breeder.

## Short-eared Owl
*Asio flammeus* · `sheowl`

- Jizz — 33–43 cm, wingspan 85–110 cm, 206–475 g; female slightly larger. Big head on a short neck, large eyes, broad wings — very long, narrow, fairly pointed. Bill short, strong and hooked.
- Plumage — Mottled tawny to brown on a straw ground, coarsely flecked red-brown with white teardrops on back and scapulars; wings and tail barred. Upper breast heavily streaked, contrasting with a very pale, almost unstreaked belly. Underwing white with a black comma at the outer coverts and black tips; broad white trailing edge above. Ear-tufts minimal, seldom visible.
- Bare parts — Bill short, strong and black; iris yellow to yellow-orange in a black ring, set in a large whitish facial disc.
- In the field — Flight floppy and irregular-beaten, moth- or bat-like, light and almost slow-motion, often gliding on stiff wings. Male song a rapid deep po-po-po of 6–20 notes carrying a kilometre; also a scratchy bark, waowk waowk.
- Similar species —
  - Long-eared Owl: At rest the erectile ear-tufts settle it; iris often orange rather than yellow, and the black round the eye vertical, not horizontal. Darker, blunter-winged, without black wing-tips.
  - Tawny Owl: 37–46 cm, span 81–96 cm, 330–800 g; robust, large rounded head with no ear-tufts, plain facial disc and dark brown — not yellow — eyes; underparts whitish, brown-streaked.
  - Northern Hawk Owl: 35–43 cm, span 69–82 cm; dark brown with off-white spotting above, black V on the hind-neck, underparts brown-barred rather than streaked, and a long banded tail.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Vastly bigger — wingspan 131–188 cm against 85–110 — and among the largest owls in the world. Size settles it as soon as it registers.

## Short-toed Snake-Eagle
*Circaetus gallicus* · `shteag1`

- Jizz — 59–70 cm, wingspan 162–195 cm, 1.2–2.3 kg. Large, long broad-winged raptor; head looks very big and owl-like when perched, eyes large and forward-facing.
- Plumage — Underside predominantly white, often almost unmarked; chin, throat and upper breast pale earthy brown; underwing lightly barred; tail with three or four bars. Upperparts grey-brown, the pale white-flecked upperwing-coverts contrasting with dark brown secondaries and near-black primaries.
- Bare parts — Iris bright yellow.
- In the field — Spends more time on the wing than its relatives; soars on flattish wings over slopes and hilltops on updraughts, hunting from up to 500 m; hovers frequently like a kestrel. Mostly silent.
- Similar species —
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Sources give no plumage characters for it, only size, and that overlaps (55–74 cm against 59–70 cm) — no separation available here.
  - Red Kite: Rufous body and coverts, long forked tail twisting as it turns, wings held in a dihedral; white primary patches against black tips; no white underside.
  - Greater Spotted Eagle: Uniformly black-brown with a yellow bill, not white below; broad long wings and short rounded tail give an almost vulture-like silhouette; nape shaggy.
  - Lesser Spotted Eagle: Dark overall with pale brown head and wing-coverts, small head and bill; white patch on the upperwing and a white V on the rump; dog-like yipping call.

## Short-toed Treecreeper
*Certhia brachydactyla* · `shttre1`

- Jizz — 12–12.5 cm, 7.5–11 g. Small streaked brown creeper; long decurved pointed bill to 12 mm, long stiff tail feathers used as a prop, and a short hind claw.
- Plumage — Upperparts dull grey-brown, bark-coloured, intricately patterned black, buff and white, rump rufous; supercilium weak and off-white; underparts dingy white, contrasting with a clean white throat and browner on the rear flanks and vent. Sexes alike. Juvenile whitish below, sometimes with a buff belly.
- In the field — Climbs trunks in small upward hops, propped on the tail, often on oak, ash and elm. Call a shrill repeated tyt...tyt tyt-tyt; song short, jerky, even-pitched, louder and stronger than Eurasian Treecreeper's.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Treecreeper: Whiter below with buff-tinged belly, flanks and vent, warmer and more spotted above, whiter supercilium, slightly shorter bill; poorly marked birds may be inseparable by sight — use voice.

## Siberian Jay
*Perisoreus infaustus* · `sibjay1`

- Jizz — 25–31 cm, 75–95 g, wingspan 40–46 cm. Smallest western Palearctic corvid; very loose downy plumage, bill small and rather short.
- Plumage — Greyish-brown; head and crown dark brown with paler forehead, chin and throat grey, breast buff; rump, tail sides and undertail rusty-red, central tail feathers ash-grey; wing bend rufous, showing best in flight; rufous streaking on the outer feathers. Sexes alike, female slightly smaller. Juvenile: browner mantle, plainer overall.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs black; eye black; nostril feathering forms a small buff tuft at the forehead.
- In the field — Flight smooth and silent, gliding tree to tree; cumbersome over open ground, forest-adapted. Clambers among branches like a big tit. Mostly silent; buzzard-like screech, mewing geä, upslurred kui; a good mimic.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Jay: Eurasian jay is bigger, 32–37 cm, pinkish-brown with white throat and black moustache, black-streaked whitish crown, white rump and a barred blue wing patch.
  - Northern Nutcracker: Nutcracker is chocolate-brown heavily spotted white, with white vent and white tail corners and broad wings, 32–38 cm; no rusty rump or tail sides.
  - Eurasian Jackdaw: Jackdaw is glossy black with grey nape and neck, 34–39 cm and 174–300 g — much bigger and darker, with no rufous anywhere.
  - Yellow-billed Chough: Alpine chough is glossy black with a short yellow bill and red legs, 34–39 cm; nothing brown or rusty.

## Siberian Stonechat
*Saxicola maurus* · `y00427`

- Jizz — 11.5–13 cm. Small, stocky chat; primaries distinctly longer than European Stonechat's — a long-distance migrant's wing.
- Plumage — Male: head and upperparts black, broad white half-collar, large white scapular patch, white unstreaked rump, orange restricted to throat and breast. Female: upperparts and head pale brown, streaked, white neck patches but no full collar, rump pale, unstreaked pinkish-buff, throat whitish. Winter male mottled and intermediate, with a whinchat-like supercilium.
- In the field — Male's call a click, like two pebbles knocked together; song high and twittering, recalling Dunnock.
- Similar species —
  - European Stonechat: Rump small, buff and black-streaked, not a clean unstreaked pale panel; more orange across the breast, a smaller neck patch, and noticeably shorter wings.
  - Whinchat: Strong white supercilium and malar stripe ringing a blackish mask, never a black hood with a full white collar; tail blackish with white bases to the outer feathers.
  - Atlas Flycatcher: Black-and-white flycatcher with a large white forehead patch and big white wing patches; no orange below, no white collar.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: White or buff crown and mantle with a black mask, and a largely white tail; larger, 13.5–15.5 cm, and no orange breast or white collar.

## Slender-billed Gull
*Chroicocephalus genei* · `slbgul1`

- Jizz — 37–40 cm, wingspan 90–102 cm. Slightly larger than Black-headed Gull; head and long dark red bill taper into one elongated wedge, and the neck looks long.
- Plumage — Adult: body pale grey, head and breast white — no dark hood in any season; primaries black-tipped; in summer the breast is faintly pink, the neck, underbody and tail flushed pink. First-year: upperparts grey, underparts white, grey mottling on crown and nape, black terminal tail band and dark areas in the wing. Two years to maturity.
- Bare parts — Bill dark red, thin and fine; legs dark red; iris yellow. Juvenile bill yellow-orange, legs yellow.
- In the field — Swims with the neck stretched long and forward and the bill angled slightly down — the clearest separation from Black-headed Gull. Calls recall that species but deeper, harder and drier, krerr; nasal grating notes at colonies.
- Similar species —
  - Black-headed Gull: Chocolate-brown hood in summer and a white leading edge to the outer wing; rounded head and short bill, not the long tapering wedge; smaller, 34–39 cm.
  - Black-legged Kittiwake: Grey wings tipped solid black, black legs and a yellow bill — never a red bill and legs; 37–42 cm, wingspan 91–105 cm.
  - Sandwich Tern: A tern: black cap, black bill with a yellow tip, short black legs, very pale in flight; 36–43 cm.
  - Common Gull: Larger, 40–46 cm, wingspan 100–130 cm; yellow legs and a short tapered greenish-yellow bill; black wingtips with large white mirrors; head streaked grey in winter.

## Smew
*Mergellus albellus* · `smew`

- Jizz — 38–44 cm, wingspan 56–69 cm, 450–750 g. Small compact sawbill, built more like a goldeneye; short bill with hooked tip and serrated edges; erectile crest.
- Plumage — Male unmistakable, 'cracked ice' or 'panda' white with fine black lines and a black patch between bill and eye; back partly black; looks very black-and-white in flight, with oval white wing-patches. Female and immature grey with chestnut forehead and crown, white chin and cheeks, black lores — 'redhead' smew. Eclipse male female-like but keeps more white in the wing.
- Bare parts — Bill short and grey; males are bigger-billed than females.
- In the field — Dives for fish, gripped by the hooked serrated bill. Rather silent: male's display a muffled frog-like creak accelerating into a hiccup, female a hoarse krrr.
- Similar species —
  - Ring-necked Duck: Angular glossy black head, black back, white breast, yellow eyes, and two white rings round the grey bill; female grey-brown throughout.
  - Common Goldeneye: Larger, 40–50 cm; male dark green-glossed head with one round white patch below the eye; female brown-headed on a grey body.
  - Surf Scoter: Male velvety black with white forehead and nape patches and a swollen orange-looking bill; female uniform brown, slightly darker above.
  - Common Pochard: Plump diving duck, sloping forehead and long bill; male chestnut-headed with black breast and pale vermiculated grey body.

## Snow Bunting
*Plectrophenax nivalis* · `snobun`

- Jizz — 15–18 cm, 26–40 g; wingspan 32–38 cm. Plump and long-winged, large for a bunting; bill rather short; tarsi feathered, unlike other passerines.
- Plumage — Contrasty black, white, brown and grey. Breeding male unmistakable: white head, neck, rump and underparts with black back and wing-tips; most secondaries, primary bases, coverts and outer tail feathers white. Female in breeding dress has grey-brown crown, nape and ear-coverts, brown wash on back and breast. In winter both show broad orange-brown fringes on the back.
- Bare parts — Bill black in summer males, otherwise yellow with a black tip; legs black.
- In the field — Ground-dwelling: walks and runs, hops only if it must. In flight the large white wing panels against dark primaries are the mark. No passerine but the Raven winters as far north.
- Similar species —
  - Lapland Longspur: Minimal white in the wing; summer male black-headed and -throated with white eyestripe and chestnut nape. Calls alike, but Snow Bunting's is liquid and rippling, Lapland's drier and rattling.

## Snow Goose
*Anser caerulescens* · `snogoo`

- Jizz — 60–75 cm, wingspan 135–165 cm, 2050–4000 g. Medium-sized goose; sexes very alike.
- Plumage — Two morphs. White morph: all white but for black primaries and grey primary coverts. Blue morph: head, upper neck and tail tip white, the rest bluish grey-brown; immature blue drab slate-grey with little or no white on head, neck or belly. Intermediates occur; juveniles generally greyish. Head often stained rusty brown by minerals where they feed.
- Bare parts — Bill pink to orange-pink with black tomia, giving a black grin patch; feet and legs rose-red; all duller in immatures.
- In the field — Very vocal, often heard from more than a mile away; a soft rising cackling go go go, koik or gä-gä-gä, flight call a short rough krä, warning notes lower, angk-ak-ak-ak.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Mid-grey-brown body with a darker brown head and neck, white rump and vent; short bill black at base and tip, pink in the middle; no white plumage; 60–75 cm.
  - Graylag Goose: Grey-brown and bulkier, 74–91 cm; rotund body, thick long neck and large head and bill; orange or pink bill with a pale nail, pink legs.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Grey-brown with dark upperwing-coverts narrowly fringed white; bill black with an orange band, legs bright orange; 65–90 cm, wingspan 140–174 cm; voice a loud honking.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Greyish brown with a white facial blaze and black blotching on the breast and belly; orange legs and a pinkish bill; 64–81 cm.

## Snowy Owl
*Bubo scandiacus* · `snoowl1`

- Jizz — 53–65 cm, 1.6–2.5 kg; males average 58 cm, females 63 cm. Big, bulky owl, near eagle-owl size; head round and relatively small, facial disc shallow; ear-tufts vestigial, 20–25 mm, rarely raised; feet and toes densely feathered.
- Plumage — Mostly white — purer than a polar bear or Arctic fox. Adult male nearly plain white, at most a few dark spots on head, ear-tufts and some primary and secondary tips. Female far more spotted, barred dark brown on crown and underparts, flight and tail feathers faintly barred. Young male as an adult female; young female densely dark-barred throughout.
- Bare parts — Eyes large and bright golden-yellow; bill black, largely hidden under dense fine white bristles.
- In the field — On the ground can pass for a pale rock or a lump of snow; usually looks earless.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the sources: separated on size — the eagle-owl is the larger, wingspan 131–188 cm, and this species only nearly reaches its bulk.
  - Great Gray Owl: Grey, not white: grey face, yellow eyes in dark circles, huge facial disc, white bow-tie under the bill, long tapering tail; 60–84 cm.
  - Short-eared Owl: Much smaller, 33–43 cm, wingspan 85–110 cm; mottled tawny to brown with a heavily streaked upper breast and barred wings and tail; flight floppy, moth-like.
  - Boreal Owl: Far smaller, 21–26 cm; brown above with pearly-white spots, whitish below with rust streaks, crown olive-brown with small white droplets.

## Sociable Lapwing
*Vanellus gregarius* · `soclap1`

- Jizz — 27–30 cm. Medium-sized lapwing, shaped like Northern Lapwing but slightly smaller and longer-legged; legs longish and black, bill short and black.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: black crown and lores framed above and below by a strong white supercilium; upper neck warm sandy; mantle, scapulars and breast grey-brown with a pinkish-beige tone; belly black grading to dark chestnut at the vent; undertail-coverts white. Winter: head pattern duller, upperparts browner, belly white. Juvenile: scaly above, breast diffusely streaked, white supercilium still bold.
- Bare parts — Bill short and black; legs long and black, dark grey in some birds.
- In the field — Almost unmistakable in flight: tricolour upperwing of black primaries, white secondaries and grey-brown coverts, and a white tail with a broad black terminal band. Call a harsh kereck, or a hoarse chattering kretj-etj-etj.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Lapwing: Long crest and broad rounded wings; black and white with a green-glossed back, black throat and breast; larger, 28–33 cm, and the shortest-legged lapwing.
  - Spur-winged Lapwing: Black crown, chest, foreneck stripe and tail against a white face, neck and belly; light brown wings and back; no white supercilium; loud did-he-do-it call.
  - Black-bellied Plover: A plover: spangled black and white above in summer with black face, breast and belly and a white rump; plain grey in winter; same length, 27–30 cm.
  - Common Ringed Plover: Far smaller, 17–19.5 cm; brown cap, white forehead, black mask and a single black breast-band; orange legs and a short orange and black bill.

## Sombre Tit
*Poecile lugubris* · `somtit3`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm. Great Tit in size and proportions but with a stout, strong bill; tail proportionately longer than Marsh Tit's.
- Plumage — Cap and nape sooty brownish-black in the male, more chocolate-brown in the female, running further down the sides of the head than in other tits; the large black bib squeezes the off-white cheek to a narrow wedge. Rest of upperparts grey-brown; underparts dull whitish with greyish-brown flanks. Juvenile duller and browner.
- In the field — Head often looks peaked when the bird stands upright, unlike Willow Tit.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Tit: Smaller, 11.5 cm, with thin bill; the dull black cap and small bib leave the whole side of the face white, and the head is not peaked.
  - Gray-headed Chickadee: Dark brown head with white cheeks, cinnamon-brown mantle and rusty-washed whitish underparts; 12.5–14 cm — go on plumage colour, not structure.
  - Marsh Tit: Glossy blue-black cap with a small bib and full white cheeks, greyish-brown above, off-white below; smaller at 11.5–12 cm and proportionately shorter-tailed.
  - Great Tit: Same size, 12.5–14 cm, but lemon-yellow below with a broad black mid-line stripe, green mantle and blue-grey wing with a white wingbar.

## Song Thrush
*Turdus philomelos* · `sonthr1`

- Jizz — 20–23.5 cm, 50–107 g; wingspan about 33–36 cm. Smaller than a blackbird and daintier, with a shorter tail.
- Plumage — Sexes alike. Plain brown above, colder in tone eastwards; faint pale supercilium, clearest over the forehead. Below cream to yellow-buff, paler on the belly, neatly spotted blackish — narrow streaks on chin and throat massing into a malar stripe, spots larger and rounder towards the belly. Underwing grey with warm rusty-yellow coverts. Juvenile: buff-orange streaks on back and wing-coverts.
- Bare parts — Bill yellowish; legs and feet pink.
- In the field — Call a short sharp 'tsip', on migration a thin high 'seep' like a redwing's but shorter; alarm an explosive rattling 'tix-itix-itix'. Song from an exposed perch: loud musical phrases each repeated two to four times, with mimicry.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Blackbird: Larger, 23.5–29 cm, longer-tailed; male all glossy black with yellow eye-ring and orange-yellow bill, female sooty-brown and only weakly mottled, never neatly spotted below.
  - Redwing: Strong creamy-white supercilium, red flanks and red underwing in flight; Song Thrush's underwing is warm rusty-yellow and its supercilium faint. Similar size.
  - Fieldfare: Slate-grey head, nape and rump against a chestnut-brown back, blackish tail, white underwing-coverts; larger, 22–27 cm; harsh 'tsak tsak' in flight.
  - Mistle Thrush: Much larger, 26–29 cm; pale grey-brown above, round black spots denser on the lower chest, white tail corners and white underwing-coverts; stocky upright stance.
