## Sooty Shearwater
*Ardenna grisea* · `sooshe`

- Jizz — 40–51 cm, wingspan 94–110 cm, 650–978 g. Large shearwater with long stiff straight wings; in active flight suggests a very small albatross.
- Plumage — All dark: looks wholly black in poor light; in good light dark chocolate-brown to dark grey, with a silvery strip along the centre of the underwing.
- In the field — Shears from side to side on stiff wings with few beats, wingtips almost touching the water; in wind uses deep dynamic glides. Vocal at night at the colonies, loud coos and croaks; usually silent at sea.
- Similar species —
  - Great Shearwater: White underparts with a brown belly patch, dark shoulder markings, black cap and a white horseshoe at the tail base — never all dark.
  - Balearic Shearwater: Smaller and usually somewhat paler, 30–40 cm against 40–51 cm, and lacks the pale stripe down the centre of the underwing.
  - Northern Fulmar: Grey and white rather than all dark, with a thick pale yellow bill, bull neck and bluish legs; no silvery underwing stripe.
  - Scopoli's Shearwater: Brownish-grey above with white underparts and a pale yellow dark-tipped bill; larger, 45–52 cm, with pale flesh legs and feet.

## Spanish Eagle
*Aquila adalberti* · `spaeag1`

- Jizz — 72–85 cm, wingspan 177–220 cm, 2.5–4.8 kg. Large eagle, somewhat smaller than Golden Eagle and somewhat larger than Bonelli's; male wingspan about 190 cm, female about 210 cm.
- Plumage — Overall darker than either Golden or Eastern Imperial Eagle: a rich blackish-brown running from the throat right down to the belly. Nape and hindneck contrastingly pale beige. Broad white band on the shoulder and leading edge of the wing, even more pronounced than in Eastern Imperial; shoulders and bend of wing conspicuously white-marked.
- In the field — Resident, unlike the migratory Eastern Imperial Eagle. Lives chiefly on rabbits, but takes many other animals: gamebirds, rodents, hares, pigeons, crows and ducks.
- Similar species —
  - Golden Eagle: Paler overall, with no pale beige nape and no white shoulder band; bigger, 66–102 cm, wingspan 1.8–2.34 m, up to 7 kg against 72–85 cm and 2.5–4.8 kg.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: A medium-sized eagle with no white shoulder band; smaller, 55–74 cm and wingspan 142–175 cm against 72–85 cm and 177–220 cm.
  - Imperial Eagle: Nearly the same size, 68–90 cm and 176–220 cm; separate on the Spanish bird's darker blackish-brown body and far more pronounced white shoulder band. Ranges do not overlap.
  - Steppe Eagle: Bulky, mainly dark brown, very thick neck, small head with a long gape-line, longish rounded tail; no white shoulder band or pale nape; often stands on the ground.

## Spanish Sparrow
*Passer hispaniolensis* · `spaspa1`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, 22–36 g, wingspan 23–26 cm. Rather large, heavy sparrow; bill slightly long and stout.
- Plumage — Male: chestnut crown, whitish cheeks, narrow white supercilium; black bib breaking up into heavy black streaking over breast and flanks; back streaked. Female: grey-brown throughout and boldly marked, pale cream supercilium, broad cream streaks on the back, light streaking on the sides, faintly grey-streaked below.
- In the field — Colonial; an almost singing murmur carries from breeding colonies. Male's display call a strident disyllabic chweeng-chweeng, cheela-cheeli; a softer tschilp at roosts.
- Similar species —
  - House Sparrow: Male grey-crowned and grey-cheeked with a clean bib and unstreaked underparts; female effectively identical. Bill slightly shorter and less stout.
  - Eurasian Tree Sparrow: Smaller (12.5–14 cm); chestnut crown with a black ear-patch on a pure white cheek, two white wing-bars, unstreaked underparts, sexes alike.
  - Italian Sparrow: Male's head pattern the same, but upperparts bright chestnut and underparts plain pale grey, lacking the black streaking; bib confined to throat and chest.
  - Dead Sea Sparrow: Much smaller (12–13 cm); male grey-crowned and grey-cheeked with a small bib, yellow neck-sides, pale supercilium and unstreaked grey-white underparts.

## Spectacled Warbler
*Curruca conspicillata* · `spewar2`

- Jizz — 12–13 cm, 8–10 g. Very small, slim Curruca; head proportionally large, primary projection short, wings short, bill narrow.
- Plumage — Male: head grey with black lores and fore-cheek and black ear-coverts, throat white, eye-ring white; back grey-brown; breast dark brown-pink, darker than whitethroat's; wings uniformly rusty-brown. Female: head browner than female whitethroat, the rusty-brown on the wing broad. Immatures very like whitethroat and western subalpine warbler — hard in the field.
- Bare parts — Legs pale yellowish-pink.
- In the field — Movements nervous and restless. Call a light dry drr, readily run into series; song light, fast and twittering, opening with whistled notes.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Larger, 13–15 cm, and less compact, with a longer primary projection; chestnut only as fringes to the secondaries, not a solid rusty wing; no black lores or white eye-ring.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Grey back and whitish underparts with no rusty in the wing; dark bandit mask through the eye and no white eye-ring; sexes almost identical.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's whole head black, not grey, with red eyes, grey back and no rusty wing panel; female brown above with a grey head.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male brick-red below with white malar streaks; female pink-flushed below with a greyer head. Spectacled shows broader rusty wing, shorter wing and paler yellowish-pink legs.

## Spotless Starling
*Sturnus unicolor* · `sposta1`

- Jizz — 21–23 cm, 70–100 g. Marginally larger than Common Starling; throat feathers conspicuously long — twice a Common Starling's — forming a shaggy beard, obvious when the bird sings.
- Plumage — Dark, oily-looking black, faintly purple- or green-glossed in bright light; wholly unspotted in spring and summer, in winter only very small pale spots from pale feather tips. Females and first-summer males show pale spots on the undertail-coverts; winter and first-winter females are spotted on back, belly and undertail-coverts, crown usually unspotted. Juvenile dull brown, darker than a young Common Starling.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow in summer, blue-based in males and pink-based in females, blackish and duller in winter. Legs bright pink; juvenile bill black, legs brown.
- In the field — Walks rather than hops; strong direct flight, looking triangular-winged and short-tailed. Noisy and a good mimic; calls like a Common Starling's but louder.
- Similar species —
  - European Starling: Spangled white, boldly so in winter, where Spotless Starling is unspotted in summer and only finely flecked later; slightly smaller, 19–23 cm, throat feathers half as long.
  - Eurasian Blackbird: Longer-tailed and without plumage gloss; larger, 23.5–29 cm against 21–23 cm; male has an orange-yellow bill and yellow eye-ring.
  - Common Myna: Brown body with a black hooded head, bare yellow patch behind the eye, bright yellow bill and legs, white primary patch and white tail tip.
  - Rosy Starling: Pink body against glossy black head, wings and tail; pale orange legs and bill; males wispy-crested. Juvenile far paler with a short yellow bill.

## Spotted Crake
*Porzana porzana* · `spocra1`

- Jizz — 19–22.5 cm. Small, compact crake, slightly smaller than Water Rail; short straight bill, short tail, long toes.
- Plumage — Brown above, spangled with small white spots, back feathers and central coverts black; breast blue-grey; flanks dark-barred with bold white spots and pale yellow streaking; undertail plain buff to sandy. Juvenile: as adult but breast brown, not blue-grey. Chick: black down.
- Bare parts — Bill short and straight, yellow with a red base; legs green to green-yellow, toes long.
- In the field — Very secretive when breeding, heard far more than seen; a loud, rhythmically repeated whiplash hwuit, hwuit carrying kilometres. Probes mud and shallow water, also picks by sight; insects and aquatic animals. Easier on migration.
- Similar species —
  - Water Rail: Long red bill, not short and yellow; flanks barred black and white without white spots, undertail white, legs flesh-brown. Larger, 23–30 cm.
  - Baillon's Crake: Smaller, 15–18 cm; bill yellow or green with no red base; flanks and undertail barred black and white rather than white-spotted.
  - Eurasian Moorhen: Much larger, 30–38 cm; red frontal shield and red bill with a yellow tip, yellow legs, white undertail and a white flank line.
  - Corn Crake: Larger, 27–30 cm; chestnut wing coverts, flesh-coloured bill, pale grey legs; flanks barred chestnut and white, upperparts streaked but not white-spotted.

## Spotted Flycatcher
*Muscicapa striata* · `spofly1`

- Jizz — 13–15 cm, 14–20 g. Small, slim songbird with a rather large head, long wings, a long rear end and short legs; bill fairly long.
- Plumage — Dull grey-brown above, off-white below, essentially unpatterned; crown, throat and breast finely streaked dark, the streaking heavier on breast-sides and flanks. Wing and tail feathers thinly pale-fringed; in fresh plumage buff tips to median and greater coverts make a paler line on the wing. Sexes alike. Juvenile: ochre-buff spots above, scaly brown spots below.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish horn with a paler base to the lower mandible; legs black; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Vocal but very discreet: call a short sharp zri, easily confused with Chaffinch or Robin; alarm more distinctive, isst-te or isst-te-te. Song subdued, pressed thin scratchy notes with some trilling.
- Similar species —
  - Cyprus Wheatear: Nothing in the sources separates them on plumage; sizes overlap entirely, 13–15 cm against 14–15 cm. Sexes alike in both.
  - Common Redstart: Orange-red tail, frequently quivered, with orange-chestnut rump, flanks and underwing-coverts; male slate-grey above. Flycatcher is plain grey-brown with a streaked breast.
  - Red-flanked Bluetail: Blue tail and rump with orange-red flanks and white throat in both sexes; male dark blue above. Flicks the tail constantly; slimmer, 13–14 cm.
  - Western Black-eared Wheatear: Whitish crown, buff mantle, black mask through the ear-coverts, blackish wings, and white back, uppertail-coverts and tail — no streaking anywhere.

## Spotted Redshank
*Tringa erythropus* · `spored`

- Jizz — 29–33 cm. Large, slim wader on long dark-red legs; bill long, fine, the tip slightly decurved.
- Plumage — Breeding: sooty-black, small white spots above. Winter: grey above, whiter below, white supercilium sharply marked; wings plain, no wing-bars; white rump running up the back as a white wedge. Juvenile: grey-brown finely speckled white above, underparts pale and closely barred.
- Bare parts — Bill red on the lower mandible only; legs long, dark red.
- In the field — Wades belly-deep and swims when it gets deeper; flight powerful; call a two-syllable whistle tju-itt, alarm kyip-kyip-kyip; song a mournful grinding trrruuiie in display flight.
- Similar species —
  - Wood Sandpiper: Smaller and finer-billed, brown-backed with long yellowish legs; rump-patch small and weakly contrasting, never a long white wedge up the back.
  - Common Greenshank: Legs green, bill grey-based and looking upturned rather than fine with a decurved tip; call a rapid fluty teu-teu-teu.
  - Common Redshank: Shows broad white on the wings as well as up the back; bill shorter and red with a black tip; marbled brown, never sooty-black.
  - Green Sandpiper: Smaller (21–24 cm) and plump; bill and legs short and dark green; wings dark above and below against a brilliant white rump; three-note whistle.

## Spur-winged Lapwing
*Vanellus spinosus* · `spwlap1`

- Jizz — 25–28 cm. Medium-large lapwing; bill strong and blunt; long legs projecting beyond the tail in flight; elongated crest feathers form a black triangle over the nape; a sharp wrist spur, rarely seen.
- Plumage — Forehead, crown and crest black; nape white, running down the neck side below the eye to the bill base and in broad wedges onto the breast sides, split by a broad black chin-to-breast stripe. Breast and belly black, vent and undertail-coverts sharply white. Mantle to rump pale brown; coverts brown white-tipped or all white; tail black, white-based.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish; legs brownish-black; iris strong red to red-brown.
- In the field — Flight slow and deliberate, lapwing-like, legs trailing past the tail. Very noisy, the call a loud did-he-do-it.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Lapwing: Long wispy crest and a green-glossed back; face white without the black chin-to-breast stripe; slightly larger, 28–33 cm, and the shortest-legged lapwing.
  - Sociable Lapwing: Black crown and eye-stripe, but each bordered white above and below; underparts white, not black; white tail with a black terminal band; wings brown, white and grey.
  - Black-bellied Plover: A plover: spangled black and white above in summer, plain grey in winter; no crest and no black neck stripe; white rump, tail barred black; 25–30 cm.
  - Common Ringed Plover: Far smaller, 17–19.5 cm; grey-brown above with one black breast-band, white forehead, orange black-tipped bill and orange legs.

## Squacco Heron
*Ardeola ralloides* · `squher1`

- Jizz — 40–49 cm, wingspan 71–92 cm, 250–300 g. Small compact heron with a short thick neck usually drawn in and a dagger bill; when it stretches up it recalls a small bittern.
- Plumage — On the ground dull ochre-yellow-brown and inconspicuous; in flight strikingly white, wings and tail white. Breeding adult: back beige-brown with a faint purple sheen, neck and flanks unstreaked cream-yellow, head streaked, nape feathers elongated. Non-breeding: head, neck and flanks clearly streaked grey, nape plumes short, mantle dull ochre-brown. Juvenile darker and more heavily streaked.
- Bare parts — Breeding: bill green-blue with black tip, legs coral-red, bare eye skin bright green. Non-breeding: bill and legs yellow-green, bill dark-tipped, eye skin yellow.
- In the field — Call a harsh croaking kaah, like a female Mallard; from the colonies a hammering nasal kak, kak, kak or a thicker ah, ah, ah.
- Similar species —
  - Black-crowned Night Heron: Black crown and back with pale grey wings and white underparts, red eyes, short yellow legs, long white nape plumes; stockier with shorter bill and neck.
  - Western Cattle-Egret: Mainly white all over rather than buff-backed with white wings; yellow bill, greyish-yellow legs, orange-buff plumes when breeding; larger, 46–56 cm, wingspan 88–96 cm.
  - Great Egret: All white and far bigger — up to 1 m tall, 80–104 cm, wingspan 131–170 cm; yellow bill with black legs and feet.

## Steller's Eider
*Polysticta stelleri* · `steeid`

- Jizz — 42–48 cm, wingspan 68–77 cm, 650–900 g. Smallest eider; compact, with a large square flat-crowned head, angled nape, long thick straight-edged bill and a relatively long tail.
- Plumage — Male (November–June) near unmistakable: white head with black chin, black eye and eye-ring, pale green nape patch and a fainter one before the eye; black collar, back and stern; cinnamon-pink underparts with a black spot on the breast-side at the waterline; long white-striped purple-black tertials, white shoulders. Female dark to cinnamon brown, pale eye-ring, blue-purple speculum white-bordered both sides.
- Bare parts — Bill grey, long and squarish with a straight upper edge; legs and feet dark bluish grey.
- In the field — Light, fast flight, looking long- and narrow-winged and pot-bellied. Eclipse male female-like but keeps the all-white inner forewing, seen as a white flank on swimming birds. Juvenile paler, speculum greyish.
- Similar species —
  - Common Scoter: Drake wholly black with a yellow bill patch and bulbous bill; female brown-grey with pale cheeks; neither shows white head or shoulders.
  - Common Eider: Much larger, 50–71 cm, with a wedge-shaped bill running into the forehead; drake white-backed, black-bellied, green-naped.
  - Common Goldeneye: Peaked round head and small triangular bill; drake has a round white patch below the eye and a golden iris; female grey with brown head.
  - Common Merganser: Far larger, 58–72 cm, with a long red serrated bill and a dark green head on a white body.

## Steppe Eagle
*Aquila nipalensis* · `steeag1`

- Jizz — 62–74 cm, wingspan 165–190 cm. Large, bulky eagle; very thick neck, small head, long wings, longish rounded and wedge-shaped tail; legs heavily, almost dishevelledly feathered.
- Plumage — Uniform dark brown, adults with darker centres to the greater coverts and, mainly in the east, pale rufous to orange-yellow patches on nape and hindcrown; wingtips blackish, tail blackish and unbarred; often a pale beige band across the lower secondaries; throat pale.
- Bare parts — Massive gape-line running back level with the rear of the eye, longer than in any other Aquila; eyes deep-set; nostril oval.
- In the field — Perches upright in the open on isolated trees, posts, rocks and straw-piles; often on the ground, standing long, walking horizontally with wingtips just past the tail; fairly tame. Call a crow-like bark.
- Similar species —
  - Golden Eagle: Larger at 66–102 cm with broader wings spanning 1.8–2.34 m; sources give no plumage marks, so separate on size and wing shape.
  - Spanish Eagle: Little in the sources beyond size: 72–85 cm and 177–220 cm span against 62–74 cm and 165–190 cm.
  - Bonelli's Eagle: Smaller and shorter-winged: 55–74 cm and 142–175 cm span against 62–74 cm and 165–190 cm; no plumage marks given.
  - Imperial Eagle: Larger and longer-winged, 68–90 cm with a 176–220 cm span; sources give no plumage marks to work from.

## Stock Dove
*Columba oenas* · `stodov1`

- Jizz — 28–34 cm, wingspan 60–68 cm, 300–365 g. Compact short-tailed blue-grey pigeon, about the size of a feral pigeon and clearly smaller than Wood Pigeon.
- Plumage — Blue-toned grey with no white on rump or wing; two short blackish bars on tertials and greater coverts; broad dark grey band at the tail-tip; wing broadly black-edged, showing a dark trailing edge in flight, underwing dark grey. Breast washed pinkish-purple; neck-sides iridescent green to purple. Sexes alike; juvenile lacks the neck patch.
- Bare parts — Bill pink with a yellow tip; eyes dark.
- In the field — Call a short deep grunting 'ooo-uu-ooh', or 'hu ru', often repeated — quite unlike Wood Pigeon's modulated cooing.
- Similar species —
  - Common Wood-Pigeon: Much larger, 38–45 cm, with white on neck and wing and pale yellow eyes; Stock Dove shows no white and has a dark underwing.
  - Rock Pigeon: Similar size, 30–35 cm, but the underwing is white against Stock Dove's dark grey; feral birds vary greatly in plumage.
  - Eurasian Collared-Dove: Grey-buff to pinkish-grey with a black white-edged half-collar on the nape, white-tipped tail, red legs, black bill, red iris; wingspan only 47–55 cm.
  - Oriental Turtle-Dove: Neck patch black-and-white striped, not iridescent green-purple; wing feathers brown with dark centres, looking scaly; tail wedge-shaped; 30–35 cm.

## Surf Scoter
*Melanitta perspicillata* · `sursco`

- Jizz — 44–46 cm; wingspan 76–77 cm, male around 1050 g, female around 900 g. Medium sea duck; big wedge-shaped bill swollen at the base, head profile flattened.
- Plumage — Male velvety black with white patches on forehead and nape; bill orange at range, patterned white, red and yellow with a black spot near the base. Female browner and near-uniform, with indistinct pale patches on the cheek below the eye and often a whitish nape patch. Juvenile paler and browner, breast and belly whitish.
- Bare parts — Male's iris white; female's bill black with green or blue tones.
- In the field — Dives with half-open wings and without a head-bob. The only scoter with wholly dark wings in flight; flightless during the simultaneous wing moult.
- Similar species —
  - Common Scoter: Drake shows no white at all, only yellow around the nostrils, against the white forehead and nape here; female has more extensive pale cheeks.
  - Velvet Scoter: Larger, 51–56 cm, and shows a big white wing patch in flight; the male's white mark sits behind the eye, not on forehead and nape.
  - Common Goldeneye: Male white-bodied with a dark green-glossed head and a round white spot below the eye; female grey-bodied with a brown head. Never all-black.
  - Common Pochard: Male has a chestnut head, black breast and pale grey vermiculated body; bill dark grey with a broad blue-grey band across the middle.

## Swan Goose
*Anser cygnoides* · `swagoo1`

- Jizz — 81–94 cm, wingspan 160–185 cm, 2.8–3.5 kg. Large, very long-necked Anser, the longest of the genus; bill long and heavy with a straight culmen. Male larger, bill and neck proportionally longer.
- Plumage — Upperparts greyish-brown with thin pale fringes to the larger feathers; crown and hindneck maroon, sharply cut against the pale buff foreneck and lower head; thin white stripe round the bill base; underside pale buff, darker streaks on belly and flanks; upper- and undertail-coverts white; wings dark and unpatterned in flight. Juvenile: duller.
- Bare parts — Bill wholly black — unique in the genus; legs and feet orange; irides maroon.
- In the field — Call a far-carrying, drawn-out, hoarse trumpeting, heard most often from birds in flight; alarm a hard short note, repeated.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Smaller, 60–75 cm; short bill pink in the middle with black base and tip, feet pink, rump and vent white; no maroon hindneck.
  - Graylag Goose: Bulkier and shorter-necked, 74–91 cm; bill orange or pink, never all black, legs pink; no maroon cap and no split pale-and-dark neck.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Bill black with an orange band across the middle, legs and feet bright orange; upperwing-coverts dark brown with narrow white fringes; no maroon crown.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: Smaller, 64–81 cm; pinkish bill with a white facial blaze, orange legs, breast dappled dark brown to black; bill never all black.

## Syrian Woodpecker
*Dendrocopos syriacus* · `syrwoo1`

- Jizz — 23–25 cm. Medium pied woodpecker, very like Great Spotted; long bill; short stiff tail spread as a prop when climbing.
- Plumage — Male: black above with large white shoulder patches and a crimson nape spot; forehead white; face and neck sides white, no black bar from nape to malar, so the cheek looks broad; white-spotted flight feathers form three wingbars; underparts buffish white, flanks faintly streaked; vent and undertail-coverts pinkish-red. Female: no crimson nape. Juvenile: crown crimson, nape spot absent.
- Bare parts — Bill long, slate black; legs greenish grey; eye black; pale feathering at the nostrils.
- In the field — Call like Great Spotted but softer, gipp rather than kick. Drum a little longer, 0.8–1.2 seconds, fading in strength at the end; faster and shorter than White-backed Woodpecker's.
- Similar species —
  - Great Spotted Woodpecker: Tail barred white and a black line runs from the shoulder to the nape and bill, leaving less white on the head; shorter bill; scarlet only on lower belly.
  - White-backed Woodpecker: Larger, 24–28 cm, with white bars across the wings rather than spots and a white lower back; male's crown red, female's black.
  - Lesser Spotted Woodpecker: Much smaller, 14–16.5 cm; broad barring on wings and lower back, no white shoulder patches and no red on the belly; male's crown crimson.
  - Eurasian Three-toed Woodpecker: Black and white only — male's crown yellow, no red on either sex; back white barred black, flanks barred; no white shoulder patch.

## Taiga Bean-Goose
*Anser fabalis* · `taibeg1`

- Jizz — 65–90 cm, wingspan 140–174 cm, 1.7–4 kg. Second largest grey goose; dark and long-winged, head squarish, bill long with depth at the base less than half its length. Females smaller.
- Plumage — Grey-brown; head and neck darker brown than the body; upperparts dark brown with narrow white feather fringes; breast pale grey, belly whitish towards the tail; fine white lines on flanks, wing edges and tail-tip; upper wing-coverts dark brown, not pale grey.
- Bare parts — Bill black at base and tip with an orange mid-band, its width varying by race; nail dark; legs and feet orange; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Voice a loud honking.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Bill short, black with a bright pink mid-band, feet pink not orange; upper wing-coverts pale bluish-grey; smaller, 60–75 cm; white rump and vent, broad white tail-tip.
  - Graylag Goose: Bulkier and larger, 74–91 cm, big-headed with a heavy orange or pink bill lacking any black; legs pink; forewing pale grey; head and neck not darker than the body.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: White blaze round the bill-base and black blotching and barring on a light grey breast; bill pinkish, wing-coverts mouse-grey; smaller, 64–81 cm.
  - Tundra Bean-Goose: Smaller and more compact; head rounder and darker brown than the short, thick neck; bill short and deep-based, mostly blackish with little orange; honking higher-pitched.

## Tawny Owl
*Strix aluco* · `tawowl1`

- Jizz — 37–46 cm, wingspan 81–96 cm, 330–800 g. Robust, squat owl; large rounded head without ear tufts, broad wings. Female much larger, 5% longer and over 25% heavier.
- Plumage — Two morphs, rufous-brown or greyish-brown above, with intermediates; dark brown lengthwise blotches above, finely vermiculated between; underparts whitish streaked brown, vent white with brown arrow marks; large white spots on scapulars and greater coverts; tail barred brown and rufous, whitish-tipped. Facial disc plain, dark-rimmed, two whitish strokes above it. Female more rufous.
- Bare parts — Bill thick, strongly hooked, sulphur-yellow with a horn to pale grey base; iris blackish-brown, pupil blue-black; eyelids bare, pale red.
- In the field — Long glides on rounded wings, less undulating and with fewer wingbeats than other Eurasian owls, usually higher up; flight heavy and slow at take-off, and silent.
- Similar species —
  - Great Gray Owl: Far larger, 60–84 cm; grey face with the largest facial disc of any raptor, yellow eyes, white bow-tie under the bill, long tail tapering to a rounded end.
  - Ural Owl: Larger, 50–64 cm; pale greyish-brown overall, boldly dark-streaked below without crossbars, and an exceptionally long wedge-tipped tail.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, wingspan 131–188 cm against 81–96 cm.
  - Short-eared Owl: Similar length but slimmer, 34–43 cm; mottled tawny with a heavily streaked upper breast, barred wings and tail, yellow-orange eyes in black rings; floppy, moth-like flight.

## Tawny Pipit
*Anthus campestris* · `tawpip1`

- Jizz — 15.5–18 cm, wingspan 25–28 cm. Large, slim pipit, bigger than Tree Pipit; tail relatively long, legs long, bill slender and narrow; hind claw relatively short for a pipit.
- Plumage — Adult upperparts plain pale sandy grey-brown; median coverts contrastingly darker with broad pale tips, showing on a perched fresh bird as five dark squares; cream supercilium ending over the ear-coverts, blackish lores, white throat; underparts pale and unstreaked but for a few fine streaks at the breast-sides; outer tail feathers cream-tipped. Juvenile: heavily dark-streaked above, spotted on the breast.
- Bare parts — Bill grey-brown and slender, lower mandible pale at the base; legs pale brown to light pink.
- In the field — Walks upright and stilt-like; flight straight and direct. Call a sparrow-like tschlippp, dip, or a schip higher-pitched than Richard's; song a short loud disyllabic chir-ree chir-ree in gliding song-flight or from a bush-top.
- Similar species —
  - Meadow Pipit: Much smaller, 14.5–15 cm; brown above, buff below with darker barring throughout, thin bill, and a conspicuously long hind claw against Tawny's short one.
  - Rock Pipit: Smoky-olive above and buff below, heavily and diffusely streaked; bill, legs and iris dark brown to blackish, not pale; 15.5–17 cm.
  - Tree Pipit: Smaller and streaked: brown stripes above, black markings on white underparts, buff breast; heavier bill; parachuting song-flight down from a tree.
  - Red-throated Pipit: Smaller, 14–15 cm, and far more striped — cap, back, rump, flanks and chest heavily streaked, whitish mantle stripes; breeding adult brick-red on face and throat.

## Temminck's Stint
*Calidris temminckii* · `temsti`

- Jizz — 13.5–15 cm, wingspan 34–37 cm, 20–40 g. Very small wader, short-legged and long-winged.
- Plumage — Drab: upperparts and head plain brown, underparts white apart from a darker breast; outer tail feathers white. Breeding adult relieved by some brighter rufous and blackish mantle feathers. Winter: dull grey to greenish- or brownish-grey above and across the upper breast, white below, recalling a tiny Common Sandpiper.
- Bare parts — Legs dull yellow to brown.
- In the field — Male display-flight hovering over the breeding ground with an endless twittering tititititi; flushed birds give a loud trilling tirrr-tirrr-tirrr.
- Similar species —
  - Red Knot: Far bigger (23–26 cm) with short dark legs and a dark bill no longer than the head; uniform pale grey in winter, cinnamon face, throat and breast when breeding.
  - Dunlin: Larger (16–21 cm) with black legs and a longer, slightly decurved black bill; black belly patch in breeding plumage; narrow white wing-bar at all ages.
  - Ruff: Much bigger and pot-bellied, small-headed and long-necked on long pink to orange legs; flight deep and slow, showing white ovals either side of the tail.
  - Purple Sandpiper: Larger (19–22 cm); dark above with a purplish gloss, breast smeared grey, rump black; bill dark with a yellow base, legs short and yellow.

## Terek Sandpiper
*Xenus cinereus* · `tersan`

- Jizz — 22–25 cm, wingspan 38–40 cm. Slightly larger than Common Sandpiper and about Wood Sandpiper's size but shorter-legged; long, distinctly upcurved bill, avocet-like though not so strongly bent.
- Plumage — Grey back, face and breast in all plumages; belly whitish; pale supercilium more or less distinct. Breeding birds add black lengthwise stripes on the scapulars.
- Bare parts — Bill black with a yellowish base, dull red at the base when breeding; legs short, yellow to orange.
- In the field — Bobs the rear body when agitated. Commonest call, usually in flight, a soft fluting hühühü of two or three syllables, sometimes five; also trilling calls and a repeated three-syllable song, dlü-rrri-rüh.
- Similar species —
  - Lesser Yellowlegs: Bill straight, slim and uniformly dark, about head length, never upcurved; upperparts mottled, breast streaked, flanks barred; longer yellow legs, 23–25 cm.
  - Marsh Sandpiper: Fine straight bill and very long yellowish legs, plus a white wedge up the back in flight; greyish-brown and mottled, not plain grey-breasted; 22–26 cm.
  - Ruddy Turnstone: Harlequin pattern of brown, black and white; short wedge-shaped bill of 2–2.5 cm, only slightly upturned, legs short and bright orange; 22–24 cm.
  - Green Sandpiper: Dark greenish-brown above with white-spotted back and brilliant white rump; short straight bill and legs both dark green; 21–24 cm.

## Thekla's Lark
*Galerida theklae* · `thelar1`

- Jizz — 15–17 cm, 30–40 g — a good sparrow's size; sturdily built, with a long, spiky, erectile crest; wings fairly short and broad-based, primaries barely visible when closed; tail short; hind claw long, 9–14 mm.
- Plumage — Crown and upperparts grey-brown, boldly blackish-streaked, finer on the nape; whitish supercilium, dark eye-stripe, pale orbital ring, whitish dark-edged malar; underparts whitish, breast-sides and flanks washed buff-brown; lower throat and breast spotted, condensing into strong streaks; underwing-coverts and axillaries grey; rump plain grey-brown, uppertail-coverts rusty. Sexes alike. Juvenile: crest shorter, back spotted, breast less streaked.
- Bare parts — Bill short, dark horn, with a paler base to the lower mandible.
- In the field — Grey underwings in flight; no white edges to wing or tail. Calls like Crested Lark's but softer and with more syllables; male sings from a bush-top, in low song-flight, sometimes in high circling flight.
- Similar species —
  - Crested Lark: Larger, 17–19 cm, bill longer and paler; browner and less grey, back and breast streaking less distinct, uppertail-coverts duller; underwings reddish, not grey; calls harder.
  - Calandra Lark: Larger and robust, 17.5–20 cm, with big black patches on the breast-sides and a white supercilium; in flight short broad wings dark below and a short white-edged tail.
  - Dupont's Lark: Slim, long-necked and long-legged with a fine, slightly curved bill and a thin pale crown-stripe instead of a crest; song a thin, melancholic, ventriloquial whistle at dusk or night.
  - Greater Short-toed Lark: Small, pale and crestless, with a strong pointed pinkish bill, pale supercilium, a dark patch each side of the neck and a dark tail; winter flocks large and compact.

## Thick-billed Murre
*Uria lomvia* · `thbmur`

- Jizz — 40–48 cm, 700–1481 g, wingspan 64–81 cm. With Common Murre the largest living auk; long body, short neck, small head, small wings, long feet; bill long, thick, pointed, tapering.
- Plumage — Black to blackish-brown on head, neck, back and wings, white below; small rounded black tail; secondary tips white, primaries dark brown. The black throat runs onto the fore-breast so that a white inverted V shows on each breast-side. Winter: lower face white, less so than Common Murre; flanks unstreaked.
- Bare parts — Bill slaty black with a white stripe along the upper mandible; gape and tongue yellow; feet pale red to grey- or green-yellow.
- In the field — Swims very high, tail clear of the water; takes off with great difficulty and never lands on flat ground. Harsh cackling at colonies, silent at sea; looks shorter than Common Murre in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Common Murre: Bill thinner and longer with no white gape stripe; head and back paler, flanks dark-streaked in winter, and it alone may show a bridled white eye-stripe; 38–46 cm.
  - Razorbill: Bill black, deep and laterally compressed with a blunt grooved tip carrying a broken white line, plus a white line from eye to bill; thick-set, 37–43 cm.
  - Black Guillemot: Much smaller, 30–38 cm; summer plumage all black bar a large white upperwing patch, with coral-red legs and mouth.

## Thrush Nightingale
*Luscinia luscinia* · `thrnig1`

- Jizz — 16–18 cm, 22–27 g, heavier birds to 34 g before migration. Robin-shaped chat; bill fairly compact.
- Plumage — Upperparts dark brown with a slight olive tinge, never rufous; wings and tail dark rufous-brown; lores and ear-coverts brownish-black; dark eye with a pale rim. Chin and throat pale buff to whitish mottled brown, sides of the throat spotted; breast feathers dark-centred, giving a clouded, mottled breast; undertail-coverts buff, faintly barred brown. Sexes alike; juvenile darker and more mottled.
- Bare parts — Bill dark brown, paler below; legs and feet pale brown; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Song loud — whistles, trills and clicks with a flute-like bell-toned pioo, broken by a rasping dserr; solemn, without any whistling crescendo. Often opens with low whistles and a few clicking notes.
- Similar species —
  - Common Nightingale: Plain warm brown above with a distinctly redder tail, buff to white below; breast unclouded, undertail-coverts plain; slightly smaller at 15–16.5 cm.
  - Bluethroat: Male has a blue throat pattern; female shows a blackish crescent and some blue on a cream throat and breast — never a plain mottled brown breast.
  - Black Wheatear: All black (female dark brown), with a white rump and a mainly white tail marked with a black inverted T; loud thrush-like song.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Pale sandy above with a white rump and uppertail-coverts and a broad white tail-base; creamy supercilium, pale brown ear-coverts, underparts plain sandy-buff.

## Tree Pipit
*Anthus trivialis* · `trepip`

- Jizz — 14–16 cm, 22–24 g in the breeding season, over 30 g before autumn migration. Small slim pipit; bill thin but heavy for a pipit, hind claw short.
- Plumage — Yellow to olive-brown above with diffuse blackish streaks, sharper on the crown; rump and upper tail-coverts greener and barely streaked; cream to yellowish below, throat, breast and neck sides yellower, breast boldly streaked and flanks much less so; outer tail feathers partly white; two pale wingbars from light-edged coverts.
- Bare parts — Bill with a pink base.
- In the field — Feeds on the ground but perches in trees far more than its relatives; song-flight unmistakable, rising from a tree and parachuting down on stiff wings, the song drawn out at the end; call a strong harsh spihz.
- Similar species —
  - Meadow Pipit: Slightly smaller, 14.5–15 cm, thinner-billed with a yellow bill base and a conspicuously long hind claw; little contrast between buff breast and belly; weak tsi-tsi call; keeps to the ground.
  - Rock Pipit: Larger, 16.5–17 cm; smoky-olive above and weakly streaked, buff below with heavy ill-defined brown streaks; legs, bill and iris dark, with a pale eye-ring.
  - Red-throated Pipit: Breeding adult has a brick-red face and throat; in autumn far more striped all over — cap, back, flanks, rump and chest; call a characteristic psii.
  - Water Pipit: Greyish-brown and weakly streaked above, pale pink-buff below; grey head with a broad white supercilium; white outer tail feathers, dark legs and bill.

## Trumpeter Finch
*Bucanetes githagineus* · `trufin2`

- Jizz — 11.5–13 cm. Small, long-winged finch with a large head and thick neck; bill very short and heavy, culmen steeply arched; tail short and forked, so the wings look long when perched but short and blunt in flight.
- Plumage — Summer male: head and neck grey, upper back pale brown; breast, belly, rump and tail pink, the tail dark at edges and centre; pink flush also over forecrown, back and wing-coverts. Otherwise plain sandy and unmarked — greyish head-sides, caramel neck and breast, dark ochre wings and tail. Winter male, female and juvenile far paler and less contrasted, juveniles browner.
- Bare parts — Male's bill vivid orange to deep red when breeding, pale orange or horn otherwise and in females, yellowish-brown in juveniles; dark eye, faint pale ring.
- In the field — Song a nasal, harsh, drawn-out buzzing mechanical trill, like a small tin toy trumpet — the source of the name.
- Similar species —
  - African Chaffinch: Male blue-grey headed with a moss-green mantle, black wings crossed by two bold white stripes, and a blue-grey white-sided tail; not plain sandy.
  - Brambling: White rump, orange breast against a white belly and orange scapulars; breeding male black-headed; 14–16 cm, and the bill is not stubby.
  - Citril Finch: Grey above with a black-streaked brown back, yellow underparts, yellow double wing-bars and a yellow face mask; 12–12.5 cm.
  - Island Canary: Yellow-green head and underparts, dark streaks on the sides, dull yellow rump, grey-green streaked upperparts; 10–12 cm.

## Tufted Duck
*Aythya fuligula* · `tufduc`

- Jizz — 40–47 cm, wingspan 65–72 cm. Small, compact diving duck with a fairly long neck and a short, broad bill widening towards the tip. Drooping tuft on the nape.
- Plumage — Male: black head, breast, upperparts and tail, flanks and belly white; tuft long and hanging. Female: brown, darker on back and head, flanks paler, some with white round the bill base but never as extensive as in the scaups; tuft shorter. Eclipse male dark brown with slightly paler flanks. Juvenile: duller grey-brown. White wing-band in flight in both sexes.
- Bare parts — Bill blue-grey, broadening to a black tip; iris golden yellow, striking in both sexes.
- In the field — Sociable, usually in tightly packed parties on the water and in flight. Mostly silent; excited males give a muffled falling trill pit piu pit pit and whistle wit-oo in courtship, female a harsh growling karr in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Common Pochard: No tuft; sloping forehead and longer bill, drake chestnut-headed with black breast and pale grey vermiculated body, bill dark grey with a broad blue-grey band. 42–49 cm.
  - Greater Scaup: No tuft and a rounder head; drake's back light, not black, head glossed green to purple; call different. Larger, 39–56 cm.
  - Ferruginous Duck: Male rich dark chestnut on head, breast and flanks with pure white undertail-coverts, and white belly and underwing patch in flight; female duller brown, dark-eyed.
  - Ring-necked Duck: Angular black head with no drooping tuft; two white rings round the grey bill, black back, white breast; female grey-brown with a white eye-ring. 39–46 cm.

## Tundra Bean-Goose
*Anser serrirostris* · `tunbeg1`

- Jizz — 66–90 cm, 135–175 cm span, 1.7–4 kg. Medium-large grey goose, dark and long-winged for the group; compact body, short thick neck, rounded head, short deep-based bill.
- Plumage — Head and neck grey-brown, the head distinctly darker than the neck; upperparts dark brown with narrow grey-white feather fringes; breast pale grey, belly whitish. Upperwing-coverts dark brown with narrow white fringes.
- Bare parts — Bill short and stout, mostly black with an orange band across the middle and a dark nail, lamellae showing; legs and feet orange to yellow-red.
- In the field — Voice a loud honking, pitched higher and more nasal than Taiga Bean Goose; silhouette in flight often squatter.
- Similar species —
  - Pink-footed Goose: Same bill pattern but bright pink where this is orange, and pink feet; upperwing-coverts pale bluish-grey, not dark brown; rump and vent white, tail broadly white-tipped.
  - Graylag Goose: Bulkier and larger, 74–91 cm; pink legs, an orange or pink bill with a pale nail, and pale bluish-grey upperwing-coverts.
  - Taiga Bean-Goose: Longer-necked, longer-billed with statistically more orange on the bill, less compact and clearly larger; calls lower and less nasal.
  - Greater White-fronted Goose: White facial blaze and black belly bars; mouse-coloured upper wing-coverts without white fringes, pinkish bill, orange legs. 64–81 cm.

## Tundra Swan
*Cygnus columbianus* · `tunswa`

- Jizz — 115–150 cm, 3.4–9.6 kg, wingspan 168–211 cm. Smallest Holarctic swan; shorter-necked and rounder-bodied than Whooper. Female slightly smaller, otherwise alike.
- Plumage — Adult all white; head and neck may take a golden or rusty hue in iron-rich water. Immature: white mixed with dull grey, head and upper neck often wholly light grey; first-summer already largely white, adult plumage in the second winter. Downy young silvery grey above, white below.
- Bare parts — Bill mostly black with a thin salmon-pink mouthline and yellow at the base, extensive in bewickii, nearly absent in columbianus; feet black; iris dark brown.
- In the field — The subspecies wintering regularly in central Europe is bewickii; on its bill the black always outweighs the yellow.
- Similar species —
  - Whooper Swan: Larger, 140–165 cm, wingspan 205–275 cm, 7.4–14 kg; longer-necked, and yellow covers more of the bill than black does.
  - Mute Swan: Bigger, 140–160 cm, wingspan 200–240 cm, 9.2–14.3 kg; white with dark grey legs and a knobbed bill, the knob larger in the male.
  - Black Swan: Black-plumaged with white flight feathers, bright red pale-tipped bill and greyish-black legs; neck long and S-curved; 110–142 cm.
  - Brant: A small dark goose, 55–66 cm, with a short stubby bill; black head and neck with a small white neck patch, dark grey-brown body, pure white undertail.

## Turkestan Short-toed Lark
*Alaudala heinei* · `tstlar1`

- Jizz — 13–14 cm, 20–27 g. Small, pale, short-billed lark; bill broad-based and finch-like, wings long with a fairly long primary projection, tail fairly short, hindclaw short; wide eye-ring.
- Plumage — Crown finely streaked blackish, crest raisable; upperparts dark red-brown to brown with broader dark streaks that fade towards the tail; median coverts darker than the rest of the wing; supercilium narrow and whitish, never bold; underparts white, breast finely streaked and, with the flanks, washed red-brown to cinnamon. Sexes alike. Juvenile: somewhat spotted below.
- Bare parts — Bill horn-grey, darker on the culmen, base of the lower mandible yellowish; legs brownish to yellow-brown.
- Similar species —
  - Mediterranean Short-toed Lark: Smaller, 13–14 cm, slightly more rufous, breast streaking heavier; otherwise near-identical, and long treated as the same species.
  - Greater Short-toed Lark: Dark patch on each side of the neck, plainer breast, stronger pointed pinkish bill with grey culmen, longer tertials and a clear pale supercilium.
  - Calandra Lark: Much larger and robust, 17.5–20 cm, with big black patches on the breast sides, white supercilium, dark underwings and a white-edged tail.
  - Crested Lark: Bigger, 17–18 cm, bulkier about head and body, with a conspicuous crest at all times and a short tail with light brown outer feathers.

## Twite
*Linaria flavirostris* · `twite1`

- Jizz — 12.5–14 cm, 15–19 g. Small streaky finch, linnet-sized but slimmer and small-headed, with a short siskin-like conical bill and a long, deeply forked tail.
- Plumage — Brown above streaked black, back dark; head and throat rusty-buff, throat unstreaked; underparts buff-white heavily streaked brown; pale wing bars and a dull white primary panel. Male: pink rump, and pink on breast and forehead, obscured by brown feather tips in fresh autumn plumage. Female: rump brown. Juvenile: as female but breast more heavily streaked.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow with a dark tip in winter, grey-brown in summer.
- In the field — Flocks, often large, outside the breeding season on coasts and salt marshes, mixed with other finches; seed-eater. Call a nasal twaa-it, and a long rising tweeiht; song fast trills and twitters with a rattling trrrrrr.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Linnet: Summer male has a red crown patch and red breast and a grey nape; all ages less streaked below, with a grey bill and a bolder white wing panel.
  - Redpoll: Red forehead patch and black bib, both lacking in Twite; rump streaked, not pink, and a broad dark streak across the vent.
  - Common Rosefinch: Bigger-billed and unstreaked; male rosy-carmine on head, breast and rump, female plain yellowish-brown and buff below. Larger, 13–15 cm.
  - Desert Finch: Stout black bill, black-and-white flight and tail feathers, and a rosy-pink slash on each wing; not streaked. Larger, 13–15 cm.

## Upcher's Warbler
*Hippolais languida* · `upcwar1`

- Jizz — 14–15 cm, mostly 10.5–13.5 g. Medium-sized Hippolais, looking stocky through the long, clubby tail; bill long and thin but broad-based with concave sides and a slightly decurved tip; wings short, forehead steep.
- Plumage — Pale grey-brown: crown and mantle light brownish-grey, rump and tail-coverts sandy-ochre, underparts dirty white. Grey-white supercilium from bill base to behind the eye, merging with a thin eye-ring; lores darker grey; ear-coverts grey-brown. Tail brown-grey at the base, blackening towards the tip, outer feathers narrowly white-fringed; pale streak on the wing; tail and wing-tips darker than body. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Bill blackish or dark grey-horn above, pale yellow or pink below; iris dark brown, often faintly reddish.
- In the field — Constantly waggles the tail, also sideways, and spreads it — diagnostic, recalling a Sylvia warbler or a chat; glides characteristically before landing. Semi-desert scrub and tamarisk thickets.
- Similar species —
  - Icterine Warbler: Smaller, 12–13.5 cm; greyish-green above, uniformly light yellow below, tail short and square-ended, wings long, legs grey to bluish; no tail-waggling.
  - Melodious Warbler: Smaller, 12–13 cm, plain brown above and distinctly yellow below, legs brown; song a fluent babbling; lacks the long tail and the tail-waggling.
  - Olive-tree Warbler: Bigger, 16–18 cm, with a long thick bill showing an obvious orange-yellow lower mandible; pale olive-grey above with a pale wing panel; call a deep chack.
  - Red-backed Shrike: A shrike, 16–18 cm, hunting from prominent perches; male grey-headed with a black eye-stripe and reddish upperparts, female brown and vermiculated below.

## Ural Owl
*Strix uralensis* · `uraowl1`

- Jizz — 50–64 cm, wingspan 103–134 cm; female 950–1150 g, male 600–850 g. Large Strix: broad rounded head, round rather flat facial disc, no ear tufts, rounded wings and an exceptionally long wedge-tipped tail.
- Plumage — Pale greyish-brown to whitish overall, back and mantle darker grey-brown with contrasting whitish markings; underparts pale cream-ochre to grey-brown, boldly streaked dark brown without crossbars. Tail rounded, evenly barred, usually five narrow pale bars and a pale tip. In flight the underwing is buffish-white with heavy dark bars along the trailing edge and tip. Northern birds palest, some nearly white.
- Bare parts — Eyes small, close-set and almond-shaped, dark brown to reddish-brown; bill orange-yellow.
- In the field — Flight recalls a buzzard but with deeper, more relaxed beats, direct and purposeful, the long white-tipped tail often slung downwards. Male's song a deep rhythmic seven-syllable hooting; calls nasal and barking, like a dog.
- Similar species —
  - Tawny Owl: Much smaller, 37–46 cm, wingspan 81–105 cm; shorter-tailed, warmer rufous or grey-brown, larger dark eyes in a plain disc, white drop-marks on the scapulars.
  - Great Gray Owl: Bigger, 60–84 cm, wingspan 135–160 cm; grey face with the largest facial disc of any raptor, yellow eyes in dark rings, white bow-tie below the bill.
  - Eurasian Goshawk: A hawk, not an owl: short broad wings, long tail, blue-grey or brownish above and barred below, no facial disc; 46–64 cm.
  - Eurasian Eagle-Owl: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, wingspan 131–188 cm against 103–134 cm, and on sheer bulk.

## Velvet Scoter
*Melanitta fusca* · `whwsco3`

- Jizz — 51–56 cm, wingspan 90–99 cm. Large heavy sea duck; neck thick at the base, bill long, broad and wedge-shaped, tail pointed. Males average 1.5 kg, females just under 1.2 kg.
- Plumage — Male glossy black with a small white mark below and behind the eye and a large white wing patch; bill dark with an orange-yellow side field, base slightly swollen, no knob. Female brown, duller and unglossed, pale spot between eye and bill, often a pale cheek patch. Both sexes show white secondaries as a wing patch, often visible swimming.
- Bare parts — Feet red; male's iris blue-grey, female's darker.
- In the field — Dives without a jump, wings already open; flocks smaller and looser than Common Scoter's. Mostly silent: male a nasal aah-ör, female a rough braa-ah… braa-ah. Tamer than Common Scoter.
- Similar species —
  - Common Scoter: No white anywhere: drake all black with a yellow bill patch and a knob at the bill base; dives with a little jump, wings folded.
  - Surf Scoter: Male's white patches are on forehead and nape, not behind the eye; bill swollen and patterned white, red and yellow; no white wing patch.
  - Red-breasted Merganser: Slim sawbill with a spiky crest and long thin red serrated bill; male green-glossed dark head, white collar, rusty breast.
  - Greater Scaup: Light blue bill with a small black nail, yellow eyes, rounded head; male has a light back and bright white flanks and belly.

## Wallcreeper
*Tichodroma muraria* · `wallcr1`

- Jizz — 15.5–17 cm, 17–19 g. Small, slate-grey climber with broad wings, a short tail, a long decurved black bill and very short legs with over-long toes.
- Plumage — Blue-grey; flight and tail feathers darker, the tail short and black with a narrow white fringe. Coverts and the basal half of the primaries and secondaries crimson to brick-red with white spots, mostly hidden on the folded wing. Male: throat black in summer, fore-breast dark grey to black. Female: throat grey or whitish. Winter birds and juveniles white-throated.
- Bare parts — Bill long, thin, decurved and black.
- In the field — Unmistakable on steep rock faces; climbs by hops and short flights, mostly sideways, flicking and spreading the wings to flash red and white. Flight like a tiny Hoopoe or a huge butterfly; mouse-like when perched. Song a high drawn-out whistle.

## Water Pipit
*Anthus spinoletta* · `watpip1`

- Jizz — 15–17 cm, 18.7–23 g. Slim, comparatively long-legged pipit with dark feet.
- Plumage — Spring adult: greyish-brown above, weakly streaked darker; head grey with a broad white supercilium; underparts pale pink-buff, salmon on the breast, whitish on the belly, streaking almost absent. Non-breeding: head grey-brown, supercilium less distinct, upperparts darker and more streaked, underparts white streaked brown on breast and flanks. Young browner, more heavily streaked.
- Bare parts — Legs and toes dark grey to dark grey-brown, sometimes paler; bill dark, base of the lower mandible yellow in winter; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Outer tail feathers white; in all plumages the tail reads three-toned lengthwise — pale beige centre, dark sides, white outer edges. Clear pale wingbars.
- Similar species —
  - Meadow Pipit: Legs pale pinkish-yellow, not dark; brown above and buff below with darker barring throughout, tail only narrowly white-edged. Slightly smaller, 14.5–15 cm.
  - Rock Pipit: Legs dark too, but with a rusty cast this bird lacks; smoky-olive above, buff below with heavy ill-defined streaks, dingier and less contrasting; pale eye-ring.
  - Tree Pipit: Pale legs, heavier bill, buff breast contrasting with a white belly; perches in trees, calls a strong spek, parachutes down on stiff wings in song-flight.
  - Red-throated Pipit: Pale legs; brick-red face and throat when breeding, otherwise striped all over — cap, back, flanks, rump, chest — with whitish mantle stripes. Call a psii in flight.

## Water Rail
*Rallus aquaticus* · `watrai1`

- Jizz — 23–30 cm, 100–190 g, wingspan 38–45 cm. Medium rail with a short tail and a long, slightly decurved red bill making up 3–4.5 cm of the length; mid-length legs, long toes.
- Plumage — Upperparts forehead to tail olive- to dark brown streaked black, heaviest on the shoulders; head-sides, throat, neck and breast to upper belly dark slate-blue, blackish between bill and eye, breast-sides browner; flanks barred black and white; undertail white with darker streaks. Sexes alike. Juvenile: blackish crown, white chin and throat, barred buff-white below. Chick all black, bill pale.
- Bare parts — Bill red; iris red; legs flesh-brown.
- In the field — Commonest call a squealing kruieh or grryitt gruigru, like the grunting of a disgruntled pig; also a groaning ååuuuh and short kipp; male's display an insistent hammering kypp kypp kypp. Flightless about three weeks after breeding.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Crake: Bill short and flesh-coloured, not long and red; wing-coverts chestnut with white bars, flanks and undertail barred chestnut and white; legs pale grey, iris pale brown.
  - Eurasian Moorhen: Larger (30–38 cm); red frontal shield and yellow-tipped red bill, yellow legs, white streaks along the flanks and white undertail — no black-and-white barring.
  - Western Swamphen: Chicken-sized with huge feet and bright plumage; heavy red bill with a red frontal shield, not a long slender decurved bill.
  - Spotted Crake: Smaller (19–22.5 cm); bill short, straight, yellow with a red base; brown upperparts dark-barred and white-spotted on the flanks; legs green.

## Western Black-eared Wheatear
*Oenanthe hispanica* · `bkewhe1`

- Jizz — 13.5–15.5 cm, 15–22 g. Small, slim, long-tailed wheatear; strong sexual dimorphism and two colour morphs; short primary projection.
- Plumage — Breeding male: forehead and forecrown white, rest of crown, nape and upper mantle ochre-buff, bleaching near-white by July; lower back and rump white; lores and ear-coverts black. Wings uniformly black; central tail feathers black, the rest white with a black terminal band widening outwards. Breast yellow-buff, belly whitish. Throat white or black by morph. Female: browner, white lower back.
- In the field — Song a dry, short, explosive strophe, sometimes with mimicry, of Barn Swallow among others. White-throated morph is the commoner one in this species.
- Similar species —
  - Northern Wheatear: Larger, 14.5–16 cm; summer male grey above with a buff throat, no white crown or ochre mantle; tail has an even black inverted T and much less white.
  - Eastern Black-eared Wheatear: Whiter, more distinctly black-and-white above; its mask reaches just above the bill base and the black throat is more extensive and ends abruptly; longer primary projection.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Sexes alike, pale sandy-isabelline throughout with no black mask; creamy-white supercilium, pale brown ear-coverts, less white on the tail; larger, about 16.5 cm.
  - Black Wheatear: All black, female dark brown, except white rump and mainly white tail with a black inverted T; larger, 16–18 cm.

## Western Bonelli's Warbler
*Phylloscopus bonelli* · `webwar1`

- Jizz — 10.5–12 cm, wingspan 18–19 cm, 7–9 g. Small, plain leaf warbler; bill small and pointed.
- Plumage — Crown and back dull grey-green with a brown tinge; rump yellow-green and contrasting; wings and tail green-toned with black in them, tertials pale-edged; throat to undertail-coverts white; supercilium whitish but indistinct. Sexes alike. Spring birds often show a pale grey panel formed by bleached, worn edges to greater coverts and tertials.
- Bare parts — Bill small and pointed; legs brown to pale grey-brown.
- In the field — Forest and woodland; insectivorous. Call a disyllabic hu-it or hy-if. Song a fast monotone trill of high notes, at first like wood warbler's but never accelerating.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Greenish-brown above, off-white to yellowish below, 11–12.5 cm; pale pinkish legs, longer paler bill, long primary projection; song a simple descending whistle, call hoo-eet.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Dumpy, 10–12 cm, 6–8 g; brown-washed dull green above, yellowish on the flanks, short whitish supercilium, dark legs, fine dark bill, short primary projection; call a monosyllabic hweet.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Smaller, 9.5–11 cm, 4–9 g; bold double yellowish-white wing-bars, yellow-margined tertials and a long yellow supercilium; almost constantly in motion.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above, off-white below, with a single wing bar in southern and western birds; bill thin, without a dark tip below; song a high jerky trill.

## Western Capercaillie
*Tetrao urogallus* · `wescap1`

- Jizz — Cock 74–90 cm, wingspan about 115 cm, 4–5 kg; hen 54–64 cm, wingspan about 70 cm. Very large, heavy grouse; extreme size dimorphism, tail rounded.
- Plumage — Cock dark grey to dark brown, breast a metallic green shield; belly and undertail-coverts black to white by race; tail black, often white-spotted at mid-length; lesser coverts white, a spot at the wing bow. Female: brown above barred black and silver, paler and buffish-yellow below, throat and breast plain orange-brown, tail rufous with coarse black bars.
- Bare parts — Bare swollen red skin over the eye in both sexes; bill yellowish-white in older birds, darker in young ones.
- In the field — Legs feathered, especially in winter; horny pectinations along the toes work as snowshoes and print clearly in snow, tracks sexed on size. Chicks hen-coloured with a black crown patch.
- Similar species —
  - Barbary Partridge: Rotund partridge: grey-brown back, grey breast, buff belly, light grey face with a broad reddish-brown gorget, rufous-streaked white flanks, red legs.
  - Black Grouse: Smaller, 45–60 cm; cock black glossed deep blue with a white wingline and white undertail-coverts, red skin over the eye, long outer tail feathers, not a rounded tail.
  - Black Francolin: Far smaller, 33–36 cm; male black with a white cheek patch, chestnut collar and white-spotted flanks; rounded tail barred white; legs reddish.
  - Chukar: Small partridge, 32–35 cm; light brown back, grey breast, white face sharply ringed by a black gorget, rufous-streaked flanks, red legs, coral-red bill.

## Western Cattle-Egret
*Ardea ibis* · `categr1`

- Jizz — Wingspan 80–100 cm; 200–600 g. Stocky, short heron with a short thick neck, usually retracted, a short sturdy pointed bill and a hunched posture; legs short, toes long.
- Plumage — Adult all white, well feathered on the chin. Breeding: orange-buff to red-brown plumes on crown, breast and back. Sexes alike, the male marginally larger with slightly longer plumes. Juvenile: white, no plumes.
- Bare parts — Bill yellow; legs grey to yellowish; bill, legs and irides briefly bright red before pairing; juvenile bill black.
- In the field — Feeds on land rather than in water; may be active at dusk or by night. Largely silent away from the colony, where it gives a quiet throaty rick-rack.
- Similar species —
  - Gray Heron: Far larger, 85–102 cm and over a kilogram; ashy-grey, not white, with a black supercilium trailing into a crest and black streaks down the foreneck.
  - Great Egret: Also white but far bigger, wingspan 131–170 cm and 700–1,500 g; yellow bill with black legs and feet, and no orange-buff plumes.
  - Purple Heron: Much larger, 78–97 cm; dark reddish-brown and grey, never white; black crown with a dark neck-stripe and dangling crest, buff-chestnut head-sides dark-streaked.
  - Black-crowned Night Heron: Black crown and back against grey wings and white underparts, red eyes, short yellow legs and long white nape plumes; never all white.

## Western House-Martin
*Delichon urbicum* · `comhom1`

- Jizz — 13 cm; wingspan 26–29 cm, 16–25 g. Compact martin with shallowly forked black tail and no elongated outer feathers; smaller and slimmer than a sparrow.
- Plumage — Steel-blue head, mantle, upperwing and tail; broad white rump; underparts wholly white, underwings included; short legs and feet feathered white. Sexes alike. Juvenile: sooty to brownish black above and dull, some coverts and quills tipped and edged white, chin dirty white.
- Bare parts — Bill small and black; eyes brown; toes and the bare parts of the legs pink.
- In the field — Noisy, above all at colonies; contact call a hard 'tsrr', alarm a sharp 'sir'; male's babbling twitter less varied than Barn Swallow's. Wingbeat 5.3 per second against the Barn Swallow's 4.4.
- Similar species —
  - Barn Swallow: Larger, 17–19 cm, deeply forked tail with long streamers; rufous forehead and throat cut off by a broad dark blue breast band; no white rump.
  - Bank Swallow: Brown above, not steel-blue; white below with a narrow brown breast band and white throat; no white rump; flight quick and jerky.
  - Pale Crag-Martin: Light brown above, pale grey throat and upper breast, dirty-white belly; short square tail with small white spots near the tips; no white rump.
  - Eurasian Crag-Martin: Ash-brown above, blackish underwing- and undertail-coverts, broad body, wings and tail; short square tail with white spots near the tips; no white rump.

## Western Marsh Harrier
*Circus aeruginosus* · `wemhar1`

- Jizz — 42–62 cm, 115–140 cm span; male 400–650 g, female 550–800 g. Largest and bulkiest European harrier, broader-winged than the others but slimmer, narrower-bodied and longer-tailed than a buzzard.
- Plumage — Male: cryptic reddish-brown, pale yellowish streaks heaviest on the breast, head and shoulders pale greyish-yellow, mid-wing panel silver-grey, tail grey, primaries black — three-toned brown-grey-black in flight. Female: chocolate-brown with pale yellow crown, throat and forewing edge and a dark stripe through the eye, the pale eye standing out; no grey in wing or tail. Juvenile: as female, less yellow.
- Bare parts — Bill black with a yellow cere; legs, feet and irides yellow.
- In the field — Glides, soars and circles with the wings held in a characteristic V.
- Similar species —
  - Hen Harrier: Smaller and lighter, 41–52 cm and 97–122 cm span; white rump at every age, male grey and white with black tips, female a barred ringtail.
  - Montagu's Harrier: Much slighter, 39–50 cm, 96–116 cm span, 227–305 g; male pale grey with black secondary bands and rusty flank streaks, female a white-rumped ringtail.
  - Pallid Harrier: Smaller, paler and narrower-winged, 40–48 cm; male whitish grey above and white below with narrow black tips, female a white-rumped ringtail.
  - European Honey-buzzard: Small-headed and long-necked with a long tail carrying two narrow bars and a broad dark subterminal bar; soars on flat wings, not in a V. 52–60 cm.

## Western Olivaceous Warbler
*Iduna opaca* · `weowar1`

- Jizz — 13–14.5 cm, wingspan 18–21 cm, 8–13 g. Medium-sized to large Iduna, slim and long-tailed; bill disproportionately long and broad, often convex-sided; legs and feet fairly strong.
- Plumage — Upperparts plain pale brown to isabelline, warmer and ochre on the rump; wing uniform, feathers without pale fringes, only the alula darker; tail tips brownish-white and very indistinct. Underparts whitish, washed yellow-brown on breast sides and flanks. Head plain — short pale supercilium from bill to eye running into a faint eye-ring, no eye-stripe. Young buffer on the belly.
- Bare parts — Bill dark grey to brown above, dull yellowish-brown below; iris dark brown; legs and feet grey-brown to greyish flesh.
- In the field — Dry open country, cultivation with bushes or scattered trees; feeds at varying heights, insectivorous. Does not wag the tail, or only rarely. Song a fast nasal babbling.
- Similar species —
  - Eastern Olivaceous Warbler: Smaller at 11.5–14 cm with a smaller bill, upperparts greyer or greener rather than brown, lores darker; flicks the tail downward, which this species rarely does.
  - Booted Warbler: Chiffchaff-sized, short-winged and pot-bellied with a square-tipped tail; clear pale supercilium and a short dark-tipped bill, not a long broad one.
  - Common Reed Warbler: Back unstreaked brown, underparts buff, forehead flattened; 12.5–14 cm, wingspan 17–21 cm; song a slow chattering jit-jit-jit with whistles and mimicry, not a fast babble.
  - Upcher's Warbler: Greyer, with longer tail, shorter wings and a longer bill; moves the tail constantly like a chat or Sylvia; 14–15 cm.

## Western Orphean Warbler
*Curruca hortensis* · `weowar2`

- Jizz — 15–16 cm, 28 g. One of the largest Curruca, clearly bigger than a Blackcap; bill long, strong and pointed; tail square-cut with white outer edges.
- Plumage — Male: plain grey back, head dark grey with blackish forehead, ear-coverts and mask below the eye, throat white, underparts dirty white, undertail-coverts diffusely mottled. Female: head olive-grey and much paler, back and coverts brownish-grey, flanks buff. First-winter as female but head paler grey, flanks and underparts distinctly buffier.
- Bare parts — Iris white to pale yellow in adult male, dark in female and young; legs black.
- In the field — Song a clear warbling liroo-liroo, repeated singly in Ring Ouzel fashion, with scolding notes; call a smacking taeck.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above with chestnut fringes to the secondaries and buff underparts; male's head grey without a black mask; iris never white.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Small and light, 12–16 g; grey head with a darker bandit mask rather than a black face, white throat, dark iris; back grey, underparts whitish.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's whole head black, not merely a mask, with red eyes; female brown above with a grey head and buff underparts, never a white iris.
  - Western Subalpine Warbler: Male grey-headed with brick-red underparts and white malar stripes; female brown above and pink-flushed below; neither has the white iris or plain whitish underside.

## Western Rock Nuthatch
*Sitta neumayer* · `rocnut1`

- Jizz — 13.5 cm in the English source, 14–15.5 cm in the German; slightly smaller than Eurasian Nuthatch. Typical big nuthatch head, short tail, powerful bill and feet; long-legged and long-billed for the family.
- Plumage — Pale-looking overall; juveniles duller than the adults. No white on the underside of the tail.
- In the field — The only nuthatch that climbs bare rock; bare rocky, mountainous ground, highest birds moving lower in winter. Feeds on the ground, wedging large items in crevices and hammering them open; also flycatches. Song a series of descending whistles. Tame.
- Similar species —
  - Eurasian Nuthatch: Blue-grey above with black eye-stripe, whitish throat and orange-red flanks; slightly larger at 14 cm, shorter-billed and shorter-legged, and a tree bird.
  - Krüper's Nuthatch: Smaller, 12.5 cm, blue-grey above with a black fore-crown; the rock nuthatch looks plain and pale and climbs rock.

## Western Subalpine Warbler
*Curruca iberiae* · `subwar6`

- Jizz — Small Curruca warbler with a naked eye-ring; the sources give no measurements.
- Plumage — Male: head and back lead-grey, underparts brick to orange-red over the whole belly, not the breast alone; white malar streak. Female: brown-grey above with a greyer head, whitish below with a pink flush. No white wedge on the second-outermost tail feather.
- Bare parts — Legs pink; eye-ring naked.
- In the field — Dry open country, often hill slopes with bushes; insectivorous. Song fast and rattling, like lesser whitethroat's; call a tek or tett, thinner than related species'.
- Similar species —
  - Greater Whitethroat: Brown above and buff below with chestnut fringes to the secondaries; white throat, no red underparts and no white malar streak; song fast and scratchy, scolding.
  - Lesser Whitethroat: Sexes alike, grey back, whitish underparts, grey head with a darker bandit mask and white throat, no red below; songs are alike, so go on plumage.
  - Sardinian Warbler: Male's head wholly black against the grey back, throat white, underparts whitish, eye and orbital ring red; female brown above with a grey head, buff below.
  - Dartford Warbler: Long-tailed, 13 cm; grey back and head, reddish underparts with a white-spotted red throat, red eye; tones muted throughout, sides dull greyish.

## Western Swamphen
*Porphyrio porphyrio* · `purswa`

- Jizz — 45–50 cm, wingspan 80–100 cm; female to 735 g, male to 685 g. Chicken-sized, very large, elegant rail, clearly larger than Coot; big red bill and frontal shield, long legs, conspicuously long toes.
- Plumage — Dark blue plumage shimmering violet-purple; undertail-coverts white. Juvenile duller, grey with grey-white feathering down the centre of the underparts.
- Bare parts — Bill, frontal shield and legs bright red, the shield paler in winter; juvenile bill and legs grey-pink.
- In the field — Climbs about in dense vegetation on its long legs and toes; no other rail does this. In flight the legs dangle and the wingbeats whirr. Very vocal: long repeated nasal hooting, cracked mechanical trills, a smacking tjuck contact call. Wetlands.
- Similar species —
  - Baillon's Crake: Tiny beside it, 15–18 cm; ochre-brown streaked above, blue-grey below, flanks barred black and white, legs olive-green, short bill with no red shield.
  - Eurasian Moorhen: Black and brown, not blue; white flank streaks and white undertail sides, yellow legs, red shield on a red yellow-tipped bill; smaller, 30–38 cm.
  - Corn Crake: Brown-black streaked buff above with chestnut wing-coverts, blue-grey face and breast, flesh bill and pale grey legs; 27–30 cm; no red bare parts.
  - Eurasian Coot: All black with white bill and white frontal shield, and a swimmer with part-webbed toes; smaller, 36–38 cm.

## Western Yellow Wagtail
*Motacilla flava* · `eaywag1`

- Jizz — 15–16 cm. Slender wagtail; long tail, wagged constantly, but the shortest-tailed of the European wagtails.
- Plumage — Male: olive to grey-green above, yellow below; back, flight-feather edges, underparts and undertail-coverts yellow, tail feathers black; throat and breast bright yellow; head pattern of breeding males varies by subspecies. Female: duller, back greyer, throat and breast pale yellow. Juvenile: less contrasted, browner above, paler and less yellow below.
- In the field — Insectivorous; open country near water, wet meadows. Nests in tussocks. Call a high-pitched jeet.
- Similar species —
  - White Wagtail: Yellow below and olive above; white wagtail is pale grey and white, larger at 16.5–19 cm, and longer-tailed.
  - Gray Wagtail: Grey wagtail is grey above with yellow limited to the vent against whitish underparts, narrow white supercilium, broken eye-ring; breeding male black-throated. Larger, 17–20 cm.
  - Citrine Wagtail: Breeding male citrine has the whole head yellow but for a black nape, grey or black back and white in the remiges; yellow wagtail's head is patterned by race.
  - Water Pipit: A pipit, not a wagtail: greyish-brown, weakly streaked above, pink-buff below, broad white supercilium, white outer tail feathers; no yellow anywhere.

## Whinchat
*Saxicola rubetra* · `whinch1`

- Jizz — 12–14 cm, 21–24 cm span, 13–26 g. Small, chubby, short-tailed chat with rather long legs; robin-sized, but slimmer and longer-winged than a stonechat.
- Plumage — Male: blackish face mask ringed by a white supercilium and malar, orange-buff throat and breast, brown upperparts mottled dark, whitish belly, small white patches on the greater and inner median coverts; rump the same colour as the back; tail dark with white bases to the outer feathers. Female: duller, mask browner, supercilium and malar buff, wing patches small or absent.
- Bare parts — Legs grey-black; bill short, straight, grey-black; iris black.
- In the field — Perches upright on posts, wire or a stem, flicking the tail slowly; hops on the ground in small rapid hops, bobbing and flicking wings and tail. Song purer and more varied than Wheatear's, often mimicking; call ju teck.
- Similar species —
  - European Stonechat: Dumpier and distinctly shorter-winged, 11.5–13 cm; male black-headed with a white half-collar, female lacks the strong supercilium and has a browner, streaked belly.
  - Siberian Stonechat: Darker above and paler below with a white rump and a conspicuous white collar; whinchat's rump matches its back. Winter males do show a whinchat-like supercilium.
  - European Robin: Orange breast and face bordered bluish grey, brownish upperparts, black bill and eyes; no face mask, supercilium or white wing patches. 12.5–14 cm.
  - European Pied Flycatcher: Breeding male black above and white below with a large white wing patch, white tail sides and a white forehead spot; broad-based black bill. 12–13.5 cm.

## Whiskered Tern
*Chlidonias hybrida* · `whiter2`

- Jizz — 24–28 cm, wingspan to 63 cm. Largest, longest-legged marsh tern; strong bill (male 29–34 mm, female 25–27 mm and stubbier, with a pronounced gonys); tail short and only forked-looking.
- Plumage — Summer adult: black cap, white cheeks; dark grey above and below, mantle silvery-grey; rump pale grey all year. Winter: forehead white, body much paler grey; black ear-coverts joined to blackish hindcrown as a C-shaped band, mottled white above; neck sides white, collar ill-defined. Juvenile: back dark brown with broad buff edges, ginger and scaly.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs red in the summer adult.
- In the field — Flight stiffer and more positive than other marsh terns. Call a characteristic krekk. Takes small fish.
- Similar species —
  - Black Tern: Neck and belly black, not dark grey; big dark patch before the eye; tail square, not forked; bill short and weak-looking, 27 mm; 22–26 cm.
  - White-winged Tern: Wings mainly white against a very dark back, rump white and tail near-white — whiskered keeps a pale grey rump; bill stubbier, 22–25 mm; blackish underwing-coverts.
  - Arctic Tern: Larger, 33–36 cm, with deeply forked white tail and pale grey underparts; upperwing translucent near the tip; whiskered is dark grey below and short-tailed.
  - Armenian Gull: A gull, far bigger at 52–62 cm; grey back, dark eyes, extensive black wingtips, short bill with a black band before the tip; no black cap.

## White Stork
*Ciconia ciconia* · `whisto1`

- Jizz — 95–115 cm; standing 100–125 cm, wingspan 155–215 cm, 2.3–4.5 kg. Large stork: long neck, long legs, long straight pointed bill, long broad wings, short tail. Males average larger.
- Plumage — Mainly white with black flight feathers and wing coverts; breast feathers long and shaggy, forming a ruff raised in courtship. Sexes identical. Younger birds show a dark bill-tip.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs bright red, the colour diet-derived; iris dull brown or grey; peri-orbital skin black.
- In the field — Soars on long broad wings; flapping slow and regular; flies neck stretched forward, legs projecting well beyond the tail. Walks slowly, neck upstretched; hunches head between shoulders at rest. Adults only bill-clatter; young hiss.
- Similar species —
  - Black Stork: Black with purplish-green sheen, white only on lower breast, belly, axillaries and undertail-coverts; bill and legs red as well; smaller, wingspan 145–155 cm.
  - Egyptian Vulture: Also white with black flight feathers, but bill slender with hooked tip, neck hackles long, wings pointed, tail wedge-shaped; legs pink, not red; white often soiled rusty.
  - Great White Pelican: Far bigger, 140–180 cm, wingspan 226–360 cm, with an enormous pink and yellow bill and pale-yellow gular pouch.

## White Wagtail
*Motacilla alba* · `whiwag`

- Jizz — 16.5–19 cm, 23–25 g; wingspan 25–27 cm. Slender, rather long-legged songbird; long narrow black-and-white tail, about 9 cm of the total length, wagged constantly.
- Plumage — Black, white and grey throughout. Male: white forehead, face and neck sides; rear crown, nape, chin, throat and upper breast glossy black, sharply cut against the white face and grey back; rump slate-grey; underparts and undertail-coverts white; two white wingbars; tail black with broad white outer edges, striking as the bird flies up. Juvenile: duller and more diffuse.
- Bare parts — Bill, legs and feet black; bill with a horn-coloured base in non-breeding plumage.
- In the field — Tail wagged up and down almost constantly. British and Irish yarrellii darker overall, males black-backed, females and juveniles dark grey. Call 'tsiilitt, tsiivitt'; alarm a sharp 'tsi-si'; song short twittering phrases.
- Similar species —
  - Western Yellow Wagtail: Smaller, 15–16 cm and the shortest-tailed of the wagtails; breeding male olive above and yellow below, whitish in juveniles; never black, white and grey.
  - Gray Wagtail: Grey above but with a yellow vent against whitish underparts; narrow white supercilium and broken eye-ring; breeding male's black throat edged with whitish moustachial stripes.
  - Citrine Wagtail: Slightly smaller, 15.5–17 cm; breeding male bright yellow on head and underparts with a black nape; winter birds brownish-headed with a yellowish supercilium.
  - Richard's Pipit: Pipit, not wagtail: brown above with dark streaks on upperparts and breast, stands very upright on long yellow-brown legs; long dark bill; strongly marked face.

## White-backed Woodpecker
*Dendrocopos leucotos* · `whbwoo1`

- Jizz — 24–28 cm, wingspan 38–40 cm. Largest of the pied woodpeckers, about blackbird size; bill long.
- Plumage — Upper back pure black, wholly without white shoulder patches; lower back and rump white, greyer towards the tail; wings strongly barred black and white; yellowish forehead patch; black malar reaching the nape; underparts cream, breast and flanks clearly streaked black; undertail-coverts pink. Male: crown red. Female: crown glossy black. Juvenile: duller, flank streaks broader, crown pale red.
