- In the field — Drumming typical: drawn out over two seconds, opening hesitant and powerful then weakening away, like a ping-pong ball dying on the floor. Calls a soft kiuk, a longer kweek, a fast laughing goeddi-goeddi-goeddi.
- Similar species —
  - Great Spotted Woodpecker: Smaller, 20–24 cm, 70–98 g; a large white shoulder patch and white spots, not bars, on the wings; scarlet lower belly; red on the nape, not the crown.
  - Syrian Woodpecker: 23–25 cm; large white shoulder patch, white spots forming three wingbars, white forehead, buffish-white underparts with reddish belly; male's crimson spot on the nape.
  - Gray-headed Woodpecker: Green back, scapulars and coverts, grey head, pale grey underparts; male red only on the forecrown, female none; black lores and a narrow black moustache; 25–26 cm.
  - Eurasian Three-toed Woodpecker: 21–22 cm and never any red: male's crown yellow. Wings and rump black, throat to belly white, flanks barred, back white barred black.

## White-faced Storm-Petrel
*Pelagodroma marina* · `wfspet`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm, wingspan 41–44 cm, 40–70 g. Storm-petrel with rounded, paddle-shaped wings, a short square black tail and strikingly long legs.
- Plumage — Pale brown to grey above — crown grey-brown, nape and back dark grey — with black flight feathers; whole underside, underwing-coverts and face white, cut by a dark grey-brown mask over eye and cheek; grey neck sides run onto the breast sides; black tail set off against an ash-grey rump. Juvenile: broader pale fringes above, shallower tail fork.
- Bare parts — Bill, tarsi, toes and claws black; webs yellow.
- In the field — Fast, restless flight swinging from side to side; at times seems to bounce over the waves with legs dangling. Grey-brown upperparts make it one of the easier petrels to name at sea, even at distance.
- Similar species —
  - Wilson's Storm-Petrel: Smaller, 16–18.5 cm; essentially dark brown in all plumages apart from white rump and flanks — no white face or underparts; feet trail past the square tail.
  - Black-bellied Storm-Petrel: Black with a white rump band, white underwings and flanks, and a broad black stripe down the belly centre; no white face; black legs project past the tail.

## White-headed Duck
*Oxyura leucocephala* · `whhduc1`

- Jizz — 43–48 cm, 580–750 g, both sexes about 700 g. Small, stocky stifftail; head thick, bill broad with a swollen base; tail long, 8–10 cm, stiff and often cocked straight up.
- Plumage — Male: chalk-white head with black crown and a dark spot at the eye, body reddish-brown to grey; some individuals entirely black-headed. Female and juvenile: grey-brown body, white face, a larger dark crown patch reaching down over the eye, and an indistinct dark stripe across the cheek.
- Bare parts — Adult male's bill clearly light blue in spring, otherwise grey; female and juvenile grey-billed.
- In the field — Legs set well behind the centre of gravity: powerful swimmer and diver, clumsy on land. Wings small and arched, yet a skilful and fast flier. Mostly silent.
- Similar species —
  - Ruddy Duck: Male has only the cheek white below a blackish cap, not the whole head, and a more intense chestnut body; female shows a blurry stripe across a pale cheek patch.
  - Fulvous Whistling-Duck: Long-legged buff-brown whistling duck, 45–53 cm, goose-like, with long grey bill, whitish flank stripes and a white rump crescent; no white head, no cocked tail.
  - Red-breasted Goose: A goose, 53–56 cm and 1000–1620 g: sooty black relieved by white, with a bright red breast; nothing stifftailed about the build.
  - Lesser White-fronted Goose: Goose with conspicuous white face, broad black belly bars, bright orange legs and a yellow eye-ring; far bigger, 53–66 cm and 1400–2200 g.

## White-rumped Swift
*Apus caffer* · `whrswi1`

- Jizz — 14–15.5 cm, wingspan 33–37 cm. Slim swift; hand long, narrow and very pointed, wings long and swept back in a crescent; tail long and deeply forked but usually held closed to a needle point.
- Plumage — Very dark blackish-brown throughout; clean-cut white throat patch standing out sharply; narrow but distinct white band across the rump, seen in flight mainly from the side; diffusely paler tips to the secondaries. Sexes alike.
- In the field — Fast, light flight with rapid wingbeats. Rather silent; a chirping trill, sip-sip-sip-siep-sip-sip, recalling a bat at a distance, or a light bouncing twitter.
- Similar species —
  - Common Swift: Larger, 16–17 cm, entirely blackish-brown with only a small pale chin patch invisible at range, and no white rump; call a loud scream.
  - Pallid Swift: Larger, 16–17 cm, chunkier and browner, with paler flight feathers, underparts and rump giving contrast; large white throat patch but no white rump band.

## White-spectacled Bulbul
*Pycnonotus xanthopygos* · `whsbul1`

- Jizz — 19–25 cm, wingspan 20–25 cm. Lively long-tailed bulbul, about the size of a small thrush; tail rather squarely cut.
- Plumage — Head near-black as a dark face mask, contrasting sharply with grey-brown breast and mantle; whitish eye-ring — the spectacles; undertail-coverts lemon-yellow; long tail feathers finely edged white. Sexes alike. Juvenile: hood browner, eye-ring less obvious.
- In the field — Fruit plantations, gardens and cities; sociable and very vocal. Song much like Common Bulbul's but delivered more calmly, without that species' jerkiness.
- Similar species —
  - Common Bulbul: Head dark brown, not near-black, with a dark eye-ring that hardly shows; underparts dull grey and undertail-coverts white in most races, not lemon-yellow; 18–20 cm.

## White-tailed Eagle
*Haliaeetus albicilla* · `whteag`

- Jizz — 66–94 cm, wingspan 178–245 cm, 3.5–7 kg. Huge, bulky, almost blocky eagle; very long thick neck, broad plank-like wings held flat when soaring, short wedge-shaped tail.
- Plumage — Adult mostly brown, with head, neck, upper breast and upper back paler yellowish-ochre; tail short, wedge-shaped and white. Juvenile: dark brown throughout with a dark tail, growing whiter with each moult and fully adult at five years.
- Bare parts — Bill very heavy and yellow, heavier than Golden Eagle's; cere and iris pale yellow; talons large and pale yellow; tarsi unfeathered to the toes.
- In the field — Sails on flat, board-like wings — a flying barn door; the long head and neck project almost as far in front as the tail does behind; deep, full wingbeats. Hardly mistakable in central Europe.
- Similar species —
  - Golden Eagle: Tail longer, never white or wedge-shaped; head projects far less, wings narrower, 1.8–2.34 m span; legs feathered to the toes and bill less massive.
  - Cinereous Vulture: Little else in the sources: separated on size, 98–120 cm and a 2.5–3.1 m span against 66–94 cm and 1.78–2.45 m.
  - Imperial Eagle: Little else in the sources: separated on size, 68–90 cm and a 1.76–2.2 m span against 66–94 cm and up to 2.45 m.
  - Spanish Eagle: Little else in the sources: separated on size, 72–85 cm and 2.5–4.8 kg against 66–94 cm and 3.5–7 kg.

## White-throated Dipper
*Cinclus cinclus* · `whtdip1`

- Jizz — 15–18 cm, wingspan 26–30 cm. Rotund, short-tailed and short-legged, looking thickset from dense plumage; bill straight and strongly compressed laterally; wings short and cupped.
- Plumage — Head and nape plain mid-brown; upperparts slate-grey scaled darker by brown fringes, looking black at distance; wings and tail browner. Throat and upper breast brilliant white, bordered below by a warm chestnut band merging into black on belly and flanks; nominate birds blackish-bellied. Juvenile: scaled grey above, white only on the chin, breast and belly mottled pale grey.
- Bare parts — Bill almost black; legs and iris brown; claws strikingly pale, almost white.
- In the field — Unmistakable in its habitat. Flight fast and straight, no undulations or glides, wingbeats very rapid. Song sweet and wren-like, heard even in midwinter, low and creaking; sharp metallic clink calls in display, normal call a short harsh zil.

## White-throated Kingfisher
*Halcyon smyrnensis* · `whtkin2`

- Jizz — 26–39 cm. Large, thrush-sized kingfisher; bill heavy, wings short and rounded.
- Plumage — Back, wings and tail bright blue to blue-green; head, shoulders, flanks and lower belly chestnut; throat and upper breast chalk-white. In flight a sky-blue carpal patch above and a large white panel below, wing tips black. Sexes alike; juvenile a duller version of the adult.
- Bare parts — Bill and legs coral-red.
- In the field — Flight rapid and direct, the short rounded wings whirring. Vocal and talkative: metallic alarm series and a fast trilling whistle as song.
- Similar species —
  - Pied Kingfisher: Black and white throughout, with no blue or chestnut; white supercilium, black breast bands and a neat crest; upperparts barred black; about 25 cm.
  - Common Kingfisher: Far smaller, about 16 cm with a 25 cm wingspan; rufous underparts and ear-patch, azure back and rump, bill mostly black, legs bright red.

## White-throated Robin
*Irania gutturalis* · `whtrob3`

- Jizz — 16 cm, wingspan 28 cm, 23–30 g. Wagtail-sized chat, larger than European Robin; bill proportionately thick, pointed and black; tail fairly long, legs long.
- Plumage — Breeding male: upperparts lead-grey to blue-grey, face black, white supercilium and a white throat running as a triangle from the bill base to the breast; underparts orange, vent white; tail black above. Female: plainer, grey-brown above with a white supercilium; underparts creamy, orange on the lower breast and flanks, upper breast grey-brown, throat patch faint; tail black above.
- Bare parts — Bill strong and black.
- In the field — On the ground sometimes stands upright with wings and tail held low; often flicks the tail up and down. Male's song a fast twittering from a bush or in flight; call a chis-it double note like White Wagtail's.
- Similar species —
  - Common Nightingale: Plain brown above with a reddish tail, buff to white below, sexes alike; no black face, white throat triangle or orange underparts; 15–17 cm.
  - Isabelline Wheatear: Pale sandy isabelline throughout, sexes alike, with white rump, a broad white tail base and a creamy over-eye streak; no grey upperparts or orange underparts; 16.5 cm.
  - Northern Wheatear: White rump and tail with a black inverted T; summer male grey above with black wings and face mask and only a buff throat; 14–16.5 cm, wingspan 26–32 cm.
  - Black Wheatear: All black, the female dark brown, but for a white rump and mostly white tail with a black inverted T; no white throat or orange underparts; 16–18 cm.

## White-winged Crossbill
*Loxia leucoptera* · `whwcro`

- Jizz — 14.5–17 cm, 25–40 g. Slight crossbill with crossed bill, short legs and forked tail; smaller-headed and smaller-billed than Parrot and Scottish Crossbills, and slimmer with a weaker bill than Red Crossbill.
- Plumage — Male: forehead, crown, nape and upperparts bright raspberry-red, brighter and pinker than other crossbills; scapulars black to blackish-brown edged pink; uppertail-coverts black fringed white. Two broad white wing-bars, the lower broadest inwards, the upper falling away obliquely, and broad sharply defined white tertial tips. Female: head and upperparts greenish-yellow, no pink. Juvenile: grey below, heavily streaked, wing-bars much narrower.
- In the field — Bill specialised on larch cones. Chip call weaker and higher than Red Crossbill's; song recalls Siskin; most distinctive is a nasal cracked piping, like a muted plastic trumpet.
- Similar species —
  - Red Crossbill: No white wing-bars or white tertial tips, though a few show weak bars — check tertials too; duller red male; larger at 20 cm against 14.5–17 cm.
  - Parrot Crossbill: Bulkier and bigger-headed, bill much thicker with the crossed tips often not apparent; no white wing-bars; 16–18 cm, 44–58.2 g.
  - Crimson-winged Finch: Not a crossbill: pale thickset finch with a heavy dull yellowish, uncrossed bill; no white wing-bars or tertial tips; 13–18 cm.
  - African Chaffinch: A chaffinch: blue-grey head, moss-green mantle, pale pinkish underparts, white tail sides and an uncrossed bill; its two white stripes sit on black wings; 13.8–18.5 cm.

## White-winged Snowfinch
*Montifringilla nivalis* · `whwsno1`

- Jizz — 16.5–19 cm. Large, stocky, heavily built passerine, clearly bigger than House Sparrow.
- Plumage — Upperparts dull earth-brown, underparts and rump white, head grey. A long narrow white panel shows on the closed wing; in flight the wing is largely white with black primaries and alula, and the white tail carries a thin black terminal band and blackish central feathers. Summer: black throat bib, veiled by pale fringes in winter. Female slightly paler.
- Bare parts — Bill black in summer, yellow to ivory in winter; legs and iris black.
- In the field — Contrasting black-and-white flight pattern recalls Snow Bunting. Song a chatter full of trills; calls rolling or creaky.
- Similar species —
  - Rock Sparrow: Streaked below, with a bold whitish supercilium, a crown stripe and a yellow throat spot; wings patterned brown, never a big white panel.
  - Italian Sparrow: Male chestnut-crowned and chestnut-backed with white cheeks; smaller, 14–16 cm, and no grey head, white wing panel or white tail sides.
  - Spanish Sparrow: Male chestnut-crowned with heavily black-streaked underparts, female grey-brown and streaked; smaller, 14–16 cm, and no white in wing or tail.
  - House Sparrow: Smaller, 14–18 cm against 16.5–19, brown and grey throughout, with no white wing panel and no black-and-white flight pattern.

## White-winged Tern
*Chlidonias leucopterus* · `whwter`

- Jizz — 20–24 cm, wingspan 50–56 cm. Small marsh tern; bill short and stubby, 22–25 mm from the feathers, decidedly shorter than the head; wings broad, tail short with a straight rear edge.
- Plumage — Breeding adult: head, neck, breast and belly black, back very dark grey, rump white, tail pale grey to whitish; upperwing largely white, underwing-coverts and axillaries black; face tinged yellowish. Female duller. Winter: black replaced by white and pale grey, crown grey-streaked, dark ear-patch, broad white collar. Juvenile: dark brown saddle isolated by white collar and pale grey rump.
- Bare parts — Bill black and stubby; legs red, longer than Black Tern's.
- In the field — Told in winter by being paler overall than Black Tern; rump white in adults, pale grey in juveniles and moulting birds and greying late in the year.
- Similar species —
  - Black Tern: Bill longer, 27 mm and nearly head length, slender and slightly decurved; legs shorter, wings narrower, tail less square; face white, forewing white, underwing not black.
  - Whiskered Tern: Stronger bill (25–34 mm) and more positive flight recalling Common or Arctic Tern; summer adult white-cheeked with red bill and legs, dark grey above and below.
  - Arctic Tern: Bigger, 33–36 cm, wingspan 76–85 cm, with a deeply forked tail; grey above, black cap, white cheeks, dark red bill and legs; never black-bellied.
  - Armenian Gull: A gull: 52–62 cm, wingspan 120–145 cm, 600–960 g, grey-backed and dark-eyed with a black band near the bill tip. Separated on size alone.

## Whooper Swan
*Cygnus cygnus* · `whoswa`

- Jizz — 7–14 kg, among the heaviest flying birds; length given as 140–165 cm and wingspan 200–275 cm across the sources. Neck held straight; head angular, crown sloping into a long triangular bill; tail short.
- Plumage — Adult pure white, head and neck often stained rusty-yellow where the water is rich in tannin or iron. Juvenile: uniform pale grey to grey-brown, paler and plainer than a young Mute Swan, bill pattern as the adult's but pink for the yellow and blackish-grey for the black; whitens through the first and second year.
- Bare parts — Bill 9–12 cm, black with yellow cere and base, the yellow reaching below the nostril and exceeding the black; iris dark brown; eyes small.
- In the field — Swims with the neck straight and the short tail held down flat to the water. Vocal: a loud trumpeting call in groups of three or four notes in flight; wings only softly swishing.
- Similar species —
  - Mute Swan: Neck often S-curved, bill shorter, tail longer and triangular-pointed; juveniles brown-and-white, not uniform grey; wings sing loudly in flight where whooper's only swish.
  - Black Swan: Black-feathered with white flight feathers, bright red bill, grey-black legs; smaller (110–142 cm, 3.7–9 kg), neck the longest of the swans and S-curved.
  - Tundra Swan: Smaller (115–150 cm, 3.4–9.6 kg); bill mostly black with a thin salmon mouthline and less yellow — whooper's always shows more yellow than black, its head more angular.
  - Bar-headed Goose: A goose, far smaller (71–76 cm, 1.87–3.2 kg), pale grey with black bars across a white head; honking call in flight.

## Willow Ptarmigan
*Lagopus lagopus* · `wilpta2`

- Jizz — 35–44 cm, wingspan 55–66 cm, 430–810 g. Medium to large ground grouse; deep-chested, neck fairly long, head small, bill broad and thick, legs short and feathered, tail short and rounded. Sexes about equal in size.
- Plumage — Male in spring: head, neck and upper breast deep red-brown, cut off sharply from white below; tail black; a large red comb over each eye. Late summer: breast and upperparts grey-buff, comb a red line. Female: combs tiny, brown more barred, white belly flecked brown. Winter: both sexes white but for black outer tail. White eye-ring always; wings white year-round.
- Bare parts — Bill short, broad, thick and dark; combs above the eyes red; tarsi and toes feathered.
- In the field — Ground-dwelling, generally below the tree line. Moults actively three or four times a year, to all-white in September. Spreads the short tail in flight. Immatures resemble adults.
- Similar species —
  - Rock Ptarmigan: Some 10% smaller, 34–36 cm, bill slenderer; plumage greyer and white-flecked where this is red-brown; male has a black eye-to-bill stripe in winter; higher, barren ground.
  - Hazel Grouse: Smaller, 35–39 cm, finely patterned grey above with brown wings and chestnut-flecked white underparts; male crested with a white-bordered black throat; tail grey, black-tipped.
  - Black Francolin: Male black with a white cheek-patch, chestnut collar and white-spotted flanks, tail black narrowly white-barred, legs reddish; female brown with chestnut hindneck; 33–36 cm.
  - Chukar: Light brown back, grey breast, buff belly, white face with a sharply defined black gorget, rufous-streaked flanks; bill coral red, legs red; 32–35 cm.

## Willow Tit
*Poecile montanus* · `wiltit1`

- Jizz — 11–14 cm, 8–14 g; wingspan 17–20.5 cm. Large-headed tit with a thin bill and a notably thick bull neck.
- Plumage — Long dull sooty-black cap running back onto the mantle, and a black bib often broadening downwards; cheeks white, reaching right up to the nape; back grey-brown, underparts buff with distinctly rufous flanks. Pale buff edges to the secondaries make a light patch on the closed wing. Sexes alike, juvenile like the adult. Eastern birds much paler than western.
- Bare parts — Bill short, stubby and black; legs grey; iris black.
- In the field — Commonest call a nasal zee, zee, zee, though the notes vary considerably; sometimes a double ipsee, ipsee repeated four or five times.
- Similar species —
  - Gray-headed Chickadee: Bigger, 12.5–14 cm and 11–14.3 g; fluffier, dark brown head, cinnamon-brown mantle contrasting with the grey wings, larger all-black bib, rustier flanks.
  - Marsh Tit: Cap glossy blue-black, not dull sooty; bib small; ear-coverts dusky brown, not white to the nape; no pale wing patch; flanks less rufous, neck slimmer.
  - Sombre Tit: Larger, 13–14 cm; brownish to grey-black cap spreads further down the head-sides and the bigger bib cuts the off-white cheek to a narrow triangle; head often peaked.
  - Eurasian Blue Tit: Blue crown, wings and tail, yellowish-green back, sulphur-yellow underparts with a dark belly line, white cheeks ringed dark blue; smaller, 10.5–12 cm.

## Willow Warbler
*Phylloscopus trochilus* · `wlwwar`

- Jizz — 11–12.5 cm, wingspan 17–22 cm, 7–15 g. Small, slim, elegant leaf warbler; bill fine, pointed, longish and pale; primary projection long, about the length of the tertials.
- Plumage — Greenish-brown to olive-green above, crown and mantle olive; off-white to yellowish below, throat and breast pale green-yellow, belly and vent white to yellow-white; strong pale yellow supercilium, longest and clearest behind the eye; wings plain, no wing-bars. Juvenile: yellower below. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Legs pale pinkish-yellow to brown-pink; bill pale, fine and pointed.
- In the field — Song a soft, wistful descending series opening with high notes and fading away, much sung in spring and early summer; call a soft disyllabic rising hoo-eet, stressed on the first syllable.
- Similar species —
  - Common Chiffchaff: Dumpier (10–12 cm), dark-legged, with short primary projection, short whitish supercilium and a fine dark bill; call a monosyllabic hweet, song a repeated zilp-zalp.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Smaller (9.5–11 cm) with bold double yellowish-white wing-bars, yellow-margined tertials and a long yellow supercilium; the willow warbler shows no wing-bars.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above, off-white below, with a single pale wing-bar in southern and western birds; song a high jerky trill, not a descending whistle.
  - Western Bonelli's Warbler: Plain grey-green back with green-toned rump and wings and whitish underparts — no yellow wash below and no bold yellow supercilium; bill small, legs brown.

## Wilson's Storm-Petrel
*Oceanites oceanicus* · `wispet`

- Jizz — 16–19 cm; wingspan 38–42 cm. Small dark storm-petrel; broad wings held less angled; square-cut tail; long legs, the feet projecting just beyond the tail in flight.
- Plumage — Essentially dark brown in all plumages but for a broad white rump reaching well down the sides, and white flanks; diffuse pale band along the upper wing-coverts; underwing uniformly dark with a faint bronze gloss, lacking any white lining. Sexes alike.
- Bare parts — Toe webbing yellow, spotted black in pre-breeding birds.
- In the field — Flight alternates short glides with loose wingbeats; glides more directly and for longer stretches on flat wings; patters and hops over the surface when feeding, wings held more raised. Follows ships; rarely seen from land.
- Similar species —
  - White-faced Storm-Petrel: White below with a white face and black phalarope-like eye-mask; pale brown-grey back, rump and wings; slightly larger, 19–21 cm; dark bill, tarsi and toes.
  - Black-bellied Storm-Petrel: White underwing and white flanks with a broad black stripe down the centre of the belly, sometimes broken; Wilson's is uniformly dark below. Feet black, not yellow-webbed.

## Wood Duck
*Aix sponsa* · `wooduc`

- Jizz — 47–54 cm, wingspan 66–75 cm, 454–862 g. Medium-sized perching duck, about three-quarters the length of a Mallard; both sexes crested, tail unusually long; eye the largest of any waterfowl.
- Plumage — Male: head and neck black glossed green and violet, nape drawn out into a crest, two white lines each side — from the bill base and from behind the eye; white throat notching up the cheek; breast purple with white spots; flanks red-violet, orange-streaked; belly white; speculum blue-green, white-edged behind. Female: olive-brown and grey, white eye-ring, whitish throat, white belly.
- Bare parts — Male: iris red, bill reddish with a black tip and pale yellow stripe below, legs and feet orange-yellow. Female: bill dark grey, legs yellowish.
- In the field — Perching duck, using tree holes cut by Pileated Woodpecker. Male's call a rising whistle, jeeeeee; female a drawn-out rising do weep do weep when flushed, and a sharp cr-r-ek alarm.
- Similar species —
  - Mandarin Duck: Male has a small red bill, large white crescent over the eye, reddish face and whiskers, purple breast with two vertical white bars and orange sail feathers; slightly smaller.
  - Gadwall: Uncrested; male patterned grey with a black rear end and brilliant white speculum; female mallard-brown with an orange-edged bill, white speculum and white belly.
  - Red-crested Pochard: Diving duck; male has a rounded orange head, red bill, black breast, white flanks; female pale brown with darker back and crown, whitish face; larger, 45–55 cm.
  - Chiloe Wigeon: White cheeks and forehead below an iridescent green-blue cap, white auricular patch, black-and-white barred breast, bluish-grey black-tipped bill; sexes nearly alike.

## Wood Lark
*Lullula arborea* · `woolar1`

- Jizz — 13.5–15 cm, roughly 20% shorter than skylark. Tail strikingly short; wings short and broad in flight; bill slender.
- Plumage — Dull pale brown above, crown and upper mantle finely streaked dark and pale buff, rump plain brown; underparts white, upper breast finely dark-streaked. Bold white supercilia meeting in a V on the nape; ear-coverts rufous; broad white malar, dark grey chin-stripe, white throat. Black-and-white greater primary coverts mark the bend of the folded wing; tail tipped white.
- Bare parts — Bill slender, brown-grey, paler at the base; legs yellowish brown to flesh-coloured.
- In the field — Crest small and usually inconspicuous. Song a melodious warbling lu-lu-lu, toolooeet toolooeet, given in a fluttering spiralling song-flight, then circling at height, and also from ground or branch; starts early in the year.
- Similar species —
  - Mediterranean Short-toed Lark: 13–14 cm; duller, dark-streaked greyish-brown above and white below with fine breast streaking, and a short stubby bill; supercilium pale but no white V meeting on the nape.
  - Crested Lark: 17–18 cm, span 29–38 cm, bulkier about head and body; crest conspicuous at all times; short tail with light brown — not white — outer feathers.
  - Dupont's Lark: 17–18 cm; slim, long-necked and long-legged with a fine slightly curved bill, thin pale crown stripe and streaked breast. Song a thin ventriloquial whistle at dawn, dusk or night.
  - Eurasian Skylark: 16–19 cm, about a fifth longer; both the tail sides and the wing's rear edge are white-edged as it flies away, and the crest is short and blunt.

## Wood Sandpiper
*Tringa glareola* · `woosan`

- Jizz — 19–21 cm, wingspan 36–40 cm, 50–90 g — barely starling-sized. Long-legged, delicate shank, built like a small redshank; bill short and fine.
- Plumage — Summer adult: back coarsely vermiculated grey, white and beige, brown with darker mottling; small diffuse brownish spots on neck and breast, merging gradually into the white belly. Winter: greyer, breast plainer. Juvenile: browner, finely spotted beige above. Bold white supercilium in all plumages, running well behind the eye to the nape. Small, weakly contrasting white rump-patch; underwing pale grey-brown.
- Bare parts — Bill short, fine, grey-black; legs long, pale yellow-green.
- In the field — Moves calmly and unhurriedly; migrants often approachable to a few metres. Legs project well beyond the tail in flight. Flight call a three-syllable whistle on one pitch, jiff-iff-iff; alarm ipp-ipp-ipp; song a fast yodelling ty-lyllti-lyllti-lyll in display flight.
- Similar species —
  - Common Greenshank: Larger — bigger than Common Redshank, itself 24–30 cm against this bird's 19–21; bill long, grey-based, looking upturned; white wedge up the back; call teu-teu-teu.
  - Common Redshank: Bigger, 24–30 cm; legs red and bill red with black tip, not fine and dark over yellow-green legs; shows white up the back and on the wings in flight.
  - Green Sandpiper: Plumper and darker, blackish-grey; underwing almost black; supercilium ends at the eye; breast cut off sharply from white belly; rump brilliant white; legs darker green.
  - Spotted Redshank: Much larger, 29–33 cm; black when breeding, very pale in winter; legs and bill red; white oval on the back in flight; call a creaking teu-it.

## Wood Warbler
*Phylloscopus sibilatrix* · `woowar`

- Jizz — 11–13 cm, wingspan 19–24 cm, 8–13 g. The largest leaf warbler; long-winged with a long primary projection and a short but broad tail.
- Plumage — Green above, belly white, breast yellowish-white; throat and long, clear supercilium lemon-yellow; pale contrasting fringes to tertials, flight feathers and greater coverts. Sexes alike.
- In the field — Two songs: a bright metallic trill accelerating, pit-pit-pitpitpit-t-t-ttt over two to three seconds, likened to a coin spinning on marble; and a falling piyy-piyy-piyy. Call sib or tüh, or a single short piy.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Greenish brown above, off-white to yellowish below with no lemon throat or bold yellow supercilium; legs pale pinkish-yellow; song a simple descending whistle.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Duller, brown-washed green with only a short whitish supercilium, dark legs and short primary projection; no yellow throat or upper breast.
  - Yellow-browed Warbler: Smaller, 9.5–11 cm, with two prominent yellowish-white wingbars and yellow-margined tertials; long yellow supercilium but no yellow throat or breast.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above, off-white below, with a single wing bar; lacks the yellow throat, breast and bold supercilium.

## Woodchat Shrike
*Lanius senator* · `wooshr1`

- Jizz — 17–19 cm, tail about 8 cm; up to 50 g. Compact, big-headed shrike with a strong toothed bill and fairly long tail; barely larger than Red-backed Shrike but 10–20% heavier and distinctly bulkier.
- Plumage — Male: upperparts largely black, underparts nearly white; crown and nape rust to orange-brown; scapulars and rump white, sometimes closing in a V on the back; white primary bases show as a wing-window at rest, a broad white band in flight; tail black. Female: duller, black greyer, crown more orange, slightly smaller. Juvenile: vermiculated brown, grey and white above, buff below.
- In the field — Black forehead runs through eye and cheek as a mask, sharply set off from the whitish throat. Only juveniles and immatures recall other shrikes, and hybrids with Red-backed Shrike occur. Race badius lacks the white primary patch.
- Similar species —
  - Red-backed Shrike: Male reddish above with a grey head, not black with a chestnut crown, and pink-tinged underparts; smaller, 16–18 cm; juveniles alike, but this bird's are paler and greyer.
  - Iberian Gray Shrike: Dark grey above with greyish-pink breast and belly, wide black mask and a clear white supercilium; white on the primaries only; no chestnut crown.
  - Great Gray Shrike: Much larger, 22–26 cm and 60–70 g, thrush-sized, wingspan 30–36 cm; grey above, with neither the chestnut crown nor the black back.
  - Lesser Gray Shrike: Black limited to forecrown, nape and face; hindcrown and back pale bluish-grey, rump paler; underparts white flushed pink on breast and belly; crown never chestnut.

## Yelkouan Shearwater
*Puffinus yelkouan* · `medshe1`

- Jizz — 30–39 cm, wingspan 70–90 cm. Medium-sized shearwater; wings held stiffly at right angles to the body so the bird looks like a flying cross; feet project well behind the tail, giving a drawn-out pointed rear end.
- Plumage — Upperparts blackish brown, the outer wing darkest, the whole upperside more brown-grey than Manx's blackish; underparts sharply cut off and mainly plain white; dark wingtips, dark tail band and a dark diagonal band across the underwing-coverts; flanks, axillaries, undertail and underwing-coverts variably browner; underwing less contrasting than Manx, the primaries grey rather than black.
- In the field — Shears low over the sea, dipping side to side on stiff wings with few beats, wingtips almost touching the water, turning dark then white. Silent at sea; colonies raucous at night, cackling higher and more drawn out than Manx.
- Similar species —
  - Manx Shearwater: Blacker above and cleanly black-and-white, underwing more contrasting with blackish primaries; feet project less beyond the tail; 30–38 cm. Strays are often not safely identifiable.
  - Barolo Shearwater: Pale face with a darkly contrasting eye, silvery panel in the upperwing, shorter more rounded wings and blue feet.
  - Balearic Shearwater: Brown above and dirty white below, much less contrasted; 30–40 cm, wingspan 76–93 cm; the two breed together on Menorca and some birds cannot be assigned in the field.
  - Bulwer's Petrel: Wholly brown with a long pointed tail and a buoyant twisting flight, picking food from the surface; smaller-bodied, 25–29 cm.

## Yellow-billed Chough
*Pyrrhocorax graculus* · `yebcho1`

- Jizz — 34–39 cm, wingspan 65–85 cm, 160–277 g. Mid-sized corvid; bill short and curved, its base thick with nasal bristles; tail proportionally long, 12–14 cm, wings short; legs strong but short.
- Plumage — Glossy black throughout. Sexes alike, the male averaging slightly larger. Juvenile: duller, with a dull yellow bill and brownish legs.
- Bare parts — Bill short and yellow; legs and toes red; iris dark brown.
- In the field — Flight buoyant, swift and acrobatic on loose deep beats, turning by fanning the tail. Commonest call a buzzing zirrrrr.
- Similar species —
  - Red-billed Chough: Bill long, slender and red, not short and yellow; slightly bigger at 39–41 cm, with longer wings and a proportionally shorter tail.
  - Eurasian Jackdaw: Plumage unglossed: pale grey nape and cheeks against a black cap, underparts slate-grey; bill short, stout and black, legs black; 34–39 cm.
  - Northern Nutcracker: Chocolate-brown, heavily spotted white, with all-white vent and white tail corners; broad-winged and short-tailed, flight undulating; bill black; 32–38 cm.
  - Eurasian Jay: Pinkish-brown body, black moustache, white rump and a bright blue black-barred wing patch; 34–35 cm, wingspan 52–58 cm.

## Yellow-billed Loon
*Gavia adamsii* · `yebloo`

- Jizz — 76–97 cm, wingspan 135–160 cm, 3.7–6.4 kg. Largest loon, heavier than a Greylag Goose; long low body, steep forehead and a big, slightly upturned bill.
- Plumage — Breeding: head and neck black with a purple gloss; upper mantle in dense rows of large white squares, rear back and flanks finely white-spotted; breast, belly and underwing-coverts pure white. Non-breeding: upperparts dark grey; head and hindneck dull pale grey-brown, passing to white on throat and foreneck, with a large white area round the eye. Juvenile: much as non-breeding.
- Bare parts — Bill large, pale yellow to ivory, slightly upturned — pale grey with a darker base in winter; legs and feet grey; iris wine-red.
- In the field — Swims with the head held so the bill points slightly upward; flies with the neck extended. Territorial call a ghostly wailing tu-u-i'-ti, tu-i'-ti, deeper than Common Loon's; also a laughing alarm and a low moaning note.
- Similar species —
  - Arctic Loon: Smaller, 58–75 cm; breeding birds grey-headed with a black throat patch and white throat-side lines; bill dark and slim, not big, pale yellow and tilted up.
  - Red-throated Loon: Smallest and lightest loon, 53–69 cm against 76–97 cm, with a sharply pointed dark bill rather than a heavy ivory one.
  - Common Loon: Very close in size, slightly smaller; separated by the bill — ivory-yellow, slightly upturned and carried tilted up here, dark in Common Loon.

## Yellow-browed Warbler
*Phylloscopus inornatus* · `yebwar3`

- Jizz — 9.5–11 cm, 4–9 g. One of the smallest leaf warblers, clearly smaller than a Chiffchaff; bill thin and pointed.
- Plumage — Greenish to greyish-green above with blackish wings, whitish below. Two prominent yellowish-white wingbars from the tips of the greater and median coverts, the greater-covert bar the longer; tertials broadly yellow-margined; long broad yellow supercilium with a dark eye-stripe beneath it. Some show a faint pale central crown-stripe, many do not.
- Bare parts — Legs brownish-pink.
- In the field — Almost constantly in motion and arboreal, so hard to watch, though not shy. Usually detected first by voice: call piercing, often disyllabic tseeweest, strikingly loud for the size; song a high medley of whistles.
- Similar species —
  - Willow Warbler: Larger at 11–12.5 cm, plain greenish-brown wings with no wingbars, only an off-white supercilium; legs pale pinkish-yellow, bill longer and paler; call a disyllabic hoo-eet.
  - Common Chiffchaff: Distinctly larger, 10–12 cm and 6–10 g; dumpy, brown-washed dull green, short whitish supercilium, no wingbars, dark legs and short primary projection.
  - Greenish Warbler: Greyish-green above with a single wing bar, not two; bill thin and lacking a dark tip to the lower mandible.
  - Western Bonelli's Warbler: Plain grey-green back with green-toned rump and wings, whitish below, small pointed bill and brown legs; larger, 10.5–12 cm, and the source describes no wingbars.

## Yellow-crowned Bishop
*Euplectes afer* · `yecbis`

- Jizz — 9.5–12 cm, 11–20 g. Tiny, chunky, very short-tailed weaver, about the size of a blue tit; bill short and conical.
- Plumage — Breeding male: forehead, crown, hindcrown and nape brilliant golden-yellow, hindneck feathers black-tipped; head-sides, chin, throat and belly black, with a broad black collar on the hindneck; yellow shoulder patch, lower back and rump yellow; wings and tail brown. Female and non-breeding male: pale brown above with dark streaking, pale supercilium, off-white below, finely streaked on breast and flanks.
- Bare parts — Breeding male's bill black, otherwise horn as in the female; legs and feet pinkish brown; iris brown.
- In the field — Sexes alike out of season — the male reverts to the streaked brown of the female; in breeding dress the vivid black-and-yellow pattern is unmistakable.

## Yellow-legged Gull
*Larus michahellis* · `yelgul1`

- Jizz — 52–68 cm, 550–1,600 g; wingspan 120–155 cm. Size very variable — small females barely exceed a Common Gull, big males near Great Black-backed. Compact, big-headed, long-legged; bill stout and blunt; wing-tips project 5–7 cm past the tail at rest.
- Plumage — Adult: head pure white even in autumn; back grey, a shade darker than Herring Gull, paler than Lesser Black-backed; more extensive black in the wing-tip, with few white mirrors. First-year paler on head, rump and underparts than Herring Gull, recalling young Great Black-backed; dark bill and eyes, dark flight feathers, sharp black tail-band. Adult-like by second winter.
- Bare parts — Legs bright yellow; bill yellow with a large red gonys spot spreading onto the upper mandible; iris lemon-yellow, orbital ring red.
- In the field — Four age-classes; adult plumage only in the fourth calendar year. No sexual dimorphism in plumage.
- Similar species —
  - European Herring Gull: Legs pink at all ages, not yellow; mantle a shade paler; orbital ring dark yellow, not red; head streaked in autumn; less black, more white in the wing-tip.
  - Great Black-backed Gull: The world's largest gull, 61–79 cm and up to 2.3 kg, with a much darker back; only the biggest male Yellow-legged approaches it in size.
  - Lesser Black-backed Gull: Mantle distinctly darker grey; smaller and slighter, 49–64 cm and 450–1,300 g. Red orbital ring is shared, so judge on mantle tone and leg colour.
  - Iceland Gull: Sources give only structure: slender, light and thin-billed, 50–66 cm and 480–1,100 g — finer-billed than the stout, blunt Yellow-legged, and usually smaller than a Herring Gull.

## Yellowhammer
*Emberiza citrinella* · `yellow2`

- Jizz — 15.5–17 cm, wingspan 23–30 cm, 20–36.5 g. Large, long-tailed bunting.
- Plumage — Male: brilliant yellow head and throat, narrow dark eye-stripe behind the eye, longish dark moustachial; back heavily streaked brown, rump unstreaked rufous, belly yellow, breast and flanks rusty with fine streaks, outer tail feathers white. Female: duller and browner, more streaked on crown, breast and flanks, throat still yellowish. Juvenile: much duller, rump paler. Winter birds less strongly marked.
- In the field — Song five to eleven strophes, as though the bird were counting: tju, tju, tju, tju, tju, tju, tjuuu. Dark tail with white outer edges catches the eye in flight.
- Similar species —
  - Corn Bunting: No yellow at all; lark-like, streaked grey-brown above, whitish below with a streaked gorget round the throat, plain brown tail, dark eye, yellowish mandibles. 16–19 cm.
  - Reed Bunting: Male black-headed and black-throated with a white collar and white underparts, back heavily barred brown; female streaked brown-headed. Smaller, 13.5–15.5 cm.
  - Rustic Bunting: Little else in the sources: separated here on size, 13–15 cm against 15.5–17 cm.
  - Cirl Bunting: Like a small yellowhammer, 15–16.5 cm; male has a black crown, eyestripe and throat and a greenish breast band; female's rump streaked grey-brown, not rufous.

## Zitting Cisticola
*Cisticola juncidis* · `zitcis1`

- Jizz — 10–12 cm. Tiny, plump, short-winged warbler; tail broad, short, rounded and graduated, often fanned and flicked.
- Plumage — Sandy-brown above, heavily streaked black; whitish to leather-yellow below, the buff deeper on the female; rump rufous. Tail dark beneath with a broad white rear edge above and pale tips. Male has less crown streaking and bolder back marks than the female; supercilium only hinted; no nuchal collar.
- Bare parts — Breeding male's bill dark, the female's pale; legs dark beige to orange.
- In the field — Skulks in grass outside the breeding season and is then very hard to see; song a monotonous short sharp zit-zit-zit, given in a bounding song-flight.
- Similar species —
  - Delicate Prinia: Sources offer no field separation: only that its darkest race is browner and more heavily streaked above with brighter buff flanks, and no measurements at all.
  - Graceful Prinia: Long tapering tail, each feather tipped black and white, and short rounded wings; 10–11 cm; keeps to shrub and tall grass with thick undergrowth.
